--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page serves as the second page of the preface (Sign 1, Page 2). It follows the initial introduction and establishes the author's methodological approach to interpreting the Song of Songs, moving from philosophical and literal interpretations to a mystical, soul-centered, and ultimately traditional Midrashic perspective.</meta>

# Preface

**Peace** be upon you. It was our will and vision to select this Scroll <note>Hebrew: *Megillah*, referring here to the Song of Songs</note> for various reasons. Some interpret it as an allegory of the **Assembly of Israel** <term>Knesset Yisrael: the mystical personification of the Jewish people</term> and the **Holy One, Blessed be He**, where the Assembly of Israel yearns and leans upon her Beloved, and He responds to her words. The words of the *Midrash* <term>traditional homiletic biblical interpretations</term> are founded upon this path. Others interpret it as being about the **Torah** and those who study it—the students' desire to merit the Torah in both its revealed and hidden aspects. Because of the "high waters" <note>a metaphor for deep wisdom</note> of the Torah, these secrets are revealed to them. On this path is the perfect sage, the divine Kabbalist, **our teacher Rabbi Solomon ibn Alkabetz the Levite**, may his memory be a blessing for the life of the world to come, in his commentary. Yet others interpret it as being about **Intellect and Matter**, secrets drawn from the books of the philosophers and commentators.

I am far from their ways, for my heart has followed the paths of intellectual inquiry and the plain meanings <note>Hebrew: *peshat*</note> of the Torah from my youth until this day. From the footsteps of our holy Rabbis, of blessed memory, I have not strayed right or left in the secret of faith. There are those who said these are the words of the **Soul**, which is hewn from beneath the Throne of Glory. In its later state, it yearns and longs for the spiritual matters it once enjoyed and took pleasure in before coming into this world. By its special virtue and with this late support, this Scroll revolves around the soul's journey. Although this path is dear to me and sweet to my palate, and I thought to choose it above all, nevertheless, I have decided to choose the first path <note>The relationship between God and Israel</note> because it is the way of our Sages of blessed memory in the *Midrashim*.

To reach the core of its explanation, I place before the reader the words of the **Zohar** <term>The primary work of Jewish mysticism</term> which were brought in the portion of *Terumah* in the name of Rabbi Yose. From these, we learn the explanation of the parts of this Scroll, from the distant to the near. I know they will be sweeter to the reader's palate than honey and the honeycomb, for he will see they are built upon a foundation of a "tested stone," a precious cornerstone, a source of living water: the words of the Sages of Truth <note>a common term for Kabbalists</note>, whose every word is like a coal of fire. Happy is the one who guards them; they descend into the inner chambers of the heart to show and teach the way in which one should walk and the deed one should do to "behold the beauty of the Lord and to meditate in His temple." 

These are the things seen and placed before the reader in the introduction to the commentary of this Scroll. It is stated in the *Tosefta* <note>referring to the *Tosefta de-Shirata*, a mystical supplement in the Zohar</note> of this Song: It is the sum of the whole Torah; it is the sum of the **Work of Creation**; it is the sum of the secret of the **Patriarchs**; it is the sum of the **Exile in Egypt** and when Israel left Egypt; and it is the supplement regarding the Sea. It is the sum of the **Ten Commandments** and the standing at **Mount Sinai**, and when Israel walked in the wilderness until they entered the Land and the **Holy Temple** was built. It is the sum of the **Holy Crown** <term>Keter: the highest divine emanation or Sephirah</term> that ascended in love and joy. It is the sum of the **Exile of Israel** among the nations and their **Redemption**. It is the sum of the **Resurrection of the Dead** until the day that is a **Sabbath to the Lord**—what was, what is, and what is destined to be in the final seventh day. Thus, the Sabbath of the Lord is entirely contained within the Song of Songs. Therefore we have learned: anyone who recites a verse of the Song of Songs and treats it like a common song in a banquet hall, the Torah girds itself in sackcloth, ascends before the Holy One, Blessed be He, and says: "Your children have made me into a common song in the banquet halls." And the Torah ascends—
->**and says**<-

<summary>The author evaluates four methods of interpreting the Song of Songs—allegorical, educational, philosophical, and psychological—ultimately choosing the Midrashic allegory of God and Israel. He cites the Zohar to argue that the Song of Songs contains the entire history and mystical structure of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Midrash, Kabbalah, Zohar, Solomon ibn Alkabetz, Allegory, Soul, Assembly of Israel, Redemption, Tosefta de-Shirata</keywords>

This follows the discussion of the Song of Songs by providing the "behind-the-scenes" details of the book's physical creation.</meta>

# The Printers’ Apology :

The printer **says**: It is known to all who enter the gates of our city <note>a flowery idiom referring to the local community or the public</note> that the nature of our work is such that it is impossible in the craft of printing for some error not to occur. [We] beg favor [for such errors], for because of this <unclear>care</unclear>, the seal was set upon us to choose and to examine all the holy [writings]. [This was done by] the exalted sage, our teacher and Rabbi, **Gedaliah Cordovero**, may his Rock and Redeemer protect him <note>Gedaliah Cordovero (1563–1625) was the son of the famous Safed Kabbalist Moses Cordovero; he was a prominent editor and publisher of his father's works and other mystical texts</note>, who endeavored with all his strength to verify and correct our judgment <note>referring to the editing and proofreading process</note>. He added from his own [wisdom], being one of the unique individuals of the generation, alongside the young man who endeavored to take his <unclear>fixed portion</unclear>, and so with his efforts and such, we trusted. They shall be prepared to print the commentary on the **Five Scrolls** <term>Five Scrolls (Hamesh Megillot): The biblical books of Song of Songs, Ruth, Lamentations, Ecclesiastes, and Esther</term> according to the commentary of

<meta>This page begins the formal commentary on the Song of Songs, following the printer's apology from the previous page. It moves from the technical aspects of publication to the exegetical analysis of the biblical text, starting with the very first verse.</meta>

<header>Commentary on the Song of Songs</header>

# **The Song of Songs, which is Solomon's:** It is stated <margin>in the Midrash Rabbah on this verse</margin>
Why [does it say], "And furthermore, because [the Preacher] was wise" <note>Ecclesiastes 12:9</note>? He weighed the matter carefully. Some say this means he bent his ear to listen. "And furthermore," Solomon weighed [the words] and he understood from his own mind. "Weighed" here means he needed to bend his ear to listen; "And furthermore," he weighed them through the **Holy Spirit** <term>Holy Spirit (Ruach HaKodesh): Divine inspiration or a level of prophecy</term>, etc. By this, they [the Sages] intended to establish and settle all the words of this Priest <note>referring to the author/commentator's perspective</note>, which serve as a preface to praise and glorify all the words of this King <note>Solomon</note>, which are worthy of one bending their ear to hear. This is true whether considering the words themselves—for even if the subject matter were of the lowliest sort, it would be worthy for one to make his ear like a funnel to hear them—and certainly regarding this "Song of Songs," which is a song of the highest quality and the choice of songs in its own right, reaching the ultimate purpose. This is even more true regarding the "material" <note>original: "חומר" (chomer), referring to the substance or authorship</note>, as he [Solomon] was the most enlightened of all the wise; thus it says, "which is Solomon's." This would be the case even if the substance came only from his own wisdom; how much more so since he weighed it through the Holy Spirit. The detail of the "Holy Spirit" is precise in the text because it is written "Song of Songs," rather than "A Song of Solomon" or "Solomon's Songs," just as it is written "The Proverbs of Solomon" or "The Words of Kohelet." Rather, "Song of Songs" signifies a song that encompasses many songs within it. This is only possible with words spoken through the Holy Spirit, for they can be divided into many facets, all of which are true and intended. However, the words of a [merely] wise man contain only a single intention, which is whatever his mind intended according to the truth as he saw it. Through the words of our Sages, of blessed memory, the reason why his father's name and his royal title are not mentioned here—unlike in Proverbs and Ecclesiastes—is settled. Since the intention here is to highlight the wisdom of Solomon above all other sages, as stated, the text mentions "Solomon" alone to indicate that the scripture refers to that quality which he possessed more than any other created being.

Furthermore, <margin>it is stated there in the Midrash by Rabbi Yochanan in the name of Rabbi Chama, in the name of Rabbi Shimon bar Abba</margin>: This song and praise is said for the One who will eventually rest the Holy Spirit upon us, like material before Him. There are many songs among all the songs; either He praises them <note>Israel</note>, or they praise Him. In the "palace of the kiss" <note>a poetic or mystical reference to a place of divine connection</note> they praise Him and say, "This is my God and I will glorify Him" <note>Exodus 15:2</note>, and in the "palace of the kiss" He praises them, saying, "He made him ride on the high places of the earth" <note>Deuteronomy 32:13</note>. But here [in the Song of Songs], they praise Him and He praises them. The Holy One, Blessed be He, praises them: "Behold, you are beautiful, my love" <note>Song of Songs 1:15</note>, and they praise Him: "Behold, you are beautiful, my beloved, truly pleasant" <note>Song of Songs 1:16</note>. And whoever has merited this intention, the author of the discourse turned to the parable, etc. Regarding the verse, it says "Which is the Song of Songs," the general rule being "A Song for Solomon," and they are at the source of the lineage... <note>The text here becomes highly technical and syntactical</note> Why "Song of Songs"? It should have written "Solomon's Songs," just like "The Proverbs of Solomon" or "The Words of Kohelet." Furthermore, why use the word "Which" (*Asher*)? It should have written "The Song of Songs for Solomon." Furthermore, why did he not mention his father's name and his kingship as in Proverbs and Ecclesiastes? To settle all this, [the Midrash] says that the intention of the scripture is not merely to inform us of the name of the author and the singer of these songs, but to say that it is fitting for the collective of Israel to say songs and praise to the One who will eventually rest the Holy Spirit upon us. Through the Holy Spirit that shall rest and be poured upon us from on high, we will have the preparation so that "many songs shall be said before Him." This "Song of Songs" is a song that precedes the resting of the Holy Spirit; the "Songs" [plural] are those that follow after it, as stated. This was the intention of the author when he said, "Let songs be said... many songs shall be said before Him," meaning after the Holy Spirit rests upon us. The matter of the Holy Spirit is precise in the text from the words "Which is Solomon's" (*Asher li-Shlomo*), rather than simply "To Solomon" (*li-Shlomo*), to say that the many songs before Him will be of the same type as those "which" were said to Solomon, which were spoken through the Holy Spirit... He said further: "Among all the songs," etc., implying that it is fitting that in it...

-> of the wise <-

<meta>catchword: with the songs</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the opening commentary on the first verse of the Song of Songs, focusing on Solomon's divine inspiration. It explains the unique phrasing of the title, arguing that the term "Song of Songs" and the lack of Solomon’s royal titles signify that the work was composed through the Holy Spirit rather than human wisdom alone.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as a formal preface to the commentary. It transitions from the general praise of King Solomon’s wisdom (discussed on the previous page) to the specific structural and spiritual goals of this manuscript. The author outlines how the work will trace the spiritual history of Israel from the building of the First Temple to the eventual Resurrection of the Dead.</meta>

### Preface

And I have thus undertaken this structure, which must be guarded, to place a crown upon the head of every man, every king, and the matter of the "Secret of the Rows" <note>original: "סוד הטורים" (Sod HaTurim); this likely refers to a specific mystical method of interpreting rows of text or a reference to a kabbalistic work</note>, etc.

<margin>Behold</margin> it is clear to us, according to the plain law of all the things included in the sections of this scroll <note>the Megillah, specifically the Song of Songs</note>, and according to these words, a strong connection exists within the collective of all the matters mentioned in the "Secret of the Holy Chapters," may their memory be for a blessing. Some of these are arranged within its specific parts and details, and some are proven within their internal progression for the needs of specific scholarly investigations <term>investigations (Derushim): homiletic or legal inquiries into the deeper meaning of scripture</term>, as the eyes of the observer will see the uprightness of the path through careful scrutiny. For the Lord desired it in some small measure, and not a single word shall fall; nothing shall be missing or excluded from this scroll in the order of its progression. 

My own speech lacks the strength to sustain me, for I have followed the words of the mouth of the wise one who speaks—Solomon—only slightly. He opened his words and the knowledge of his lips spoke clearly concerning that which came before, and concerning the goodness of his own time and the perfection of his dwelling <note>referring to the era of the First Temple</note>. For their days were like his days following the building of the Holy Temple, reaching the height of spiritual and physical degrees and perfection. He sought to take what is spiritual and elevate it to the seat of perfection, just as it was on the day of the standing at Mount Sinai, when "their filth ceased" <note>a Talmudic reference to the Israelites becoming spiritually purified and immortal at the revelation of the Torah</note>, so that the essential attachment with the Blessed Name might be increased. We shall write of this further in the section "In the Lord he chose well," as it is told by his **Holy Spirit** <term>Holy Spirit (Ruach HaKodesh): Divine inspiration or a level of prophecy</term>, may its memory be for a blessing.

The order of the investigations of its parts, which were explained above, moves from time to time and from matter to matter. According to the order of the ages, all these things have turned toward this point—from that ancient time until the era that includes the Resurrection of the Dead and until the "Day that is entirely Sabbath" <note>original Aramaic: "יומא דכוליה שבת" (Yoma de-kulei Shabbat); a mystical reference to the Messianic age or the World to Come</note> for the Lord. All other matters will come one by one within the text, as will be explained previously. And after these things:

->We shall come to the explanation with the help of<-
->He who prepares strength for the weary, and to those with no<-
->might, He increases<-
->power.<-
->For you, may they be acceptable, etc.<-

<summary>This preface outlines the author's intent to explain the "Secret of the Rows" within the Song of Songs, linking the text's spiritual perfection to the era of King Solomon and the Sinai revelation. The author frames the commentary as a chronological and spiritual journey leading toward the Resurrection and the eternal Sabbath.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal introduction to the commentary on the Song of Songs. It moves from a general discussion of Solomon's wisdom to a specific analysis of the book's title and the nature of its divine inspiration compared to other biblical works like Proverbs and Ecclesiastes.</meta>

->Commentary on the Song of Songs<-

# The Song of Songs, which is Solomon's: It is stated
<margin>In the Great Midrash <note>original: "Midrash Rabbah," a collection of ancient homiletic interpretations of the Bible</note> on this verse: Why [was he] wiser than all of them—Calcol and Etan <note>referring to 1 Kings 4:31 where Solomon's wisdom is compared to other famous sages</note>? If they had spoken [from their own minds]...</margin>
You would have needed to incline your ear and listen more than to the words of Solomon, Calcol, and Etan; if they had spoken from their own intellect, you would have needed to incline your ear and listen more than to those spoken through the Holy Spirit <term>Ruach HaKodesh: Divine inspiration or prophecy</term>, etc. The sages labored in this to establish and settle all the words of this priest <note>a metaphorical reference to the author or teacher</note> which serve as a preface to praise and glorify all the words of this King <note>Solomon</note>. It is fitting for a person to incline his ear and listen, for if the matters themselves are worthy in their own right—even if the speaker were merely a common person—one should still incline his ear like a funnel to hear them. This is the meaning of weighing "who is superior." Furthermore, regarding the song itself, it is worthy to the ultimate degree, and all the more so regarding the speaker, who was the greatest of all wise men—this is why it says "of Solomon." This would be true even if he spoke only from his own wisdom; how much more so when he spoke through the Holy Spirit. For the text specifies the matter of the Holy Spirit by writing "The Song of Songs" and immediately follows with "which is Solomon's." Otherwise, it should have said "The Songs of Solomon," just as it is written "The Proverbs of Solomon" or "The Words of Kohelet <note>Ecclesiastes</note>." Rather, the "Song of Songs" refers to a song that includes many songs within it. This quality belongs only to words spoken through the Holy Spirit, for they can be divided into many facets, and all of them are true and good in their intent. However, the words of a [merely] wise man contain only a single or double meaning, being limited to whatever the speaker intended and starting according to the literal truth. From the words of our Sages of Blessed Memory, it is settled why Solomon’s father’s name <note>David</note> and his royal title are not mentioned here as they are in Proverbs and Ecclesiastes. Since the intent here is to highlight Solomon's wisdom above all other sages, he is mentioned by name alone to show that the scripture intends to highlight that which made him more chosen than all others, as we have said.

---

<margin>And further</margin>
It is stated there in the Midrash: Rabbi Yochanan in the name of Rabbi Chama, in the name of Rabbi Shimon bar Abba: For every praise they saw <note>referring to the Jewish people</note>, the Holy Spirit was destined to rest upon us. As it was said before: "many songs." In all other songs, either he <note>God</note> praises them <note>Israel</note>, or they praise him. In the Song of the Sea <note>Exodus 15</note>, they praise Him and say: "This is my God and I will glorify Him." In the Song of Moses <note>Deuteronomy 32</note>, He praises them: "He made him ride on the high places of the earth." But here <note>in the Song of Songs</note>, they praise Him and He praises them. He praises them: "Behold, you are beautiful, my love," and they praise Him: "Behold, you are beautiful, my beloved, indeed pleasant," etc. The intention is that even the author of that statement found the verse difficult: "The Song of Songs," which is a song of praise to Solomon. If the plural "Songs" is used because of the greatness of the songs, why not write "Solomon's Songs" like "Solomon's Proverbs" or "The Words of Kohelet"? Furthermore, why use the word "Asher" <note>a relative pronoun meaning "which is"</note>? It should have written "The Song of Songs to Solomon." Furthermore, why did he not mention his father’s name or his kingdom as in Proverbs and Ecclesiastes? To settle all this, he said that the intent of the scripture is not merely to inform us of the author's name and the nature of this song, but rather to say that it is fitting for the collective of Israel to say songs and praise to the One who is destined to rest the Holy Spirit upon us. Through the Holy Spirit that shall rest and be poured upon us from on high, we will have the preparation to say many songs before Him. This is the meaning of "The Song of Songs"—a "Song" before the Holy Spirit rests, and "Songs" are those that follow after it, as we have stated. This was the author's intent in saying: "As we have said, and through it many songs are said before Him," meaning after the Holy Spirit is rested upon us. The matter of the Holy Spirit is precise in the text where it says "which is Solomon's" <note>Asher LiShlomo</note> and did not simply say "to Solomon" <note>LiShlomo</note>, to say that the many songs said before Him will be like a precious kindness to Solomon <note>the name Solomon/Shlomo is also related to the Hebrew word for Peace/Perfection, Shalom</note>, as if he were saying them through the Holy Spirit. He further said: "In all the songs..." chapters and lives, etc.

<meta>Catchword: And the songs</meta>

## Preface

And the desire of this historical structure is hidden, and to place a crown upon its head is a miraculous thing; every single word of the Song of Songs <note>original: "Shir HaShirim"</note> is thus, so far his words. Behold, it is clear before us as a fundamental law that all the matters included in the parts of this scroll <note>original: "megillah"</note> and according to these words, form a strong connection, bound together with all the "pearls" mentioned in the secret of the chapters of old <note>referring to ancient esoteric traditions or perhaps the work *Pirkei de-Rabbi Eliezer*</note>, may their memory be a blessing. Some of these are ordered within its parts and specific details, and some are proven within them and for their sake, to link the expositions <note>original: "derushim," homiletic or deep explanations</note> of its details as the eye of the reader desires, who gazes rightly upon the horizon. For God desired that it be awakened and that not a single thing fall from all the matters—many as they are—that will be included in this scroll, in the book of wisdom which has strengthened me. For the beginning of the words of the mouth of the wise speaker, Solomon, peace be upon him <term>peace be upon him: "Alav HaShalom," a traditional honorific for the deceased</term>, and the opening of his words and the knowledge of his lips, spoke clearly regarding the essence of its tapestry and the goodness of his time and the perfection of his state. For Israel in his days, after the building of the Holy Temple <note>original: "Beit HaMikdash"</note>, were at the height of virtues and successes, both physical and spiritual. The congregation was diligent to raise them to the highest levels of the ladders, like the day of the standing at Mount Sinai when their impurity ceased <note>This refers to a Talmudic tradition that the spiritual "filth" or inclination toward evil brought by the serpent in Eden was removed from the Israelites when they received the Torah</note>, so that the essential cleaving <term>cleaving: "Deveikut," a state of intense spiritual attachment to the Divine</term> with the Blessed Name [God] would be intensified, as it is written that He chose well. From there, Solomon separates by his Holy Spirit to the rest of the expositions of its parts, which were chosen above, moving from time to period and from matter to matter, and in the chronological order of them all: the entire conduct of the world from that time until the completion of the Resurrection of the Dead <note>original: "Techiyat HaMetim"</note> and until the "Day of Distress" <note>original: "Yoma de-Chiku," possibly referring to the end of days or the final judgment</note> and its causes. All other matters will come one by one within,

# Song of Songs

The covenant of King David <note>original: "Daitiki David Malka," using the Greek-derived term *diatheke* for covenant</note> was joined with them, and they are the four spirits of the holy covenant, the sacred cause. For this reason, there are four words in the opening of the first verse <note>Referring to "Shir HaShirim Asher li-Shelomo" (Song of Songs which is Solomon's)</note>, representing the perfect and holy spirit of the covenant. Open your eyes and see how many hints they have embedded in the Scripture; the very number of words points to a great and inner mystery. We already know that all these matters are hinted at and sealed within hidden and internal concepts. It is not appropriate to reveal the essence of their intention, as these are matters that are the "secrets of the world" <term>secrets of the world: "kivshono shel olam," referring to deep divine mysteries not meant for public disclosure</term>; we have only written those things close to the plain meaning <term>plain meaning: "Peshat," the literal or direct level of interpretation</term> as an explanation of the text.

According to our path in explaining this scroll, it will be clarified in a concise manner. Behold, the expansion of the matter centers upon the following point on which the explanation turns: Solomon, peace be upon him, sought through his spiritual perception—for in his days, Israel was at the peak of their level, higher than they had ever been in fullness and tranquility. There was no adversary and no evil encounter; every man lived under his vine and under his fig tree. They lacked nothing of the greatness and virtues of this world: greatness, sovereignty, wealth, ease, and quiet. For "the moon was at its full" <note>A traditional Jewish metaphor meaning the Jewish people's spiritual and political radiance reached its zenith during Solomon's reign, the 15th generation from Abraham</note>, as is known from the words of our Sages of blessed memory.

Therefore, he merited to knock at the door and supplicate before the Blessed One to raise Israel to an even greater level and stage, following the pattern of the boundaries and the standing at Mount Sinai. At that time, their spiritual impurity <note>original: "zohamatan," referring to the residue of the primordial sin of Eden</note> ceased and their bodies and physical matter were purified until it was entirely uprooted from them. They achieved great perceptions that the tongue is unable to describe, as is known from the words of our Sages and as explained in the Midrashim and the Book of Zohar. This was his primary request and his heart's desire. For all the matters of this world, which they had already achieved to their ultimate end as they wished, were considered by him as nothing, like a vapor, because they are closer to loss than to reward. In their current state, they are covered in darkness compared to His palace. He desired that they might step forward and increase their strength, to be enclosed in the Torahs and to find bundles upon bundles of laws, both light and stringent. As our Sages said, the purity of the eye hinders the desire <note>An interpretive rendering of a difficult phrase suggesting that physical distractions hinder spiritual attachment</note>, for they should not be casual in their attachment to the Blessed One and His service, lest He hide His face from them and they be left to the physical world and external servitude. For if they did not complete their souls as is proper to do, they would realize that in all generations, there was no generation that stood at such a height of excellence and success as that generation.

This would be a portion for the truth and for the sake of attaining true attachment and purity without doubt. For they had already exhausted the ultimate end of worldly arrangements and successes, and they recognized that this—the physical world—is not the true rest or the true inheritance. This was unlike other generations who did not dwell by this "river" <note>A metaphor for the abundance of Solomon's era</note>; one could not test this yearning in them, for one might say that if they had achieved the ultimate successes of this world, they would not have considered it vapor. Therefore, the text precedes the song by saying: **The Song of Songs, which is Solomon's**, for this realization belonged to him and his generation, and this gift was not for other generations. He called it the "Song of Songs" because it is comprised of many matters and themes that have existed from the beginning of the world's creation until the end of the seventh day <note>A reference to the Messianic age or the cosmic Sabbath</note>. It is a song that contains many types of songs. Regarding this, our Sages also said: "The Song of Songs is the Holy of Holies," because it includes the matter of the World to Come <term>World to Come: "Olam Ha-Ba," the spiritual afterlife or future redeemed state</term>. In it, the chosen ones would be built and refined with extra holiness and an inner holiness, "within and within" <note>meaning the most sacred interior</note>, achieving many hidden perceptions—concealment within concealment and holiness within holiness, as is known to those who hold fast to the truth.

With these words he began, and his request was:
**"Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth, for [your love] is better..."**
In the name of the entire holy nation.

# Commentary

The songs follow the cessation of the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Ruach HaKodesh"</note> regarding that which we read from the Scriptures. Therefore, since the intention of the text is to teach us something else regarding its inherent weakness, the Scripture writes in praise of this high level. This is because in all other songs, one sees only the praise of the One <note>original: "Hazi Hazi," an Aramaic expression for "look and see"</note> or the praise of Israel, whereas here both are combined. As it is taught in our chapter regarding the verse "a ring" <note>Referring to Proverbs 11:22, often used in Rabbinic analogies for the Song of Songs</note>, the Song of Songs is a song comprised of two types of songs.

Based on these words explained by our Sages of blessed memory, we can understand the explanation of the Maharamez <note>Rabbi Moshe Zacuto (1625–1697), a leading Kabbalist in Italy</note> elsewhere: "All the songs are holy, but the Song of Songs is the Holy of Holies" <note>original: "Kodesh Kodashim"</note>. When he says "all the songs are holy," he means they contain either the praise of the Holy One, blessed be He, or the praise of Israel. But the Song of Songs is the "Holy of Holies" because it contains two "holies"—the two types of praise mentioned above.

In the *Book of Splendor* <note>original: "Sefer HaZohar"</note>, in the section of *Terumah* <note>Exodus 25:1–27:19</note>, Rabbi Yose opened his discourse and said: "*The Song of Songs which is Solomon's*—King Solomon awakened this song when the Holy Temple was built. At that time, all the worlds, both upper and lower, were sustained in a single perfection. And although the colleagues disagree on this, this song was only uttered at the completion, when the lower world was hurried along and the Holy Temple was built below in the likeness of the Temple above. At the moment the Temple below was built, there was a joy before the Holy One, blessed be He, like no other since the day the world was created. On that day, the beloved ones <note>The people of Israel</note> were united; when the House of Mercy was built, a single deep and fortified unity spread through all the worlds. Light shone for all worlds, the world was sweetened, and all the windows of the worlds were opened to shine. There was no joy in all the worlds like that day. Then, the upper and lower beings opened their mouths and sang a song—and that is the Song of Songs, the song that the musicians sing to the Holy One, blessed be He. Just as there are shifts of watches <term>watches: "mishmarot," referring to groups of Levites or angels who serve in a rotation</term> on earth, so too there are in the heavens, standing one opposite the other. On the day the Temple was built, they were all established in their places. A pure spirit began to shine, and this praise was prepared for the Supreme King—the King to whom peace belongs <note>A mystical play on the name "Shelomo" (Solomon), which shares a root with "Shalom" (Peace)</note>. This praise is superior to all previous praises since the day the world was created. For this reason, it is considered the Holy of Holies in the Scripture. It was already written that on the day the Temple was established, loves would be awakened and songs would rise above and below."

Because of this, we find that the song is joined by great and various species that were awakened. These are the celestial singers, who also flutter and awaken toward the upper realm, so to speak. It is a "Song of songs"—a song belonging to those great songs that oversee all the worlds.

The author of the Zohar lengthened his discussion on this matter, and the essence of these spirits explains all the words of the text with precise accuracy. I have seen that they said "Song of Songs" refers to all the singers and musicians—meaning that the celestial singers and the angelic watches established above in their places were included in this song, standing opposite the watches on earth. All the worlds were perfumed, made bright, and illuminated with a clear light until the ultimate purpose was reached. This song was established for the Supreme King, the King to whom peace belongs—and this is the meaning of "which is Solomon's" <note>original: "Asher li-Shelomo"</note>.

They also scrutinized the word "which" <note>original: "Asher"</note>, noting its intention is to indicate the uniqueness of this song, it being the most praised and unique in its excellence, fitting for the King to whom peace belongs. This praise is superior to all prior praises. They also derived meaning from the word "which" <note>original: "Asher"</note> as we have said. Furthermore, they noted the presence of a "Large Shin" <note>original: "Shin Rabati," a scribal tradition where the letter Shin is written larger than others</note> to indicate the 87 branches of Judgment <note>original: "Paz anfei ha-din," where "Paz" equals 87 in gematria, referring to the 87 verses of the book</note> hinted at therein, as is known. This suggests that they also were awakened and caused others to awaken to sing this song.

They further scrutinized their words regarding the explanation of the Maharamez, that the "Song of Songs is the Holy of Holies," meaning that perfection was found in everything. Examine this closely and you will find it. Furthermore, it is written there: "We have learned: *Song* is one, *Songs* is two, making three..." <note>A traditional midrashic counting of the words to derive deeper meanings</note> until it reaches the Holy Chariot <note>original: "Ratikia," an Aramaic term for the Divine Chariot or Merkavah</note>...

The throne <note>original: "Diteika," a Greek-derived term used in the Zohar to refer to a throne, arrangement, or disposition</note> of King David is meant to be joined with them, and they are the four winds of the throne—the holy, high Cause. Therefore, there are four words in this first verse, hinting at the complete and holy spirit. Open your eyes and see how much greatness is found in the writing; even the number of words serves as a hint to a great and internal matter. We already know that all these matters are hidden and sealed within secret and inner things, and it is not fitting to reveal the core of their intent, as they are the deep secrets <note>original: "Kivshonei Shel Olam," literally "the world's hidden furnaces"</note> of the world. We have written only those things close to the plain meaning <note>original: "Peshat"</note> in this explanation of the text.

Regarding our path in explaining this Scroll, it shall be clarified in a different way. Here is the expansion of the topic at the pivot of the intention upon which this explanation turns: Solomon—may peace be upon him—asked this of his Master in his spirit. In his days, Israel was at the highest level—none higher—in peace and tranquility. There was "neither adversary nor evil occurrence" <note>1 Kings 5:18</note>; every man sat "under his vine and under his fig tree" <note>1 Kings 4:25</note>. They lacked nothing of the greatness and virtues of "This World" <note>original: "Olam HaZeh"</note>—greatness, kingship, wealth, tranquility, and quiet. For "the moon was at its full," as is known from the words of our Sages of blessed memory <note>A metaphor for the peak of the Jewish people's spiritual and national cycle</note>. Therefore, he merited to knock at the door and enter into a "marriage" before Him, may He be blessed. This elevated Israel to a great level and degree, similar to the reality of the standing at Mount Sinai, where their impurity ceased and their bodies and physical matter were refined, until the "Evil Inclination" <note>original: "Yetzer HaRa"</note> was uprooted from them. They achieved great insights that no tongue can speak, as is known from the words of our Sages and as explained in the Midrashim and the *Book of Splendor* <note>original: "Sefer HaZohar"</note>.

This was the essence of their request and their plea to find refuge from their physical nature. For all the matters of "This World" <note>original: "Olam HaZeh"</note>, which they had already achieved to their ultimate end, were like nothing to them. Their desire for these things was considered as vanity and void, because they were closer to loss than to reward. Since their kingship and their rest were as one, they were now hidden from the "Palace of the Temple" <note>original: "Heikhal"</note>. They came to advance and increase wisdom, to recount the laws, and to seek out Divinity from the circles and groups, both light and heavy <note>Refers to different levels of Torah study and mystical grouping</note>, as mentioned above. 

This "veil" and "darkness" of the eye <note>A metaphor for the physical body or the material world</note> hinders the desire, for they would not be awakened in their "Cleaving" <note>original: "Dveikut," the mystical state of attachment to God</note> and His service, and He would hide His face from them, leaving them to be "consumed" and "passed over." This is besides the fact that they would not be able to perfect their souls as is proper for performing the commandments and the living spirit. For in all generations, there was no generation that stood at such a height of virtue and success as that generation. 

It was specifically from this state that the request for "true love" and the attainment of "Divine Cleaving" arose without a doubt. For they had already exhausted the pleasures of "This World" to their very end, as well as its final shames and vanities. They recognized that without this rest, this inheritance, and this Divine bliss, there is nothing. In other generations, which were not like this "river," this request would not be tested in them, for one could say that because they had not attained the end of worldly pleasures, they did not yet view them as vanity. Therefore, the text begins: *The Song of Songs which is Solomon’s*—for to him and his generation, this request and gift were appropriate, and not to other generations.

And it is called "The Song of Songs" because it is comprised of many things and matters that existed from the beginning of the world's creation until "After the Seventh Day" <note>original: "Batar Yoma Shevi'ita," a reference to the Messianic era or the World to Come</note>. It is a song that contains many types of songs within it. This is the meaning of the Sages' statement: "The Song of Songs is the Holy of Holies" <note>original: "Kodesh Kodashim"</note>. This is because it includes the matter of "The World to Come" <note>original: "Olam HaBa"</note>, where the chosen ones reside in body and soul with extra holiness and inner holiness, "within the innermost" <note>original: "Lifnai u'Lifnim"</note>, and with the attainment of many hidden dwellings—a world within a world, and holiness within holiness, as is known to those who believe in the wisdom of these matters. This refers to the "Songs," and we see what preceded it in the text. Therefore, he says that the intent of the scripture is to teach us something else regarding the simple meaning of the verse <note>original: "peshutei ha-katuv"</note> in praise of this high level. This means that the sum of the "Songs"—God forbid one should think otherwise—is that he praises Him, and they [Israel] praise the One who bestows power. How can both be true? As it is explained: "The Song of Songs" refers to a song comprised of two types of songs. Based on these words, we find an explanation from the Sage <note>The OCR reads "MaHRaMS," likely referring to a specific commentator like Rabbi Moshe Alsheich or a similar authority</note> elsewhere on the teaching: "All the songs are holy, but the Song of Songs is the Holy of Holies." But "the Song of Songs" is "Holy of Holies" because it contains "two holies," meaning two types of praise as we have said.

At that time, all the worlds—above and below—were sealed in one single perfection. And although the colleagues disagree on this, [some saying] this song was only said at Sinai when the moon was at its full; yet [the primary view is that] when the Holy Temple was built below, it was modeled after the Temple above. In the hour when the Holy Temple was built below, there was no joy before the Holy One, Blessed be He, since the day the world was created, like that day."

The House of Mercy <note>original: "Beit Rakhamin"</note>: when the House of Mercy was built below, it was built with it [the one above], and they were established in all the worlds, and light was brought to all the worlds, and the world was founded, and all the upper windows were opened to shine. There was no joy in all the worlds like that day! Then those above and those below opened and said the song. And this is the "Song of Songs"—the song of those who play music <note>original: "de-innun minagnin"</note>, who sing to the Holy One, Blessed be He. Just as there are various spirits and shifts of guards on the earth, so too it is in the firmament, and these stand corresponding to those. On the day the Holy Temple was built, they were all established in their blessings. There was never a song like that song to bring light. And this song was established for the Supernal King—the King to whom peace belongs <note>original: "Malka de-shalma dilei," a Kabbalistic wordplay on the name Solomon (Shelomo/Shalom)</note>. This praise is superior to all previous praises. The day this praise was revealed in the world, perfection was found in everything. Therefore, it is "Holy of Holies" in the book. Originally it was written: "On the day the Holy Temple is established, the holy ones above and below will awaken in song." Because of this, we find many great levels and many kinds [of angels] that were awakened, for they sing a superior and beloved song to the One Above. It is a "Song" of those who are "Great Songs" <note>The text suggests "Song of Songs" means the greatest of all celestial melodies</note> who sing over all the worlds.

Furthermore, consider this matter carefully, for "the eyes of the Lord" <note>referring to divine providence or the "spirits" mentioned</note> oversee all the words of the scripture with precision. I have seen that the meaning of "Song of Songs" <note>original: "Shir Ha-Shirim"</note> refers to the Song of the Songs and the Singers. These stand corresponding to those. <meta>This page continues the mystical commentary on the Song of Songs, moving from the dedication of the Temple to the specific spiritual state of Israel during the Revelation at Mount Sinai. It interprets the "fragrance of oils" as the reception of the Torah and the purification of the soul.</meta>

-> ### Song of Songs <-

### For the fragrance of your goodly oils, your name is oil poured forth; therefore do the maidens love you:
<note>Song of Songs 1:3</note>

Following the compassionate path we explained regarding Isaac, etc., the intention here is the state of the Holy Standing <note>original: "Ma'amad HaKadosh," referring to the assembly at Mount Sinai</note> and its unique quality. This occurred at the time of the first two commandments, when there was more than just a cessation of spiritual impurity <note>original: "pessikat ha-zohama," a kabbalistic concept referring to the removal of the "filth of the serpent" during the Revelation at Sinai</note>. It was a state of being uprooted [from worldliness] and standing upon the mountain. Even though there were different types [of people] and not all were praiseworthy, one might say now—God forbid—that there could be a way to merit that most precious state, or at least to be within the realm of the state that followed it, which was hidden from them in the mystery of the Divine Speech that separated and designated them. 

For this reason, the text says: **For the fragrance of your goodly oils**. This means that when the Blessed One saw that the Torah is called "oil"—as is written in the Midrash—it implies that because we "smelled" the fragrance of Your goodly commandments and accepted them, and prepared ourselves as has been our way until now, we understood this greatness. This preparation came from the radiance of Your Divine Presence <note>original: "ziv shekhinatekha"</note>, which is like oil poured from vessel to vessel, whose scent spreads, allowing people to recognize it. This is the meaning of **your name is oil poured forth**. 

**Therefore do the maidens love you**: These "maidens" <note>Hebrew: "alamot"</note> are the souls <note>Hebrew: "neshamot"</note> who loved You and attached themselves to You immediately at the beginning of their spiritual journey. By achieving this great perception—the likes of which had never occurred until that day—they received a full measure of the essence of Your name and Your Divine Presence, as it is said, "poured forth." 

Regarding the spiritual path of His congregation, there was no "fragrance" [equal to] the state of the commandments in their entirety, which was the cessation of impurity. He says that the state of that Standing at Sinai was precious to the ultimate degree regarding the perfection of the soul, enabling it to be attached to its God. Even if they only attached themselves to a "handful of salt" <note>a metaphor for a small but essential preservation</note> apart from the specific commandments, nevertheless, all the hidden things and secrets are contained within every single utterance. Even the most distant matters that will only be understood in the days of the Messiah were perceived then as a "fragrant seal." 

As it is written in the **Book of the Zohar, Portion of Yitro**: Rabbi Yose taught that the radiance of those celestial voices was a supreme light. In those "crowns," all the prophets, all hidden things, and all judges gazed, until the time of the King Messiah. Concerning this, it is written: "And all the people saw the voices." It was for the sake of the **fragrance of your goodly oils**, which are the aforementioned claims. He calls them "fragrance" because they are perceived from a distance, just as a person smells a scent from afar. 

Furthermore, this refers to another even greater perception: the Sovereignty of the Torah, which was the appointment of the Holy One, blessed be He, from the side of its uniqueness and refinement. He calls it **oil poured forth**; therefore, regarding this state of perception, **maidens love you**. Their understanding is not hindered, for nothing can prevent [attachment to God] in such a state.

In the **Midrash**, it says: **For the fragrance of your goodly oils...** Rabbi Isaac said in the name of Rabbi Tanchum bar Chiyya: "There are two types of oils: the oil of Priesthood and the oil of Kingship... **your name is oil poured forth**; Your name becomes magnified through anyone who occupies themselves with the Mishnah <note>The first major written collection of the Jewish oral traditions</note> of the Torah." 

According to their path, we may well choose our own: the intention is that there are three crowns—the Crown of Priesthood, the Crown of Kingship, and both are associated with the language of "oil" because they require anointing. Their goodness is the **Crown of Torah**, which is unique and refined, encompassing all wisdom. Aaron merited and took the Crown of Priesthood; David merited and took the Crown of Kingship. However, the **Crown of Torah**—anyone who wishes to take it may come and take it. Therefore, he says **for the fragrance of your oils**, referring to the two crowns we merited in perfection while in this state; they are undoubtedly "good." But the most choice crown of all is the Crown of Torah, which is compared to **oil poured forth** because its fragrance spreads, and through it, Your name is magnified. Therefore...

<summary>This page provides a mystical and midrashic interpretation of Song of Songs 1:3, linking the "oils" to the Revelation at Sinai and the "Three Crowns" of Priesthood, Kingship, and Torah.</summary>
<keywords>Mount Sinai, Torah, Priesthood, Kingship, Zohar, Midrash, Divine Presence, Shekhinah, souls, oil, anointing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically interpreting the spiritual elevation experienced by the Israelites during the Revelation at Sinai. It transitions from the general "fragrance of oils" to the specific state of the soul when the Ten Commandments were spoken.</meta>

# Commentary

And he led them to the level that belonged to them at the Standing at Mount Sinai <note>original: "Ma'amad Har Sinai"</note> as we heard from His mouth. Our intention here is the words of compassion <note>original: "rachmanut"</note> which we heard from the Mouth of Power <note>original: "Pi HaGevurah," a traditional Jewish term for God as the Source of Revelation</note>. For we see, according to our intended way, that the ultimate purpose was the uprooting of the Evil Inclination <term>Yetzer HaRa<span>The innate drive toward physical desires or selfishness, often personified as a force to be overcome through spiritual discipline.</span></term> and the study of Torah <note>original: "Talmud Torah"</note>. The state of Israel at that time was far more elevated than the state they achieved later in the wilderness, as a miracle occurred for them during the speaking of the Commandments.

As we learn in the Midrash of Rabbi Yehuda: He says that at the hour Israel heard, "I am the Lord your God," the study of Torah was fixed in their hearts; they would learn and never forget. But the ministering angels came and said: "Will the flower of Zion <note>original: "Pirchei Tziyon," likely referring to the spiritual crowns given to Israel at Sinai</note> flourish among us?" and so they returned to a state of learning and forgetting. 

Rabbi Nehemiah says: At the hour they heard, "You shall have no other gods," the Evil Inclination was uprooted from their hearts. But the Angels of Death came, and immediately it returned to its place. 

One who contemplates the words of Rabbi Yehuda will find great power in them. For when physical bodies—which are usually coarse—become thin, refined, and purified from the dross of the Evil Inclination, nothing prevents the study of Torah from being fixed in their hearts. When their intellects are perfect, it follows that they would be whole and clinging to the Living God without any obstacle, rising to a level higher than the ministering angels, even while they are in this world in body and soul. Therefore, this state was chosen initially to merit that specific level. 

The Elders/Sages wrote regarding the verse: **For your love is better than wine**: 
<note>Song of Songs 1:2</note>
It means that Your love and Your words are undoubtedly better in this state. For one who perceives the distance from You sees it as more bitter than anything, more than all the arrangements of this world <note>original: "Ha-Olam Ha-Zeh"</note>, its heights, and its successes. As the commentators have written, "wine" is used here as a metaphor for the affairs of this world.

Specifically, regarding the totality of the Commandments, it says that they yearned for the words of compassion. Even when they said, "You speak with us and we will hear," they had already reached a great level where their spiritual impurity <note>original: "zohamatat," referring to the primordial "filth" of the serpent in Eden</note> had ceased. They were prepared for the good, even if they had previously possessed the desire to choose evil; nonetheless, it was now distant from them.

Regarding the totality of that Standing, it says: "O that they had such a heart as this, to fear Me and keep all My commandments always." For with the successes and perfection of this world, there is no preoccupation or desire for them compared to the Divine love, as it says: **For your love is better than wine**.

It is possible to explain the end of the verse differently, regarding the many efforts Solomon made at the beginning to please his Creator. It is known that once they began to speak together, and the connection of Israel was fixed in the heart, the physical parts of the body—the material forces—usually assist in the inclination toward evil. There is no doubt that one must toil to overcome the Evil Inclination and its power. 

The reward is great when one subdues it. However, when the Evil Inclination is suppressed and the physical body is refined and thin, there is almost a loss in this station regarding the reward [for the struggle]. Yet, regarding punishment, it would be greater if they were to sin, as they are free to do so but have no assisting force toward evil. 

Therefore, Solomon emphasized: **For your love is better than wine**, to show that having Your love before his eyes and in his heart was more precious than any worldly pleasure. Clinging to Him, may He be blessed, and the friendship of His love in this state—far from the material—is certainly greater than the arrangements of the Evil Inclination that might overcome a person. 

Woe! For I have seen "sons of ascent" <note>original: "bnei aliyah," a term for those who reach the highest spiritual heights</note> and they are few. In the Midrash on the verse "He brought me to the house of wine," it hints that "wine" refers to the Evil Inclination. This supports our explanation that "Your love" <note>original: "dodecha"</note> refers to a state of friendship and affection. The intention is that Your love is "good" in the context of Your beloveds when the physical wall is in a good state, like the Standing at Mount Sinai. This is better than the "wine"—the worldly matters and the "arrangements" of the Evil Inclination—which are likened to wine. For in that state, a person can truly be righteous and not sin.

<summary>This page explores the Midrashic tradition of the Revelation at Sinai, explaining how the Israelites were temporarily freed from the "Evil Inclination" and the "Angel of Death." It interprets the "wine" of Song of Songs as a metaphor for worldly desires that distract from the superior "love" of the Divine connection.</summary>
<keywords>Standing at Sinai, Revelation, Evil Inclination, Yetzer HaRa, Midrash, Rabbi Yehuda, Rabbi Nehemiah, Song of Songs, Divine Love, Reward and Punishment, Worldly Affairs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs 1:4. The previous page discussed the spiritual elevation and the temporary removal of the Evil Inclination during the Revelation at Sinai. This page focuses on the transition from the fear experienced at Sinai to the joy found in the Tabernacle and the Temple, exploring the linguistic connection between "drawing" the soul and the "dwelling" of the Divine Presence.</meta>

# Song of Songs

Our lot would have been difficult; on the contrary, it will be easier because the error of those people will not weigh heavily upon us. And we said, **"Let not God speak with us, lest we die,"** <note>original: "v’lo yedaber imanu Elohim pen namut" — quoting Exodus 20:19, where the Israelites are terrified by the direct voice of God</note> because they were unable to endure that great Speech. For there is **rejoicing in exultation and joy in You** <note>A play on Song of Songs 1:4: "Nagila ve-nismecha bach" (We will exult and rejoice in You)</note>, and it appears as exultation and joy in You in all the laws I have written for us in the Torah. This is unlike that Standing <note>original: "Ma'amad" — referring to the Standing at Sinai</note> where there was a place for the absence of joy, and a falling away during the blow of receiving the Torah, for they feared the "Left Side" <note>In Kabbalistic thought, the Left Side (Smol) represents Judgment and Awe, as opposed to the Right Side of Mercy and Joy</note>.

He mentioned the "Reed" <note>possibly a metaphor for the pen of the scribe or the breath of life</note> to revive them, for in His wisdom, He removes all violence. Now we live through the portion that reveals **exultation and joy in You** through the knowledge of the Torah, its inner dimensions and its solutions. This is to say, and it is further emphasized, that while in this state, they will mention His beloved qualities, may He be blessed, from within the sweetness of the Torah. By attaining all the inner facets of the Torah and the **hidden secrets** <term>Razin Temirin<span>The "hidden secrets" or mystical dimensions of the Torah that are not apparent in the literal text.</span></term> within it, they will recognize His greatness, may He be blessed.

The passion to cling to You in **uprightness** <note>original: "be-yishrut"</note> will grow strong, without any outward show or deviation at all, for nothing is too small for Him. And we also say: **"We will mention your love more than wine; the upright love you"** <note>Song of Songs 1:4</note>. It appears that "we will mention your love" refers to those You love—the holy ones, the merciful ones, and the righteous. They were "Your beloved" even **"more than wine,"** which is to say, even while they were among the desires and arrangements of this world in its current state. Despite all this, they preceded... <note>The text here is slightly truncated</note> because they were chosen for the collective recognition, to say that **"the upright love you."** This means they loved You with a straight and simple heart, for they recognized and believed in Your greatness, something that was not granted to the rest of the nations.

<margin>And in the **Midrash** they interpreted the word **"Draw me"** <note>original: "Mashcheni"</note> in many ways: as a language of **collateral** <note>original: "mashkon"</note>, as the **Tabernacle** <note>original: "mishkan"</note>, as **dwellings** <note>original: "tishkonot"</note>, and as a **neighborhood** <note>original: "shechuna"</note>.</margin>

It seems possible that they interpreted it this way, beyond its plain meaning, because He says **"Draw me"** in the singular, and then **"we will run"** in the plural. According to our way, it is explained well through the term **Tabernacle** <note>original: "Mishkan"</note>, referring to the Holy Temple, which is also called a *Mishkan*. The proof is in Tractate Shevuot, showing that the *Mishkan* is called a Sanctuary and the Sanctuary is called a *Mishkan*. 

The explanation of the verse is that now there is a unity, for it was not outside the law and the line that we sent this. For now they had a greater recognition of this than at first, because they had the Holy Temple. Through its influence, they were clean of all iniquity, and the spirit of holiness and purity spread within them. The Lord **dwells in their midst**, and by the power of our request, they were more prepared for their redemption. This is **"Draw me"**—meaning, my **Tabernacle** is the reason that **"after You we will run"** to attain this perception. 

And even though through the blow of the Temple we were tested, **"The King has brought me into his chambers."** My Commander is He who dwells in the Sanctuary, and we were worthy to enter within it. Nevertheless, in that which we merited, He is the strength and my spirit, in **exultation and joy in You**, in a constant life of wholeness in Your thought, without scattering or the occurrence of evil. But now, under the yoke of the sins of iniquity, which acts like a screen over our state, we are cast into another land, and we are in a state of the **hiding of the Face** <term>Hester Panim<span>A theological concept where God's presence is hidden from the world, usually as a result of human sin, leading to a period of suffering or exile.</span></term>, in a way that is not **exultation and joy in You**. 

Therefore, my heart pardons my request and my plea. Know in truth that now, with the preparation of the Sanctuary which I have made, **"we will mention Your love"** even more than at the time of the giving of the Torah—which was the Standing at Mount Sinai. For at Sinai, they did not yet have the preparation of the Sanctuary. And this is more than the level of **"the upright"** <note>original: "Misharim"</note>, meaning the straight and the righteous who loved You at that time. This interpretation of *Misharim* as "the upright people" seems to be the interpretation of the Translator <note>likely referring to the Aramaic Targum</note>, may his memory be a blessing, to one who examines his words closely. Also in the Midrash, they applied "the upright" to the opposite case; this was the view of **Ben Azzai** <note>Simeon ben Azzai, a famous 2nd-century Jewish scholar known for his piety and diligence</note> and his colleagues.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Song of Songs 1:3-4, transitioning from the overwhelming experience of Revelation at Sinai to the intimate relationship between God and Israel. It builds on the previous discussion regarding the temporary suspension of the Evil Inclination, now focusing on how the "scent" of the commandments and the "oil" of the Torah refine the soul.</meta>

# Song of Songs — Commentary 8

Therefore, by way of a "how much more so" argument, **"the maidens love you"** <note>Hebrew: *alamot ahevucha*. The author treats "maidens" (*alamot*) as a reference to Israel or as a pun on "worlds" (*olamot*)</note>, meaning that Israel loves You, a love that encompasses them all. As it is said, because this love is more precious than the other two <note>Likely referring to the "Crown of Priesthood" and "Crown of Kingship" mentioned later</note>, its gift was planted in a state involving "maidens" <note>or "hidden things/worlds"</note>. Because their inclination and physical matter act as an obstacle, He acted with power as if using a "winepress" <note>original: *machbesh* — suggesting a forced or high-pressure spiritual environment</note>, just as it was at the Standing at Mount Sinai. There, they achieved great perceptions of the secrets of the Torah, which were revealed to them as previously mentioned.

Alternatively, it is possible the intention refers to **"because of the fragrance of your good oils"** and the two aforementioned "Crowns." <note>The Crown of Priesthood and the Crown of Kingship</note>. There is also a third, more precious crown: **"your name is poured oil,"** <note>original: *shemen turaq shemecha* — Song 1:3</note> through which Your name is magnified. Therefore, Israel—who are called "maidens" according to the words of the Sages in the Midrash—**"love you,"** meaning they hope and long to continue this affection with You. The intention is that however You strengthen [them], they will be worthy of the rank and state that was once dominant. For by being so refined in their physical bodies, they will perceive the Torah like "maidens" <note>pure and receptive</note>. Furthermore, since they desired and gave less significance to physical recognition, strengthening their attachment to God, there will be no sin to cause a distance or a break. Israel is "the portion of Your face," may He be blessed, and they stood constantly in a way that the Crown of Priesthood and the Crown of Kingship were also in their proper form, just as it was in our original state.

<margin>There is a Midrashic teaching that this verse is the speech of the Holy One, Blessed be He, to the Community of Israel.</margin>

For this reason, the Sages explained: **"Your name is poured oil"**—just as this oil is bitter at its beginning but sweet at its end, so shall your beginning be full of pain, etc. Just as this oil is only improved through crushing, so Israel only performs repentance [through suffering], etc. They also interpreted this in the context of their departure from Egypt: our father [Jacob] was on his bed when the Holy One, Blessed be He, said to him, "Go," etc. In this way, we can explain according to our method that this was the response of the Community of Israel to Solomon at his table. She said, **"Because of the fragrance of your good oils,"** meaning: because of the scent of your oils, which are your commandments and good deeds, which are now very, very good. This is because you are overcoming your inclination and your physical nature in your "house" and your "pasture," bending the *Sitra Achra* <note>The "Other Side" — a Kabbalistic term for the forces of evil or impurity</note> that opposes you. In this way, their sweetness flows until your name reaches the level of **"poured oil"**—which, when poured from vessel to vessel, its fragrance increases. As the Sages of blessed memory said: it is like a flask of *foliatum* <note>original: *pelyaton* — a precious aromatic oil mentioned in the Talmud</note>, etc. Therefore, because you are at this level, the "maidens"—meaning the two **worlds** <note>Hebrew: *olamot*</note>, the Upper World and the Lower World—**"love you."** They long for and yearn for your closeness due to your fulfillment of the Torah and commandments. Israel will not be at a lower level than Solomon; rather, they will be almost like angels, lacking nothing and doing no evil. If only the strength of their deeds would reach the Lower World, if not the Upper!

->**Draw me after you, let us run; the king has brought me into his chambers. We will exult and rejoice in you; we will extol your love more than wine; rightly do they love you.**<-

It is possible that this word <note>"Draw me"</note> is fitting for King Solomon and others like him who are perfect in Torah and commandments, for the people of that generation were not lacking. Therefore, he said that it would not be a difficult or impossible thing even for the physical mass [of the people]. Thus, **"Draw me,"** etc.—meaning, "Initially, begin to draw me and those of my age after You." All of Israel is easily bound together, as mentioned, as companions to achieve the aforementioned state. He stated another argument: that they would easily achieve their desire because this is not a new thing. **"The king has already brought me into his chambers"** at the Standing at Mount Sinai; we stood there and merited this state in a way that [it will not be lost].

<summary>This page interprets the "fragrance" and "oil" of Song of Songs as the refining power of the Torah and commandments. It contrasts the forced spiritual elevation at Sinai with the voluntary devotion of Solomon’s generation, emphasizing that spiritual perfection allows one to influence both the Upper and Lower Worlds.</summary>

<keywords>Song of Songs 1:3-4, Mount Sinai, Solomon, Poured Oil, Sitra Achra, Two Worlds, Repentance, Crown of Priesthood, Crown of Kingship, Midrash</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically addressing verses 1:5–7. It transitions from the high spiritual state of the Revelation at Sinai to the reality of human fallibility and the "stains" of sin, using the metaphor of physical darkness versus light. There is a slight discrepancy in page numbering; while the header says 7 (ז), it follows the themes established in the previous section.</meta>

# Song of Songs — Commentary

One who serves the wise <note>original: *meshamesh chakhamim* — a term for a dedicated disciple</note> and has bathed will return to his former state, and his youthful light will be whitened. This is the matter of the "blackness" that can be corrected during the Days of Repentance and through the ritual bath of purity. Specifically, this is the meaning of **"I am black but comely."** We say to the nations of the world who deny God: "What are you to us? We are like the son of a king who went out into the wilderness of the city; the sun beat down upon his head and his face became darkened. But upon returning to the city, with a little water and a little washing in one of the bathhouses, his body was whitened and he returned to his beauty as it was before." So too are we—Heaven forbid—regarding the sins of our ancestors <note>original: *avodat kokhavim* (idolatry) or the "sin that aged us"</note> etc.

The author offers another argument: this "blackness" was somewhat accidental, caused by physical companions who overcame him and caused him to sin. These external factors were his undoing and the cause of his blackness. Therefore, he asks to remove the cause so that the effect might also be removed. This is the meaning of **"my mother's sons were incensed against me."** As the Midrash says: "They made this fierce anger burn against me; they made me **a keeper of the vineyards,**" which refers to the affairs and desires of this world, "and **my own vineyard**"—meaning the service of God and the perfection of the soul—"**I have not kept.**"

It is possible that the "blackness" mentioned in the pathways of the world refers to a specific, known sin: the Sin of the Golden Calf, from which we have not yet been fully purified. That act was like a blemish upon the Great and Awesome Standing <note>The Revelation at Mount Sinai</note> with all His wonders and the great visions their eyes beheld. Undoubtedly, that sin cast a "blackness" upon them, hindering their spiritual breakthrough. Regarding the verse **"Look not upon me because I am swarthy,"** this means there was a reason to look away, because our "sun" was tanned. This "sun" refers to the Sun of the Sun <note>original: *Chur hacheres* — a play on words for the sun's heat or brilliance</note>, as Rashi (of blessed memory) wrote at the beginning of Ecclesiastes. It also refers to the "Moon" which is called "Sun," as it is written, "For the Lord God is a sun and a shield" (Psalm 84:11). The intention is that through the miracles and wonders, God returned to us, but the Sin of the Calf caused a doubt, providing a reason for "looking upon" our blackness.

Nevertheless, the text returns to say: **"Look not upon me because I am swarthy,"** even though there was reason to look, **"because the sun has tanned me."** The reason we should not be looked upon with disdain is because the "mother's sons"—referring to the Mixed Multitude <note>original: *erev rav* — the non-Israelites who joined the Exodus and were traditionally blamed for the Golden Calf</note>—were the cause. They were like foreigners raised among us, and they caused the straying. This follows the opinion of some of our Sages, particularly in the *Zohar*, that their evil "Sukkah" <note>likely a reference to their corrupt dwelling or influence</note> caused it for me, **"and they made me a keeper of the vineyards."** As the verse repeats: "My own vineyard I have not kept," which refers to the service of God, as mentioned before.

---

<columns>2</columns>
**Tell me, O You whom my soul loves, where You graze Your flock, where You make it rest at noon; for why should I be as one who wanders beside the flocks of Your companions?**

<column-break/>

Here, King Solomon reveals another argument regarding Israel’s standing before their King and their request. This matter concerns His blessed self and His glory. The issue is that when Israel exists in this state—in the thickness of their physical nature and their lowliness—evil is close at hand. This leads them to sin and to increase their transgressions.

---

Because of their iniquities and evil deeds upon the Holy Land, the Holy One, Blessed be He, will cast them off from it. They will go into exile among the nations who deny His Name and will be enslaved under their hand. This constitutes a *Chilul Hashem* <note>The desecration of God's Name</note>, where His glory and holiness are belittled. This is especially true during the intensity and strength of the subjugation and exile, such as during times of decrees and expulsions. His wrath upon them includes every sickness and every plague not written in the Book. Yet, the Holy One, Blessed be He, remains distant and suffers alongside everyone enslaved within His people, as if it were possible to say so, as it is written: "In all their affliction, He was afflicted" (Isaiah 63:9). And therefore...

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a sunburned prince to explain that Israel's "blackness" (sin) is temporary and reversible through repentance. It specifically identifies the Sin of the Golden Calf and the influence of the "Mixed Multitude" as causes for spiritual darkening while highlighting God's shared suffering in exile.</summary>
