<meta>This page continues the philosophical and allegorical commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically addressing verse 1:5 and the beginning of 1:6. Building on the previous page's theme of spiritual "stains" and purification, it explores the dual nature of the human being: the coarse physical body and the Divine soul.</meta>

<header># Commentary</header>

# **I am black but comely, O daughters of Jerusalem, as the tents of Kedar, as the curtains of Solomon.**

When Solomon viewed himself as being of a high and elevated rank within his inner chambers, it might seem to any listener that such a claim is laughable. One might say that humans lack the necessary preparation due to their physical matter, and that their souls follow holy matters while worldly affairs separate them from the Soul that is close to God. This is especially true in the eyes of the "multitudes of the high ones" <note>The angels or celestial beings.</note> who are pure, clear, and spiritual. To them, it seems remote that a physical being could be sustained in such a state; it seems as far from man as the east is from the west.

Therefore, Solomon turned his face toward them and called them **"Daughters of Jerusalem,"** referring to the fact that they surround the "Jerusalem on High" <note>The spiritual archetype of the earthly city.</note>. He then declared: **"I am black but comely."** By this he meant: "I recognize my essence; I am 'black' from the perspective of physical matter and the material body, but I am 'comely' from the perspective of the soul, which is a part of God from above." 

This aligns with what Nachmanides <note>Rabbi Moses ben Nachman (Ramban), a 13th-century scholar.</note> wrote regarding the verse *And He breathed into his nostrils the breath of life* (Genesis 2:7): that he who blows, blows from within himself. Thus, the soul originates from a place of holiness that is even higher than the sanctity of the angels. It follows that even if, from the perspective of the body and physical matter, I am **"as the tents of Kedar"**—which appear ugly, black, and like rags from the outside but contain every precious thing inside—from the perspective of the soul, I am **"as the curtains of Solomon."** This refers to the Holy Temple, which is a tabernacle and a dwelling place for the Divine Presence <note>original: *Shekhinah*</note>. So too does the soul have an affinity with the spiritual realm and is prepared for the Divine Presence to rest upon it and cleave to it forever. If so, it is not far-fetched that I might achieve the prophetic level I requested.

Alternatively, **"I am black but comely"** means: I know full well that in my current level there is a side of "blackness," which refers to natural human failings—where the "primordial filth" <note>A reference to the *zuhama*, the spiritual impurity introduced by the serpent in Eden.</note> has not been fully purged, the physical urges grow strong, and the material body assists these urges with a desire that increases heat. From this side, I am indeed black. Yet, despite this, from another side I am "comely" and beautiful through good deeds that rise to a high degree of importance, as we wrote above regarding the verse *Because of the savor of thy good ointments.*

However, my current state is **"as the tents of Kedar,"** which are moved from place to place. So too, I wander—sometimes toward merit and sometimes toward guilt, sometimes toward evil and sometimes toward good. But I liken the ideal of my state to **"the curtains of Solomon."** As our Sages of blessed memory said: *The curtains of Solomon* refer to "the One to whom peace belongs" <note>A wordplay on Solomon (*Shlomo*) and Peace (*Shalom*), referring to God.</note>, the One who spoke and the world came into being. Just as His heavenly curtains never moved from their place, so too on this side do I strive for a precious and unshakable desire—that I should forever be prepared for Your service, and that the radiance and existence of my soul's perfection should be cleaved to the Blessed Name of God.

---

# **Look not upon me because I am swarthy, because the sun has tanned me; my mother's sons were incensed against me; they made me a keeper of the vineyards, but my own vineyard I have not kept.**

Because he wanted to argue to the "Daughters of Jerusalem" that there was no total ruin or complete darkness here, but only the blackness of physical matter, he explained that there is a corresponding beauty—the soul. Regarding this "blackness" that exists in one's character, which refers to the sins and impurities that a person is full of from head to toe, both light and severe: this is indeed a great veil separating him from his redemption. 

To this he said: **"Look not upon me because I am swarthy."** By using the word "swarthy" <note>original: *shcharchoret* — a diminutive or intensified form of black.</note>, he means a "doubled blackness." He suggests that this state is capable of being repaired, for it is not something natural to me. Rather, it is an accidental occurrence, like a person whose face has been darkened by... 

<meta>catchword: the sun</meta>

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs 1:5-6, explaining the "blackness" as the physical body and the "beauty" as the Divine soul. It argues that human sin is an accidental "tanning" rather than an inherent quality, allowing for spiritual restoration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the Song of Songs. While the previous page focused on the soul's purity despite the body's "blackness," this page moves to verse 1:8 and 1:9, discussing the communal responsibility to care for the vulnerable, the importance of education for the young, and God’s mercy during the Exile, using the Exodus from Egypt as a precedent.</meta>

# **Song of Songs**

Regarding the righteous, it is said: **"Go forth by the footsteps of the flock"** (Song of Songs 1:8). These are the humble and the lowly who are "down in the dust." It is fitting to gather them into the "footsteps of the flock," meaning the most marginalized and disadvantaged members of the nation. One must strive to send them aid by providing for all their needs. Furthermore, the verse states: **"And pasture your kids beside the shepherds' dwellings."** These "dwellings" are the *Batei Midrashot* <note>Houses of Study, the traditional centers of Jewish learning.</note>. The verse implies that the scholars are the "shepherds" of the people in matters of religion, law, and justice. It mentions **"your kids"** to teach us that even for the smallest members of the nation, justice must be executed without favoritism toward the great, as it is written: *Ye shall hear the small as well as the great* (Deuteronomy 1:17).

Alternatively, the verse may mean: If you do not know the path for yourself, despite being a "chest of treasures" <note>Hebrew: *Kupat Nechasim*; a metaphor for a person filled with knowledge and good deeds.</note>, and the comforts of the soul have not been realized due to the weight of your character traits and actions, then **"Go forth by the footsteps of the flock."** This suggests that those "inferior" ones in the flock are like the "heels" <note>Hebrew: *Akev*; the word for "footstep" is related to the word for "heel," the lowest part of the body.</note> which are at the very bottom of the body. This refers to the wicked and the sinners of Israel who have gone astray. **"Go forth"** to them to bring them back in repentance, for in doing so, you will mend them, and your reward will be very great. This is as written in the *Zohar* <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah).</note> at the beginning of the portion of *Terumah*. 

Coupled with this, strive to **"pasture your kids,"** who are the young children of Israel. Support them in the **"shepherds' dwellings,"** meaning they should remain in the synagogues and the houses of study, following the paths of the Torah and good character. For the traits acquired during childhood and youth will not depart from them even when they grow old (Proverbs 22:6). They will always conduct themselves by these traits. Let the young ones not abandon their path in their youth to turn toward leisure, play, and mockery; for in the end, it is difficult to separate oneself from such habits, and they may fall into a "corrupt culture." Therefore, it says: **"And pasture your kids,"** etc.

**I have compared thee, O my love, to a steed in Pharaoh's chariots** (Song of Songs 1:9). It is possible that here the Holy One, Blessed be He, also replied to the matter she <note>The "Beloved," representing the Nation of Israel.</note> asked above:
**"Tell me, O thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest [thy flock]..."** (Song of Songs 1:7). He explains that regarding the matter of exile and servitude—even though the people might deserve the full intensity of exile due to the severity of their actions—He will conduct Himself toward them with kindness and mercy. This is like the verse that states: *I will not reject them, nor will I abhor them, to destroy them* (Leviticus 26:44). This truth is verified by the servitude they endured when leaving Egypt. They were not strictly worthy of being redeemed according to the letter of the law, for they were defiled by the idols of Egypt and were themselves idolaters. As the Guardian Angel of Egypt argued against them [at the Red Sea]: "These [Egyptians] are idolaters and these [Israelites] are idolaters!" <note>A famous Midrashic motif where the angels question why Israel should be saved if they had also sinned.</note> In the end, God turned to the Attribute of Mercy to strengthen them so they might be saved, ensuring their shameful and cursed deeds did not hinder them.

The argument used by Moses our Teacher was similar to his reasoning regarding the Golden Calf in the verse: *Lord, why does Your anger burn against Your people?* (Exodus 32:11). As our Sages of blessed memory said in *Shemot Rabbah* <note>A collection of homiletic interpretations (Midrash) on the Book of Exodus.</note>, Chapter 43: Why did Moses mention the Exodus from Egypt there? Moses said: "Master of the Universe, from where did You bring them out? Was it not from Egypt, where everyone worshipped lambs?" <note>The Egyptians viewed the lamb as a deity; the Israelites had been submerged in this culture.</note> 

Rabbi Huna said in the name of Rabbi Yochanan: This is like a parable of a wise man who opened a perfume shop for his son in a market of harlots. The location was a marketplace of harlots, the street was notorious, and the trade was one of perfumes <note>The son was given a "triggering" environment and a "tempting" trade.</note>. The boy acted according to his youth and fell into a corrupt culture. When the father’s friend found him with a harlot, the father began to scream, "I will kill him!" The friend said to him: "You ruined this lad yourself, and now you scream at him? You ignored all other trades and taught him only the perfume trade; you ignored all other streets and opened a shop for him only in the market of harlots!" 

So did Moses say: "Master of the Universe, You ignored the whole world and enslaved Your children only in Egypt, where they worshipped lambs!"

<summary>This page interprets the Song of Songs as a call to care for the poor and educate the young in communal institutions. It uses a famous Midrashic parable to argue that God shows mercy to Israel during exile because their spiritual failings are often a product of the "corrupt" environments in which they are placed.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, righteous, humble, education, kids, shepherds, Batei Midrashot, Zohar, repentance, Moses, Egypt, mercy, parable, perfume shop, idol worship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs 1:7–8. The previous page introduced the "footsteps of the flock" as a metaphor for the humble and the marginalized. This page deepens the discussion on the Divine-human relationship during the Exile, interpreting the "heat of the noon" as the intensity of national suffering and the "veiled woman" as a metaphor for the Divine Presence (Shekhinah) in mourning.</meta>

# **Commentary**

<columns>

Therefore, His desire and will were that they should be drawn out from the winepress <note>Hebrew: *Makhbes*; a metaphor for the crushing pressure of suffering or exile</note> toward the good through freedom, so that they would not reach a state of bitterness. Thus, His name would not be profaned among the nations, and the Master would not be grieved. This is what she whom my soul loves told me: The Blessed Name <note>God</note> lacks nothing in His understanding of Israel. According to the number and the thought of the Lord... and this is what Jacob [said] etc. **"Tell me... where you pasture and where you rest [the flock] at noon"** <note>Song of Songs 1:7</note>. This is to say: in the peak intensity of the exiles, which burns like the heat of the sun at noon. For it is difficult for you to see your servants beaten and dying, while Solomon <note>In this allegorical tradition, "Solomon" often refers to God, the "King to whom peace (Shalom) belongs"</note> bears it. **"Why should I be as one who veils herself"**—meaning like one who mourns in their rebellion. He established this like a rope that wraps around a flaw, weeping over the flocks of your companions—meaning the [other] nations.

These nations are stationed under the rule of the celestial Princes <note>In Jewish thought, every nation has a presiding heavenly angel or "Prince," except Israel, which is under God's direct providence</note>, whereas the portion of the Lord is His people. He called them "your companions" in relation to Babylon. He bequeathed the nations to them and appointed one Prince over every wall. But He, may He be blessed, chose Israel, and Jacob is the lot of His inheritance. Something similar to this is explained there in the Midrash <note>Traditional rabbinic homiletical commentary</note>; look it up there.

This is desired regarding "the flocks of your companions"—to say that it refers to the Divine Presence <note>Hebrew: *Shekhinah*</note>, as stated at the beginning of the verse, **"whom my soul loves."** This is said for His benefit; it is difficult for Him when they are in the "noon" [of suffering] and He is blocked. The Exile, as stated, is based on another claim regarding them: they are the "flocks of the companions" of the Blessed Name—the wicked nations. It will be difficult in your eyes because of them. This also seems to be the opinion of our Rabbis, of blessed memory, on the phrase "the flocks of your companions": when he went to your companions, they would consume me... What do they respond to this?

Furthermore, it may be said that he intended another matter: my desire is to extinguish the remnants in the thunder. For while we were on our land and the Holy Temple was on its foundation, the Merciful One provided for every holy thing. When sin increased, the flow from above—which was constant—went instead to the trade of the nations under the Princes. They suck from the sweetness... and He sends water across the outskirts. Because of sins, the children were exiled from their Father's table, and we groan upon the earth. To all the Princes of each nation, He gave a portion. This is the essence of the matter: "A handmaid who displaces her mistress" <note>A reference to Proverbs 30:23, allegorically describing the nations ruling over Israel</note>. We stand at a distance, weakened and veiled in the corners of the house, without a share of the spoils to be distributed, while He gives prey to his household and a portion to his maidens. This is what is meant by: **"Why should I be as one who veils herself beside the flocks of your companions?"**—these are the nations who deny [God], placed under the rule of the Princes as mentioned.

This is the response of the Bride <note>The Assembly of Israel</note> at the beginning of "Solomon," in the name of the Assembly of Israel, as she was supplicating concerning the weight of the Exile and the depth of the transition in its severity. For without a doubt, it would come upon her and she would be consumed while in this "winepress," for she did not want the rebukes to be consumed by...

<column-break/>

**If you do not know, O fairest among women,**
**go out by the footsteps of the flock and pasture**
**your kids by the shepherds' dwellings.**

<column-break/>

The injuries are light... it is written, "for the grace of the clay..." etc. 
**"If you do not know"** how to act with will so that you may be the "fairest among women," like a merchant who loads the [burden]. The mark and the blows until the entanglement of the flesh—knowledge of this will lead you to fall into the place of Exile. A preparation was sent to you alone that will suffice to lighten the Exile and to endure their heat. It shall also be for a sufficiency, which is to behave with [justice], as the scripture says: "Zion shall be redeemed with justice, and they that return to her with righteousness" <note>Isaiah 1:27</note>.

</columns>

It concludes with the beginning of the divine response, urging the people to follow the "footsteps of the flock" and seek redemption through justice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs. The previous page discussed the soul's plea to God (the "Shepherd") to be led to the "pasture" during the heat of the exile. This page moves into verse 1:9 and 1:10, exploring how God justifies and beautifies Israel through the lens of comparison and the gift of the Torah.</meta>

# **Song of Songs**

With kindness and mercy, even though their evil deeds forced them to return to their [sinful] growths and to be cut off by their own hands, as will be explained—God forbid that they should ever lose their special status <note>Hebrew: *Segulah*; referring to the concept of Israel as a "treasured people"</note>. Rather, the merit of the Patriarchs <note>Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</note> works within them. He began to choose them, not because they were intrinsically worthy, but because of His blessed goodness, to bring them under the wings of the Divine Presence <note>Hebrew: *Shekhinah*; the indwelling feminine aspect of God</note>. He said: **"I have likened you, my darling, to a mare among Pharaoh’s chariots"** <note>Song of Songs 1:9</note>. 

The intention here follows the words of our Sages of blessed memory in the Midrash <note>A genre of rabbinic literature that interprets biblical texts through homily and story</note>: It is written: "O Lord, the Lord has gathered His servant in judgment," and it is written: "Lord, do not come into judgment with Your servant." David <note>King David, the traditional author of Psalms</note> said before the Holy One, Blessed be He: "Master of the Universe, when You are judging the wicked, judge me [as well]; but when You are judging the righteous, do not come into judgment with Your servant." The reason is that since the King bestows good upon the righteous and punishes the wicked, when He judges the wicked and judges [David] alongside them, David will be considered righteous by comparison, and his merit will be established. But when He judges the righteous, the King is strict in His judgment, and [David] might be considered wicked by comparison and found guilty. 

These matters are clear: when the Name, may He be blessed, brought plagues upon Egypt, He turned to Israel and compared them to the Egyptians. By virtue of this comparison, they were considered righteous and worthy of being brought close under the wings of the Blessed One. This is the meaning of **"to a mare among Pharaoh’s chariots I have likened you"**—I measured you against them so that you would be worthy of being My beloved.

## **Your cheeks are lovely with ornaments, your neck with strings of beads.**
<note>Song of Songs 1:10</note>

He says that even after this, He advises and clarifies
->in the world that they might be close<-
that despite their unworthiness, the Blessed One's desire was based on total kindness. He looked upon them in light of their future value, as explained above: when they stood at Mount Sinai to receive the Torah. This is **"Your cheeks are lovely with ornaments"**—referring to the two Torahs, the Written and the Oral <note>The "Written Torah" refers to the Five Books of Moses; the "Oral Torah" refers to the traditions and interpretations later codified in the Talmud</note>, as our Sages of blessed memory said in the Midrash. He says their "cheeks"—the organs of speech—were beautified by the acceptance and study of the Torah. Even if they had not yet received it, the rest of the body's members were beautified by the anticipation of the commandments they were destined to receive and perform. 

This is also the meaning of **"your neck with strings of beads."** The "neck" is a vital part of the body, as explained in the Zohar <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah)</note> regarding the verse "On our necks we were pursued" <note>Lamentations 5:5</note>. Although it possesses natural beauty, the soul's true beauty is not physical but that which is acquired through the commandments. This beauty then extends to the rest of the limbs. The core of the matter is that they were elevated before Him at the moment they were about to receive the Torah, being viewed as if they were already "full of commandments like a pomegranate." If this was not already true in reality, it was true through His kindness.

Alternatively, it means that the Blessed One showed kindness by looking toward whatever small merit they possessed, even if it was only a thought or a desire. This is what our Sages said in the Midrash: Rabbi Levi said, "The Israelites had four good 'hopes' <note>Hebrew: *Kavuyot*; lines of hope or expectation</note> at the Sea: they hoped for the Torah, they hoped for the Festivals, they hoped for the Tabernacle, etc." This is **"Your cheeks are lovely with ornaments"**—the hope for the Torah. **"Your neck with strings of beads"** refers to the hope for the Tabernacle <note>Hebrew: *Mishkan*; the portable sanctuary in the desert</note>, which is called the "neck" as we wrote regarding the Zohar on the verse "on our neck" mentioned above. He says **"with strings of beads"** <note>Hebrew: *Charuzim*; also implying "connected" or "linked"</note> because the Tabernacle connects the upper and lower worlds, linking everything together into one vessel. 

Alternatively, it means: **"Your cheeks are lovely with ornaments"** from the moment of the Giving of the Torah; the speech of Israel became beautiful when they spoke and said: "We will do..." <note>Referring to the famous declaration of the Israelites at Sinai: "We will do and we will hear" (Exodus 24:7)</note>

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs 1:9–10, explaining how God judged Israel favorably by comparing them to the Egyptians and how the beauty of the "cheeks" and "neck" represents Israel's commitment to the Written and Oral Torah and the Tabernacle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs 1:9. The previous page established that King David sought to be judged alongside the wicked so that his relative merit would shine. Here, the author applies this logic to the Exodus from Egypt: even though the Israelites were culturally and spiritually "submerged" in Egyptian paganism, God chose to view them through the lens of mercy and their future potential.</meta>

נ <note>Page 50</note>
-> ### **Commentary of Sh.Y.** <-

...forgiving, and they learned from them the [use of the] harp, and even they were with the cloud. Therefore, he said: "The rod which you hold." <note>This likely refers to the staff of Moses and the miraculous protection in the wilderness.</note> From where is it proven? From the "Seal." <note>original: *Chotam*</note> Thus, we find that while they were submerged among the Egyptians <note>original: *Mekharis*, a variant or error for *Mitzrim* (Egyptians)</note>, they too were like them in the impurity of idol worship. There is an aspect of "intermingling," and it is on the side of **Kindness** and **Mercy**. 

This is the meaning of the verse: **"To my mare..."** etc. <note>Song of Songs 1:9: "I have likened you, my darling, to a mare among Pharaoh’s chariots."</note> That is to say: because you were like a mare among Pharaoh’s chariots—submerged among them—there was a reason to keep you at a distance. Yet, did I not do otherwise? Rather, I saw you in a different light, for **"I have likened you"** and I considered you worthy of drawing near to Me and becoming **"my darling."** From this perspective, I acted with the quality of Mercy in that redemption. Know that He behaves in this same way during the current exiles.

It is possible to connect this to the concept of **Kindness** after the deed <note>referring to a later historical or spiritual stage</note>. Since their actions were lacking, they were not worthy of redemption, especially because they were submerged among the Egyptian idol-worshippers, as we have said. Nevertheless, the Holy One, Blessed be He, who "places the seal before Him," acts according to the future and not merely based on their current state of grace. This is one of His kindnesses: He does not look upon future evil deeds <note>meaning He does not punish in advance</note>, but judges a person "as he is now." <note>A reference to the legal principle *Ba-asher hu sham*, "where he is now," derived from the story of Ishmael in Genesis 21:17.</note> Regarding Ishmael, it is written: *"For God has heard the voice of the youth where he is,"* meaning in his current state of relative innocence. However, regarding the "future good," God *does* look upon it, even if the person is currently wicked. 

This is the secret of: **"Because you were like a mare among Pharaoh’s chariots"**—meaning mixed and submerged among them. I did not see any current goodness or worthiness in you, yet **"I have likened you"** and envisioned you before Me in your future form as **"my darling."** This is as the Midrash states: **"My sustainer"** <note>original: *Parnasati*; a wordplay on *Ra'yati* (my darling/friend), suggesting that Israel "sustains" God through their service</note>, for they would provide for My "countenance" through the daily offerings <note>Hebrew: *Tamidin*</note> every day. This follows the verse: *"The one lamb you shall offer in the morning..."* <note>Exodus 29:39</note>. 

Rabbi Judah bar Simon said: Israel used to sacrifice two daily offerings <note>Hebrew: *Tamidin*</note> every day—one in the morning and one in the afternoon. The morning offering was sacrificed for the transgressions committed during the night, and the afternoon offering was sacrificed for the transgressions committed during the day. Consequently, no person ever spent the night in Jerusalem with a sin in their hand, as it is said: *"Righteousness lodged in her."* <note>Isaiah 1:21</note>. 

Behold, besides the aspect of "sustenance" mentioned regarding the sacrifice—concerning the awakening and binding of the Upper Lights and all the worlds, as is known—there is also a portion given to the "multitudes of the side of impurity" <note>referring to the *Sitra Achra* or the "Other Side" in Kabbalah</note> to stop and shut their mouths so they do not approach to prosecute, as is explained in the Book of the **Zohar** in many places. Furthermore, regarding the statement about "sustenance," another explanation is that the flow from the sacrifices touches both those below and those above. This is because when Israel is clean and pure from the filth of sins and the rust of guilt, there is no place for the "Prosecutor" <note>original: *Ba'al Din*; the Accuser</note> to argue, prevent, or withhold the flow of abundance and goodness designated for those below. Then, the day of great goodness and blessing is in His hand to give and bestow. 

From this perspective, the Assembly of Israel is called **"my darling,"** as if her intention were to "sustain" <note>provide for</note> the Blessed Name. For when sins increase, the strict Judgment increases, and the Power Above is "prevented" from performing goodness and kindness, as in the verse: *"You weakened the Rock who bore you."* <note>Deuteronomy 32:18</note>. Since the purity from sin is drawn through the sacrifices, as the Sages of blessed memory explained, the Holy One, Blessed be He, called them **"my darling"** <note>or "my sustainer"</note> because of the two daily offerings, through which no man ever lodged in Jerusalem with a sin in his hand.

It is also possible to say that with this verse, He began to improve the "image" of the core redemption of Solomon <note>likely referring to the building of the First Temple</note>, regarding the change of status and character of Israel for the better, as in days of old during the Standing at Mount Sinai... and the essence of the image is that in days gone by, He brought them from Egypt to this status, even though they were not worthy by law...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs, transitioning from the discussion of the "mare among Pharaoh's chariots" to the specific merits and failures of Israel during the Exodus and the Revelation at Sinai. It contrasts the "good fragrance" of obedience with the "bad odor" of the Golden Calf.</meta>

# Song of Songs

...as points of silver, which is a mixture of "points of blood" as previously mentioned. The words of this interpretation are divided into two explanations which will be arranged within the text to make them easier to understand:

## While the king was at his table, my nard gave forth its fragrance.

<margin>According to</margin> the first explanation—which is the response of the Exile—he provides a reason for why the Holy One, blessed be He, passed over the sin of the Golden Calf. This was due to the prayer of Moses, who "stood in the breach" to turn back God's wrath from destroying them, causing His mercy to yearn for them despite His anger. This refers to what happened to Israel at the time they received the Torah, when they famously declared: **"We will do and we will obey"** <note>original: *Na'aseh ve-Nishma*</note>. Without a doubt, they acquired great perfection and infinite importance through these words. This explanation follows the words of Rabbi Judah as found in the Midrash: "While the King of Kings, the Holy One, blessed be He, was at His table in the heavens, Israel gave off a good fragrance before Him at Mount Sinai and said: 'All that the Lord has spoken, we will do and we will obey.'" He notes that these words served to remove the filth of the sin of the Calf, much like a good scent <note>The "nard" or "spikenard" mentioned in the verse is an expensive aromatic oil</note> is effective in a place of waste to remove a bad odor and cleanse the impurity. 

<margin>And according to</margin> the second path, the text here contains the essence of His response. For behold, anyone with a heart can see and understand how greatly the Holy One, blessed be He, acted with kindness to bring them to this state—to perfect their bodies and souls, as I wrote above. Yet, like Adam, "they transgressed the covenant" <note>A reference to Hosea 6:7, comparing Israel's failure to Adam's</note> and violated the law in that very state they were in. This is the meaning of **"While the king was at his table..."** etc., following the opinion of Rabbi Meir in the Midrash: "While the King of Kings, the Holy One, blessed be He, was at His table in the heavens, Israel gave off a bad odor and said to the Calf: 'These are your gods, O Israel!'" Therefore, what business do you have with Him regarding this "sickness" <note>The spiritual malady of idolatry</note>, for it shall remain with you as stated. 

<margin>And according to</margin> the first path in this verse... <note>The text here discusses the "first commandments" and Moses' role</note> regarding the state of the first Tablets, of which Moses asked. This is all a parable, as mentioned. It also says that they had already reached a point of great desire but could not endure it, for they immediately said to Moses: "Let not God speak with us [lest we die]" <note>Exodus 20:16</note>. Thus, they left that good perfection—the sight of which deserves to be called "fragrance." In this sense, **"my nard gave forth its fragrance"** means that the nard "gave away" or "surrendered" its scent <note>The author is playing on the Hebrew word *natan* (gave), suggesting that the fragrance departed or was lost</note>, similar to the phrase "Sihon did not *give* [permit] Israel to pass." This is the intention according to the path already written.

## A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me; he shall lie between my breasts.

In this verse and the one following it, the Midrash alludes to the three merits of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. They said that the **"bundle of myrrh"** refers to **Abraham**. Just as myrrh is the head of all spices and its scent only spreads through heat <note>literally "in a furnace," referring to Abraham being cast into the furnace by Nimrod</note>, and whoever gathers it finds their hands embittered, so too was Abraham the head of all the righteous. His deeds were not fully known until he was cast into the fiery furnace, and he "embittered" and afflicted himself with suffering. 

**"A cluster of henna"** <note>original: *Kopher*</note>—this refers to **Isaac**, who was bound upon the altar like a "ransom" <note>also *Kopher* in Hebrew</note> that atones for the sins of Israel. 

**"In the vineyards of Ein Gedi"**—this refers to our father **Jacob**, who entered his father's presence in a state of fear while wearing a disguise. He wore the **"skins of the kids of the goats"** <note>original: *Gedi* means "kid," creating a wordplay with the place name "Ein Gedi"</note> and took the blessings from them, becoming the "eye" <note>original: *Ein*</note> of the world. 

According to their way of interpretation, it is possible to settle the matter according to our own path: the Holy One, blessed be He, redirected the evil path they were on during the intensity of the Exile so that it would be handled with kindness and mercy, just as it was in the days when they left Egypt. Despite the sin of the Calf, He said now that they had great "advocates" <note>original: *paraklitin*, from the Greek *parakletos*</note>, and these are the Holy Patriarchs, for...

<sig>g g 2 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Merit</meta>

<summary>This page explores two interpretations of the "fragrance" at Sinai: one where Israel's obedience (Na'aseh VeNishma) acts as a sweet perfume covering the stench of the Golden Calf, and another where the "fragrance" is the bad odor of idolatry itself. It then links the spices mentioned in the Song of Songs to the merits of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs 1:11. The previous page discussed how Israel's declaration of "We will do and we will obey" at Mount Sinai acted as a "good fragrance" that counteracted the spiritual "bad odor" of the Golden Calf. This page explores two specific interpretive paths for why God rewarded Israel with "gold and silver" despite their failings.</meta>

# Commentary

**"We will do and we will obey."** <note>original: *Na'aseh ve-Nishma* — the famous declaration of the Israelites at Sinai</note>. Just as there are specific parts and rhythms in a poem, so too were the words of this song spoken concerning the staff of the tribe and the Children of Israel. As it is pressed in the Midrash <note>A traditional Jewish homiletic interpretation</note> regarding the poetic verses used by the liturgists, the intention is that they acquired life, strength, and great perfection through their "good speech." By this, they prepared themselves to receive the Torah at that great assembly and to cling to the Blessed Name. What was chosen for this verse will be based on two interpretive paths, showing how our writing and arrangement distinguish the grammatical nuances that follow, with God's help.

**According to the first path, it revolves around** the intensity of the Exile. It suggests that besides the kindness ->**We will make for you circles of gold with studs**<- which the Blessed Name performed with His ->**of silver.**<- overpowering strength and through the receiving of the Torah—meaning, an even greater kindness beyond those—He acted at the time of the making of the Golden Calf. At that moment, they were liable for total destruction, as He said, "Let me alone, that My anger may blaze against them and consume them" <note>Exodus 32:10</note>. Despite all this, He showed mercy in His abundant kindness and was long-suffering. This is what is meant by "We will make for you circles <note>The Hebrew *turi* can mean rows, circles, or 'turns' of time</note> of gold": all those "turns" and times of gold which Israel stripped from their ears. The fact that they were stripping this gold away should have made them liable for the strength of the fire of judgment; however, the Blessed Name looked upon it and transformed great kindness for us, turning those "turns" into something for your benefit. He had mercy on you **with studs of silver**, meaning by mixing in the Attribute of Mercy—which is likened to silver—to beautify it. He recognized them in their hidden strength, and through the awakening of mercy, you were included in what I spoke to do for you. 

When it says, "We will make for you," it is a narration of the kindness the Holy One, blessed be He, showed to Israel at that time. Through this closeness, He desired to mix in mercy for their benefit, so they would not be judged according to their actual deeds. 

**According to the second path, it revolves around** the essential virtue of Solomon. It suggests that although they were pushed away from that status at the assembly of Mount Sinai, it was only through the side of kindness and by turning toward some small merit they possessed—even though it was small and incomplete—that they could act with such vigor. Despite this, the Blessed Name acted with "loving-kindness" <note>term: *hasidut* — acting beyond the strict letter of the law</note> and said: "Even though your merit is not sufficient, My love for you brings you to a state where you are blessed and pure." This is like vessels and jewelry made of gold; the making of the vessel requires it to be engraved in the iron furnace to separate all dross and rust from it, leaving only pure and clean gold. So He did for you: this is the matter of "removing the filth" <note>original: *zohama* — spiritual impurity</note>. This is what is meant by "We will make for you circles of gold": with your forgiveness, though you were pushed away, you began to be covered with merit. He prepared mercy to do so, and this is "with studs of silver." 

In the Midrash, "circles" (*turim*) is interpreted as "two turtledoves" <note>The Hebrew word for turtledove is *tor*</note>, or "two rows of wheat." One refers to Moses, and elsewhere to a "good gift." Our Sages of blessed memory also interpreted this from the language of "leaving over" <note>Hebrew: *v'totar*</note>. This fits our chosen theme: that He would make a "remainder" to stay with the Children of Israel like pure gold, by which their filth, transgressions, and the coarseness of their materiality would cease. 

As the Midrash also explains, "circles" (*turim*) can be a language of "times" or "seasons," as in "When the turn (*tor*) of each maiden came" <note>Esther 2:12</note>. This refers to another kindness that the Blessed Name performed with His overpowering strength, as mentioned in the verse "Leaping over the mountains" <note>Song of Songs 2:8</note>—meaning He leaped over the planned calculations and the ends of the exile. The meaning is that although the decree was for four hundred years, they did not stay that long, for He showed mercy on the count of "Descend" <note>note: The Hebrew word *R'du* (Descend) has a numerical value of 210, representing the actual years spent in Egypt instead of the decreed 400</note>. Although it would seem this was not a matter of strict justice but of pure kindness, nonetheless, the Blessed Name did it through the side of Justice mixed with Mercy. As it is written, "in a land not theirs" <note>Genesis 15:13</note>, and from the tidings of Isaac, etc. This is what is meant by "circles of gold"—meaning the times of redemption through the "Judgment" associated with the nature of gold—"We will make for you with points..."

<summary>This page provides two primary interpretations of Song of Songs 1:11, explaining how God used mercy (symbolized by silver) to temper judgment (gold) following the sin of the Golden Calf and during the Egyptian exile.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Midrash, Golden Calf, Attribute of Mercy, Silver and Gold, Sinai, Exodus from Egypt, R'du, Gematria, Moses, Solomon</keywords>

Having previously discussed the Israelites' declaration at Mount Sinai, the author now transitions to the significance of the Tabernacle and the Holy Temple as the permanent dwelling place for the Divine Presence among the people, and how Israel maintains its spiritual "beauty" even through the hardships of Exile.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
**Song of Songs**

And a word of accompaniment was granted to them at the time of their approach, and this refers to the Holy Temple <note>original: *Beit HaMikdash*</note> which was already present within our camps. It existed through a state of attachment <note>original: *dveikut* — a state of intense spiritual cleaving to the Divine</note> to the Blessed Name, and the Lord dwelt in their midst. From all of this, a spirit of purity and extra holiness rested upon them. Through this, the coarseness of physical matter was transformed and overturned, and sins were removed, just as occurred during the standing at Mount Sinai. They merited holy splendor and purity to such an extent that there was no place for a spirit of impurity to spread among them. This holiness was for them a "double-wrapped cord," and this is the meaning of: ->**"A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me..."**<- <note>Song of Songs 1:13</note>. Through this "binding" of the bundle of myrrh, they brought the Blessed Name near; therefore, "my beloved is mine" and clings to me. The restoration for all of this, according to the understanding of "He shall lie between my breasts," is the matter of the Holy Temple, as I have written. The Blessed One chose to bless the paths there together. "Lodge afar," and let the bundle of myrrh be upon the Merciful path <note>original: *derekh ha-rachamon* — likely an interpretive method focusing on God's mercy</note>, and "between my breasts he shall lie" refers to the inner path. You shall see this as King Solomon, peace be upon him, stated in his response, and it is correct.

### A cluster of henna is my beloved to me, in the vineyards of En-Gedi.

This verse has already been explained regarding the arrangement of the locations, mentioning those who are distant and those who merit following in the "tracks of the lambs" <note>A reference to Song of Songs 1:8</note>, according to the path of our Sages of blessed memory <note>original: *Hazal*</note> in the Midrash. According to the "Merciful Path" and according to our intellectual and physical path: because he mentioned the matter of the Holy Temple which was in our camps—where they acquired and toiled to gain a binding in holiness and purity, being attached to the Blessed Name until He left the upper realms and dwelt among the lower beings—he returned and said that it was not hidden from His eyes that this was a great level for them. However, because they were not accustomed to it, he was concerned that it might cease today or tomorrow. Therefore, he asked for a change in status so that it might endure forever, so that their physical nature and matter would not collapse. He desired for God to strike a path of kindness <note>original: *hesed*</note> to provide cover and atonement <note>A wordplay on *kopher* (henna) and *kapparah* (atonement)</note>. For it is known that the Merciful One is compassionate to all sinners. This is what he was asking: to increase his nature and intellect within the boundaries of life's troubles and worldly matters. Just as the "cluster of henna" <note>original: *eshkol ha-kopher*</note>—because [the plant] is cherished and cannot be easily depleted, and it gives fruit many times during the study of Torah—so too a few good deeds are significant, acting like the "cluster of henna" in the vineyards of En-Gedi.

### Behold, you are beautiful, my love; behold, you are beautiful; your eyes are doves.

According to the Merciful path, this increases, for there is yet a hidden depth here to respond to the matter of the Exile <note>original: *Galut*</note>, which is what Solomon intended at the end of the verse "I acquired for myself," etc. This relates to the "desecration of the Name" <note>original: *Hillul HaShem*</note>. As our Sages of blessed memory said, "Solomon sinned," etc., as I have written. And now, like one returning his hand to this, he says: **"Behold, you are beautiful."** That is to say: I recognize the flaw that exists while you are in Exile, yet you remain beautiful through the commandments <note>original: *mitzvot*</note>. As they interpreted in the Midrash: beautiful in "positive commandments" and beautiful in "negative commandments," as well as in the specific details of the commandments (see there). And not only this, but also another beauty: **"your eyes are doves."** As our Sages of blessed memory interpreted: Just as this dove stretches out its neck for slaughter, so too is Israel, as it is said, "For Your sake we are killed all day long" <note>Psalm 44:23</note>. Therefore, even if they are revealed to be far from God (Heaven forbid), nevertheless, from another perspective, there is the "Sanctification of the Name" <note>original: *Kiddush HaShem*</note>. This means they sanctify His Name in public and hand themselves over to be killed for the fulfillment of His commandments and His laws.

According to the path of tradition: **"Behold, you are beautiful, my love"**—meaning, you have now reached, through...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs. The previous page discussed the indwelling of the Divine Presence in the Tabernacle and the Temple, and how this holiness shielded Israel from impurity. Here, the author explores the "merit of the ancestors" and the specific theological concept of the death of the righteous as a form of communal atonement.</meta>

# Commentary 1

Their merit stood by them during the Exodus from Egypt and also during the sin of the Golden Calf, just as Moses said: "Remember Abraham..." etc. <note>Exodus 32:13</note>. And to sustain them in the later generations with grace, you shall show mercy through the merit of the fathers. What would become of them? For they have nothing else to lean upon. This is the secret of the ->**"bundle of myrrh"**<- <note>Song of Songs 1:13; original: *Tzror ha-Mor*</note>. Abraham was the cause of my freedom <note>A wordplay on *Mor* (myrrh) and *Dror* (freedom)</note> and stood to assist me during the Exodus from Egypt and also afterward during the sin of the Golden Calf. He <note>God</note> lies between his hands and will not distance Himself from me. And in his company are Isaac and Jacob, just as it says: ->**"My beloved is to me a cluster of henna..."**<- <note>Song of Songs 1:14; original: *Eshkol ha-Kofer*</note>.

Furthermore, our Sages of blessed memory interpreted: Why does the Assembly of Israel say before the Holy One, Blessed be He, at the time she is struck by the hand of "my beloved": "He is my bitter one" <note>original: *mori li* — a pun on *Mor* (myrrh) and *Mar* (bitter)</note>? My beloved is my anger, and He desires it. Day by day, it is good to say to the Attribute of Justice: "Enough! You are... [unclear]" and from his poverty on my behalf... On the mountain, what is the "cluster" <note>original: *Eshkol*</note>? **A man in whom everything is found** <note>original: *Ish she-hakol bo* — a classic rabbinic wordplay on the word *Eshkol*</note>: Scripture, Mishnah, Talmud, Tosefta, and Aggadot. The "henna" <note>original: *ha-Kofer*</note> is he who atones <note>original: *m'chaper*</note> for the sins of Israel. "In the vineyards of En Gedi"—these are the patriarchs of the world, whose deeds you follow like kids <note>original: *gedayim* — a pun on *Gedi*</note> and in the flow of blessings, for they are the "eye of the world" <note>original: *ein ha-olam* — a pun on *Ein* (spring/eye)</note>.

Indeed, their intention is to explain the "bundle of myrrh" <note>original: *Tzror ha-Mor*</note> from the language of "bitterness" <note>original: *marah*</note> and "rebellion" <note>original: *merirut*</note>. It appears that when the Name, may He be blessed, brings evils and troubles upon the generation, at that time He becomes a "generation and love" <note>meaning He shows a difficult form of love</note> to the Name. This occurs by removing the righteous person from the generation; his death atones, as it is written regarding the King Messiah: "It is enough; now stay Your hand" <note>2 Samuel 24:16</note>... and this is "between my breasts he shall lie," meaning the great life-force within him. One can say to the Attribute of Justice: "Enough!" The Holy One, Blessed be He, says "He shall lie" <note>original: *yalin*</note>, meaning he shall die, for the death of the righteous is an atonement like the Red Heifer <note>A reference to the Talmudic teaching that the death of the righteous atones just as the Red Heifer ritual does</note>. "Lying" <note>lodging/sleeping</note> is like when Rabbi So-and-so passed away... and they said "he shall lie" because the Holy One, Blessed be He, takes him and makes him poor for my sake.

He then explained who this is who can say to the Attribute of Justice: "Enough!" It is the "cluster of henna"—the man in whom everything is found, etc. It is he who atones for the generation, and the decree and the evil are removed. Furthermore, the reason the Name, may He be blessed, is appeased by this and atones for the generation is through the merit of the patriarchs of the world, etc. And regarding "in the vineyards," our Sages of blessed memory said "for the sake of," meaning for the sake of the vineyards of En Gedi.

According to these words, it is said that he should go his way. The intention is to say that the Holy One, Blessed be He, due to the lack of Great Ones to protect and save in a time of trouble, takes the righteous and the greatest among them. And all of this is [for] righteousness. Perhaps it is necessary to confess in this the merit of the early patriarchs. It would have been better for them to have the signs of the... <unclear>m-ch-v</unclear> according to their illness, and the Exile would be removed from its root. They would not be struck unless the righteous one was taken before his time due to their evil deeds, lest the pursuer chase their life... <unclear>n-d-m-t</unclear> in the damage of the greatest among them.

According to the second way, King Solomon—in the name of all Israel—spoke well, for it is not fitting to... [unclear] forgive. They stood in the secret of the Lord and to receive His way, and they joined their... the verse. For our Sages taught that there was a mixture of leaven and <term>Mixed Multitude (Erev Rav): a group of non-Israelites who joined the Exodus and were traditionally blamed for instigating the Golden Calf</term> among them. These were the ones who rose up and stumbled over the lack [of Moses] and made the Calf. After there was a mixture of dross and divisions among them, that evil happened to them. But to sustain them now, they were all clean, clear, and pure, without any mixture of impurity at all.

This is the "bundle of myrrh," for it is a property of myrrh that it does not... [unclear] in her state of impurity and loves its scent. It appears that it is like... toward impurity. And the Blessed Name did not bring them into the category of "forgiveness" [if] there was any mixture of impurity in them at all. Therefore, if the Name, may He be blessed, brings them to the... that King Solomon would forgive, it would be established and endure in their hands forever and ever, in such a way that the Name, may He be blessed, would be attached to them always without any interruption at all. And this is "between my breasts he shall lie." Or it means that they had a great preparation now to establish the matter...

The author also contrasts the purity of the idealized Israel with the spiritual failures caused by the "Mixed Multitude" during the episode of the Golden Calf.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, transitioning from the merit of the ancestors to the spiritual significance of the union between God and the Assembly of Israel as symbolized by the Holy Temple.</meta>

# Song of Songs

Even if we were to arrange it according to the Assembly of Israel, "Behold, [our couch is verdant]" follows the path of the Midrash <note>Homiletic rabbinic interpretation; original: *Midrash*</note>: just as this bed is for affection and for fruitfulness and multiplication, so too, before the Temple was built, our book tells us that Judah and Israel were as numerous as the sand of the sea.

Alternatively, it means "our couch is verdant" refers to the cleaving <note>Spiritual attachment; original: *devekut*</note> and connection to the Holy One, Blessed be He, and the Assembly of Israel, which at that time was undoubtedly "verdant" and very abundant. This connection is called by the names "bed" and "couch," resembling the union of a man and his wife. Behold, in this too, the intention follows the path mentioned above.

Or it may mean: "Behold, you are beautiful, my beloved," how good it is for us through the introduction of true faith through His hand. The good deeds that Israel performs in this world cause a renewal and an extra blessing to return to the upper realms. They become unified and bound together as a covenant, and Heaven forbid that it should be interrupted without any delay at all. From them, the flow will also be separated and poured down to the lower realms without bitterness or [interruption] in its reception. This is clear and there is no need to elaborate on it. Also, the generation that lived during the building of the Holy Temple <note>original: *Beit HaMikdash*</note> in its time knew they had attained perfection, so that the Name, may He be blessed, would be unified through them with the description "beautiful" because of the goodness He would pour upon them in this revealed world, and also with the description "pleasant" for the subtle and spiritual influence He would pour in a hidden way in the upper realms. All of this was through the medium of the Holy Temple. Faith, out of their fear of Him, would [not] cease from any generation... [The author discusses the permanence of the Temple]. He made the Holy Temple for them "verdant" forever, standing in its freshness without any aging at all, and there would be no interruption in it at all. Any lack would only be from the side of the cause... that is to say, "our couch is verdant" refers to the Holy Temple, as is explained in the Midrash.

### The beams of our houses are cedars, our rafters are cypresses:

According to the second path, it means that in their "beds," this will be the strength and fortification in the Holy Temple, and it will not come to destruction. For good deeds and the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments <note>original: *mitzvot*</note> will keep it established on its foundation. As the Prophet said: "They are the Temple of the Lord" <note>Jeremiah 7:4</note>. If they do not improve their actions, they are the cause of the destruction and the loss of the Temple and Israel, and the Divine Presence <note>The indwelling presence of God; original: *Shekhinah*</note> departs from the lower beings. But in the merit of the Holy Temple, it is an eternal building. This is the secret of **"the beams of our houses are cedars"**—all of this is my prayer <note>original: *chaloti*</note>, for through the purification of action, the building will endure forever and ever. it will be a permanent structure like a building constructed with cedar beams and cypress wood, which is a lasting, strong, and powerful building forever, never to be seen in ruins.

Alternatively, he speaks regarding the parts of the body and its limbs. Even though they are within the category of physical matter, it was nevertheless possible for them to experience nullification and change due to the nature of matter and the overcoming of the [Evil] Inclination. But it is not so; he compares the limbs of his body and his soul to be pure and refined in a way that they will be strong in their cleaving to the Holy One, may He be blessed, without change. Like a building built with cedar beams, which is an extremely strong building, firmly fixed and never collapsing. He also said, **"our rafters are cypresses,"** using the metaphor of a house that has an upper ceiling and a lower ceiling padded upon it. Regarding the lower one, he said "our rafters," etc., which are sturdy and refined. In this likeness, he speaks of the limbs of the body, for there are "upper" and "lower" limbs. The ultimate intention is to say that all of them, even the lower ones, shall reach the point of cleaving even more to their God. They shall be enduring in the service of the Creator like a building built without flaw, and with vigorous strength, they shall remain attached.

<summary>This page interprets the "verdant couch" and "cedar beams" of the Song of Songs as metaphors for the spiritual stability provided by the Holy Temple and the righteous use of the human body in God's service.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Holy Temple, Midrash, cleaving, Holy One Blessed be He, Assembly of Israel, cedars, cypresses, destruction, Torah, commandments, Alshekh</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically exploring the spiritual and physical harmony achieved during the era of King Solomon and the First Temple. It builds on the previous discussion of the "verdant couch" as a symbol of divine union and national abundance.</meta>

# Commentary on the S[ong of Songs]
## Page 78

The Holy Temple brought a great perfection to the world. The word "beautiful" is written and is worthy of being read in the passage "my companion" <note>Hebrew: *ra’ayati*; a term of endearment used by God for Israel in the Song of Songs</note>, as it is stated in the Book of the Zohar <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah)</note> in the portion *Acharei Mot*, of blessed memory: "When Solomon’s desire was for [the benefit of] all Israel, they were all in high, meritorious levels... and on the day Solomon completed the house below, the Matronita <note>Aramaic for 'The Queen,' referring here to the *Shekhinah* or Divine Presence</note> prepared the house for the King, and their Master joined them as one, and the light shone with joy and perfection; then joy was found for all, above and below" <note>original Aramaic: *וכי חפיקת ליה לשלמה... ונהירו בחדוא שלימו*</note>. Behold, it has been explained how great the perfection of that generation was through the Holy Temple; the upper and lower worlds were bound and unified. Regarding this, it says: *Behold, you are beautiful, my companion.* And concerning this beauty, you have another beauty "from your eyes" <note>Song of Songs 1:15</note>. The meaning of the word "to you" (*lach*) implies that kindness enters within you. This means your affairs are in a state where they can lean toward both evil and good; through the "good" of the physical material and the body as it is now, it becomes "good for you." This results in a great beauty, like the power of physical ornaments and the "inclination toward evil" <note>term: *Yetzer HaRa*; the innate human impulse toward physical desires, which can be harnessed for holy purposes</note> being used to walk in the paths of integrity and the service of Heaven. For this state, though it is close to attachment to God and perfection, nevertheless, good deeds and the fulfillment of commandments are not as "precious" [in one sense] and the reward is diminished when there is no longer a source of resistance or mercy. Therefore, stay in your [current] level and do not seek things that are too great or wondrous for you.

->And regarding what they expounded<-

of blessed memory in the Midrash <note>Homiletic rabbinic teachings</note>: Just as the dove brought restoration <note>Original: *chazarah*; likely referring to the olive branch brought to Noah, signaling the world's renewal</note> to the world, so too Israel brought restoration to the world. And everything is beautiful, for the Holy One, Blessed be He, said to the Assembly of Israel: "You have enough now." At the time of perfection, kindness was within it, and you attained kindness through the Holy Temple, for *Behold, you are beautiful, my companion.* The word "my companion" (*ra’ayati*) implies being a "shepherd" <note>A play on the Hebrew root R-A-H, meaning both 'companion' and 'to graze' or 'to provide'</note> and providing sustenance; your "eyes are doves," which means bringing restoration to the world and sustenance to all the worlds, giving life to their souls even in their exile. So what more do you need to seek from the spirits? According to your sweetness, your grain offering is sufficient; let there be kindness for you, and may it be good for you and your soul. And by this, I know, O Lord, I know that through our [actions]...

->Behold, you are beautiful, my beloved, also pleasant, also<-

now, as of today: a great level.

->Our couch is verdant:<-

Solomon concluded with hidden kindness regarding matters involving the body and the affairs of this world, its successes and the perfection of its goodness. He also addressed what hinders the soul, and the lack or perfection of the material state while we are close to You and clinging to You. For all of this was clearly attributed to Solomon and the permanence of the Temple. This is what is meant by *Behold, you are beautiful, my beloved, also pleasant.* The term "beautiful" (*yafe*) refers to physical and material things, while "pleasant" (*na'im*) refers to subtle, spiritual matters, as in the verse "May the pleasantness of the Lord our God be upon us" <note>Psalm 90:17</note>. These descriptions are applied to Him, may He be blessed, even though no attributes truly apply to Him; rather, He is perceived through His influence and His actions. When He bestows physical goodness upon us, we call Him "beautiful," since He bestows things that are called beauty. When He bestows spiritual goodness, we call Him "pleasant." Therefore, it says: *Behold, you are beautiful, my beloved, also pleasant.* Besides these two, we have another benefit, which is the matter of the children and the offspring that follow: *behold, it is also verdant*, meaning it is great in both quantity and quality. This is the meaning of *also our couch is verdant.* Even with all these perfections present, I will not retreat from my petition, because I seek something eternal and existing without interruption...

<summary>This page provides a kabbalistic and midrashic commentary on Song of Songs, explaining how the First Temple created a bridge between the physical and spiritual worlds. It distinguishes between "beauty" as physical blessing and "pleasantness" as spiritual flow, noting that the era of Solomon represented a peak of national and cosmic harmony.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, transitioning from the end of chapter 1 to the beginning of chapter 2. It interprets the metaphorical descriptions of the beloved (Israel) as a rose or lily, focusing on the spiritual state of the Jewish people during their long exile and their ultimate transformation in the era of redemption.</meta>

# Song of Songs
## Page 12

This can be explained according to our method: the intention is to say that although the literal meaning of the verse suggests that Israel receives many evil troubles from the Blessed One because of their wicked deeds, they do not flee from Him because of these troubles or out of spite. This is because they know the ultimate purpose is for their own good and great benefit. The end of the verse indicates that the Blessed One will find favor in them, and they will flourish with good deeds through their closeness to Him, as their "mouth becomes fermented" <note>This likely refers to the "wine" of Torah or prayer becoming aged and potent</note>. Despite all this, in their state of "sickness" <note>The Hebrew *machala* (sickness) is used here to describe the weakened state of exile</note>, they were exempted from standing before the Place <note>term: *HaMakom*; "The Place," a traditional Jewish name for God, emphasizing His omnipresence</note> under the claim that "The Lord is righteous" while they are in exile.

Specifically, regarding the words in the verse **I am the rose of Sharon**, we have learned that the word "rose" <note>original: *chavatzelet*</note> suggests being "hidden" <note>original: *chavak*</note> and the "bride of the Sharon" who is pale, lacking spirit. Just as a door is lacking when it is closed, the "Sharon" represents the fruit of the valleys—all the depths of trouble. The meaning of the verse is: "Even though I am now in my 'sickness' in the midst of troubles, I am still beloved within them." These troubles were not given for harm, but rather as a burden meant to refine them. For the Blessed One guides me like clay; though I may seem like a thorn or a dry branch, I am like something placed in the moisture of good deeds in the "palm of the valleys" <note>original: *kapat ha-amakim*</note>, the depths of suffering, which gives you the strength of silence.

It is possible to further explain that since their "sickness" was known, they were prepared to achieve healing after a period of time in the future to come. This is because the heart of the wise will be removed from within them <note>The author is referring to the transformation of the human heart</note> and the "inclination toward evil" <note>term: *Yetzer HaRa*; the innate human impulse toward selfishness or sin</note> will be uprooted from them. Since they are prepared and encouraged for this, it is not far-fetched to bring the matter closer and hasten it before the appointed time. At this time, the "rose of Sharon" is diminished in our strength, like a rose hidden in its bud <note>original: *kallah*</note> within this Sharon; it is hidden and concealed within us, but is destined to be revealed in the future by the Lord at the time of our redemption. For then we will be like the **lily of the valleys**, called so after it has grown large. Since this is the case, it is simple to announce the coming changes; therefore, we are prepared for this. Our "sickness" will then become a light repair of our ways, removing what is distant and hastening the fulfillment of the promise. Thus, the following verse says how good it is, prepared for our benefit. Just as there are "good days," so too is Israel prepared for the redemption of tomorrow.

->**As a lily among thorns, so is my beloved** — this means that it is enough for them to have the prior protection<-
->**among the daughters** — so they may have more ease and greatness<-

And the kingdoms of this world are sustained through their proximity to them. All the wicked are like nothing; were it not for Israel, they would not even have a temporary life. This is the secret of **as a lily among thorns**—just as the lily exists among thorns, so the "hidden strength" is surrounded by thorns and briars. When the thorns are agitated, it is known that the one who passes by them and struggles will be pricked. But the "hollow depth" <note>referring to the protected space of the lily</note> is not there for the sake of the thorns, but for the sake of the lily. For the owner of the garden only desires the preservation of the thorns and provides for them for the sake of the lily. This is the likeness of "my beloved among the daughters," meaning among the other nations. As we have learned, the entire intention is to say that even in this state, they have prepared a great perfection. Why should they have to endure any more greatness? It is possible to say further in this way: that this level of "sickness" and "fermentation" belonged to them even while they were among the thorns, troubled and exiled from their land. This is the secret of **so is my beloved among the daughters**. This is the answer to why Solomon was described as "one who is veiled" <note>referring to Song of Songs 1:7</note>. The meaning is that although the "thorns" (nations) have much goodness and blessing, they only exist because of and through the proximity of Israel. Therefore...

It argues that the suffering of exile is a refining process and that the nations of the world are only sustained by God for the sake of the merit of the Jewish people living among them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically analyzing the opening verses of Chapter 2. The previous page established the metaphor of Israel's "sickness" in exile and their enduring love for God. This page moves into the specific imagery of the rose and the lily.</meta>

# Commentary
## Page 2

Clinging to the Land of Israel... an adversary and a "bad occurrence" <note>This likely refers to the "Satan" or the personified Evil Inclination acting as an obstacle to spiritual success</note> to withhold the good from us.

It is possible to connect this verse with: **"I am the rose of Sharon, the lily of the valleys"** (Song of Songs 2:1). The Community of Israel <note>The personified collective of the Jewish people</note> spoke this to bring them back from the sign of Solomon. The intention is that while they were once like a building constructed of cedars and cypresses—which was a strong structure meant to stand forever as a divine work—our current state of perfection is instead like the "Rose of Sharon" <note>original: *chavatzelet ha-sharon*</note>. This rose lacks strength in its beauty; its moisture is fleeting, lasting only until the morrow. However, if the level of perfection were to rise, it would be like the "Lily of the Valleys" <note>original: *shoshannat ha-amakim*</note>, which, because it grows in a deep, sheltered place, maintains its strength and moisture for a much longer time. Even when it eventually dries out, it remains crowned with beauty. Therefore, the confusion did not cause fear; for if they were like the building of cedars and cypresses, they would be strong and established for the Lord forever, as we have said.

It is stated in the Midrash <note>A collection of ancient rabbinic homilies and interpretations</note> regarding the verse: "As long as it is small, he calls it a *chavatzelet* (rose/bud); once it grows, he calls it a *shoshana* (lily)." Why is it called *chavatzelet*? Because it is "hidden in its shadow" <note>original: *chavuya be-tzilah*, a Hebrew wordplay on the word *chavatzelet*</note>. These words can also be explained according to our method: the author likened the level of perfection to a *chavatzelet* when Israel is in a lowly state—small and hidden, representing a fleeting perfection. But if they rise to a great level, as occurred in certain generations, they would be like the "Lily," which, despite all its beauty and goodness, is still destined to dry out eventually, as we mentioned.

And if this refers to the construction of the Holy Temple, it follows this same path. "Rose of Sharon" refers to the physical benefits that flowed to the entire nation through the existence of the Holy Temple in the matters of this world, as is known. "The Valleys" refers to the spiritual benefits and the acquisition of living, beautiful things. Though these are beautiful in their essence and vitality, they do not have an eternal physical existence in this world, for their time eventually comes. It is also possible to say the verse intends to contrast the building of the Temple with the perfection of the physical human body. This is why it says: **"I am the rose of Sharon, the lily of the valleys,"** and in the preceding verse: **"The beams of our house are cedars, and our rafters are cypresses."** This is a correct interpretation.

It is further stated in the Midrash regarding the verse: **"As a lily among the thorns"** (Song of Songs 2:2). Rabbi Hanin said: "Just as this lily shrivels when the heat beats down upon it, yet blossoms when the dew comes, so too as long as the shadow of destruction <note>original: *shmad*, referring to religious persecution or national catastrophe</note> exists in this world, it is as if Israel is shriveled. But when the shadow of the world's sickness passes, Israel continues to grow more and more vibrant." We can see that the lily is sometimes shriveled and sometimes vibrant. Perhaps this is because in its youth, its moisture is constant; so too in our current state, if we stand for a moment in perfection by overcoming and humbling the Evil Inclination <note>term: *Yetzer Hara*; the innate human impulse toward selfishness or sin</note>, the situation can flip in an instant, and the Evil Inclination may prevail, causing a lack of perfection.

And regarding what is further stated in the Midrash: Rabbi Abba bar Kahana said, the Community of Israel said before the Holy One, Blessed be He: "I am beloved even when I am crushed by ancient troubles. And when the Holy One, Blessed be He, redeems me from these troubles, I shall flourish with good deeds like a lily and sing a song before Him..."

# Song of Songs 14

...which is sweet to my palate, and this is the "clinging" <note>original: *dveikut*—a mystical state of attachment or communion with the Divine</note> to the Blessed One and the secrets hidden within the secrets of the Torah. I did not desire the abundance <note>original: *marav*</note> or its likeness. Or, it may mean "As an apple tree among the trees of the wood," etc., for since the apple tree possesses both goodness and a deficiency—namely, the sweetness of its fruit and the goodness of its scent as its virtues, but the sparseness of its shade as a deficiency during the heat of the day—so too is the relationship of the Blessed Name with Israel. Even the pious among them, who reach the status of "children," possess some of this; for physical matter and the "Evil Inclination" <note>original: *yetzer*—the innate human drive toward selfishness or physical gratification</note> sometimes cause an increase in wrongdoing. For this reason, "as an apple tree among the trees of the wood, so is my beloved among the sons," because they sometimes distance themselves from Him. However, "In his shadow I delighted to sit" always, without interruption, just as I have sat now. And even if the reasoning <note>original: *sevar*</note> in the physical realm is somewhat diminished—the "diminishment" being a teacher—nevertheless, "my palate" blocks this, for "his fruit is sweet to my palate." My palate serves His bosom with all that is good, in that which is good for it within the "Separate Intellect" <note>original: *sechel hanivdal*—a philosophical term for a pure spiritual intellect detached from physical matter</note>.

->**He brought me to the house of wine, and his banner over me was love.**<-

Behold, after his anointing and his sweetness with the Blessed Name regarding the matter of his "sickness" <note>referring to the "love-sickness" mentioned in the previous verses</note> and his request to be able to restrain himself from the sweetness of "good taste and knowledge" <note>a reference to Psalm 119:66</note> for all who long for Him, because he was becoming imprinted with that state. His soul "longed and even fainted" <note>a reference to Psalm 84:2</note> to attain it with a fierce passion, and to be absorbed in it without moderation. This is because he had already tasted the sweetness of the clinging of that standing—and its great pleasantness—at the time of the receiving of the Torah at Mount Sinai. He knew the value of its excellence and how far his hand could reach. Therefore, his soul longs even for the "bush" <note>the burning bush</note>; he could not remove the "thorn" <note>a play on the word for "bush," *sneh*, and "thorn," *kotz*</note> from his heart. To this, he says, "He brought me to the house of wine." Just as wine is used to praise Israel, so Israel longs for the Holy One, Blessed be He, to bring them to the great distance of this "wine," which is Sinai. He built for me there the banners of Torah and commandments and many good things, and with a strong "palate" I received them. Furthermore, this followed the great exhaustion in attaining "clinging" <note>*dveikut*</note> to the Blessed One and the refinement of bodies and souls.

Or, it may mean according to this path: the "banner" of the Holy One, Blessed be He—just as his banner is his messenger, his banner is his might, and his banner is his strength—they were bound over me with love. This indicates the greatness of the "Providence" <note>original: *hashgacha*—Divine supervision of human affairs</note> which I attained then, at that time. Therefore, "I will surely remember, and my soul will be bowed down within me" <note>a reference to Lamentations 3:20</note> to "renew our days as of old." According to the words of Rabbi Meir, the verse is arranged before me so that the Community of Israel should contemplate me and say: "Since I desired the [Golden] Calf, He began to melt away Israel," etc. This means that the "palate" intends to choose the choice things, for in his heart and palate it is possible to exist forever in the service of the Blessed One. For the "palate" <note>the inner sense</note> brought him to the point of being so strengthened in Him that he ruled over it, whether when he was in the good state—where the banner of the Holy One, Blessed be He, over him was love (which was the time of the giving of the Torah)—or it may mean that in this "sickness" of his, the deficiency was not so great due to a lack of reasoning during the interruption of the "bread" <note>original: *nohama*</note> as it was at the time of the standing at Mount Sinai. For it is possible that the "palate" could be recognized as leaning toward an evil path, as happened at that time, which brought the "palate" to show its dominion. For even though the banner of the Holy One, Blessed be He, was over me with love at that time, the reasoning remains in its place, as it is possible to lean toward the "left" <note>the side of severity or sin</note>, even if it is somewhat distant.
