<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs, specifically exploring the metaphors in Chapter 2. The previous page discussed the "Rose of Sharon" and the "Lily of the Valleys." This page transitions from the metaphor of the lily among thorns to the apple tree among the trees of the forest, focusing on the relationship between the People of Israel, the Divine, and the other nations of the world.</meta>

# Commentary

...they should prevent and not delay in their eyes, for the weariness will consume them and they are finished for nothing <insert>further</insert>. And it is possible to say that "my beloved among the sons" refers to the kings of the nations, and He elevated them to a high level, for it is known that they were added to and increased greatly for the sake of Israel when they were fulfilling the Torah <note>The Five Books of Moses and the collective body of Jewish law and tradition</note> and the commandments. This follows the concept that the "Divine Presence <note>original: *Shechinah*—the localized, feminine aspect of God's presence in the world</note> in the lower realms is a necessity for the High," like a lily, etc. That is to say: just as the lily exists among the thorns, meaning Israel among the nations who exist only for Israel's sake—like that lily—so too is this comparison: "my beloved among the daughters," meaning my beloved and my provider among the ministering angels. 

The main answer is that although they are compared to a lily, and even if the situation changes and it remains so, nonetheless, according to the second comparison, it must be because of this that the appearance and the boundary change. Behold, they are like the ministering angels, yet the angels do not have as much significance over them, since they are sustained for their sake. In this way, the seemingly redundant word "so" <note>In the verse "so is my beloved..."</note> fits well, for regarding the comparison, is it not already written "as" in the word "as a lily"? This is easy to understand.

And in the Midrash <note>A genre of ancient rabbinic literature that interprets biblical texts through stories and parables</note>: What is this "pricking"? When the lily is placed among the thorns, it is pricked and beaten, yet its heart is directed upward. So too with Israel: even though they were subjected to land taxes <note>original: *arnoniyot*—an ancient Roman tax on produce</note> and poll taxes <note>original: *borsaniyot*—likely referring to burdensome levies or specific vocational taxes</note>, their heart is directed toward their Father in Heaven. The intention is that through this, Greatness is very much publicized; for even though they were beaten with stripes and sufferings, they did not turn their backs on Him, may He be blessed, but rather suffered, their hearts directed toward reconciliation. This fits our approach well: to announce and make heard that the purpose of the Exile is for good and not for evil, and a great benefit is overturned from it. It is not proper to grieve over the change in status, for they are forced to believe in it for nothing in order to flee from the Exile.

## As an apple tree among the trees of the wood, so is my beloved among the sons. In his shadow I delighted to sit, and his fruit was sweet to my palate.

Regarding what is written in the Midrash: Just as everyone flees from this apple tree during the heat of the day, and why is this? Because it has no shade to sit in its shadow, so too the nations of the world fled from sitting in the shadow of the Holy One, Blessed be He, on the day the Torah was given. One might think Israel did the same? Therefore, the text says: "In his shadow I delighted to sit"—I desired Him and I sat; it was I who desired Him, and not the nations. 

Behold, these words according to their plain meaning align with our approach. For as to what the plain meaning of the Blessed One meant by "as a lily among thorns," etc., it was to say that all the nations were sustained and existed for the sake of Israel. This was fulfilled for Israel so that their strength would be "so" <note>referring back to the specific wording of the verse</note> because of what happened at the time the Torah was given: the nations rejected the Torah of the Lord and fled from Him, while Israel cleaved to Him—not because of their perfection afterward, for they were not yet worthy of it. 

The intention of the Sages' words is that just as the apple tree is deficient among the trees of the forest—for even though great benefit is drawn from it due to its fruit and its scent, which is better than other forest trees, nevertheless, in the time of heat, people flee from it and do not care for the sweetness of its fruit or its scent—so too with the nations. Even though by receiving the Torah they would have cleaved to the Name, may He be blessed, and achieved perfection and great benefit, nevertheless, because of the "heat" and the "burning"—the punishments and sufferings found in the Torah—they fled from Him. One might think Israel was the same—that because of the likeness of the "heat" they wanted to flee, but accepted it only under duress, as the Sages said that "He overturned the mountain upon them like a tub"? Therefore, the text says: "In his shadow I delighted to sit," meaning the answer to everything is: like an apple tree that does not prevent the heat, Him I desired, and I sat under Him, for His fruit—all the fruit—

# Song of Songs

His left hand is attached and necessitated under my head, etc. Or, one might interpret this regarding the conduct of this state: after His palate was found to be sweet, if one does not prioritize the physical temperaments <note>original: *timperamenti*—referring to the four bodily humors or general physical dispositions</note> and bodily matters for the sake of the body's preservation. For ultimately, there is a body and matter, and even if it is refined and pure, these things should nonetheless be secondary, while the primary matters should be spiritual things that provide for the breath of the soul and its inner spirit, which is carved from the Divine. These are represented by "His left hand"—meaning the things associated with the "Left," which are the physical needs required for this world. These are "under my head," meaning they are secondary to the head, just as the left hand under the head helps with the attachment but is not the primary focus. The focus of the things associated with the "Right" refers to the flow of spiritual matters for the perfection of the soul, as it says: "His right hand embraces me." Just as the right hand represents the primary embrace and attachment, so too shall these spiritual matters prevail within one's essence during this temporal state, and they shall be the primary focus of everything, as we have said. / And it is possible to say further in this way: that the material things associated with the Left did not provide assistance by being followed; rather, they prove the rule by being "under my head," subjugated beneath it by the guidance of the intellect. They do no harm because of their being made "foreign" <note>rendered secondary or treated as an outside necessity rather than an inner desire</note>, and "His right hand embraces me" as stated, for it was the total preventative force against the material.

And it is possible that after he spoke with voices <note>expressed himself</note> as one who minimizes and reveals his whole heart and his strength in the conduct of the aforementioned noble state, behold, his heart trembled and a shudder seized him, lest the ministering angels <note>original: *malahei ha-shareit*—the angels who serve in the heavenly court</note> should be jealous of him. After they heard his words—his heart being like a great wellspring—they wondered how Israel could ascend to such a level of perception, for it would undoubtedly be a great height.

->I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem,<-
->by the gazelles or by the hinds of the field,<-
->that you stir not up nor awaken love<-
->until it please.<-

Their merit is infinite, and they would have a great addition and a rank above them <note>the angels</note>. They might become jealous of the matter such that not only would they not be a help, but on the contrary, they would prosecute them and say, "What is man that You should remember him," etc. Therefore, "I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem"—the intention being the ministering angels as mentioned above. He adjured them that they should not oppose Israel in their unique spiritual virtue, as he goes on to explain: "if you stir up," etc. And "stir up" is from the language of "awakening," as some commentators have written. And he mentions "by the gazelles <note>original: *tzeva'ot*—a play on words between 'gazelles' and 'hosts/armies'</note> or by the hinds of the field" because the matter is divided into two aspects, one level above another. As we said above, there is the level of the general assembly at Mount Sinai where their "spiritual impurity ceased," or the level of attaining spiritual cleaving <note>original: *dveikus*—mystical attachment to the Divine</note> which is primarily in its innermost palace and is of greater significance. He called the more significant one "the hosts" <note>armies</note> to hint at the category of the celestial hosts, who are distinguished from coarse matter and substance, and are all like refined and intellectual matter. / And to the latter level he said "or by the hinds of the field," which are light in movement and have no heaviness, even though they are physical and material. Thus, the intention is to say that he was adjuring them not to oppose their spiritual virtue, whether it was in the category of the "hosts" or in the category of the "hinds of the field," as we have said. / And if you wish to interpret "hosts" according to its plain meaning, it is nonetheless possible that it indicates further refinement and distinction, as it is said: "run like a deer and be brave like a lion to serve your Creator."

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and mystical commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically analyzing Chapter 2, verses 5 and 6. The previous page explored the balance between physical existence and spiritual transcendence. This page deepens that exploration by examining the "sickness of love"—the intense spiritual longing for the Divine—and how the study of Torah and the performance of commandments refine the human soul to the point of angelic transparency.</meta>

# Commentary

He said this to diminish his words, for he already 
presently possesses some things that are lacking, 
and because of them, he is compelled to turn toward merit and goodness.

### Sustain me with raisin cakes, refresh me 
### with apples, for I am sick with love:

Their request and "illness" <note>Referring to the "sickness of love," an intense spiritual longing.</note> shall be sustained by them more than it was at the time of the Giving of the Torah. These shall serve as a support for her and awaken her from her weakness. He said "with raisin cakes" <note>original: *ashishot*</note> as our Sages of blessed memory wrote: these refer to the two tablets—the Written Torah and the Oral Torah. For now, they possess both as an acquired inheritance, having meditated upon and studied them for a long time. At the standing at Mount Sinai, they did not yet have their full sufficiency, for even their request had not yet been fully received. Furthermore, you said "refresh me with apples"—these are the commandments <note>original: *mitzvot*</note> and the deeds whose scent of merit wafts like apples. These are the deeds that Israel upheld until that time. This was a state that Israel did not possess when they left Egypt, except for a few commandments they were given at Marah. And she said "for I am sick with love," meaning that even if these two—the Torah and the commandments—are not enough to achieve this great perfection, they will nonetheless serve as a support and strength because "I am sick with love" for the Blessed One. This great desire and passion, having reached the level of "sickness," is sufficient in itself to be a preparation for achieving Him and the mystery of the mystery of attachment <note>term: *deveikut* — a mystical state of cleaving or union with the Divine</note> to the Blessed One Himself. Therefore, it is proper to seek support and a staff so that my request may be granted and sustained in my hand.

It is also possible that his words are directed toward the high angels, that they should be righteous advocates and argue in defense of Israel so they may merit that state. This is the meaning of "Sustain me, refresh me," etc. He says further "with raisin cakes" <note>original: *ashishot*</note> that they should have stability and settledness against the "illnesses" of the heights and the supernal lights, so they may receive influence from them and be clothed in them. For they shall achieve this through the appointment of their status which we mentioned, so there will be no dividing screen between them and spiritual matters. Even in those levels above them—which are the perceptions of merit and subtlety which, due to their great distinction, are likened to apples that possess both taste and scent—they shall have the strength to withstand them because they are "sick with love" for the Blessed One. The entire intention is to attach oneself to Him and to enjoy the supernal radiance in the world of perfection. This is impossible unless the murkiness and thickness of the material body is removed and becomes very softened. For then, based on the body being pure and clean, it will be clothed in a spirit of purity and holiness and be covered by the supernal light, level upon level. One will then look, turn, and achieve supernal perceptions, for there is no barrier. 

And the commentators explained "raisin cakes" <note>original: *ashishot*</note> as glass vessels filled with wine. This fits our approach: that eventually the material of the angels will rule over it more, and the merit of perception will be of such importance that the soul will be pure and clear like glass. This is like the saying: "Since the day I was remembered, my angels are like white glass," etc. In this way, their material substance will be pure and clean, filled with the "wine of the Torah," for through the bond of their purity they will be granted success in the Torah without end. "Refresh me with apples" means to be within the category of apples, which possess taste and scent without ceasing. All of this is through the change of status that was hoped for, as we have said.

Here he hinted at the core of his request and its conclusion:
### His left hand is under my head, and his 
### right hand embraces me:
To be worthy of an essential attachment with 
the Blessed Name without any separation at all. This 
is "His left hand is under my head," etc. As our Sages of 
blessed memory brought in the Midrash of Rabbi Yochanan, 
deriving it from this verse: "To love the Lord your God and to cleave to Him."

->**Be Strong!**<-

The author explains that these spiritual practices refine the physical body until it becomes transparent and pure, allowing for direct communion with the Divine and the angelic realms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical and philosophical analysis of the Song of Songs. Having previously discussed the "sickness of love" as a spiritual longing, the author now explains how the performance of the 613 commandments (mitzvot) refines the physical body, making it a vessel for divine light. This leads into an interpretation of Song of Songs 2:8–9, focusing on the "Voice of the Beloved" and God's proximity to the Jewish people even during exile.</meta>

# Song of Songs

Friends, as the truth is well-known: there are 248 positive commandments <note>original: *mitzvot*</note> corresponding to the 248 limbs of the human body, and 365 negative commandments corresponding to the 365 sinews. When a person turns away from evil and observes the negative commandments, the sinews are strengthened against illnesses and maladies, and the physical density <term>density: original "aviyut," referring to the spiritual "coarseness" of physical matter that blocks divine light</term> of the body's matter is weakened. Similarly, when one performs a positive commandment, the limbs are clothed and joined in total refinement, leaving no room for the "pollution of the profane" to rest upon them. Therefore, when a person fulfills even a few commandments, a spirit of purity is immediately bestowed upon those specific limbs associated with them. These limbs then possess a radiance and a holiness that the person can use to prepare the rest of the body. 

This is the hidden meaning of the order of the commandments, and it is easy to understand. Physical matter is like the power of venom and death, and only the "medicine" <note>The Torah/commandments</note> can purify the limbs. Through this, kindness is drawn down to darken even the "illuminated matter" <note>likely referring to the ego or the deceptive light of the physical world</note>. Regarding the power of the wicked and the danger of loss to the forces of impurity, the evil must be recognized to perform a miracle for the "illuminating friend." One must at least darken the light that shines for the other "friends" <note>likely referring to the limbs or spiritual faculties</note>. This is the meaning of the phrase "a transgression extinguishes a commandment." <note>A famous Talmudic dictum stating that while a sin can cancel the reward of a specific deed, it cannot extinguish the light of the Torah itself.</note> Sleep is "sweeter than honey," but these matters are long and ancient, and this is not the place to return to our main subject. 

The truth is that there is no change in the Divine state—God forbid—except in terms of wisdom. Existence is never denied, for in every generation, the "children of the ascent" and the "remnants whom the Lord calls" <note>referring to the spiritually elite who maintain a connection to the Divine</note> maintain it. Even within the general vitality of the people, it exists partially. One can see this; God forbid they should listen in a time of evil. In their bitterness, they cease their strength, but they begin to perform acts of kindness. 

In this verse, he speaks about the horses: "The voice of my beloved!"—meaning the influence of the Blessed Name to sweeten the aforementioned state. Because of its great refinement, he calls it a "voice," which is not a coarse physical thing. This influence is the "bride" <note>the Shekhinah or Divine Presence</note> through which we can demonstrate our love for Him. This is the meaning of "Behold, he comes" <note>Song of Songs 2:8</note>, as explained. He is "leaping over the mountains," meaning he leaps to purify the food and the path over the mountains and hills. These "mountains" are the unique righteous ones <note>original: *tzaddikim*</note> who exist in every generation. He looks at the righteous among them, for these "mountains" are the courts of law... as Isaac said... 

Solomon said this through the Holy Spirit <note>original: *Ruach HaKodesh*</note> because he already knew from the Blessed Name what our word and intent were. The intention is not to keep the matter hidden for a long time or for distant periods. Do not wait "until it pleases" <note>referring to verse 2:7</note> in the sense of saying that its fulfillment is impossible in the near future. Rather, the Blessed Name will fulfill His desire and His oath. His complete accompaniment and the "matter" of revealing them through the seventy walls—he said that this is not the case! For "the voice of my beloved" and the matter of His response is "Behold, he comes," and he is known to him. The core of His intent is that He is "leaping over the mountains," and His palate <note>His "taste" or will</note> searches for them so they may be merit-worthy and he may prepare himself so they are worthy of Him by right, as will be explained later. In this way, the matter is very close, in the sense of "Today, if you would but listen to His voice" <note>Psalm 95:7</note>. The interpretation of "mountains and hills" refers to the dominion of the walls <note>exile/barriers</note> over Israel, as the Midrash explains at the end of this verse:

# Commentary

<margin>Rabbah</margin> their loss to indicate more density and materiality, and everything follows one path. In the *Midrash Rabbah* <note>original: *Midrash Rabbah*, a collection of ancient rabbinic homilies on the Bible</note>, this is said in the name of Rabbi Meir: The Holy One, blessed be He, said to Israel: "If you stand by my oath, I will make you like a star of above; but if not, I will make you like a star of below." It appears that in the verse "And he shall live by my statutes," <note>original: *v’chai be-chukai*</note> a letter is missing, which is interpreted as the power of the "upper" state; the exchange of the attribute refers to the "lower" state. This aligns well with how the word "they lived" <note>original: *hayu*</note> is written in a deficient form <note>In Hebrew, words are often spelled "deficently" (without certain vowels), which the Sages interpreted as having hidden meanings</note>, as they have written etc. "And if not," etc. This supports the first path. And according to what they further interpreted there regarding the aspects of the exchange of the attribute <note>referring to the Divine attributes of Mercy and Judgment</note> concerning the Tribes, etc., they also interpreted the phrase "a crown was thrown." This also settles our intention, for He mentioned to them in the oath these merits and their righteousness which stands forever, to shut the mouths of the accusers <note>original: *mekatregim*, the spiritual prosecuting forces</note> so they do not accuse, and to show that they have something to rely upon and are prepared for the redemption of their nation. 

It is also possible to say that this verse represents the words of God, blessed be He, to the holy nation, and "Daughters of Jerusalem" is meant literally. The intention is to swear them not to hasten the End <note>original: *le-maher et ha-ketz*, referring to the forbidden attempt to force the arrival of the Messiah through human means</note>, to return their "measure" through the signs of the soul's markings <note>original: *simanei hamachas*, likely referring to the spiritual "stamps" left on the soul by the performance of the commandments discussed on the previous page</note> to their levels in the first degree or an even higher one. As it says, "among the ewes or the rams of the field," for there is "yet a vision for the appointed time." Then the Teacher of Righteousness shall come, and they will retreat from their silence one after another. "If you do not wake or rouse the love until it pleases"—this refers to the future to come. As it says in the Midrash on the verse "I watched," etc., it mentions two "arousals" regarding the hidden levels, meaning a separation in the Midrash by necessity. This supports what Rabbi Hoshaiah said: The Holy One, blessed be He, said to Israel, "Wait for Me, and I will make you like a star of above," etc.

The grace of God, blessed be He, agrees to his redemption. He understood and gained insight from the evidence of the matter that it is not yet a "time of favor" to achieve what is sought. All the more so if these are the words of God to the nation; it has already been heard from them that—Heaven forbid—He is bringing it now to fulfill their longing, but there is "a time for every desire," and the fulfillment will be possible as it says, "until it pleases." Therefore, Solomon now wished to bring tidings that Israel should not despair of mercy, for through the "markings" <note>of the commandments</note> they will undoubtedly achieve it in the future. Even now, God, blessed be He, accompanies and hovers over Israel; it is not the aforementioned "markings," Heaven forbid, that are quickly removed. Rather, they must prepare themselves in such a way that they are worthy of it, through the labor and toil in the Torah and in the "Measure" <note>The structure of the commandments</note>. This is not by way of a miracle or a Divine desire to act beyond the letter of the law—as it was at the standing at Mount Sinai—as will be explained later.

At the beginning of his words, he faithfully announced that the matter of the nation's merit and its righteousness, and the verse regarding the removal of their "pollution" <note>original: *zohamatam*, referring to the spiritual filth of the serpent/original sin</note>, will be fulfilled in his generations through the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments, as the law requires. This occurs among the specific "individuals of distinction," the "holy ones who are in the earth" in every generation. Also, in a general way, God, blessed be He, supervises the bodies of human beings as a whole. In a place where He chooses one member of the group who is refined and pure through the proper fulfillment of the commandment associated with that member, He clothes him in a spirit of holiness and purity. His "pollution" is ceased, and from him comes assistance to all the other members of the body, if a person wishes to choose the good and fulfill the other commandments that depend on the other

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, transitioning from the internal preparation of the soul to the historical and spiritual awakening of the Jewish people. It interprets the call to "Arise" through the merits of the Patriarchs and views the end of the "winter" as the end of the Egyptian exile and the purification of the physical body at Mount Sinai.</meta>

# Song of Songs

According to the *Midrash* <note>original: *Midrash*, ancient rabbinic homiletical interpretations of scripture</note>, the phrase "Arise" means to urge yourself to action, and "go, my beauty" follows what we have already explained. In the homiletic tradition, the Sages interpreted this as the "help" we explained in the verse regarding <term>Merit of the Fathers (Zechut Avot)<gloss>the spiritual credit or protective influence earned by the righteous deeds of ancestors</gloss></term>. They said: "Arise, daughter of Abraham," for it is written of him, "Go forth from your land and your birthplace"; "daughter of Isaac," who refined me and beautified me upon the altar; "and go for yourself, daughter of Jacob," who listened to his father and mother, as it is said, "And Jacob listened... and went to Padan Aram." 

According to our path regarding the "hidden sage," the phrase "Arise, my love, my beauty" means to urge yourself toward preparation, so that you may reach the ultimate level of your perfection. Do not let the goal seem distant or the obstacles insurmountable, for "go for yourself" means to look within yourself. There, you will find the merit of the fathers, which assists in attaining the <term>Kingdom (Malchut)<gloss>in mystical thought, the final sphere of divine emanation that relates to the physical world</gloss></term>. Their merit will be your aid and benefit if you prepare yourself to be worthy of it and reach their level of understanding. This is accessible to everyone, for the "grace of every man" <note>likely referring to the inherent potential in every person</note> is close to it.

---

->**For lo, the winter is past, the rain is over and gone.**<-

The Sages said in the *Midrash*: This refers to the great danger our ancestors faced in Egypt. "The rain is over and gone"—these are the forty years in the desert where neither the soul was at rest nor was the "acquisition" <note>referring to spiritual or physical property</note> lacking. Rabbi Tanchuma said the primary burden and labor of Israel in Egypt consisted of sixty aspects, etc. 

We learn from their words that "winter" and "rain" represent the time of the cold season and the primary state of "distance" from the Divine. The most intense distance is during the time of "rain," when God pours down rain upon the earth. When there is no rain, it is called "winter," and it does not involve as much toil or distance. Therefore, the general time of exile is compared to "winter," but the specific time of the harsh enslavement in Egypt is compared to "rain," due to the weight of the labor.

According to our path regarding the coarseness and thickness of the spirit: These material qualities do not relate to the attribute of Mercy and are thought to be in a state of "frozenness." This has already happened in the past, even without great preparation. "For lo, the winter is past"—this means the distance of the physical matter and its coarseness has completely passed away. This occurred when the people heard the first two of the Ten Commandments. Even if they could not sustain that level due to weakness—saying to Moses, "You speak with us and we will listen"—nevertheless, the soul and the spirit were refined, and the thick, coarse materiality passed away and ceased. Just as "winter" eventually departs entirely, the "filth" and coarseness of the physical body left of its own accord, almost without preparation. Therefore, "Arise, my love," for nothing shall prevent you from reaching the state you seek.

---

->**The flowers appear on the earth; the time of singing has come, and the voice of the turtledove is heard in our land.**<-

This means that Israel has the capacity to perform <term>Commandments (Mitzvot)<gloss>divine deeds or obligations that connect the person to God</gloss></term> that lead to great peace and perfection. These acts are great helpers in achieving the desired perfection if people stir themselves to action. Although the "depths" of trials and actions occur "on the earth"—meaning in the Land of Israel—it is known that performing a commandment in the Land of Israel is many times more significant than doing so outside the Land. In the Land, there is a spreading of holiness and purity, a nature clean of all dross and "rust," without any mixture of the profane or the impure. Service of God there is done in purity, and there is no end to the reward for the deeds performed in the Land.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, moving from the general concept of spiritual preparation to the specific mechanisms of divine providence. It explores how God "peeks" through the physical world and the historical ruins of the Temple to reach the soul, especially through the merit of the ancestors and the act of repentance.</meta>

### Commentary

With intense desire from Him and with a swiftness like a gazelle, or at least like a fawn of the deer; for even if its swiftness is not exactly like the gazelle, it is nonetheless swift across the face of the earth. The "fawn of the deer" is swifter due to its small size. In the Holy Tongue <note>original: *Lashon HaKodesh* (Hebrew)</note>, may it be blessed, this all points to one meaning: He comes quickly, like a resting place for all mankind, to stand at one’s right hand to help. And behold, "He stands always behind our wall"—this refers to the <term>Physicality of the Body (Chomer HaGuf)<gloss>the material substance of the human form, viewed in mystical thought as a barrier to spiritual enlightenment</gloss></term>, which acts as a separating screen between us and Him, hindering the attainment of perfection and communion. 

"He peers through the windows"—this means that when a person opens a window through the "clay" <note>the physical material</note> of their body, he allows a separation between himself and the Perceivable One. These "windows" are the sensory organs, which become purified through the "blows" <note>refined through life's trials or spiritual discipline</note> they have endured. For as the density of the physical material is refined and its "impurity" ceases, the organs become like windows. The Blessed Name looks through them and gazes from them to assist the "company of the body." These are the windows and lattices; according to the greatness of the <term>Commandment (Mitzvah)<gloss>a religious duty or good deed</gloss></term>, so shall the windows be large or small, and the lattices clear.

<gloss>M.</gloss> According to the second path, it is said that since their purification will involve a delay in the restoration of their status due to the passage of time—for God has distanced their exile so that the exiles will not endure—the "blemishes" remaining in their hands have delayed the goal of the redemption from coming quickly. Regarding their restoration—that is, the Holy Temple which was built for You by Solomon—it is through His providence that the Lord dwells among us. Our grace has not been entirely cast away; rather, the Blessed Name is with them, like the likeness of a gazelle: sometimes hidden, sometimes seen, as it says in the *Midrash*. Therefore, they will easily reach the end of His favor with the Blessed Name if they improve their actions and rectify their deeds. 

This is the meaning of "My beloved is like a gazelle etc. Behold, he stands behind our wall"—this refers to the wall of the Holy Temple, and specifically the **Western Wall**. As it is explained in the *Midrash*: Why the Western Wall? Because the Holy One, Blessed be He, swore that it would never be destroyed. He "peers through the windows" of the Holy Temple, etc., until from these great insights there is a movement upon the people of Israel to complete their practical resolution and the fulfillment of the Torah, returning to Him as in the days of old, through grace, as is explained in the following verse.

The phrase "peering through the windows, etc." refers to another preparation: the matter of the **merit of the Patriarchs and Matriarchs**. This is as it says in the *Midrash*, that He looks for the righteous in every generation to join them to the matter of the Holy Temple. This also follows the first path, as the intention is one and the same.

Here, he explains the path and the intent of Repentance <note>original: *Teshuva*</note> that God desires. **My beloved answered and said to me: "Arise, my love..."**—The Blessed Name sought the best for the matter from Israel’s side. The explanation is: **"...my beauty, and go for yourself"**—improve yourself through the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments. By doing so, they will go and travel from this level to the longed-for level. Even some of the claims he mentioned regarding the preparations lead to this, and they can achieve it on their own if they wish, and He will not withhold His grace from them if they prepare to act. All of this is included in His blessed answer. Just as He wanted them to acquire it in the first verses, regarding the matter of freedom, through their own actions and toil, He says here: "Arise for yourself, my love, my beauty." This means: Arise for yourself, for your own rest and benefit, and "go for yourself"—meaning, go to the place you desire. For you must earn this through the "sweat of your brow" and the labor of your own hands, not as a gift given in your youth out of pure grace.

# Song of Songs

...the rock, and amidst the pride of <term>Materiality (Chomer)<gloss>the physical substance of the world, often seen as a spiritual barrier</gloss></term> and the soul of the body; and in the secret and hiddenness of the upper level that you seek from there, He says: **“Show me your appearance, let me hear your voice.”** The intention here refers to **Good Deeds** and to **Study**, as explained in the <term>Midrash<gloss>ancient rabbinic homilies and interpretations of the biblical text</gloss></term> of our Sages: <note>original: *Midrash Chazita*</note> “Show me your appearance”—this refers to Good Deeds; “Let me hear your voice”—this refers to Study. **“For your voice is sweet, etc.”**—this means your voice, even in the lowly state you are currently in, is sweet to Me; and likewise, your appearance in your current state is comely. This is because they are engaged in the Torah and the labor of subduing materiality and impurity, and for this, the reward is very great. Both study and action acquire a "grace" that leads to the ultimate purpose, as is known. 

Even if these words we have written regarding these verses are brief, the "Voice of my Beloved" <note>referring to a previous verse</note> interrupts the sequence of the "delight" mentioned above. Nevertheless, Solomon expanded upon them by making known the response of the Holy One, Blessed be He, to the <term>Assembly of Israel (Knesset Yisrael)<gloss>the personification of the Jewish people in relationship with God</gloss></term> with words of reconciliation and love. This was done so that they would know the affection the Holy One has for Israel and so they would not despair of mercy; for even in this state of exile, they are beloved before Him, may He be blessed—how much more so if their state were to change and they were to rise to their full sovereignty. 

In the Midrash, they applied this verse to the <term>Eternal House (Beit Olamim)<gloss>The Holy Temple in Jerusalem</gloss></term>: **“O my dove, in the clefts of the rock”**—for they were hidden in the secret place of the Eternal House. **“Show me your appearance”**—this refers to when "Solomon assembled" <note>original: *Yakahel Shlomo*</note> the people. **“Let me hear your voice”**—as it is written, "it was as one [voice]" <note>referring to the dedication of the Temple</note> for the practitioners of prayer and the singers, a single voice. **“For your voice is sweet”**—this refers to the Song of the Levites; **“and your appearance is comely”**—these are the Sacrifices. 

Following their path, Solomon spoke in the name of the Blessed One. After he made known the "Leader" <note>God</note> of this significant state, the matter depends on the people: if they urge themselves on, they will achieve their desire, and He will hasten to come to them. Whoever is able should hasten to establish and sustain the "Grace of the Foundation" <note>spiritual stability</note> so they do not lose it. This is the matter of the Temple. This is the secret of **“O my dove... in the clefts of the rock,”** meaning, in the "Rock" that is their dwelling place—the Holy Temple, which is a hidden strength and a stronghold. It is in the "secret of the upper level" of His thought, for the Temple below is aligned exactly with the Temple above. Regarding this, I sing: **“Show me your appearance,”** meaning, act in such a way that you show me your appearance, etc. This implies a transformation so that you shall not be cut off from your place, for the Song heard within the Temple is sweet to Me, and the act of Sacrifices is comely before Me. This is the secret of **“For your voice is sweet and your appearance is comely,”** according to the Midrash of our Sages.

<columns>2</columns>
According to what is heard from the words of our Sages in the Midrash, the word **“Catch”** <note>original: *Achazu*</note> is a figurative term used for the past tense. They said: "Seize for us, kill for us." It is not written "Catch" <note>as a future command</note> but **“Catch [have caught] for us,”** they have closed in on us to isolate us. Following their path, this fits with his intention:

<column-break/>

### Catch for us the foxes,
### the little foxes that spoil the vineyards;
### for our vineyards are in blossom (Smadar).

Solomon warned Israel that in order to reach the desired goal, or at least to maintain the Grace with them today—which is the Holy Temple—they must be diligent and swift regarding "light" <term>Commandments (Mitzvot)<gloss>deeds often overlooked or "stepped on with the heel"</gloss></term>. As it is said, "And it shall come to pass, because (*Ekev*) you listen" <note>a play on the word *Ekev*, which means both "because" and "heel"</note>. They must be diligent to guard themselves from "light" transgressions, for these are what cause the Grace to fall into a trap from which there is no healing. When one is negligent, these sins cause one to fail. This is the secret of **“Catch for us,”** meaning: you should surely know that foxes have "caught" and seized our portion. These "foxes" are the "Destroyers" created from transgressions, as is known—when a person commits a sin, an accusing destroyer is created to hinder and stand before him to prosecute the one engaged in holy work.

# Commentary

...in the land and with haste. And this is what is said: **“The blossoms have appeared in the land,”** <note>Song of Songs 2:12</note> which has not been the case since ancient times. The commandments <note>original: *mitzvot*</note> were (not) entered into the land in handfuls, for **“the time of the pruning <note>or "singing"</note> has arrived,”** meaning that at every moment and every hour, the time for <term>Orlah<gloss>the biblical prohibition against eating fruit from a tree for the first three years after planting</gloss></term> and <term>Terumah<gloss>the priestly tithe given from the harvest</gloss></term> arrives. Thus, the matter of the testing of <term>Materiality (Chomer)<gloss>physical substance or the human body, often seen as a barrier to the spiritual</gloss></term> and its coarseness will not delay the time. Furthermore, **“the voice of the turtle-dove is heard in our land,”** <note>Song of Songs 2:12</note> which refers to a "Great Traveler" <note>A pun in Hebrew: *Tor* (turtle-dove) sounds like *Tayar* (traveler/scout)</note>, namely the <term>Shekhinah<gloss>the Divine Presence of God dwelling within the physical world</gloss></term> among the lower beings in the Holy Temple, which is the primary location and the depths of the inheritance, as it is written: “To the rest and to the inheritance, etc.” <note>Deuteronomy 12:9</note> Through a small strengthening of great beginnings toward spiritual attachment, they will easily reach their ultimate purpose in both states, like clay in the hand of the potter.

<column-break/>

->**The fig tree has ripened its green figs, and the vines**<-
->**in blossom have given off their fragrance. Arise, come, my darling**<-
->**my beautiful one, and come away.**<-

<column-break/>

But just as there was perfection in them through the fulfillment of the commandments in the Land, and also the primary dwelling of the Shekhinah among the lower beings, so too the study of the Torah is a great perfection that is lacking; for the study of the Torah is equal to them all. It is also

---

a great entrance to merit the refinement and subtlety of Materiality. We find many of their priests engaged in the Torah, moving back and forth from the Torah to the Prophets, and from the Prophets to the Writings. God forbid that through their very steps they should become inflamed and gathered into the Life that descends from above, as occurred at the Standing at Mount Sinai. If one were to rush through it <note>perhaps referring to the speed of study or a specific poetic meter</note> regarding the verse of "your covering" in verse, and especially when they engage in the **“Work of Creation”** <note>original: *Ma'aseh Bereshit* - the study of the origins of the universe</note> or the **“Work of the Chariot”** <note>original: *Ma'aseh Merkavah* - the highest form of Jewish mysticism involving the vision of Ezekiel</note>. This matter also exists within them through effort, and this is the meaning of **“The fig tree has ripened its green figs,”** to speak of the beginning of the matter of Torah. It is like the teaching regarding Joshua, that “he did not depart from within the tent,” etc. <note>Exodus 33:11</note> Regarding the mentioned verse, the ripening fig shall eat its fruit; the intention is that the Torah has already begun to ripen and prove its presence. These are the Sages, the masters of Torah, wisdom, and knowledge. It says **“ripened its green figs”** because they have not yet finished being refined to the point that their Materiality is as pure and clean as it was at the Standing at Mount Sinai when they received the Torah; but they have a beginning like clay. **“And the vines in blossom have given off their fragrance”**—this refers to the families of Israel who are likened to vines. Even while their fruit is still just a blossom, they already give off a fragrance. This refers to the **“schoolchildren”** <note>original: *Tinokot shel beit raban* - whose pure breath is said to sustain the world</note>; the breath of their mouths is pleasing before the Blessed Name without end. Therefore: **“Arise, come, my darling, my beautiful one, and come away,”** meaning, hurry to be worthy of perfection in your youth, for its grace is but a moment; **“and come away,”** meaning, go and wash yourself and see that your "palate" <note>spiritual taste</note> is almost clean of all the past, and every person is washed and turned away from all those dangers and beginnings as we have said.

<column-break/>

->**O my dove in the clefts of the rock, in the hiding**<-
->**of the step, show me your appearance,**<-
->**let me hear your voice, for your voice**<-
->**is sweet and your appearance is comely.**<-

<column-break/>

Solomon said further through the Holy Spirit <note>original: *Ruach HaKodesh*</note>, by the goodness of the Blessed One: that even though Materiality and the separate parts—like nard and saffron—obstruct <note>unclear: possibly *satanu* - hindered</note> by the strength of Materiality and its coarseness, they were not, because of this, separated from the Blessed One, nor were they loathsome in His eyes, God forbid, God forbid! Rather, there is a great guidance before Him in great distinction, which is the beginning of their Torah and their good deeds.

---

And this is what is said: **“O my dove in the clefts of the rock.”** This compares Materiality and the body in its coarseness and its laws to a "rock," and in the sense of "and my walls," <note>God forbid</note> a heart of stone. The intention is: "My dove, even though you are in the clefts of the rock..."

<meta>The catchword "הסלע" (the rock) indicates the first word of the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page interprets the metaphors of the Song of Songs (blossoms, figs, and vines) as stages of spiritual growth, from the performance of agricultural commandments to the high mysticism of Torah study. It emphasizes that even the "coarseness" of the physical body (the "rock") does not prevent a soul from being "comely" to God through study and good deeds.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Torah study, Shekhinah, Materiality, Joshua bin Nun, Ma'aseh Bereshit, Ma'aseh Merkavah, school children, spiritual refinement</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, transitioning from the discussion of the Divine Presence (Shekhinah) in the Temple to the themes of exile, redemption, and the soul's yearning for God during the dark "nights" of history. It interprets the famous imagery of the gazelle and the "mountains of separation" through both a national and a mystical lens.</meta>

# Song of Songs

As it is said, and as we will say further on: "Until the day breathes," etc.
The meaning is: until the light of the day breathes forth.

->**Until the day breathes and the shadows flee, turn, my beloved, and be like a gazelle or a young stag on the mountains of separation.**<-

The light is like a watcher, and the intention refers to the redemption given to the righteous in the world to come <note>original: *l'atid lavo*</note>. This is what is written: **"Arise, shine, for your light has come,"** <note>Isaiah 60:1</note> etc. Opposite this, **"the shadows"** refers to the shadows of evening, meaning the periods of exile. This light will undoubtedly **"break forth like the dawn"** <note>Isaiah 58:8</note> for the appointed time of the end, the "heart of stone" will be removed from our flesh <note>Ezekiel 36:26</note>, and death will be swallowed up forever <note>Isaiah 25:8</note>. Regarding this, I offer a gift and a supplication before You, O Lord our God, in the name of all Israel: **"Turn, my beloved, be like..."**
This means: cause the times of exile to turn and revolve quickly, to hurry and hasten the <term>End (Ketz)<gloss>the predestined time for the final redemption of the Jewish people</gloss></term> before its decreed time. This is similar to what happened during the Exodus from Egypt, as the Sages (of blessed memory) said regarding the phrase "leaping over the mountains" <note>referring to Midrashic traditions that God "skipped" over the calculated end-date to rescue Israel early</note>.
This is why it says: **"Be like a gazelle,"** etc., for they are light on their feet and swift in their movement. Regarding this swiftness, the verse mentions two levels, one preceding the other; for this comparison, it says **"like a gazelle"** or **"like a young stag."**
And regarding **"the mountains of separation"** <note>original: *Harei Bather*</note>, the Sages (of blessed memory) said that He should hasten to stand upon the "mountains of separation," which refers to the time of the future to come. For that will be the time of the separation of the <term>Zohama<gloss>a spiritual "grime" or impurity that, in Kabbalistic thought, has been attached to the human body since the primordial sin in Eden</gloss></term> from the body, as is known. The word *Bather* is related to the phrase: "this heifer into pieces <note>original: *batrim*</note>" <note>Genesis 15:10, referring to the Covenant of the Pieces</note>.
In the Midrash, Rabbi Berakhiah said: "Until the day seethes with the fire of My wrath, I will breathe <note>the Hebrew word for "breathe" (*yafuaḥ*) shares a root with "blow" or "fan a fire"</note> upon you to fan the fire." And what is meant by **"the shadows flee"**? These are the shadows of grief and sighing.
The primary intent here concerns the matter of the exiles and their redemption; thus, the explanation of the verse follows the path we set at the beginning of our words. It is also possible that by saying **"Turn, be like,"** it intends to show that "Salvation belongs to the Lord," as it is written: **"He is righteous and victorious"** <note>Zechariah 9:9</note>. This is for your own benefit and good.

->**Upon my bed at night I sought him whom my soul loves; I sought him, but I found him not.**<-

After addressing our request and petition, and seeing that its fulfillment seemed impossible <note>likely referring to the delay of redemption</note>, the author now explains the reality of the <term>Cleaving (Devekut)<gloss>a state of profound spiritual attachment or communion between the soul and the Divine</gloss></term> that existed for Israel with the Holy One, Blessed be He. This was renewed in his days through the dwelling of the <term>Shekhinah<gloss>the manifest Presence of God</gloss></term> among the lower realms, specifically its rooting in the Holy Temple that he built.
He <note>King Solomon, the traditional author</note> stood and pondered in his mind before the building was completed: would his actions be effective? By what merit would he reach the ultimate purpose? Would there be a delay on the part of Israel because they were not worthy?
This is the meaning of **"Upon my bed at night"**—when his thoughts were free and he could descend to the depths of the matter. **"I sought a place for the cleaving of Him whom my soul loves"**—meaning the Blessed Name—with the <term>Knesset Yisrael<gloss>the "Assembly of Israel," viewed mystically as a spiritual entity representing the collective soul of the Jewish people</gloss></term>. In this search, **"I found him not,"** for I did not find a place of merit where Israel would be worthy of this in all their various groups and divisions, as will be explained in the verse through "Mercy."
Alternatively, it may mean that since he previously stated the reason for our request regarding the exiles—as explained above on the verse **"Tell me, you whom my soul loves"**—after seeing that the fulfillment was not immediate, he said: Truly, concerning the "sickness" of the exiles, **"on my bed at night I sought him whom my soul loves"** through my own cleaving. But it was not granted to me, for **"I sought him but I found him not,"** seeing that the exiles were necessary and a matter of prior decree...

<summary>This page provides a messianic interpretation of the "gazelle on the mountains," viewing it as a plea for a swift end to exile and the purification of the soul from physical impurity. It also depicts King Solomon meditating at night on whether the people of Israel are worthy of the Divine Presence dwelling in the Temple.</summary>
<keywords>Redemption, Exile, Gazelle, Mountains of Bather, Rabbi Berakhiah, Holy Temple, Devekut, Shekhinah, Knesset Yisrael, King Solomon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically focusing on chapter 2, verses 15–16. The translator notes that the scribe consistently uses the letter 'ס' (Samekh) in place of 'ש' (Shin), a common orthographic variation in certain manuscript traditions.</meta>

# Commentary

## Commentary

And so it is with the commandment of the Creator, the King of Mercy, who teaches "defense counsel" <note>original: *sanegoria*, a legal term used in heaven for spiritual advocacy on behalf of Israel</note> as is explained in many places in the Book of the Zohar. To these destroying angels <note>original: *malachei mashchit*</note> he gave the name "foxes," for they plot and are as cunning as foxes against a person to drive him from the world. After the action of the "foxes," they seize him with the memory of all impurity and withhold good from him. It is not the "heavy ones" from grave sins that are meant—for a person is naturally quick to correct himself of those—but rather those who are caught and seized by the "little foxes." This is a hint to the destructive forces created from minor sins that a person "tramples with his heels" <note>a reference to a Talmudic idiom regarding sins people treat as insignificant</note>. These "foxes" damage the vineyards even more than the great sins, for a person is wary of the great ones and shrinks from them. **"For our vineyards are in blossom"** <note>original: *semadar*, meaning the early stage of a grape's growth</note>—this means that the Children of Israel are valued like vineyards. When they are in the "blossom" stage, which is the beginning of their spiritual growth, they are most susceptible to damage and ruin. All this is meant to warn people to be careful not to stumble in these minor sins, or to rectify them after an accidental error. By doing so, they will reach the desired goal. This is especially true for the persistence of the law, which is to say <margin>And if the meaning of "catch" [ahazu]</margin>—the meaning of the word "catch" [ahazu] is also an expression of longing and waiting according to our interpretation. The warning itself is to seize those "little foxes" who damage the vineyards of the Lord of Hosts, the House of Israel, and to "catch" them through rectification and by guarding against them, so they may be swallowed up and destroyed. Then they will not stand as a "reminder of iniquity" <note>a biblical phrase for sins that block divine favor</note> to hinder one's path. For when you do this, even if **"our vineyards are in blossom,"** a good scent will rise from them—the scent of commandments and good deeds—for there will be no adversary and no evil mishap to extinguish or darken them. In this way, we shall be attached to the Living God... or at least through the persistence of kindness, they will merit being here with us today, with this time and intention, as follows: <margin>It is also possible to interpret "little foxes"</margin> "Little foxes that spoil the vineyards" may be a hint regarding the young children of Israel, who must be educated in their youth and disciplined in the way of God's service. Through the "way of grace," they will easily acquire proper character in a permanent way; even when they grow old, they will not depart from it. But if, God forbid, they cast off the yoke in their youth, it is difficult to place it upon them after they have grown up in "freedom"—starting from when they were small, having been led astray into a bad culture. They "spoil the vineyards," and it is necessary to "catch" and seize them to turn them from their evil way while they are small, for afterward, rectification becomes difficult. The intention in this follows the interpretations written above, and all point to one purpose.
It means that he returns to the conclusion of his words:

->**My beloved is mine and I am his; he who grazes among the lilies.**<-

Behold, the intention is to reach the goal of <term>Cleaving (Dveikut)<gloss>The mystical state of spiritual attachment or communion between the soul and the Divine</gloss></term> to the Blessed One through the "hidden stations." Since the Blessed One promised that this would not be [lost], for He is mindful of the "Form of Judgment," Israel shall merit this. He "sold Himself" <note>an idiom for God committing Himself to the people</note> through the fitness of their deeds. However, because the entire nation could not [always] fulfill the vow, he concludes that in the times of this world, there is no strength for total cleaving. Rather, the Blessed One is for Israel as a portion, as a leader, and as the "Shepherd of Israel," acting as a father to a son, or a groom to a bride, according to the generation. This is as the Sages (of blessed memory) said in the Midrash on the verse **"My beloved is mine and I am his."** The essence of His "grazing" is **"among the lilies"**—this refers to the unique individuals, the chosen few <note>original: *yechidei segulah*</note> who serve as a <term>Chariot (Merkavah)<gloss>A mystical term for individuals or objects that serve as a direct vehicle for the Divine Presence in the physical world</gloss></term> for His powerful <term>Divine Presence (Shekhinah)<gloss>The indwelling presence of God on earth</gloss></term>. Divine influence rests upon them, and from them, it spreads to all the creatures of the world. This is the meaning of "He who grazes among the lilies," and the matter is clear. This shall continue until the "End of Days" when His kingship over us shall be revealed...

<summary>This page provides a homiletic and mystical interpretation of Song of Songs 2:15–16. It explains the "little foxes" as minor sins or the need for early childhood education, and describes the "lilies" as the righteous individuals who draw the Divine Presence into the world.</summary>
<keywords>foxes, vineyards, blossom, minor sins, education, Tikkun, cleaving (Dveikut), lilies, Shekhinah, Chariot (Merkavah)</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs. While the previous page discussed the "little foxes" (Song of Songs 2:15) as metaphors for minor sins, this page transitions to chapter 3, interpreting the soul's search for the Beloved as a search for spiritual merit and a way to avert the Babylonian exile.</meta>

# Song of Songs

## Commentary

To specify, "Until when, O simple ones," etc., and from here we shall return, for here too Solomon spoke with investigation and homiletic inquiry. He established four classes of people to determine if there was within them—or within even one of them—a place for attachment to the Blessed Name with Israel, to benefit His people, like the building of the Temple where the Divine Presence <note>original: *Shekhinah*, the indwelling presence of God</note> would dwell in the work of his hands. He suggests that in the fourth class, this connection occurs by force (and if not in perfection, as will be explained). 

And this is the meaning of the verse: **"I will rise now, and go about the city"**—this refers to the first class, those lacking wisdom in the city, who are enclosed outside the wall, breached in the physical open towns and streets. **"In the markets"** refers to the second class. **"In the squares"** refers to the third class. And regarding all of these, **"I sought him, but I found him not,"** because for those who follow after their own worldly ways, there is no merit for such a connection. 

Therefore I said, "I will go unto the great men," and these are the sages of the fourth class, who are the merciful ones, the judges, and the leaders. This is what is meant by: **"The watchmen that go about the city found me"**—these are the "Guardians of the City" <note>original: *Neturei Karta*, a traditional Aramaic term for the scholars and righteous individuals whose spiritual merit protects a city</note> who are associated with those engaged in the Torah and communal needs. I said to them the thought of my soul—meaning, is there any merit in you so that your spirits may cleave to Him, and is your service from your study and your depths for the sake of Heaven? 

His intention in this was that through these sages, a place might be found to grasp the aforementioned powers. If they were perfect in their service, it would be possible for the Divine Presence to dwell in the work of his hands, and the Blessed Name would dwell among the Children of Israel, and they would have an attachment to Him.

The verse says **"They found me"** rather than "I found them," because the sages of this group found him through his temperament, for from their power comes his quest. Yet they were not in a state of perfection, for they required "Heavenly Assistance" <note>original: *Siyata d'Shemaya*</note>. And as it says: **"Scarcely had I passed from them"**—meaning, if there was not perfect merit from them (because in their actions there was a bit of a mixture of worldly thought and minor flaws in their study), nevertheless, "scarcely had I passed from them" refers to an ascent of one level: the seeking of Heavenly assistance and Divine grace. 

This continued **"until I found him whom my soul loveth."** In this aspect, the merit of the Rabbis, the "Guardians of the City," was insufficient without the aid of Grace. I persisted **"until I brought him into my mother’s house"**—which is the Temple—**"and into the chamber of her that conceived me"**—which is the Holy of Holies <note>the innermost sanctum of the Temple</note>. For I knew that this would stand by the power intended for the ultimate goal, and so it was; I brought the matter into action, and "brought him to my mother’s house," and so on, with mercy.

According to a second interpretation, this refers to when Israel was destined for the Babylonian exile. The exile had already begun with the deportation of Jeconiah, King of Judah, and many people with him. As is known, a great multitude still remained in Jerusalem with their king, Zedekiah, at their head. If all or even some of them had turned back to God, the Blessed Name would have had mercy on the remaining remnant so as not to complete their exile. Therefore, the prophet Jeremiah said: **"Run to and fro through the streets of Jerusalem, and see now, and know, and seek in the broad places thereof, if ye can find a man, if there be any that executeth judgment, that seeketh the truth; and I will pardon it"** <note>Jeremiah 5:1</note>. 

Solomon said this here through the Holy Spirit, for he sought a remedy for the exile and did not find it. He sought the Blessed Name: **"I will rise now, and go about the city in the markets and in the squares"** to seek **"him whom my soul loveth"**—meaning, that the people should be attached to the Blessed Name. He considers the state of their Divine service to see if it might be found in enough strength among the inhabitants of Jerusalem to protect them from being exiled. But **"I sought him, but I found him not."**

<summary>This page interprets the soul's search for the Beloved in Song of Songs 3:2-4 as Solomon's search for spiritual merit among the classes of Jerusalem to invite the Divine Presence into the Temple and, later, to avert the Babylonian exile.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Solomon, Temple, Holy of Holies, Divine Presence, Neturei Karta, Babylonian Exile, Jeremiah, Zedekiah, Jeconiah, spiritual merit</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs chapter 3. The previous page established that King Solomon is categorizing society into four classes to see if any are worthy of the Divine Presence. This page dives deeper into those four classes using verses from the Book of Proverbs as a cross-reference.</meta>

# Commentary

<header>Commentary</header>

The text begins by addressing the spices <note>original: *rekhakheha*</note> in the Babylonian exile, as selected in the Midrash <note>Traditional collections of Jewish homiletic teachings that explain the hidden meanings of the Bible</note>. It mentions that the phrase "going about" is similar to the expression "he fell upon his bed," meaning a state of illness. Likewise, the Midrash asks: What is the meaning of "upon my bed"? It refers to the "night" of exile, where one does not die but is "fallen upon his bed" and requires support, for these matters are clear.

> **"I will rise now, and go about the city in the streets and in the squares; I will seek him whom my soul loves; I sought him, but I found him not."** <note>Song of Songs 3:2</note>

According to the first interpretation, it appears that within the general population, there are four classes of people. There is the class of the "wise" who are actually pursued by physical desires and the vanities of this world—their material nature <note>original: *chomer zoraman*, a phrase implying a heavy or base physical drive</note> is so strong that they do not distinguish between what is permitted and what is forbidden, or between the sacred and the profane; they spend their lives busy with nothingness. 

The second class consists of the general craftsmen and artisans who sit in their shops within the city streets. 

The third class are the wealthy merchants who also frequent the markets, anxious over their trade and their wealth; this is their entire focus. 

The fourth class, higher than them all, are the princes and judges who sit at the head of the kingdom to express opinions and lead the people; this is their way all their days. 

Behold, King Solomon intended to address these four classes in the Book of Proverbs when he said: *"Wisdom cries aloud in the street"* etc., *"In the bustling squares she calls"* etc. <note>Proverbs 1:20-21</note>. He said that Wisdom addresses these four classes because, heaven forbid, they have each turned to their own way and their own gain—these in their idleness and pleasures, these in their craft and trade, these in their commerce and wealth, and these in their high status and authority. They have forsaken the Lord and chosen their own ways and deeds, even when a "companion of the house" <note>meaning a righteous person or communal leader</note> seeks to do justice and charity. 

Therefore, Wisdom raises her voice with words of rebuke to return them to the good. Regarding the **first class**, he said, *"In the street she cries"* <note>original: *ba-chutz tarona*</note>—meaning to those standing "outside" the way of the Lord and His service. She "cries" (*tarona*), meaning she speaks her words in a melodic way, with grace and kindness and without haste, to make the words pleasant so they will enter their ears. Since they are idle and have no pressing business, he does not say she uses a "shouting voice," because even with a low voice, if they pay attention, they will hear and find life. Furthermore, *tarona* can be read in the plural <note>original: *taronah* can be linked to the idea of multiple sounds</note>, suggesting that "even two voices can be heard" <note>original: *trei kalei mashtamei*, an Aramaic legal principle regarding whether one can distinguish two voices speaking at once</note>, for they spend their time in emptiness; if the words do not benefit them, they at least do not overwhelm them.

Regarding the **second class**, he said, *"In the squares she lifts her voice"*—these are the shopkeepers and craftsmen sitting in the city squares. He says "she lifts her voice" because, since they are busy and eager for financial gain, one must call to them with a great voice, like a person standing in a distant place who must shout so they will hear. It is possible he uses a singular language here because it is not "pleasant" for them to listen while they are drowned in the pursuit of money; in their case, "two voices are not heard" <note>meaning they cannot multitask or listen to spiritual rebuke while focused on profit</note>.

Regarding the **third class**, he said, *"In the bustling streets she calls"*—these are the merchants who are in places of great commotion and many people. Of them, he says "she calls," for a single voice is not enough since they are even more eager for money, as their profit depends on that <unclear>path</unclear>.

Regarding the **fourth class**, he said, *"At the city gates"*—which is the place for the judges. To them, "she utters her words," and shouting or calling is not applicable to them, for they are prepared to hear the words of whoever speaks. Thus, the moral rebuke of Wisdom is directed toward them in this way.

<meta>catchword: which details</meta>

<summary>This page uses Proverbs 1 to interpret Song of Songs 3:2, categorizing society into four groups—the idle, the craftsmen, the merchants, and the leaders—and explaining how Divine Wisdom must adapt its "voice" to reach each group based on their worldly distractions.</summary>
<keywords>four classes of society, King Solomon, Proverbs, Wisdom, Song of Songs commentary, commerce vs. spirituality, judges and leaders, Babylonian exile</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs, moving from the description of the various social classes to the merits of the Jewish people that make them worthy of the Divine Presence. The previous page discussed the four classes of people in exile; this page explains how the acceptance of the Torah and the merit of the Patriarchs allow for the "ascent" from the wilderness.</meta>

# Song of Songs

<header>Song of Songs</header>
<sig>I 6 h</sig>
<page-num>21</page-num>
<meta>catchword: And therefore</meta>

Among them are those who are compassionate, making a place for the Divine Presence <note>original: *Shekhinah*</note> to dwell among them, having received it from ancient days. This refers to the acceptance of the Torah. For "Who is this?" <note>Song of Songs 3:6</note> implies that while all the other nations refused to accept it, Israel did. This is a great merit that draws the Divine Presence down to the lower worlds. This is as the Sages taught in *Midrash Rabbah* on the verse, "And let them take for Me an offering" <note>Exodus 25:2</note>, using the parable of a king who had an only daughter, and so on. This means the "bed" <note>a metaphor for the place of union with God</note> exists because the acceptance of the Torah causes the Divine Presence to dwell. 

This is the secret of "Who is this... coming up from the wilderness?" <note>Song of Songs 3:6</note> It asks: what is the cause of her elevation and ascent from the wilderness? It is because of the "wilderness"—namely, the reception of the Torah there, as is explained. If so, the worthiness of their merit is clear, reaching unto the heavens. This is the secret of the phrase "like pillars of smoke"; by this power, they are worthy of this high level. Furthermore, she possesses the "merit of the ancestors" <note>term: *Zechut Avot* — the spiritual credit inherited from the righteousness of the Patriarchs</note>, a great and strong center that gives power and strength to the "branches" of the tribe. 

This is the secret of "perfumed with myrrh and frankincense, with all the powders of the merchant." As our Sages of blessed memory interpreted: 
"Myrrh" refers to our father Abraham; just as myrrh is the head of all spices, so was our father Abraham the head of all the righteous. 
"Frankincense" refers to our father Isaac, who was offered up like a handful of frankincense upon the altar. 
"All the powders of the merchant" refers to our father Jacob, whose "bed was complete" <note>original: *mitato shelemah*; a traditional idiom meaning all of his children were righteous and none were rejected</note>, and no defect <unclear>like this</unclear> was found in them. For they are compassionate in all matters of holiness, and it is fitting that you should take pity on the body of Jerusalem, that you should receive the Divine Presence, about which I have adjured you.

It is also possible that "perfumed with myrrh and frankincense" refers to the parts of the Torah. The Written Torah contains two parts: positive commandments and negative commandments. The negative commandments are likened to "myrrh" <note>original: *mor*</note>, as the Sages said that anyone who harvests it finds their hands are embittered; so too a person is embittered and finds it difficult to turn away from the pleasure of sins and their indulgence. Regarding the positive commandments, he says "frankincense," for they are sweet, heartening to the soul, and supportive of it. And regarding the Oral Torah, which consists of the words of the seeker and the wise, he says "and from all the powders of the merchant."

---

### Behold the bed of Solomon; sixty warriors are around it, of the warriors of Israel. <note>Song of Songs 3:7</note>

He also mentioned a great preparation for this attachment: the place where the Divine Presence dwells in the lower realms. This ensures that the ministering angels will have no cause to complain. He calls it "the bed of Solomon," meaning the Holy Temple <note>original: *Beit HaMikdash*</note>. He calls it a "bed" because just as a bed is designated for the union of husband and wife, so the Temple is designated to maintain the union of the Holy One, Blessed be He, with the Assembly of Israel here below. It is fitting that this "bed" be made with perfect intention, directed toward the dwelling place of the Name, may He be blessed, and the dwelling of His strength. 

It is also written that "sixty warriors" surround it. This refers to all the priestly watches, all the Levite watches, and the twelve divisions of Israel. As they said in the Midrash, they are all righteous and "warriors" in their ability to conquer their internal desires <note>term: *Yetzer Hara* — the "evil inclination" or base impulse</note>. This is the meaning of "warriors of Israel." Their merit is sufficient to sustain the matter and to guard Israel, even if the community as a whole lacks merit. All of this is an argument against the angels—that God's submission to dwell below is not purely an act of unearned grace, for there indeed exists the merit of these individuals, and these merits cause the fulfillment of His word, as we have said.

---

### They all hold swords, expert in war; every man has his sword upon his thigh, because of fear in the night. <note>Song of Songs 3:8</note>

He explained the strength of the aforementioned "warriors": they are warriors against their own desires. They are always standing armed on guard so that the inclination does not overcome them and cause them to stumble, even slightly...
