This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs Chapter 5, specifically verses 5 and 6, focusing on the soul's attempt to repent and why it was initially unsuccessful.</meta>

### Page 33
### Song of Songs
### Signature: G 1
<meta>catchword: The King</meta>

...and their like. This follows the path of some commentators who explained that "Shema‘i" <note>Hebrew: *Shema‘i* — a name meaning "one who hears/obeys"</note> is a nickname for the heart. Even though the physical body is at the "opening of the innards"—which are known to be the vessels of nourishment and life—and its descent into the palm of their desire is what sustains the body, so too the elite among the warriors are the cause of the survival of the entire war effort, as is known.

<margin>She <note>The "She" refers to the Assembly of Israel or the individual soul</note> said that after her innards had lacked [the Divine Presence] for a time, she began to prepare herself to awaken for repentance. She put the matter into action by seeking out the internal forces and intentions for an awakening, and immediately [her] hands...</margin>

## I rose up to open to my beloved; and my hands dropped with myrrh, and my fingers with sweet smelling myrrh, upon the handles of the lock:

"Dropped with myrrh" refers to a pleasant and established scent resulting from the good deeds I sought to do and fulfill, serving as a support and aid to strengthen my repentance <note>Hebrew: *Teshuvah* — "returning" to God</note> and my abandonment of the evil path. However, the matter was not complete, nor was it particularly strong; it was only "for the face" <note>An idiom meaning "superficial" or "outward appearance"</note> and "from the lip outward" <note>Meaning "lip service," not from the heart</note>. Particularly concerning the general "wall" <note>A metaphor for the heart's defenses or spiritual integrity</note>, in its state of lack, the gates of the heart had already stumbled, and we were conquered by the Evil Inclination <note>Hebrew: *Yetzer HaRa* — the innate human impulse toward selfishness or sin</note> and the physical body. Ultimately, [the goal was] to cast off the yoke and the mountain of transgressions. 

And this phrase, "**and my fingers with sweet smelling myrrh**" <note>The Hebrew *mor over* literally means "passing myrrh"</note>, means that the good scent was fleeting and passing; the myrrh was not standing or lasting. It was "**upon the handles of the lock**," meaning it was because of the "handles of the lock" that the gates stumbled and became as weak as physical matter. 

In the Midrash regarding "**to open to my beloved**," it refers to opening with repentance. "**And my hands dropped with myrrh**" refers to bitterness <note>A wordplay: "myrrh" (*mor*) sounds like "bitterness" (*mar*)</note>, for He decreed a "cutting" <note>A punishment or decree</note> that passes through <note>Hebrew: *avar*</note> the Euphrates <note>Hebrew: *Perat*</note>. He who passes, passes; he who does not pass the Euphrates shall not pass, and so on. According to the Midrash of Mar bar Yitzchak, the extra language indicates bitterness. 

According to our path, it says: "I rose to open for my beloved in repentance," but even at the time of the awakening, I did not abandon my evil habits and my bad deeds entirely. They were not in abundance as before, but rather like the appearance of "drop by drop." My hands did not drip with the bitterness of sins and transgressions, and in the exile alone, I moved slowly. Nor did He <note>God</note> flee with the "wrath" <note>Hebrew: *evrah* — another wordplay on *over* (passing)</note> that accompanies Him, blessed be He—wrath and anger—to enter and close the paths of repentance, refusing to accept the awakening at all. This is the meaning of "**and my fingers with sweet smelling myrrh** [passing myrrh] **upon the handles of the lock**"—where "passing" (*over*) is from the language of "wrath" (*evrah*), as they explained it in the Midrash. 

He used the word "**my fingers**" to show that our general state in exile and the specific details which we "plastered" with our fingers <note>A reference to Ezekiel 13:10 regarding "plastering a flimsy wall," meaning a superficial or false spiritual fix</note> were serious matters. Therefore, the awakening was not effective enough to bring the repentance into full action.

<margin>She said that after some time, she was awakened and began to return in repentance in actual practice, and this is "I opened," etc. The truth is that the awakening was small, as we said above on the verse "Open for me," which the Sages of blessed memory explained as being "like the eye of a needle." To this he alluded here: "I opened for my beloved," for the term "for my beloved" corresponds to the word "for me" [used by God earlier].</margin>

## I opened to my beloved; but my beloved had withdrawn himself, and was gone: my soul failed when he spake: I sought him, but I could not find him; I called him, but he gave me no answer:

This was the "serious matter" mentioned above. Nevertheless, it did not avail because the decree had already been strengthened due to the length of time they persisted in their evil deeds. A great and complete repentance was required, not just a partial awakening and incomplete repentance of the general "wall," such as occurred in the days of King Hezekiah.

<summary>This page interprets the soul's attempt to repent as described in Song of Songs, explaining that the initial effort was superficial ("lip service") and lacked the depth required to overturn a long-standing Divine decree. The author uses wordplay between "myrrh," "bitterness," and "wrath" to illustrate the internal and external obstacles to spiritual reconciliation.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: The previous page discussed the soul's initial attempts to rise and open to the Beloved, describing a superficial stage of repentance that was not yet fully heartfelt. This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs Chapter 5, shifting focus to the internal struggle between the physical body and the intellect, and the transition from the First Temple to the Babylonian exile.</meta>

# Commentary

And in the explanation of the plain meaning of the scripture, it appears that this is the desire of the Assembly of Israel <note>Hebrew: *Knesset Yisrael* — the personification of the Jewish people in relation to God</note>. This is also the way of the poet-narrator, following this same path. The meaning is that the longing of the Assembly of Israel is to say: "I have returned to open a door for repentance." For I had already stripped off the garment of holiness and replaced it with another when I turned away from following the Lord to commit sins and to cling to the other forces that pull at the heart. After them, you smiled <note>original: *hayyachta* — likely meaning "consented" or "were lured"</note> to return and promise that the matter is very small and almost involuntary, because the Evil Inclination <note>Hebrew: *Yetzer Ha-Ra* — the innate impulse toward self-gratification or physical desires</note> and the material nature have already prevailed, and the intellectual power has been humbled. 

And this is what is meant by "from the forces to establish his knowledge." And this is why it says, **I have washed my feet** <note>A reference to Song of Songs 5:3</note>, meaning: I have arranged and prepared my "feet," which are the vessels of the material body. **How shall I soil them?** For from their perspective, spiritual purity is considered "soiling" because it lacks the pleasure and desire of the soul; it is the opposite of the material, as it is known that they are rivals to one another—when one falls, the other rises. The majority of the strength of the intellect and the rational soul signifies a lack to the physical body, as if it were a "soiling" <note>original: *tinnef*</note>, because purity is filth and rebellion is "washing" to the materialists, who call good evil and evil good.

->**My beloved put in his hand by the hole, and my bowels were moved for him.**<-

He said that at first, His desire (blessed be He) was like the likeness of a voice alone; when one hears a call from a distance without much loss or sickness. But in this, it is like a material force pushing from the outside upon the heart. And this is what is meant by "turning in the way of awakening for repentance." But because of the bitterness of their evil hearts, they continue to add insult to the blow. Behold, if the beginnings and warnings had been frequent and early—as it was during the First Temple in the days of Isaiah, Jeremiah, and the other prophets—they would have leaped and skipped [to repent]. This is like the likeness of a dreamer who softens his words to speak with his companion, speaking with sweetness. 

But here, he only **put in his hand** to awaken her, that she might set her heart to hear his words. This is the secret of **My beloved put in his hand by the hole, and behold, then my bowels were moved for him**—meaning, the internal limbs began to awaken for Him (blessed be He). For every desire, the awakening was in potential, though it had not yet emerged into action.

And in the Midrash <note>Ancient rabbinic homiletic commentary</note>, Rabbi Abba bar Kahana asked: What is the nature of this "hole"? It is the place where Mordecai <note>The hero of the Book of Esther</note> grew up. This is what the Assembly of Israel said before the Holy One, Blessed be He: "Master of the Universe, all the miracles You performed for me through Cyrus <note>The Persian King who allowed the return to Zion</note> were only to keep me alive; it would have been better to perform them for me through a righteous person <note>original: *tzaddik*</note>." 

Nevertheless, **my bowels were moved for him.** Our Sages, of blessed memory, following their own path, explain this verse in relation to the redemption from the Babylonian exile. It was difficult for them, and they said: "My beloved put in his hand by the hole"—that the "hole" is the place of Mordecai's explanation, pointing to the heels of the suffering and the transition to redemption and salvation. They explained that because the redemption came through King Cyrus, who was not a righteous man <note>original: *beltei tzaddik*</note>, it was as if He **put in his hand by the hole.** For His "great hand" of redemption was sent through a "hole," which refers to Cyrus, who was as unrefined as a donkey <note>original: *chamor*</note>. 

However, according to our way, the words follow their plain meaning. The intention is that when the [prophetic] eyes saw that words and beginnings would not suffice for the Children of Israel, and their "bowels" did not move, He sent His hand to them through the "hole"—hinting at the "creatures of the hole," which are scorpions and serpents. He said these are a metaphor for the sufferings and the exile. Just as it was in the First Temple, He incited the kings of Persia and the kings of Assyria against them, as explained in the Prophets. And then, "my bowels were moved for him"—meaning that only through this groaning and movement did they feel and begin to awaken their internal forces.

It uses the "hole" in the door as a metaphor for the suffering and historical figures (like Cyrus) used by God to stir the hearts of the people when prophetic warnings failed.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s discussion on the soul’s internal struggle and the transition from the First Temple to exile, this page focuses on the intensified suffering of the Assembly of Israel under foreign rule. It interprets the "wounding" and "stripping" mentioned in Song of Songs as the physical and spiritual persecution faced in exile, specifically the attempt to strip the Jewish people of their connection to the Torah.</meta>

-># Song of Songs<-

It is a matter that touches the soul; they also strived to make them <note>The Jewish people</note> turn from their faith and imposed evil decrees upon them to abolish the commandments of the Torah, either all of them or some of them. Regarding this, it says: **"They struck me, they wounded me"** <note>Song of Songs 5:7</note>. **"They struck me"** refers to the blows against the body; **"they wounded me"** refers to the blows against the soul and its wounds. The text expands on this part and says: **"The guardians of the walls took my veil from me,"** where the intention of the "veil" refers to the Torah and its commandments, which the Children of Israel wrap themselves in. It is an essential garment for the body, for the commandments of the Torah are set in number and weight according to the count of the limbs and the sinews <term>Gidim: veins or sinews</term> that are in the body, as is well known <note>In Jewish tradition, the 613 commandments correspond to the 248 limbs and 365 sinews of the human body</note>.

## After being distanced, she even strengthened herself in exile: "I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem,"
## Under the rule of the nations: "If you find my beloved, what will you tell him?"
## And she is powerful yet stripped of the cloak of holiness: "That I am lovesick."
## And then she shone, remembering even her earliest days
## When she sat upon the lap of the King.

Holiness clings to her God, and in the bitterness of her soul, she spoke with the angels of the high levels, who are the **"daughters of Jerusalem"** as mentioned above. She adjured them that they should reach out and give their hand so that the Lord might awaken to the sound of their words when they speak of her destruction. After these events, the wrath of the Lord would be in its initial strength, but the "wrath of the King"—the King of the World <note>God</note>—is tempered, so that He might incline His ear to their statement as they act as her legal advocates <term>Sanigoria: a defense or plea in favor of someone</term>. They shall say that she is **"lovesick"** for His service, and she regrets and comforts herself over her initial deeds. All her salvation and all her desire is to be attached <term>Dvekus: spiritual attachment or "cleaving" to the Divine</term> to His love, as in the days of old and as in ancient years upon the holy mountain. **"If you find my beloved, what will you tell him,"** etc.

And they asked, **"What will you tell him?"** for it would have been more appropriate to say, "Tell him something great." The Assembly of Israel <note>Hebrew: *Knesset Yisrael*—the personification of the Jewish people</note> spoke, and the intention was to tell a different story than what some of them might have thought. They thought they should tell Him of the greatness of her pain and her troubles, and how all her pursuers overtook her "between the straits" <note>A reference to Lamentations 1:3, describing the period of national mourning</note>, and how every day’s curse is greater than the day before with the renewal of decrees and troubles, so that the mercies of the Beloved <note>God</note> might be stirred for her, and He would contain her and lead her from trouble to rest. This was to show that she was not sinning out of spite, for the troubles of the body are like a fleeting breath, and all her wisdom was consumed by the troubles of the soul due to the absence of holy attachment. This is especially true when she was in the "land of Amalek" <note>Symbolic of the ultimate enemy or a place of deep spiritual concealment</note>, which causes even greater distancing. She longs for Him while the "walls" <note>The foreign rulers or the physical constraints of exile</note> hinder her hand from fulfilling the Torah and its commandments. This is the meaning of **"What will you tell him? That I am lovesick."**

In the Midrash <note>Ancient homiletic interpretations of scripture</note>, it says: "Just as this sick person looks forward to healing, so the generation in Egypt looked forward to redemption." Our entire path fits well with this, for she did not despair of the attachment <term>Dvekus</term> even if she was mingled among the nations. She was not entirely assimilated to the point of leaving the "Champion of her youth" <note>God</note>, and she did not forget the religion of her God’s commandments. On the contrary, she suffered harsh idolatry and evil decrees so as not to transgress the religion, hoping for Heavenly mercy to return to her initial state of attachment.

## Behold the daughters of Jerusalem, which are the names of the angels: "What is your beloved more than another beloved, O fairest among women?"
## As mentioned above, or the nations of the world according to the opinion: "What is your beloved more than another beloved, that you thus adjure us?"
## The one who spreads the proven matter and the strength of her words
## For in their eyes, it is a wonder why you long for this.

And also, her soul was consumed with longing after the attachment of her Beloved, for according to the logic of the intellect, she should have wept to Him—

<summary>This page interprets the suffering of the Jewish people in exile as a physical and spiritual wounding that attempts to strip them of their "veil"—the Torah. Despite these persecutions, the Assembly of Israel is described as being "lovesick" for God, seeking the advocacy of angels to express her enduring desire for spiritual reconnection.</summary>

# Commentary

The King; and therefore, they did not have [the opportunity to repent] because the decree had already been sealed. This is the meaning of the verse: **"My beloved had turned away and was gone"** <note>Song of Songs 5:6</note>. It means that He passed by and crossed over my presence in a hurry, while I was in a state of "hiding of the Divine Face" <term>Hester Panim: a theological state where God's presence is not felt and suffering occurs</term> from Him, and the decree of exile was not annulled. Regarding this, the verse says: **"My soul failed me when he spoke,"** meaning that I was terrified and I rose up, and my soul departed: how could I not have heard His voice in His mercy when He was knocking? And how did I not open myself to Him and wake up to return in repentance in haste? For I sought Him now and did not find Him; I called Him, etc.

The reason for this "seeking" and "calling" refers to the two exiles. Regarding the exile of the Ten Tribes <note>The Northern Kingdom of Israel exiled by Assyria</note>, it says: **"I sought him but did not find him."** Regarding the exile of Judah and Benjamin <note>The Southern Kingdom</note> and the destruction of the Holy Temple, it says: **"I called him but he did not answer me."** This lack of response applies to one who is already distanced; one calls to Him, but He does not answer. The intention is that after the "turning away" <note>original: "חמק" (chamack)</note>, the Blessed Name began to distance Himself from us. The Ten Tribes had already been exiled, and I saw the enemy dragging the seal and the destruction melting [the foundations]. In that state, I called to Him in prayer and supplications, but He did not answer me, for the decree had already been issued without remedy or repair.

It is possible that the phrase **"turned away and was gone"** <note>original: "חמק עבר" (chamack avar)</note> corresponds to these two aforementioned exiles. Regarding the exile of the Ten Tribes, the language of "slipping away" <note>original: "חמק" (chamack)</note> is appropriate, as it was a turning away of the Face, but not a total "passing," because the Holy Temple was still on its foundation and part of the Land of Israel was still inhabited. However, regarding the exile of Judah and the destruction of the Temple, the language of "passed on" <note>original: "עבר" (avar)</note> is appropriate, which signifies a complete removal, as I have written.

<margin>This verse comes according to its literal sense to explain what was said above: "turned away and was gone," etc.</margin>
->**"THE WATCHMEN WHO GO ABOUT THE CITY FOUND ME"**<-
<margin>"I sought him," etc. The intention is that after Israel was already struck by the exile of the Ten Tribes and their prayer was hindered, the hand of the enemies grew strong.</margin>
->**"THEY STRUCK ME, THEY WOUNDED ME; THEY TOOK MY VEIL"**<-
->**"FROM ME, THE KEEPERS OF THE WALLS."**<-

[They came] upon them and achieved the "seal of the vessels" <note>likely referring to the capturing of the Temple vessels</note> of the enemies who were surrounding the walls of Jerusalem and besieged it. The city was breached before them, and troubles increased—troubles of the sword, killing, and destruction—as is known from the scriptures which say what happened to the people of God who suffered at the time of the destruction. Regarding this, it says: **"They struck me, they wounded me."** Not only this, but they also moved away from the holiness of the rampart, and the Holy Temple was destroyed, for the people had desecrated it with all manner of great and numerous evils. 

Had the Holy Temple remained on its foundation, they would not have felt their misfortune so acutely; they would have been comforted by dwelling on their ramparts and in the building of their Sanctuary. For they would have been protected by all the holiness and purity that hovered over them, covering them like a veil or a cloak that wraps the whole body of the one who wears it. However, the essence of their being "cut off" was the destruction of the Temple and the exile. For through this, the power of holiness and purity was taken from them, and they entered under the dominion of impurity, for the air of the land of the nations is impure, since they were under the sixty sparks of the "husks" <term>Klippah: Kabbalistic term for the forces of evil or impurity that conceal the divine light</term>, as is known. This is the meaning of: **"The keepers of the walls took my veil from me."**

It is also possible that "the keepers of the walls" refers to the angels who previously guarded the walls of Jerusalem and saved her. They were the compassionate ones who provided protection in Jerusalem and the Temple. As it says in the Midrash <note>Lamentations Rabbah</note>: "Every knowledge prevailed in it"—this refers to Michael and Gabriel, etc. This is what is meant by **"they took my veil from me,"** for if they had not participated in the burning of the Temple, no creature in the world would have been able to treat it with such contempt. It is also possible to interpret **"they struck me, they wounded me"** as referring to a blow to the body and a blow to one's wealth, for besides the great killing...

<summary>This page interprets the Song of Songs as a historical allegory for the two exiles of Israel. It explains the physical and spiritual suffering of the people as the removal of a divine "veil" of protection, linking the "keepers of the walls" to both the conquering enemies and the withdrawing guardian angels.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the allegorical commentary on Song of Songs, specifically exploring the description of the "Beloved" (God) as "white and ruddy," signifying the balance between Mercy and Judgment. It follows the previous discussion on the exiles and the soul's longing to return to Divine favor.</meta>

...rising up indicates their transgression, while the "horse" symbolizes Judgment <term>Din: The divine attribute of strict justice and law</term>. The proof of this is that He does not stop with them as if intending to destroy them, though He decrees evil upon them and places them in the "siege" of troubles and judgments. Even in the place of exile, the heart will return to the thought of being near Him and serving Him, for they realized that all this was for their ultimate benefit. In this way, He was always her "Beloved," speaking to her in many ways: sometimes as a father of supplications with Mercy <term>Rachamim: The divine attribute of compassion and mercy</term>, and sometimes in His essence as a judge, conducting Himself with Law.

It is possible that the "ruddy" aspect <note>referring to Song of Songs 5:10, "My beloved is white and ruddy"</note> refers to the Lord, like a refined wine, because He withheld life by His will—since He desires the vitality of His own essence—and thus, His will is what provides bread and sustenance. This also applies to the "ruddy" quality of the Exile: even though it seems inferior due to the harshness of Judgment, it is actually a force that prepares the foundation for Mercy. 

This is similar to what we heard in the Midrash <note>Genesis Rabbah, Chapter 44</note>, which says:
> The Holy One, blessed be He, said to Abraham: "Which do you prefer your children to descend into—Gehenna <note>Hell or spiritual punishment</note> or the foreign Kingdoms <note>Exile under earthly empires</note>?" Rabbi Hanina bar Papa said: "Abraham chose the Kingdoms for them." Rabbi Yudan, Rabbi Idi, and Rabbi Ami bar Hanina said: "Abraham chose Gehenna, but the Holy One, blessed be He, chose the Kingdoms for him."

This seems difficult, but the point is that the "Kingdoms" (Exile) were chosen for the benefit of Israel, to strip away their sins and evil deeds so they would not suffer in Gehenna eternally. This explains the phrase **"My beloved is white and ruddy,"** to say that even when He acts with Judgment, His strength is hidden within, for He is always "my Beloved," standing in friendship and the love of Naomi <note>original: "נעמי" (pleasantness/loveliness)</note> and in Mercy.

The verse says **"distinguished among ten thousand"** <note>Song of Songs 5:10</note> to say that despite being "distinguished among the chariot" <note>original: "דגול מרכבה"</note>—as it is written: *"Thousands of thousands ministered to Him, and ten thousand times ten thousand stood before Him"* <note>Daniel 7:10</note>—He did not desire the celestial beings. Instead, He sought to rest His Divine Presence <term>Shekhinah: The indwelling presence of God on earth</term> within me, to always strive for my benefit and good, like a devoted protector. Therefore, if He hastens to be divided for the sake of His love <insert>and the wisdom of the Lord</insert>, our Sages said of Him: "In Egypt I acted and I passed through the land of Egypt," and also "at the Sea." For as it says, *"The Lord passed through to smite the Egyptians,"* and regarding the Sea, *"The children of Israel walked on dry land in the midst of the sea."* At the Sea, He conducted Himself as it is said, *"The Lord shook the Egyptians into the midst of the sea."* 

The Sages explained "white and ruddy" as referring to Mercy and Judgment, and they centered this on the "Presence of the Lord." In Egypt, there was "violence," and there was a need for Mercy for Israel and Judgment for the Egyptians. Similarly at the Sea, as the Midrash Rabbah says: "With one hand He was sinking the Egyptians, and with the other hand He was saving Israel."

By examining their words (of blessed memory) according to our path, we answer several questions with good taste and knowledge. We see why she [the Community of Israel] yearns for the love of her Blessed Beloved: because she remembers all the goodness and kindness she received from Him in ancient days. She begins with the fact that she was chosen to be His people, and the Lord brought her close to His service until the time of the end—the time of exile. As we will explain, His strength remained firm even during the Egyptian era, for He needed to act with Judgment and Mercy simultaneously, both in Egypt and at the Sea, even though she [Israel] was not inherently worthy of it. This was despite the "Accuser" <term>The Satan or Prosecuting Angel</term> pointing to the "idolatry of Egypt" at the altar. Her love for Him begins in that bitterness, as it is written: *"The Lord whose goodness is prepared for all."*

## His head is as the finest gold; his locks are curly 

## and black as a raven. <note>Song of Songs 5:11</note>

According to the first approach, here she begins to recount the reception of the good that was spoken of regarding the Exodus from Egypt. The matter is compared to the "Head," which is the beginning of the body from top to bottom and its essential part, as is known. What a miracle it was that the Lord began to manifest His full power, might, and sovereignty in the world through His laws and services! Likewise, all sovereignty is symbolized by the Head, for that is where the royal crown is placed. And this is...

It cites the famous Midrash of Abraham choosing Exile over Gehenna and begins a physical-spiritual description of God's 'Head' as the beginning of His sovereignty during the Exodus.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the allegorical commentary on the Song of Songs, transitioning from the discussion of the "horse" of Judgment and the "wine" of Divine will to a dialogue between the nations and the Assembly of Israel regarding the nature of her Beloved (God) and her persistent devotion despite the hardships of exile.</meta>

# Commentary

Back and not face <note>Refers to a state of Divine hiddenness where God turns His "back" rather than His "face" toward His people</note> and distancing oneself from Him. When that generation acted in such a way, it was said of her: "What is your beloved..." <note>Song of Songs 5:9: "What is your beloved more than another beloved, O you fairest among women?"</note> meaning, what makes your Beloved so different from all other "beloveds" (the gods of other nations)? Since you are the "fairest among women," possessing no flaw, disgrace, or ugliness more than other women that would cause such a distancing, then why do you pursue Him? For with other lovers, when they distance themselves from those who desire them and do not pursue them, the women also stop chasing after them. You, too, with your Beloved, should have behaved in this manner, for you are not inferior to other women; rather, you are more distinguished, the "fairest among women." From this, you should learn that it was not because of your lowly value or inferiority that He did this to you—for Heaven forbid that He should lack any great advantage over all other beloveds in the ultimate sense of beauty and excellence.

Nevertheless, the matter is still a wonder: "What is your beloved more than another beloved?" That is, to what height does the measure of His excellence and beauty rise over all other "beloveds," that the style of His nature and the power of His unique quality should produce such a strong love? For this reaches the very limit of the definition of love, to the point that it has acted upon you so that you have become weak and "fallen into a bed of illness" <term>Sick with love: A state of intense spiritual longing for the Divine, as mentioned in Song of Songs 5:8</term>. Such a state requires a great advantage in Him, especially since you are the "fairest among women."

By way of example, the nations say to the Assembly of Israel <term>Knesset Yisrael: The personification of the Jewish people in their relationship with God</term>: "Since you were the most beautiful and virtuous among the nations in terms of holiness and purity, even if you had sinned many times, it was not fitting that your God should hide His face from you. For to which of the other nations would He turn to bestow influence and goodness? The other nations are full of sin, and the virtue of their deeds is far worse than your worst actions, O chosen nation! If so, why do you, Assembly of Israel, pursue your Beloved? How is your Beloved different from the gods of the other nations? Since He has shown you that He no longer desires you, you too should not desire Him or remain constantly under the 'yoke of His illness' <note>The burden of suffering for His sake</note>."

And if you should teach us that this "wound" in me is greater than all other illnesses, and there is no end to His greatness and His excellence over everything—nevertheless, what is this "business of uniqueness" that is so lacking, specifically regarding you, Assembly of Israel? Especially after He has driven you away from His presence, why do you still make yourself ill for the sake of His love?

It is possible to further explain the repetition of "What is your beloved more than another beloved" based on two perspectives: the absence of benefit and the arrival of harm. Regarding both, the nations said: "How is your Beloved different from every other beloved?" You, the "fairest among women," are the opposite of most women who love their partners because of the benefit they receive from them. When the goodness and benefit are gone, the love is annulled. Yet, according to my students, your love for Him is not "dependent on a specific thing" <note>A reference to Ethics of the Fathers 5:16: "Love that depends on a specific thing, when that thing passes, the love passes; but love that does not depend on a specific thing never passes."</note>. Even so, for love to remain in its place even in the absence of benefit—and specifically when it is replaced by the arrival of harm, suffering, and burden—is surely rare. Most would distance themselves from love and put hatred and enmity in its place. But in a place where, despite all this, you still make yourself ill for the sake of love, it is undoubtedly a wonder of wonders.

This was the case between the nations and Israel. The Holy One, blessed be He, removed goodness and benefit from her; and not only that, but He humbled her to the dust and set against her His "four sore judgments" <note>See Ezekiel 14:21: sword, famine, wild beasts, and pestilence</note> as is known. Regarding these two perspectives, the verse repeats "What is your beloved more than another beloved," rising above them to emphasize the strength of our "imprinting" and the power of the source, as mentioned above.

<note>Song of Songs 5:10</note>

Now she (the Assembly of Israel) answers her companions (the nations), explaining that she is right to make herself ill for the love of this Beloved. For this Beloved is very different from all other "beloveds"; they do not enter into the same category or comparison at all, being as far apart as the "shot of a bow." First, because He is great and knowledgeable in the "wrath of the form" <note>The structure of Divine Judgment</note> of her Beloved, which requires of her a strong piety. This is understood from the whiteness...

Israel responds by asserting God's unique excellence and explaining that her love is not dependent on material gain but is an essential, spiritual bond.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the allegorical analysis of Song of Songs 5:12, moving from the general beauty of the Beloved (God) to a specific discussion of His "eyes," interpreted here as His Divine Providence during the Exodus from Egypt and the revelation at Mount Sinai.</meta>

# Song of Songs

...a wall over them, and he comforted them for their benefit. Immediately, the **Attribute of Mercy** <note>Hebrew: *Middat HaRachamim*, the aspect of God that acts with compassion rather than strict justice</note> replied to the Holy One, Blessed be He: "Is it for their harm that they transgressed? They did not transgress except out of the pressure of subjugation and the confusion of the mind." He [the Attribute of Mercy] stood as an advocate, like a defense attorney seeking merit according to the Divine will. As soon as the **Prince of the Sea** <note>In Jewish lore, the angelic being appointed over the oceans</note> heard that the wrath with which he had been filled against Israel was now turned back upon Egypt, it is written: *And the waters returned* <note>Exodus 14:28</note>, meaning they returned to their natural state upon the Egyptians. These are his words. 

Thus, it is clear that besides the accusation of the Prince of Egypt—who claimed, "These [Egyptians] are idolaters and these [Israelites] are idolaters"—**Samael** <note>A prominent figure in Jewish mysticism often serving as the accuser or the personification of evil</note> also joined in to accuse and harm Israel. It was necessary for the Blessed One to supervise them with an eye of compassion to nullify the accusation and to find a defense for them, by washing away their iniquity and forgiving them, removing the coarseness that was within them. 

This is the meaning of **"His eyes are like doves"** <note>Song of Songs 5:12</note>: that the eyes of His providence were upon her [Israel] to forgive her sin like doves. **"By the channels of water"** refers to the waters of the Sea of Reeds <note>The Red Sea</note>, which were like milk. It is possible that the comparison to doves is intended to match another metaphor mentioned by our Sages of blessed memory regarding this verse: "Just as this dove is faithful <note>original: *mat'aya*</note> and recognizes her mate and will never exchange him for another..." This is what he intended here. Even though the accusation was that they had worshipped idols and exchanged God for another, the Holy One found merit for them—that they were like doves and did not truly exchange Him for another, for there was grace and goodness within them like milk. 

To indicate the removal of the strict **Judgment** <note>Hebrew: *Din*, the attribute of God that demands accountability and punishment</note> that covers the sin, it says **"washed in milk."** It was necessary to wash the eyes of Providence and to sweeten and perfume them with simple mercies, symbolized by the whiteness of milk. For despite everything, the sin of idolatry was present, but in His mercy, He found merit for them—that the sin was merely external, while the internal [goodness] remained in its warmth. By this, the aspect of Providence was shifted from a state of full rage to a state of complete mercy, not only to save them from drowning in the sea and to rescue them like milk, but also to bring them goodness and benefit. For the "spoil of the Sea" was greater than the "spoil of Egypt," as our Sages of blessed memory said.

According to a second interpretation of the previous verse regarding the Sea of Reeds, it is possible to continue this verse as referring also to the miracles of the Sea, noting their specific details which were as great as milk.

However, it is more closely related to the **Standing at Mount Sinai** <note>The event of the Giving of the Torah</note> and the receiving of the Torah, which was an essential good for souls and for their longings. He says **"His eyes are like doves,"** meaning the Utterances of the Torah, which are "eyes" to illuminate the souls and to achieve the perfection of the soul and attachment to Him. For this reason, the **Torah** is called Torah—because it "points" <note>Hebrew: *morah*, a play on the word Torah</note> the way in which they should walk and the deeds they should do. Similarly, the Psalmist says of the commandment of the Lord that it is *pure, enlightening the eyes* <note>Psalm 19:9</note>. 

This holy comparison of "eyes" is used, and he says **"like doves"** based on the comparison of our Sages in the Midrash: "Just as the dove brought light to the world..." <note>A reference to the dove in the story of Noah's Ark</note>. And the phrase **"by the channels of water"** refers back to His "eyes," meaning the **Ten Commandments** which are founded and rooted upon the rivers and streams of the Torah, which is famously called "water." For all parts of the Torah—its general principles, its specific details, and the hints of its commandments—are included within the Ten Commandments. 

Not only are all the parts of the literal and revealed meaning included, but also the hidden parts, the inner dimension of the Torah and its secrets. As it is written in the **Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note> on the portion of *Yitro*: "There was no voice that was not clear with a light that all the prophets, all the leaders, and all the generations to come until the King Messiah looked upon." This is the meaning of **"washed in milk,"** for milk indicates the secrets of the Torah and its mysteries. As it was written above: *Honey and milk are under your tongue* <note>Song of Songs 4:11</note>. Our Sages of blessed memory interpreted this: as it is taught regarding the secrets of the Torah that were there, the **Elders of Pumbedita** <note>A major center of Torah study in ancient Babylonia</note> said to Rabbi Joseph: "We say that the **Account of the Chariot** <note>Hebrew: *Ma'aseh Merkavah*, the highest and most restricted level of Jewish mystical study</note> should be as 'honey and milk under your tongue'—matters that are sweeter than honey and milk should stay under your tongue [kept hidden]."

And it is possible to say **"washed in milk"** refers to merit, fitness, and the beginning of whiteness in general...

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs 5:12, linking the "eyes like doves" to God's merciful providence during the Exodus and the enlightening power of the Ten Commandments at Sinai. It emphasizes that the Torah contains both revealed laws and hidden mystical secrets, described as "sweet as milk and honey."</summary>

# Commentary

At that time, Israel rose to greatness, significance, and high value, becoming as desirable as refined and purified silver—the most significant and superior of nations. This indicates the distinction of the good and the benefit bestowed upon Israel, as well as a blessing for being chosen, for from them would come great blessing and abundant wealth. This is the proper way to understand it. 

Behold, our Sages of Blessed Memory <note>Hebrew: *RaZal*, an acronym for the Jewish communal authorities of the Mishnaic and Talmudic eras</note> interpreted the word "purified" as referring to the reception of goodness and benefit. Regarding the guilty, however, the "eye" of judgment looked away from mercy toward acts of vengeance and great legal punishments. They said: **"His locks are wavy"** <note>Song of Songs 5:11</note>, meaning that the "locks" of His hair—which are associated with the powers of Divine Judgment—were "heaps upon heaps" <note>A wordplay on the Hebrew *taltalim* (wavy) and *tali talim* (heaps upon heaps)</note>. This implies that every single blow dealt [to the Egyptians] was doubled and quintupled <note>A reference to the Passover Haggadah's discussion that if God's "finger" brought ten plagues, His "hand" at the sea brought fifty</note>.

In the words of our Sages, the harshness is like the "interpretation of the raven." They explained that just as the raven is black, all the aspects of the world gathered to whiten the "wing" of the raven, but they were unable. This is a parable for the spiritual forces according to our method. According to the path already explained in the previous verse regarding the power over Egypt, it is possible to link this verse to expand the explanation of the matter, specifically regarding the judgment in Egypt.

It is more likely that the previous verse refers to the judgment in Egypt, while this verse refers to His "canopy" and His intention to be crowned with the **Crown of His Mercies** <term>Keter: In Jewish mysticism, the "Crown" is the highest emanated level of Divinity, representing the primordial will</term>, the Crown of Goodness. Though there was fear, He was primarily compassionate due to the great significance of [Israel, who is like] **fine gold** <note>Hebrew: *Ketem paz*, from Song of Songs 5:11</note>. 

The "Crown" here indicates a distinction before the Blessed One—even more so than the ministering angels. As our Sages of Blessed Memory said: "When Israel emerged from the sea, the angels came to offer a song of praise before the Holy One, Blessed be He. The Holy One, Blessed be He, said to them: 'Let My children [Israel] come first.'" <note>A famous Midrash where God prioritizes the prayers/songs of suffering humans over those of eternal angels</note> 

Even though Moses and Israel were lower in rank, this song was more precious than the song of the angels. The Holy One, Blessed be He, said to the angels: "It is not because I despise your service, but because they [Israel] are flesh and blood; they must sing first before they die." But for the angels, as long as they desire, they remain alive and eternal. In any case, to satisfy the minds of the angels, He spoke to them thus. From this, we see that the Holy One, Blessed be He, desired the song of Israel greatly, wanting them all to merit His protection. If so, we call this **"fine gold"** (*Ketem paz*). This is a great indication of the high status of Israel, for we have reached this level.

Regarding the vengeance against the guilty, He says **"His locks are wavy"** because the blows at the Sea were numerous, difficult, and powerful, exceeding the blows in Egypt, as explained in the Midrashic studies—examine them and you will understand. He also mentions **"black as a raven"** because they were in the absolute "blackness" of strict judgment.

-> Transitioning from the darkness of judgment to the whiteness of mercy, for He does not withhold His kindness forever. <-

Following the path of compassion from the previous verse:

## **His eyes are like doves by the channels of water, washed**
## **with milk, and fitly set.**

The remainder of the power over Egypt is discussed here, specifically regarding the splitting of the Sea of Reeds and the even greater goodness that the Holy One bestowed upon Israel. Even the "eyes" of His providence were upon them to save them from drowning in the Sea of Reeds despite the "Great Accuser" standing against them. 

As it says in the Midrash <note>Exodus Rabbah</note>: "**The waters were a wall** (*chomah*) **to them**"—read it instead as "wrath" (*chemah*). The Prince of the Sea <note>The angelic representative of the ocean</note> stood before Him and said: "Sovereign of the Universe! These [Egyptians] served idols, and these [Israelites] served idols! Why do You perform miracles for them?" And He made His voice heard to the Prince of the Sea, and at the beginning...

It highlights a Midrashic tradition where God prioritizes Israel's "Song at the Sea" over the praises of the angels because of human mortality.</summary>

# Commentary on the Song of Songs

And **"towers of perfume"** <note>Song of Songs 5:13</note>: they could not endure it, and they swept away from themselves all the "moisture" <note>Hebrew: *lachluach*, referring here to physical material or spiritual vitality</note> as is known. Behold, the text speaks in praise of the **Two First Commandments** <note>The first two of the Ten Commandments, which tradition says were heard by the Israelites directly from God's mouth rather than through Moses</note>, comparing **"His cheeks"** to **"a bed of spices."** This means that just as a spice is attached to its soil and maintains its growth—and because it is attached, it remains established and lasts in its freshness for many days, its flavor staying within it and its scent not fading—so too were the first two commandments. 

When they heard them from the mouth of the Blessed One, the will and the rest of the souls among the Children of Israel were very great. They achieved a "seal" upon their health <note>Or "clarity of vision"</note> unto the very end of their inner essence. The secrets and mysteries included within the commandments were grasped with great subtlety. Furthermore, the study of the Torah was fixed in their hearts; they became scholars and did not forget. 

This is as they said in the *Midrash Rabbah* on the verse **"His cheeks [are like a bed of spices]..."** and **"towers of perfumes."** This means that the "moisture" of the souls alone achieved a great level of perception and derived an essential pleasure from it. The same was true for the soul when the "moisture" was within it. Even the bodies achieved great perception, for their physical matter was refined and their <term>zohama</term> <note>A mystical term for the spiritual "filth" or "impurity" introduced to humanity by the serpent in the Garden of Eden</note> ceased, until there was no longer a dividing veil preventing the perception of subtle and hidden matters. They were mingled with and took pleasure in those words along with the soul, even though the soul experienced more "rest" <note>Hebrew: *nachat*, meaning spiritual satisfaction</note> than the physical life-force, for every part received according to its preparation.

Regarding the physical part, it says **"towers of perfumes"** <note>Hebrew: *migdalot merkachim*</note>, because "perfume-making" <note>Hebrew: *merkachat*</note> pertains to things from which spices are blended for food or medicine. In such things, there is a "taste" which is coarser and more physical than "scent." Thus, they perceived both "taste" and "scent," each on its own level at the ultimate point of subtlety possible for it. 

The image of these two commandments was unique. Regarding the rest of the commandments, it says **"His lips are lilies"** <note>Song of Songs 5:13</note>. This is compared to the beauty of lilies and roses after they have been plucked. This implies that while their scent is not as strong or powerful as when they are attached to the ground, they nonetheless possess a great and precious fragrance. So too with the other commandments: though the perception of them was not as subtle or perfect as the first two—since they were delivered through an intermediary <note>Moses</note>—nonetheless, the people achieved a great perception through them. 

As we have written regarding the *Zohar* <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note>, this is like the scent of lilies which is very fragrant, but **"dropping liquid myrrh"** <note>Hebrew: *notfot mor over*</note>. This means the scent is precious, even though it is "passing" <note>Hebrew: *over* can mean "liquid," "flowing," or "passing/transient"</note>. This is like the passage in the Midrash: "With every word, our souls departed... but when they heard it, they returned to being those who learn and forget." They said: "Just as a staff of flesh and blood passes away, so too his teaching passes away." <note>This refers to the transition from God's eternal word to Moses's mediated teaching, where human forgetfulness returned</note>

Alternatively, **"His cheeks are like a bed of spices"** means the first commandments were like a garden bed that is focused and distinct from all other beds in the garden by being so fragrant that its scent wafts far away. So were the aforementioned two commandments distinct among the rest, being beloved as they came from the mouth of the Blessed One. 

They were also **"towers of perfumes."** As the Midrash says: "Just as this tower of spices contains all types of spices," so too they perceived within them all parts, details, and the finest sub-details with a complete and clear understanding—in the literal sense (*Pshat*), the structured sense (*Tur*), the revealed and the hidden, and the 'hidden of the hidden.' From His mouth came knowledge and discernment. 

Included in this is that the "perfume-blends" and fragrant things completely remove a bad odor and distance the "filth" (*zohama*) to a great degree. So too, the first commandments removed the **Evil Inclination** <note>Hebrew: *Yetzer HaRa*</note>, distancing it and uprooting it from Israel. As the Midrash says: "When Israel heard 'Thou shalt not have [other gods],' the Evil Inclination was uprooted from their hearts." 

**"His lips are lilies"** refers to the rest of the commandments; their scent was also wafting like lilies. However, they were not on the level of the "bed of spices," for the perception achieved through them was like a scent that...

<summary>This page provides a mystical and homiletic commentary on Song of Songs 5:13, distinguishing between the first two commandments heard directly from God and the subsequent eight heard through Moses. It uses the metaphors of "attached spices" versus "plucked lilies" to explain the difference between eternal, unmediated spiritual perception and mediated, transient learning.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the allegorical analysis of Song of Songs 5:12 ("His eyes are like doves") and transitions into 5:13 ("His cheeks are like a bed of spices"). The author continues to link these physical descriptions to the spiritual experience of receiving the Torah at Mount Sinai.</meta>

<header>Commentary</header>

...in general, and as mentioned above, there was no voice that was not clear, etc. This is a spiritual comparison in the heart, for **milk** beautifies the eyes and improves their sight, as the commentators, of blessed memory, have written. He says, **"sitting in fullness"** <note>Song of Songs 5:12</note> to teach that these Commandments—God forbid one should think they are lacking or redundant—stand upon the "fullness" of the Torah in total perfection, without any deficiency at all, for they contain a complete intention. This serves as a powerful explanation of what was said above regarding **"streams of water"** <note>Song of Songs 5:12</note>; since no voice was heard to fall upon the multitude, He made known their power—that a complete intention, until the very end, was included in every word, and nothing was missing. 

This is as it is written in the *Zohar*, portion *Emor* <note>original: "פרשה אמור"</note>: "Every single word was full of all those flavors, all those hidden and deep matters, secrets and mysteries, like a full vessel that is overflowing," etc. Furthermore, one can interpret **"His eyes are like doves"** to mean that His words are like light; they looked through the hole of the wall <note>Perhaps referring to the "lattice" through which God looks in Song of Songs 2:9</note> collectively, like the image of doves looking through a hole at the world. Specifically, it was not only the external beauty of His face that drew them slightly toward the world to guide and lead them toward the desired perfection, but even when their souls left them, it was for their own great good. For the departure of their souls purified them, as discussed in the *Zohar*, portion *Emor*: 

<note>The following is a translation of the Aramaic Zohar passage cited in the text</note>
"At the hour when the Holy One, Blessed be He, revealed Himself and began to speak over them, they were established and mended, and the souls of Israel departed. That soul, which had no speech below, was engraved in the four winds of the world; they were full and descended. When the soul ascended, it gazed from the mountains of pure balsam and looked upon that dew from above. It was then cherished within the 'merchachis' <note>likely a corruption of *merchatz*, 'baths' or 'cleansing places'</note> of Israel, and their souls were restored to them, cherished and engraved in their place upon the tablets of stone. And so it was with every word." End of quote. 

There is also a spiritual comparison in the heart regarding the "washing" of the Commandments and their esteemed nature from the "mountains of pure balsam" and the "snow from above." These represent the ultimate refinement and whiteness, like the image of **milk**. Regarding their return and being "set" in their place on the Tablets, the verse concludes **"sitting in fullness,"** for after being washed in milk to return the souls, they sat in their place in their rest upon the stone tablets.

### His cheeks are like a bed of spices, towers of
### perfumes; his lips are lilies,
### dripping liquid myrrh.

According to the first path, the previous verse spoke of the Splitting of the Sea of Reeds. Here, in this verse, it speaks of the Standing at Mount Sinai. Let us examine the matters simply: the **cheeks** and the **lips** are the instruments of speech. The "cheeks" encompass the mouth as a whole, while the "lips" are well-known. The intention is that the Commandments were divided into two parts: 

1. The "hidden" five—the first Commandments which we heard "from the mouth of the Almighty" <term>Mi-pi Ha-Gevurah: a term for direct divine revelation without a mediator</term>.
2. The second part—the remaining Commandments which were spoken through a mediator, namely Moses our Teacher, peace be upon him <note>Hebrew abbreviation: מרע"ה</note>, as it is written: "Go near and hear..." <note>Deuteronomy 5:24</note>. 

He attributes the first, hidden ones to the **"cheeks"** and the rest to the **"lips,"** because the cheeks are larger in measure and quantity than the lips. Similarly, those [first] Commandments possessed greater power, having been spoken from the mouth of the Almighty, and the people could not endure them as they did the other Commandments. Furthermore, the lips indicate more "revelation," whereas the cheeks are more covered and further from the source of speech. Thus, the other Commandments were spoken through a mediator and were not heard [directly] from the Blessed One like the first two Commandments. 

There is another fitting comparison: the lips "cut" <note>articulate</note> the speech. While the speech is still within the cheeks, its sound is not heard as a distinct hearing, indicating "hiddenness." Thus, the first two Commandments, due to their great hiddenness...

<summary>This page uses mystical sources like the Zohar to explain that the Ten Commandments were perfectly "full" of meaning. It distinguishes between the first two commandments, heard directly from God (symbolized by the "cheeks"), and the subsequent ones heard through Moses (symbolized by the "lips").</summary>

# Song of Songs

And due to the greatness of their subtlety <note>The Hebrew word *dakut* refers to spiritual refinement or microscopic detail</note>, they could not endure it, and their souls weakened over the study, as is known. Now, the text speaks in praise of the Ten Commandments and divides them into a **"bed of spices."** This means that just as spice is connected to its root and the source of its greatness, the measure of its being connected allows it to exist and endure for many days; its taste remains within it and its scent does not fade. This is not the case after it is plucked—God forbid—for it then begins to wither, as is known. 

In this likeness were the **first two commandments** <note>The commandments "I am the Lord your God" and "You shall have no other gods," which tradition says were heard directly from God</note>. For when they were heard from the mouth of the Blessed One, the placement of the souls within the Children of Israel was very great. They achieved a "seal" upon their health until the very end of their inner depths, including all the general secrets and mysteries within them in great subtlety. Furthermore, the study of the Torah was fixed in their hearts; they would learn and not forget, as the Sages said in the *Midrash Rabbah* on the verse **"they shall melt..."** <note>original: "ימק' וכו'"</note>.

The text says **"towers of perfumes"** to suggest that it was not only the souls that achieved this great portion and were inscribed with the very essence of the Law—the "scent" that the soul enjoys—but even the bodies achieved a great distinction. Their physical matter was refined and their spiritual "filth" <note>original: "זוהמתן", referring to the primordial impurity of the serpent in Eden</note> ceased, until there was no longer a dividing veil preventing the perception of subtle and hidden portions. These were made and inscribed upon their very limbs, even though their placement was more prominent in the bodies than in the souls, for every part received according to its preparation. 

And regarding the portion of the body, it says **"towers of perfumes,"** for "perfuming" refers to when one mixes spices for food or medicine. In this, there is a physical essence and taste, and it contains more than just a scent. That is to say: taste and scent were each at their own level, in the ultimate possible subtlety. 

The image of these "flowers" applied to the first two commandments. Now we return to the other commandments. The text says, **"His lips are lilies"** <note>Song of Songs 5:13</note>. This is like lilies that are closed after they are plucked. This suggests that although their scent is not as strong or powerful as when they are connected to the root, they still possess a great and significant fragrance. So too were the other commandments; although their perception was not as subtle or total as the first two—since they were delivered through the Prophet <note>Moses</note>—nevertheless, Israel achieved a great portion through them, as we have written in the name of the *Zohar*, likened to the scent of lilies which is very "covered" <note>hidden or subtle</note>.

It also says, **"dropping liquid myrrh,"** <note>original: "מור עובר" - literally 'passing myrrh'</note> meaning like drops from a source of precious scent, even though it is "passing" or temporary. This is like what is said in the Midrash: when they came to receive them, they said to Moses, **"You speak with us, and we will hear..."** <note>Exodus 20:16</note>. At that point, they returned to a state of learning and forgetting. Just as a staff of flesh and blood <note>a human being</note> passes away, so too his teaching passes away <note>The "passing myrrh" symbolizes the transition from eternal, divine memory to human, forgetful learning</note>.

Alternatively, **"His cheeks are like a bed of spices"** means the first commandments were like a "bed of spices"—a courtyard within a garden that is specifically designated and unique among all the beds of the garden, being so fragrant that its scent wafts afar. So were these two commandments unique among the rest, being spoken from the mouth of the Blessed One. 

They were also **"towers of perfumes."** As it says in the Midrash: Just as this tower of perfume contains all types of spices, so too they perceived within these commandments all the parts, details, and sub-details in a complete and clear perception—both the literal meaning <term>Peshat: the plain, surface level of text</term> and the secret meaning <term>Sod: the esoteric or mystical level of text</term>, the revealed and the most hidden of the hidden, from the source of Knowledge and Understanding. 

Included in this is that a multitude of perfumes and fragrant layers completely remove a bad odor and distance "filth" to a great degree. So too, the first commandments removed the Evil Inclination <note>original: "יצהר" (error for יצר)</note>, distanced it, and uprooted it from Israel. As it says in the Midrash: At the moment Israel heard "You shall have no other [gods]," the Evil Inclination was uprooted from their hearts.

**"His lips are lilies"** refers to the other commandments. Their scent also wafted like lilies, but they were not at the level of the "bed of spices," for the perception of subtlety within them was only like a scent which is...

# Commentary

Generally, as mentioned above, there was no voice so whispered as this one in its clarity, etc. This is compared to **"washed in milk,"** for milk beautifies the eyes and improves their sight, as the commentators of blessed memory have written. It says **"fitly set"** <note>original: "יושבות על מלאת" (yoshvot al milat); literally "sitting upon fullness"</note> to teach that these Commandments—and indeed the entire book of the Torah—sit upon the fullness of the Torah in perfection, without any lack at all, for everything is included within them. 

This is a powerful explanation of what was said above regarding the "streams of water," for no contradiction was heard from them regarding the Word. Everything informs us that the Torah is a treasure from beginning to end; it is all-inclusive, and not a single thing is missing from it. As it is written in the *Zohar* in the aforementioned portion: "Rabbi Shimon said: Every single word was filled with all those tastes <note>meaning levels of interpretation</note>, with all those hidden matters and occupations, secrets and mysteries, like a vessel that is overflowing and full of everything, etc."

Furthermore, <unclear>it says</unclear>—and it is possible to explain it this way—that "His eyes" refers to His Commandments. Just as a wall <note>the Hebrew text here uses a metaphor comparing the return of the soul to a wall and the ignorance of the nations to doves</note> brought back to the wall of folly like the likeness of the doves, who brought a chirping to the world. Specifically, they did not only look toward the surface in their anointing, but generally looked toward the world to guide it toward the attainment of spiritual perfection. Rather, even for their own sake, a great light was revealed to them, for He revealed to them according to their capacities. 

This occurred as explained in the *Zohar* in the aforementioned portion: "At the hour they received the revealed and the hidden, to speak of upper and lower worlds, their souls [the Israelites] flew out and departed. That Wisdom, that Word, was flying from above to below, engraved by the four winds of the world. It ascended and was found on the **Mountain of Pure Balsam** <note>original: "טורא דאפרסמונא" (Tura d'Afarsamona); a Kabbalistic reference to the source of divine overflow</note>, and it was found in that **Heavenly Dew** <note>original: "טלא דלעילא" (Tala d'Le'eila); the dew of resurrection</note>. It was then spoken into the nostrils of Israel, and their souls returned to them. It was then engraved upon the tablets, and so it was for every word." End of quote. 

If so, the phrase **"washed in milk"** refers to the washing of the Commandments and their strength from the "Mountain of Pure Balsam" and the "Heavenly Dew," which are the ultimate in subtlety and whiteness, resembling milk. Regarding their washing and their engraving upon the tablets, it says **"fitly set,"** for after their washing in "milk" to return the souls, they sat in their place in their rest upon the Tablets of Wisdom. 

According to the first path, the verse says:

### His cheeks are like a bed of spices,
### Towers of perfumes; 
### His lips are like lilies, 
### Dropping liquid myrrh.

The previous verse spoke of the power of the splitting of the Red Sea; here, the verse speaks of the matter of the Standing at Mount Sinai. These matters are easily merited because the "cheeks" and the "lips" are the instruments of speech. The cheeks represent the mouth as a whole, and the lips are well known. 

The intention is that the Commandments were divided into two parts: 
1. **Part One:** The first two commandments, which "we heard from the mouth of the Almighty" <note>The first two of the Ten Commandments were heard directly from God, while the rest were mediated by Moses</note. 
2. **Part Two:** The rest of the commandments, which were spoken by the "Mediator," who is **Moses our Teacher** <note>original: "מרע"ה" (Mera"h) - abbreviation for Moshe Rabbeinu, Alav HaShalom</note>, as it says, "Go near and hear..." 

He attributed the hidden part <note>the direct revelation</note> to the **cheeks** and the revealed part to the **lips**. This is because the cheeks have a greater measure and volume than the lips; likewise, the power of those [first two] commandments was greater, having been spoken from the mouth of the Almighty, and they [the Israelites] could not endure them as they did the other commandments. 

Furthermore, the lips indicate more revelation, as they are external and further from the core and source of speech. Similarly, the other commandments spoken by the Mediator were not heard from His mouth (blessed be He) in the same way as the first two commandments. There is another comparison: the lips "cut" the speech <note>articulate the sounds</note>, for as long as the speech is within the cheeks, it is not heard. One who hears it in silence... they were symbols of the hidden. Likewise, the first two commandments, due to their great hiddenness...

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the allegorical commentary on Song of Songs chapter 5. Having previously discussed the "eyes" as the clarity of the Torah, the author now transitions to verse 5:13 ("His cheeks are like a bed of spices") and 5:14 ("His hands are rods of gold"), interpreting these as symbols of the Tabernacle, the sacrificial system, and the balance between Divine Justice and Mercy.</meta>

And regarding the benefit mentioned above: for within it there was a mixture below, and there, in the giving of the Torah, doubts were resolved—meaning His utterances that were spoken in the **Assembly of the Congregation** <note>original: "קהל מועד" (Kahal Moed); referring to the gathered Israelites at Sinai or the Tent of Meeting</note> are like the likeness of **roses**. Since the *Zohar* (Portion *Shemot*) says this is understood to be red and radiant, therefore, etc. And so it is in the Torah: it contains both **Justice** and **Mercy** <note>original: "דין ורחמים" (Din ve-Rachamim)</note>—Justice for those who transgress it, and Mercy for those who fulfill it. The likeness of Justice is a necessity in the Assembly of the Congregation. As we said previously regarding the Midrash *Leviticus Rabbah*, even though the Torah was given, Israel had to be surrounded by it until it was given in the Assembly of the Congregation. If so, the likeness of roses is even more necessary here than at the Standing at Mount Sinai.

It says **"dripping with liquid myrrh"** <note>original: "נוטפות מור עובר" (notfot mor over)</note> regarding the intention mentioned in the previous explanation: for the Torah was a power fit to subdue the **Evil Inclination** <note>original: "יצר" (Yetzer); the internal impulse toward wrongdoing</note> and to remove it, as our Sages of blessed memory said: "If this repulsive one <note>the Evil Inclination</note> meets you, drag him to the House of Study."

It is possible to explain "His cheeks are like a bed of spices" in this way: as referring to the **Sacrifices** <note>original: "קרבנות" (Korbanot)</note> that they would offer in the Tabernacle, about which it is said, "My offering, My bread, etc." And they also said in the Midrash on the verse "I have compared you, my love": Rabbi Yonatan said, "From my decorations—for they knew Me through the two daily offerings every day, etc." It says **"like a bed of spices"** regarding the fragrant scent; this is the scent of the sacrifices that ascends on high, "a pleasing aroma to the Lord." It says **"bowers of perfumes"** <note>original: "מגדלות מרקחים" (migdalot merkachim)</note> regarding the many types of sacrifices that were there, including the scent of the **Incense**, about which it is written, "the work of a perfumer, a blended ointment, etc."

It says **"His lips are roses,"** meaning His utterances speaking of the matter of the sacrifices are roses—meaning like the likeness of roses. This is the likeness mentioned in the *Zohar* (Portion *Pinhas*): it is understood that this <note>the rose</note> has a scent and is red; when they refine it, it turns to white, but its scent always remains with it. So too, the Holy One, blessed be He, conducts His world in this way, for were it not so, the world would not endure. A sinner is called "red," as it is said, "Though your sins be like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow, etc." Thus we find the vital intent of the beauty of the sacrifices is to atone for iniquity and to whiten the redness of sins, so that His Mercy might suppress the anger caused by sins. Its entire existence is Mercy, for according to the simple line of Justice, iniquity would not be atoned for by a sacrifice.

Regarding the firstborn sacrifices, it says **"dripping with liquid myrrh,"** from the decree of the Holy One, blessed be He: for the daily offerings that Israel offered every morning were offered for the transgressions committed at night, and those of the afternoon were offered for the transgressions committed during the day. Thus, no man ever slept in Jerusalem with a sin in his hand, as our Sages of blessed memory said in many places, including the *Midrash Rabbah* on the verse "I have compared you, my love." For this likeness, it says **"dripping with liquid myrrh,"** for the bitterness of the myrrh <note>The Hebrew word for "myrrh" (Mor) is linguistically related to "bitter" (Mar)</note> removes the foulness, as we said above.

## HIS HANDS ARE RODS OF GOLD SET WITH BERYL; 
## HIS BELLY IS A POLISHED IVORY OVERLAID WITH SAPPHIRES.

->According to the first path from the<-
->previous verse, he spoke of the Standing at<-
->Mount Sinai; here in this verse, he speaks of the matter of the<-
->Tabernacle and its benefits and the goodness drawn<-

to Israel from it. The intention is that its locations should be like a circle and a wide-reaching tradition. It is known that in the Tabernacle there were places one inside the other, one more sacred than the other. Regarding all of them generally, he says **"His hands are rods of gold,"** meaning they are like handfuls and wheels that are intertwined in a machine. Thus the Tabernacle was like a wheel, turning the upper to the lower and the lower to the upper, returning in a constant cycle. The intention is that from there, the service of the lower beings would ascend on high, and the influence of the Name, blessed be He, would descend.

It begins to explain the "hands of gold" as the mechanical and spiritual intersection between the earthly Tabernacle and the divine realms.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the allegorical commentary on Song of Songs chapter 5. The previous page explored the "eyes" as symbols of the Torah's clarity. This page transitions to verse 5:13 ("His cheeks are like a bed of spices") and its relationship to the Tabernacle (Mishkan) and the dual nature of Divine abundance.</meta>

-># COMMENTARY<-

Regarding the matter of **"His hands were dripping with liquid myrrh"** <note>original: "ידיו היו נוטפות מור עובר"; a variation or continuation of the commentary on Song of Songs 5:5/5:13</note>, of blessed memory, this is like the likeness of myrrh which removes decay from the seed. Upon their reception [of the Torah], their filth <note>the spiritual impurity brought into the world by the primordial serpent</note> ceased. With these [commandments], it was like the level of the first Commandments given by our Master below. Another interpretation: "He was with us," etc. The word "He gathers" <note>referring to a specific Midrashic derivation</note> returns to its place as stated in the **Pesikta** <note>a collection of homiletic teachings on the Torah and festivals</note> regarding the filth of the first serpent; this is the secret of "dripping liquid myrrh."

Behold, all that I have written follows the path of **Rabbi Joshua ben Levi**, who holds the opinion that Israel heard only two commandments directly from the mouth of the Holy One, Blessed be He: **"I am [the Lord your God]"** and **"You shall have no [other gods before Me]"**. This is the consensus view. However, even according to my masters who hold the opinion that Israel heard *all* the Commandments from the mouth of the Holy One, Blessed be He—as found in the **Midrash** <note>traditional Jewish homiletic interpretation</note> on the verse **"Let him kiss me [with the kisses of his mouth]"** <note>Song of Songs 1:2</note>—it is possible to explain the verse that says **"His cheeks are like a bed of spices"** as referring to the Ten Commandments that they heard from His mouth, may He be blessed. 

Their "drippings" flow onto the Scroll of the Torah which Moses wrote as a "double" <note>a copy or repetition</note> at Sinai during the forty days and forty nights and in the **Assembly of the Congregation** <note>the gathered community at the Tabernacle</note>, and he spoke it to the children of Israel. This would follow the paths mentioned above. According to this path, the previous verse speaks of the Standing at Mount Sinai and the reception of the Torah. It is possible that this verse is explained similarly regarding the reception of the Torah and the additional explanation of the distinction between the first two Commandments and the remaining weightier commandments. 

However, it is more plausible to explain that it speaks of the construction of the **Tabernacle** <note><term>Mishkan</term>: the portable sanctuary built by the Israelites in the wilderness</note> where Israel dwelt in the desert, in which the Holy One, Blessed be He, caused His **Divine Presence** <note><term>Shekhinah</term>: the localized, indwelling presence of God on earth</note> to rest. It is known that the Tabernacle provided two benefits to Israel:

1. It was a place prepared for the reception of **Abundance** <note><term>Shefa</term>: the flow of divine energy, blessing, or physical sustenance</note> and the goodness and food that descends from above through the Divine Presence dwelling within it, and from there it spreads to the whole world.
2. It was a place specifically designated and intended for Divine speech, utterances, and commands. Since the Tabernacle was established, Divine speech was no longer heard below except from within it, as it is written: **"And the Lord spoke to him from the Tent of Meeting, saying..."** <note>Leviticus 1:1</note>. Furthermore, the Sages said in the Midrash **Leviticus Rabbah** that Israel was not punished for the Torah—even though it was given to them at Sinai—until it was written for them in the Tent of Meeting.

Regarding the first benefit, it says **"His cheeks are like a bed of spices,"** because the cheeks encompass the mouth and surround the place where food is consumed. Therefore, he attributes His dwelling—in the context of the food and abundance within it—to the "cheeks." He says that the abundance and goodness flowing within it were exceedingly great in quality and quantity. 

Regarding **Quality**, he says **"like a bed of spices,"** which is a unique bed among the beds of the garden, possessing the greatest excellence and importance, whose scent wafts afar. 

Regarding **Quantity**, he says **"towers of perfumes"** <note>original: "מגדלות מרקחים" (migdalot merkachim)</note>, a term of many and various types, to include all kinds of food and abundance in the world, all of which are fragrant. 

It is also possible that he intended to refer to two parts of the abundance that flowed to Israel through the Tabernacle:
1. **Soulic and Spiritual Abundance:** This is the power of holiness and purity that spread throughout the camp because the Divine Presence dwelt within it, spreading its wings of holiness over them. Each individual would clothe themselves in it according to their preparation and level, so that they were prepared for the service of the Blessed Name and to cling to Him. He uses the likeness of a **"bed of spices"** for this, for its only purpose is a pleasant scent—a subtle matter from which **Wisdom** <note>or "the soul's intellect"</note> derives pleasure.
2. **Material and Physical Abundance:** This is the matter of food and goodness required for livelihood and the sustenance of the body. Regarding this, he says **"towers of perfumes,"** for perfumes are prepared for eating. This is a likeness to physical food, and also indicates its importance; for its essence was for Israel, and from it, only the "dregs and refuse" <note>the leftovers or spiritual "exhaust"</note> flowed to the other nations. Therefore, he compares it to perfumes which have both taste and a fragrant scent.

The author explains that the Tabernacle served as the central conduit for both spiritual holiness (wisdom) and physical sustenance for the nation of Israel.</summary>
