# SONG OF SONGS

For this reason, it is called a "carved work of ivory" <note>original: "חריץ זכת חלב"; likely referring to the "ivory" (*shen*) mentioned in Song of Songs 5:14</note>, and "filled with beryl" <note>original: "ממולאים בתרשיס"; a reference to the precious stones set in the High Priest's breastplate or the crystalline appearance of the heavens</note> upon the expanse. And their purity is like wine within it, for all the food, the produce, and the fruits growing within it <note>referring to the Land of Israel</note>, when they are nurtured from the source of holiness and purity, they grant and increase purity in those who consume them. Thus, they are found prepared for every holy matter and for the service of the Creator, may He be blessed, through the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments.

And they are **"filled with beryl"**—this refers to the totality of the Land as a whole, and to Jerusalem which is in the center. This is to say, it is like the "expanse of the Garden of Eden," for its holiness and purity are higher than the general holiness of the Land of Israel. It is **"covered and adorned with sapphires,"** which refers to the entirety of the Temple Mount and all the specific areas from there and upward, until the place of the **Inner Sanctum** <term>Kodesh Kodashim; the Holy of Holies, the innermost chamber of the Temple</term>. For all of these are levels of holiness, one above the other, just as our Sages of blessed memory taught us.

->**HIS LEGS ARE PILLARS OF MARBLE, SET UPON**<-
->**BASES OF FINE GOLD; HIS APPEARANCE IS LIKE LEBANON, EXCELLENT**<-
->**AS THE CEDARS.**<-

According to the first path—where the previous verse spoke of the Tabernacle—this verse speaks of Jerusalem and the Land. Behold, our Sages of blessed memory said in the **Midrash** <note>traditional homiletic commentary</note>: "**His legs**—this refers to the world," which was created in six days, as it is written: "For in six days the Lord made..." <note>Exodus 20:11</note>. The reason for this is based on the scripture: "And the earth is My footstool" <note>Isaiah 66:1</note>. Furthermore, just as the legs are the lower limbs in the body, so too this physical world is the lowest of all the worlds. For all [the worlds] are bound and grasped together like a single body, as is known to those who possess knowledge. 

However, according to our own path, we shall say that this specifically refers to the Land of Israel. For it is the essence of the world by virtue of its holiness and purity, and compared to it, all other lands are considered lowly. Just as "Israel" is the primary intent in the creation of humanity, so too the Land of Israel is the primary intent in the creation of the world. If not for the sake of the Land of Israel, the world would not have been created. Regarding it, the verse says, "He stands His legs" in the world, for there is the dwelling place of His glory, may He be blessed.

This is the meaning of **"His legs are pillars of marble"** <note>original: "שש" (shesh); which means both 'marble' and 'six'</note>. This means to say its pillars are of "six," meaning just as the number six represents the thickness and strength of a structure, for it is thick and powerful, and furthermore it is white and bright—so too is the Land powerful and strong in its produce, its fruits, and the abundance and sustenance that arises within it. Moreover, it is clear and pure in its holiness and purity, as has been said.

Or, it may mean "pillars of six" in that the Land acts like pillars that support the "six directions" <note>the dimensions of the physical world: north, south, east, west, up, and down</note> which comprise the entire world. For from the Land, sustenance and abundance are drawn to the whole world, as it is found in the **Book of the Zohar** <note>the foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note>, section 156, of blessed memory:

"The inhabited world is divided... the world is settled around a single point <note>the Foundation Stone in Jerusalem</note>... and what is that? It is the Holy Land. The Holy Land is the sustenance of the world, and within the sustenance of the Holy Land is Jerusalem, and within the sustenance of Jerusalem is the Holy of Holies. And all goodness and all food of all times always descends from above, and there is no place in all times that does not receive nourishment from there."

Thus, it is clear that from this unique place in the Land of Israel, the existence of the entire world is nurtured. Regarding this, the verse said: **"His legs are pillars of marble,"** as mentioned. 

And it says: **"set upon bases of fine gold."** The intention here is the letters of the Torah, regarding which it is written: "More to be desired are they than gold, even much fine gold" <note>Psalm 19:11</note>. For the letters of the Torah are the foundation of the world, because through the combinations of the letters, the world was created. As our Sages of blessed memory said: "**Bezalel** <note>the architect of the Tabernacle</note> knew how to combine the letters with which heaven and earth were created." 

And it says: **"His appearance is like Lebanon,"** referring to the prophecy, as the Sages said: "In the future, he will be recognized..."

<summary>This page interprets Song of Songs 5:14-15 as an allegory for the Land of Israel and Jerusalem's role as the spiritual and physical foundation of the world. It links the "pillars of marble" to the six days of creation and the "fine gold" to the Hebrew letters of the Torah used by Bezalel to build the world.</summary>

# COMMENTARY

The path of transition for the upper spiritual worlds to the lower realm follows a constant cycle, returning and surrounding. This is the meaning of the phrase **"rings of gold"** <note>original: "גלילי זהב" (gelilei zahav); Song of Songs 5:14</note>.

As for the mention of **"gold,"** this refers to the great importance of the **Tabernacle** <term>Mishkan; the portable sanctuary used by the Israelites in the desert</term> and its components. Gold is the most precious of metals, and indeed, the entire structure was overlaid with gold, as it is written: "And you shall overlay the boards with gold" <note>Exodus 26:29</note>. It also says, **"filled with beryl,"** <note>original: "תרשיס" (tarshish)</note> because beryl is a fine and precious gemstone, considered even more excellent and significant than gold.

Similarly, although the Tabernacle was constructed from seemingly physical materials, its holiness was of the highest order because its interior was filled with a profound spirituality and refinement. This was the intention of the wise craftsmen who performed the work of the Tabernacle: to draw down the holy emanations of the Celestial Tabernacle to the earthly Tabernacle, which served as its physical counterpart. The entire spiritual host of the heavens descended and rested within the lower Tabernacle. Thus, it became a fitting place for the **Divine Presence** <term>Shekhinah; the indwelling presence of God in the world</term> to dwell among those below. This is why it is described as **"filled with beryl,"** providing a beautiful reason and understanding for why the Tabernacle is likened to **"rings of gold"** according to what I have written.

As for the phrase **"His body is a block of ivory,"** <note>original: "מעיו עשת שן" (me'av eshet shen); Song of Songs 5:14. Literally "His innards/belly are a block of ivory."</term> this refers to the most unique place in the Tabernacle: the area behind the curtain, the **Holy of Holies** <term>Kodesh Kodashim; the innermost chamber where the Ark of the Covenant was kept</term>. This is an internal and hidden place, much like the internal organs (*me'ayim*) within a body. It is compared to a **"block of ivory"** because ivory is white, powerful, and strong. Just as the nature of an elephant’s tusk is known to be strong and mighty, so too does this place relate to the ultimate "whiteness" of pure Mercy. For there, the Divine Presence dwells specifically between the poles of the Ark. Through the enduring victory of the Tablets of the Covenant placed in the Ark, the Torah remains constant, sustaining the Divine Presence among the lower beings. This was the original intention from the beginning. 

It is as our Sages of blessed memory explained the verse "Take for Me an offering" in **Midrash Rabbah** <term>An ancient collection of homiletic teachings</term> with a parable: A king had an only daughter, and he told her husband, "Build a small chamber for me so that I may dwell with you." <note>This refers to God giving the Torah (the "daughter") to Israel and asking for a Tabernacle so He could stay near them.</note> It is also possible that the Holy of Holies is called a **"block of ivory"** because the service performed there once a year on the **Day of Atonement** <term>Yom Kippur</term> was the primary source of atonement and purification for sinners, both generally and individually. Thus, this part of the sanctuary is associated with whiteness, as it is written: "Though your sins be like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow" <note>Isaiah 1:18</note>. 

Furthermore, after the obstacle of sin is removed and forgiven, the path is cleared for the descent of abundance and great goodness to the House of Israel, specifically through the incense offered on the inner altar. This is a service of unsurpassed heights, for through it, all the upper and lower worlds are bound and united, and they are renewed with a great celestial flow. 

The phrase **"adorned with sapphires"** refers to the two golden **Cherubim** <term>Keruvim; angelic figures atop the Ark</term>. Thus, all these descriptions relate to the Holy of Holies—the "block of ivory" referring to the essence and strength of the place itself, while the specific objects within it, which represent the importance and cause of the site, are all **"adorned with sapphires."** Sapphires are pure, radiant, and clear, in addition to being an incredibly strong and firm substance. As the Midrash says: "The anvil was struck and the hammer split, but the sapphire remained whole in its place." <note>A traditional teaching about the indestructible nature of the sapphire from which the Tablets of the Law were carved.</note> So too were the important things kept there—the Tablets of the Covenant, the two golden Cherubim, and the inner altar—for their merit and clarity are undoubtedly very great. They are powerful and strong things that maintain and uphold the welfare and benefit of Israel. 

If one chooses to apply this phrase to the Tablets of the Covenant alone, it would also be correct, for they were hewn from a quarry of **precious sapphire** <note>original: "סנפלינון" (sanpelynon); a Greek-derived Hebrew term for a type of sapphire or high-quality gem.</note>. 

According to this second path of interpretation—whereas the previous section spoke of the Tabernacle—the explanation of this verse now shifts to the inheritance and possession of the **Land of Israel**. Generally speaking, all the places within the Land of Israel were like **"rings of gold"** due to the immense abundance and the great goodness found within its nature and its unique spiritual quality.

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs 5:14, linking its imagery to the Tabernacle, the Holy of Holies, and the spiritual power of the Torah. The author explains how physical gold and gemstones represent the spiritual conduits between the celestial and earthly realms.</summary>

# SONG OF SONGS

Since all the "tastes," "times," and "clinging powers" <note>This refers to Kabbalistic concepts of spiritual timing and the attachment of souls to the divine</note> mentioned are reflected in one another, the beauty of the verses describes the reception of goodness—both in general and in detail—within every generation.

As it has been explained in the **Zohar** <term>The central text of Jewish mysticism, often called "The Book of Radiance"</term> in many places, and specifically in the section on the portion of *Lech Lecha* regarding Haran <note>Genesis 12</note>, the matter of **sacrifices** <term>Korbanot; offerings brought to the Temple</term> acts as a cause to nullify harsh **judgments** <term>Dinim; the attribute of divine justice or rigor</term> and the power of the **husks** <term>Kelipot; "shells" or "shards" that represent forces of impurity or evil in Kabbalah</term>. The sacrifices draw down divine "will" into both the upper and lower worlds and bestow blessings upon all existence.

It is possible to explain this verse in the context of the **Holy Temple** <term>Beit HaMikdash</term>. The sacrifices, which are the "high matters" offered within it, are the reason for "sweetening" and establishing the judgments, drawing down the "sweetness" of sustenance to the lower world, as well as abundant goodness and flow to all worlds. This is the meaning of the phrase **"His palate is most sweet"** <note>Song of Songs 5:16; original Hebrew: "חכו ממתקים" (hikko mamttakim)</note>. This means that the offerings "tasted" by His "palate" (speaking metaphorically of the Divine) are the source of sweetness for the worlds, bringing benefit and distancing the harm of the *kelipot* by sweetening and stabilizing them. In this way, all of God’s actions in the world are performed through the attribute of **Kindness** <term>Hesed</term> rather than through the attribute of Judgment.

This is why the verse continues: **"He is altogether lovely; this is my beloved and this is my friend."** This follows the interpretation found in the Zohar (portion *Vayikra*) on the verse "Eat, friends; drink, and be drunk, beloveds," <note>Song of Songs 5:1</note> regarding the sacrifices. Those who have access to the **True Wisdom** <term>Hokhmat HaEmet; a traditional term for the study of Kabbalah</term> will find this interpretation to be entirely correct.

Now, we can look at this through the first method mentioned earlier: that the previous verse discussed the inheritance of the Land of Israel, and this verse refers to the Holy Temple in general. However, according to the second method—where the previous verse already referred to the Temple—we explain this verse as referring to the **Holy of Holies** <term>Kodesh HaKodashim; the innermost chamber of the Temple</term>. The service performed within it, even though it happened only once a year <note>on Yom Kippur, the Day of Atonement</note>, was of immense value and great benefit. Performed by the **High Priest** <term>Kohen Gadol</term>, the benefits drawn from it—in terms of distancing harm and bringing near the blessings mentioned above—were of a higher degree than the service performed throughout the year in the outer Temple. This is due to the sanctity of the place, the status of the one offering it, and the nature of the service itself. There is no need to elaborate further on this, as the matters are clear.

One can also offer a different detailed explanation for this entire sequence of verses—from verse 10 <note>Song of Songs 5:10</note> until this one. This approach focuses on the kindness God has shown to the nation of Israel and His wonders in the creation of man. God endeavored to act with great kindness, leaning man toward the path of life, divine service, and the pursuit of spiritual perfection.

Furthermore, God created "supports" to maintain this intention. Because "the inclination of man's heart is evil from his youth" <note>Genesis 8:21</note>, it was necessary to establish supports and pillars, which are the **Torah** and the **Temple Service**, as a "chosen material." We have already explained this discourse at length in the introduction to our commentary on the **Book of Ecclesiastes** <term>Kohelet</term>. Today, we return to it briefly.

It is explained in the books of the ancient sages, and particularly in the **Book of the Reed** <note>original: "ספר קנה" (Sefer HaKanah); a 14th-century work of Kabbalistic ethics and secrets</note> and the Zohar, that the human body—in its entirety and all its parts—is a mirror image of all the upper spiritual worlds. The "vessels" found there correspond to the limbs and worlds of the upper realm where holiness, purity, and spirituality reside. Through this secret, man can perceive the whole. This applies not only to the general structure but also to the individual parts of the body, such as the bones, sinews, flesh, and skin. As has been explained...

...and so on, to indicate the great level of **prophecy** <term>Nevuah; the state of receiving divine communication</term> and its perfection over all other types of wisdom. He returns to this idea by saying it is like the image of **Mount Lebanon**, which is distinguished and high above all other mountains. Just as Lebanon contains many types of large and tall trees, there are many levels within prophecy. As it is said in the **Zohar** <term>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</term>, there are ten levels of prophecy. According to what was written regarding the specific parts of Lebanon, they are mentioned by the name of Lebanon—such as **Hermon**, as it is written: "Hermon and Lebanon." Perhaps the ten types of "Hermon" refer to the "breasts of beauty," <note>A metaphorical reference to spiritual nourishment</note> corresponding to which there are ten levels of prophecy. Like the "donkey and the wine" <note>original: "כחמור וחמר" (ke-hamor ve-hemar). A wordplay on matter (homer), donkey (hamor), and wine (hemar) often used in philosophical texts to discuss the relationship between physical matter and spiritual form.</note> in the physical matter, just as Hermon sits upon the **Crown of Kingship** <term>Keter Malchut; the highest and lowest spheres of the divine emanations, here linking the peaks of spiritual experience to earthly rule</term>, He returned the inheritance to Israel. For He established the Kingdom for them at that time, as it is written regarding **Saul, the chosen of the Lord**. Although He distanced him for a time, He returned and chose him. To show that the Kingdom in Israel is a matter of distinguished importance—powerful, strong, and existing forever—even in a time when there is an interruption, it is written: "The scepter shall not depart from Judah." <note>Genesis 49:10</note> In the future, the "crown will return to its former glory" without a doubt. To illustrate all of this, he said "Like Hermon," for they are unique in height, power, and strength like a "donkey" <note>A metaphor for physical strength/substance</note>. According to the second path of the previous verse in the Jerusalem Talmud, "merchant," this verse refers to the Holy Temple; it is more straightforward. Stand upon this same previous path.

->Behold, after mentioning the reception of goodness:<-

## HIS PALATE IS MOST SWEET, AND HE IS ALTOGETHER LOVELY.
## THIS IS MY BELOVED, AND THIS IS MY FRIEND, O DAUGHTERS OF JERUSALEM.

He returns to the word "His palate" until the end of the verse to hint at the Land, as explained according to the first path. The memory of the Land was the beginning of God's work. As was explained, he returns generally to show His goodness and the strength of the Beloved’s love for her <note>The community of Israel</note> all the days she remains on the **Holy Height** <term>The Temple Mount</term>. First, she said, **"His palate is most sweet"** regarding the words of the Blessed One in every single era and every generation, which He spoke to her through His servants the **Prophets**—words that are refined, sweet, and precious, preserved and sweeter than honey and the drippings of the honeycomb. These words guide her, straighten her path, and bring her to His prophecy and the state of perfection and true **clinging** <term>Deveikut; a state of meditative attachment to the Divine</term>. 

Not only this, but all His actions toward her are acts of kindness, involving life and wisdom every single day—goodness, miracles, and wonders, both in general and in specific detail, both revealed and hidden. For His kindness toward her is eternal; this is the meaning of **"and He is altogether lovely."** He was precise in saying "altogether lovely" to include all the actions of the Blessed One which He performed for her—whether through **Mercy** or through **Judgment** <term>Din; the attribute of divine justice/rigor</term>—they are all "lovely." For the actions of Judgment were not for harm; they were for the good, to atone for iniquity, to awaken repentance, to perfect the soul, and to repair the distortions within souls. 

Truly, for the generation of His kindness, He has the grace to perform wonders for the **Daughters of Jerusalem** <note>Metaphor for the souls of Israel or the nations watching the relationship between God and Israel</note> so they wonder and say, "What is your beloved more than another beloved?" <note>Song of Songs 5:9</note> For justice dictates that she should long for and hope for clinging to Him. At the start, He returned to His love, even though it had already belonged to Him. Therefore: **"This is my beloved, and this is my friend, O daughters of Jerusalem."** 

Regarding the essence of **"This is my beloved and this is my friend,"** what they said in the Zohar (Portion of Vayikra) is well known in the explanation of the verse: "We have become friends... and they became beloveds." <note>original Aramaic: "חינון דתחבין דא לרא ולא משתכחין תדיר... וחיכון דמשתכחין תדיר ולא אתכסיין ולא מתפרדן..."</note> The explanation is: "Beloveds" are those who love one another but are not always found together <note>Representing a fluctuating spiritual state</note>, while "Friends" are those who are found together always and are never hidden or separated. Because there is a time in the **Assembly of Israel** <term>Knesset Yisrael; the spiritual archetype of the Jewish people</term> of essential clinging without any separation, covering, or "hiding of the face" at all, and also a time of weakened clinging and a slight hiding of the face, she therefore said: **"This is my beloved and this is my friend,"** to say...

# Song of Songs

This means to say that "his locks" <note>Song of Songs 5:11: "His locks are wavy and black as a raven."</note> and his parts are "wavy," meaning they are high, hanging heights and upper worlds. As was said regarding the "canopy," they are simply "black as a raven." According to the **Midrash** <term>A genre of rabbinic literature that interprets biblical texts through homily and legal analysis</term> of our Sages, of blessed memory, following their path: these refer to the traditions of the **Torah** <term>The Five Books of Moses; specifically here referring to the Oral Tradition</term>, which are praised when they are "black"—meaning "blackened" by the intensity of study or "black" like the darkness of war in public debate. The Holy One, blessed be He, said: "They are sweet to Me," etc. Thus, the physical matter is interpreted similarly: even though the limbs of the body and its parts are material, physical, and seemingly lowly or "ugly," they are sweet to Me because they are a mirror-image of all the upper worlds, as mentioned. 

Regarding the soul—which is entirely remembered through the merit of the **Vital Soul, Spirit, and Higher Soul** <term>Nefesh, Ruach, and Neshamah; the three-tiered structure of the soul in Jewish mysticism</term>—it is said: "His eyes are like doves by the brooks of water, washed with milk" <note>Song of Songs 5:12</note>. For "his eyes" are the image of the eyes from which light emerges to illuminate man; so too do they illuminate the body. All of its accompaniment—which are the powers of the soul—are "like doves by the brooks of water." As our Sages, of blessed memory, said in the Midrash: "Just as the dove brought light to the world," etc., so too these two powers bring light to the body, as we have explained. 

And he said "by the brooks of water" because the responding eye always overflows from the waters of the upper influence. For the three powers of the soul—the <unclear>NRN"S</unclear> <note>Likely an acronym for Nefesh, Ruach, Neshamah, and Sekhel (Vital Soul, Spirit, Higher Soul, and Intellect)</note>—are bound and clarified one within the other: the Vital Soul in the Spirit, the Spirit in the Higher Soul, and the Higher Soul in the Holy One, blessed be He. As it is written: "The soul of my lord shall be bound in the bundle of life with the Lord your God" <note>1 Samuel 25:29</note>. 

He then said "washed in milk" because they are renewed in the upper nature, which is associated with the "whiteness of reward," even while they are in the lower world within the human body. They "sit upon the fullness," meaning they dwell in the body upon the "fullness" and perfection of the body, to illuminate it, to fill its lack, and to complete its perfection as mentioned. 

And regarding the pure, holy, and spiritual place where He placed man in order to guard and maintain his purity: this is his nature, of which it is said, "His cheeks are as a bed of spices" <note>Song of Songs 5:13</note>, which flows with delights and pleasures found there. Even though these are spiritual things and "eating" or "sustenance" do not apply to them, nor do "eating vessels" or "taste," nevertheless, "The Torah speaks in the language of human beings" <note>original: "דברה תורה כלשון בני אדם" (Dibra Torah ki-leshon bnei adam). A fundamental principle in Jewish philosophy, popularized by Maimonides, explaining that the Bible uses physical metaphors to describe God so that humans can understand</note>, as our Sages, of blessed memory, said. 

Additionally, there is a power that flows from the spiritual into the physical, and therefore he attributed to them the "vessels of sustenance" and food. He said, "His cheeks are as a bed of spices," meaning the arrangements, tastes, and types found there. He attributed them to the "cheeks" which are around the vessels of sustenance and food, as mentioned. He said "as a bed of spices" regarding the quality of the arrangements and inventions found there, and "towers of perfumes" <note>Song of Songs 5:13</note> regarding the quantity of the types of arrangements found there, according to the way we explained above in the first method.

He said "his lips are lilies" regarding the teachers of the Torah who were occupied there with things that are very sweet. Through the book that his master <unclear>R.Y.H.L.</unclear> clarified for him there, they were undoubtedly "dropping liquid myrrh" <note>Song of Songs 5:13</note>. This means that just as myrrh removes foulness, so too did he remove the filth and the destruction of the **Evil Inclination** <term>Yetzer HaRa; the innate human impulse toward selfishness or physical gratification</term> and material matter, due to the preparation of the place and the occupation within it. 

Furthermore, included in this is the abundance of perfumes and fragrant things that completely remove a bad odor. Similarly, the "evaporation of the arrangements" there would refine the person and the matter from which his body is built. 

Regarding the Torah, she said: "His hands are rods of gold" <note>Song of Songs 5:14</note>. He called the Commandments "hands," based on the phrase "The hand of the Lord was upon me" <note>Ezekiel 37:1</note>, for they were all spoken from the mouth of the **Divine Might** <term>Gevurah; a name for God emphasizing His power and judgment</term>. Some of them Israel heard directly, and some were said to Moses, who acted as the intermediary between Israel and the Holy One, blessed be He. He called them "rods of gold" because they would return Israel to a better state in their end, as mentioned above. 

He said "filled with Tarshish" because the Commandments are filled with all the secrets of the laws of the Torah and the **Five Books of Moses** <term>Chumashim</term>. As it is said in the **Zohar**: "Every single word was filled with all those flavors, all those decrees, companions, and branches," etc., as mentioned above.

# Commentary

As it is explained in the section of the **Zohar** <term>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism, traditionally attributed to Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai</term> on the portion of *Yitro* <note>The Torah portion containing the Ten Commandments (Exodus 18:1–20:23)</note>, of blessed memory: "The inner vessel <note>original Aramaic: *cheres*—literally 'clay' or 'potsherd,' here referring to the internal spiritual form</note> is within its own garments. They were made according to the supernal pattern, and they are hidden according to the pattern we mentioned regarding the chariots and the ladies-in-waiting. The 'inner vessel' is folded over those ladies-in-waiting and chariots, standing outside. And this is the spirit found on the 'other side.' The skin that covers everything is like the heavens that cover everything. All of these are garments intended to prepare the inner vessel within—the spirit of the vessel. Everything below is according to the pattern above. Regarding this, it is said: 'He shall create another way in His image,' etc."

Thus, it is explained that all parts of man are a model of all the worlds, upper and lower—from the soul and its levels down to the visible firmament of the heavens. It is sufficient to say that for the man whom God has chosen, he is very precious in the eyes of God. His physical matter is inclined toward spirituality to perform the sacred service, the service of his Creator, may He be blessed. Besides this, his "dust" <note>Referring to the physical elements of the body</note> was already taken from a holy place—the place of his atonement—as our Sages of blessed memory said <note>A tradition that Adam was created from dust gathered from the site of the future Altar in Jerusalem</note>. Beyond this interpretation, this is also a compelling reason to incline him toward the "pure waters" and to transform matter into form.

Through all this, there is great preparation to make the "vessel" <note>the body</note> precious in its qualities and the nature of the body itself. Regarding this, "He breathed into his nostrils the breath of life" <note>Genesis 2:7</note>, which emanates from beneath the **Throne of Glory** <term>The highest realm of divine manifestation in Jewish mysticism</term>, to illuminate and straighten the "ropes" of the body and to make it shine "like the brightness of the firmament" <note>Daniel 12:3</note> through his devotion to God and the fulfillment of His sacred service, thereby becoming refined and attached to the Living God and the King of the Universe.

Furthermore, to fulfill this intention, He placed him in a very prepared location, as it is written: "And the Lord God built a garden in Eden from the east, and there He placed the man whom He had formed" <note>Genesis 2:8</note>. However, since man still required great assistance—given that he possesses an **Evil Inclination** <term>Yetzer HaRa; the innate human drive toward physical gratification or selfishness</term> from his youth, and is hindered by his natural tendencies, and because a spirit from the "other side" <note>the Sitra Achra or realm of impurity</note> passed before him and caused the touch of impurity—God established supports for him so he would not stumble from the straight path. The Compassionate One gave the paths of the **Torah**, which contains righteous statutes and judgments to teach man the way he should go and the deeds he should do.

As stated in the **Zohar**, portion of *Yitro* (page 82), of blessed memory: "In how many ways does the Torah testify to a man so that he will not sin before his Master! In how many ways does it give him counsel so he does not stray from his paths to the right or the left! In how many ways does it give him counsel on how to return to his Master so He may forgive him! For we have learned that death and thirteen types of counsel are given by the Torah to man so he may be reconciled with his Master," etc.

Behold, it is clear that the Torah is a teacher and guide for the "vessel" <note>the physical person</note> so he does not sin; and if he has sinned, how he may repair what he has twisted and how he may gather the perfections of the soul. Furthermore, the Torah is an "antidote" against the Evil Inclination, as our Sages of blessed memory said: "If this repulsive one <note>the Evil Inclination</note> meets you, drag him to the House of Study" <note>Talmud, Kiddushin 30b</note>. Even if one has sinned, God gave him the repair of the service in the **Holy Temple**; for through the sacrifices, he will be atoned for, and the spirit of impurity and the "shell" <note>Kelipah; the spiritual layer that hides divine light</note> will be removed from him, allowing him to return and cleave to the spirit of holiness and purity, as is known.

The **Assembly of Israel** <term>Knesset Yisrael; the personification of the Jewish people, often portrayed as the bride of God</term> mentions all of these things in praise of her Beloved, who has bestowed much goodness and kindness upon her to guide her toward spiritual perfection and essential devotion, to return to the light of the face of the Living King. The portion of these goods granted to her is better than all the goods of this world. How could she not boast in His praise?

Regarding this, she said first: **"His head is as the finest gold"** <note>Song of Songs 5:11</note>. This refers to the matter of the body and its dust, which came from a chosen and holy place—the place of his atonement, the site of the **Foundation Stone** <term>Even HaShetiyah; the rock in the Temple in Jerusalem from which the world was said to be created</term> from which the world was founded. "He desired it" <note>original: *ratzehu*</note> means to say "its beginning"—that is, the beginning of creation. She mentions that the matter of the body itself is like "finest gold" because of its great importance and level. Furthermore, **"his locks are wavy..."**

<meta>catchword: He desired</meta>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the mystical commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically interpreting the physical description of the "Beloved" as a symbolic map of the human soul and its connection to the Divine. It moves from discussing the hair and eyes to the cheeks, lips, and hands, linking each to the levels of the soul and the study of Torah.</meta>

# Song of Songs

This means that his stature, his groups, and his various parts are **curls**—meaning that they contain "mounds upon mounds" <note>A play on the Hebrew word *taltalim* (curls), which the Sages interpreted as *tillei tilim* (heaps of heaps) of laws and secrets</note> of very high visions and upper worlds, as it is said concerning those who desire them. They are **black as a raven**, which our Sages, of blessed memory, interpreted according to their way: these are the students of the Torah who make their appearance "black" <note>original: *she-shacharan shechorot*—a metaphor for exhausting oneself in study or living humbly</note> for the sake of their learning, yet they illuminate our work in public. The Holy One, Blessed be He, said: "They are pleasant to Me," and so on. This is what the physical matter says: even though the limbs of the body and its parts are material and lowly like clay, in His eyes they are pleasant to Me, for they are a model of all the upper worlds, as mentioned.

And regarding the vision that was entirely clear in the prophecies of the **Nefesh, Ruach, and Neshamah** <term>The three primary levels of the soul in Jewish mysticism: the vital/animal soul (Nefesh), the emotional/spirit (Ruach), and the intellectual/divine soul (Neshamah)</term>, she said: **His eyes are like doves by the brooks of water**. She called them "His eyes" because they are like eyes from which light emerges to illuminate a person; so too do these soul-levels illuminate the body. Our Sages of blessed memory said regarding their visions: the powers of the soul are like "doves by the brooks of water." As they said in the Midrash: just as the dove brought light to the world <note>A reference to the dove in the story of Noah's Ark</note>, so too these two powers bring light to the body, as we have explained. It says **by the brooks of water** because they are constantly being flowed into by the waters of the supernal influence. This is also the law of the "Place": they are vaulted and clarified within one another—the *Nefesh* within the *Ruach*, the *Ruach* within the *Neshamah*, and the *Neshamah* within the Holy One, Blessed be He, as it is written: "And the soul of my lord shall be bound in the bundle of life with the Lord your God" <note>1 Samuel 25:29</note>.

It further says they are **washed in milk**, meaning they are completely refined by the supernal nature which is designated for pure whiteness. Therefore, while they are in the lower world within the human body, they are **fitly set** <note>original: *yoshvot al milet*—literally "sitting upon fullness"</note>, meaning they dwell in the body to provide it with "fullness" and perfection, to illuminate its deficiencies and complete its wholeness, as stated.

And regarding the pure, holy, and spiritual place where He placed man to guard and maintain his purity—which is the heart—it says: **His cheeks are like a bed of spices**, which yields delights and pleasures found there. Even though these things are spiritual and do not involve physical eating or food, nor physical organs of eating and taste, the Torah speaks in the language of human beings, as our Sages of blessed memory said. Furthermore, the spiritual power flows even into the physical. Therefore, the organs of food and eating are attributed to them metaphorically. It says **his cheeks are like a bed of spices**, meaning the arrangements, tastes, and praises found there, and it attributes them to the "cheeks" which surround the organs of food and eating. It says **like a bed of spices** regarding the quality of the arrangements and the perceptions found there, and **towers of perfumes** <note>original: *migdalot merkachim*</note> regarding the quantity of the types of arrangements there, according to the way we explained above in the first method.

It then says: **His lips are lilies**, referring to the secrets of the Torah which he was occupied with, involving very hidden matters. This was through the book that **Rabbi Raziel** <note>The *Sefer Raziel HaMalakh* (Book of Raziel the Angel), a famous grimoire of Jewish mysticism</note> brought down to him. These lips, without a doubt, were **dripping liquid myrrh**—meaning, just as myrrh removes foulness and rot, so too did this occupation remove the filth and impurity of the **Evil Inclination** <term>Yetzer HaRa—the human drive toward selfishness or sin</term> and physical matter, depending on the preparation of the one receiving and engaging with it. Included in this is the abundance of perfumes and spiced things that completely remove a bad odor; so too the spread of these spiritual arrangements would refine the person and the matter from which his body is built.

And regarding the Torah, she said: **His hands are rods of gold**. She called the blessings of the "hands" by this term, following the expression "the hand of the Lord was upon me," for all of them were spoken by the mouth of the Almighty. Some of them Israel heard directly, and some were told to Moses, who was the intermediary between Israel and the Holy One, Blessed be He. She called them **rods of gold** because they surrounded Israel to restore their souls to them, as mentioned above. And it says they are **set with beryl** <note>original: *memula’im ba-tarshish*</note> because the Commandments are filled with every hidden law of the Torah and the Five Books of Moses. As it is said in the **Book of the Zohar**: "Every single word was filled with all those tastes [nuances], with all those decrees, companions, and branches," etc., as mentioned above.

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from a discussion on the soul's levels (Nefesh, Ruach, Neshamah) to a broader cosmological view of the human body as a reflection of the Divine. It uses passages from the Zohar to explain the creation of Man and the purpose of the Torah and the Temple in refining human nature, eventually returning to the exegesis of the "Beloved" in the Song of Songs.</meta>

# Commentary

It is explained in the **Book of Zohar** <note>The Zohar is the foundational work of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah), composed in a mix of Aramaic and Hebrew.</note>, in the portions of *Shemini* and *Yitro*, of blessed memory: "Man is the internal essence within his garments," <note>original: "Adam ihu penima'ah lego malbushin di leh"</note> following the version of the concept itself, and so on. As we have said, regarding those "chariots" <note>A reference to the *Merkabah* or Divine Chariot, symbolizing the structures of the celestial realms</note> and those who travel within them, before he is closed off at times, he is assisted by the chariots and stands outside. This refers to the **spirit** <term>Ruach: the middle level of the soul, associated with emotion and movement</term> found on the **"Other Side"** <term>Sitra Achra: the Kabbalistic term for the realm of evil or impurity that opposes holiness</term>. Furthermore, the one who relies on all of them is "Man," according to the version of the love of the heavens that covers everything; these are the garments. The internal will of Man is prepared within them, and all of this is to rectify according to the version of the concept. Regarding this, it is written: **"And God created man in His own image"** (Genesis 1:27). Behold, it is explained that all parts of man are a model of all the worlds, upper and lower, including all their components, up to the firmament of the heavens visible to us.

Indeed, there is no doubt that because he is "Man," he possesses two powers: one from his Divine aspect and one from his physical matter. The first is **spirituality**, to perform the holy service—the service of his Creator, may He be blessed. Aside from this, our "dust" <note>Referring to the physical body created from the earth</note> comes from a holy place, the place of his atonement, as our Sages of blessed memory said. <note>A Midrashic tradition states that Adam was created from dust gathered from the site of the Altar in Jerusalem, so that the earth itself would help him achieve atonement.</note> There is no doubt that this body is a necessary cause to incline him toward eternal life and to transform "matter into form." In all this, God desired to make man upright in the very mechanics and features of the body itself. Because of this, **"He breathed into his nostrils the breath of life"** (Genesis 2:7), which is emanated from beneath the **Throne of Glory** <note>In Jewish thought, the Throne of Glory is the highest point of creation, where the souls of the righteous originate</note> to illuminate and guide the limbs of the body. This light warns him to shine like the "radiance of the firmament" through his cleaving to the Blessed One and the fulfillment of His service—the holy service—to be refined and purified, and to cling to the Living One, the King of the Universe.

Furthermore, to fulfill His intent, He placed him in a prepared location, as it is written: **"The Lord God planted a garden in Eden to the east, and there He put the man whom He had formed"** (Genesis 2:8). However, because he was "Man," he still required great assistance, as "the inclination of man's heart is evil from his youth" and is blocked by seventy obstacles and more. Also, because the spirit of man passes over his windows <note>A metaphor for the senses or the fleeting nature of human thoughts</note> and causes contact until the end, He prepared supporters for him so that he would not stumble from the straight path. In His mercy, He gave the **Torah**, which contains righteous statutes and judgments to teach man the path he should walk and the deeds he should do. As it says in the Zohar, portion *Yitro*, section 82: "In how many ways does the Torah illuminate man, so that he may return before his Master? In how many ways does it give him counsel so that he does not stray from the paths to the right or the left? In how many ways does it give him counsel on how to return before his Master so He may forgive him?" For we have learned: "One hundred and twenty and ten and twenty <note>The text likely refers to a specific mystical count of the Torah's commandments or counsels</note> pure counsels does the Torah give to man to be uplifted in his existence," and so on. 

This clarifies that the Torah is the guide and director for man so that he does not sin; and if he has sinned, how he may repair what he has twisted and how he may acquire the perfection of the soul. Furthermore, it serves as an **antidote** <term>Tavlin: literally 'spice' or 'seasoning,' used by the Sages to describe the Torah as the only effective cure for the "bitterness" of the evil inclination</term> against the **Evil Inclination** <term>Yetzer HaRa: the innate human drive toward physical gratification or selfishness</term>, as our Sages said: "If this repulsive one meets you, drag him to the House of Study." <note>Babylonian Talmud, Kiddushin 30b</note> Additionally, He gave him the repair of the service in the **Holy Temple**, for through the sacrifices he would be atoned, and the spirit of impurity and the "shell" <note>Kelipah: a Kabbalistic term for the layers of impurity that conceal divine light</note> would depart from him, and he would merit to cling to the spirit of holiness and purity, as is known.

Behold, all of these things were mentioned in the **Song of Songs** with the beauty of its words—that He created them with great goodness and kindness to bring and guide one toward spiritual perfection and essential cleaving, to behold the light of the face of the King of Life. This state is far superior to all the goodness of life in this world. How could one not strive for this preparation?

Regarding this, the Merciful One said: **"His head is as the finest gold"** (Song of Songs 5:11). This refers to that body and its dust, which came from a chosen and holy place—the place of his atonement, from the site of the **Foundation Stone** <term>Even HaShetiyah: the rock in the Holy of Holies in the Temple from which the world was said to have been started</term> from which the world was founded. "His head" means his beginning—that is, the beginning of his creation. It mentions that the material of the body itself is comparable to "fine gold" because of its great importance and high level. Furthermore, **"his locks are wavy..."**

It identifies the Torah and the Temple service as essential tools given by God to overcome the Evil Inclination and achieve spiritual perfection.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the exegesis of the Song of Songs, moving from the philosophical nature of the human soul and its Divine reflection to the collective relationship between the Community of Israel and God. It specifically addresses how this bond persists even during the "exile" and "hiding of the Divine face" mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

# Song of Songs

...with the living and with the dead. This signifies that despite the apparent distance of her Beloved <note>The "Beloved" in this context refers to God, while the "Beloved girl" or "Bride" refers to the Assembly of Israel.</note>, it is not a total separation. Rather, it is a state of "presence" and a "hiding of the face" <term>Hester Panim: a theological concept where God’s providence is concealed, often during times of suffering</term> brought about by bad deeds; yet, she does not despair of Divine mercy. In the **Great Midrash** <note>The *Midrash Rabbah*, an ancient collection of homiletic interpretations of the Bible</note>, it is stated: "My Beloved" refers to the Holy One, blessed be He; "to His garden" refers to this world; "to the bed of spices" refers to Israel; "to pasture in the gardens" refers to the synagogues and houses of study; "and to gather lilies" refers to the act of taking the righteous away from Israel <note>A poetic way of describing the death of the righteous</note>. This concludes the quote.

You can see with your own eyes that the Midrashic explanation perfectly aligns with our path: even when Israel is in exile, the Holy One, blessed be He, rests His Divine Presence <term>Shekhinah: the feminine, indwelling aspect of God on earth</term> among Israel and finds delight in the synagogues and houses of study. And because of the abundance of His mercy, when evil deeds are rampant, His attribute of strict Justice strikes the righteous and removes them from the world in order to atone for the generation. For the death of the righteous brings atonement. This is the meaning of the verse: "And yet for all that, when they are in the land of their enemies, I will not cast them away, nor will I abhor them, to destroy them utterly" (Leviticus 26:44). Thus, God forbid that there should be total abandonment; there is only a slight "hiding of the face," but not a complete distancing.

It is also written in the **Book of Zohar** <note>The primary text of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah)</note> in the portion of *Shemot* (Exodus):
"He descended to this garden," which is the Assembly of Israel, because it contains the "bed of spices," comprised of all the spiritual sides and fragrances of the World to Come. When the Holy One, blessed be He, descends into this garden, all the souls of the righteous who delight there emit a fragrance. At that moment, "the fragrance of your breath is like apples" <note>Song of Songs 7:9</note>. These are the souls of the righteous belonging to the side of Isaac. All those souls of the righteous who were in this world, and all those souls destined to descend to this world—all of them stand in this garden. In this garden, they all exist in a renewed state, in the same form and image they had in this world, appearing to the wise.

You see that our words follow in the footsteps of the Sages, of blessed memory, and are close to the plain meaning <term>Peshat: the literal or direct meaning of the biblical text</term>, for we do not deal here with the deepest hidden mysteries.

->**I am my beloved’s and my beloved is mine; he browses**<-
->**among the lilies.**<-
(Song of Songs 6:3)

She further expands her words and strengthens
->them, for it is well known that even<-
now, in my exile, she fulfills the Torah and the commandments. She has not cast off the yoke. Many individuals within the Assembly of Israel even give their lives to "sanctify the Name" <term>Kiddush HaShem: martyrdom or acting in a way that brings honor to God</term> and risk themselves for the sake of the Torah and the commandments. They all suffer the yoke of exile and the harsh decrees of the wicked, yet they do not deviate from the religion to break the covenant of their God.

Regarding this, she says, **"I am my beloved's,"** meaning it is already public knowledge that I belong to my Beloved. From this, it is understood that **"my beloved is mine,"** for He has entered into my distress with me through His Divine power. Even if we possess "bundles of sins" and iniquities that place us under the yoke of the "Husks" <term>Kelipah: literally 'shells' or 'husks,' representing the forces of impurity that conceal holiness</term> in this bitter and hidden exile, He nevertheless **"browses among the lilies."** This means He walks among the righteous of the generation, for no generation is devoid of righteous and pious people—at least the "Thirty-Six Righteous" <note>The *Lamed Vav Tzadikim*: a tradition that the world is sustained by the merit of 36 anonymous righteous individuals</note> as our Sages, of blessed memory, taught. The fact that He continues to "pasture" or sustain us is in the merit of these righteous ones in the generation.

Alternatively, **"I am my beloved's"** may mean: How could one despair of mercy? God forbid. One must awaken in repentance <term>Teshuvah: the process of returning to God</term> so that "I" return to "my Beloved." Then, immediately and instantly, "my Beloved" will be mine, helping us to cling to Him, for He desires the repentance of the wicked. This stems from the merit of the patriarchs and the ancient, pure source from which we were carved. This is the meaning of **"He browses among the lilies."**

And in the **Book of Zohar**, portion of *Shemot*: "**My beloved is mine**"—He chose me, and I chose Him. **"He browses among the lilies."**

# Commentary

Above: He spoke of a similar matter regarding the **inner dimensions of the Torah** <note>Hebrew: *Pnimiyut HaTorah*, referring to the mystical or esoteric meanings of the scripture</note> and its secrets, which are radiant, clear, and strong like a "molten mirror" <note>A reference to Job 37:18, implying the Torah reflects Divine truth with powerful clarity</note>. This is what he meant by "encrusted with sapphires" <note>Song of Songs 5:14</note>, resembling crystal, as the **Maharash** <note>Likely Rabbi Solomon Alkabetz, a 16th-century kabbalist and poet</note>, of blessed memory, wrote. This also refers to the Holy Temple, whose "legs are pillars of marble" <note>Song of Songs 5:15</note>, following the path I wrote above. Furthermore, the phrase "His appearance is like Lebanon, choice as the cedars" refers to **Prophecy** and **Kingship**, both of which strengthen spiritual perfection and "clinging" <term>Dveikut: a state of meditative attachment to God</term>. For the prophets, through their words and rebukes, disciplined Israel, while the King, through the awe of his sovereignty, binds the threads of the nation together to acquire the treasure of their lineage—the beauty of God on earth, as is known. Thus, all the assurances of complete goodness are gathered here, as stated.

Behold, as the Daughters of Jerusalem <note>Often interpreted in midrashic literature as the gentile nations or the angelic hosts</note> observed the goodness of heart of the Assembly of Israel and the depth of her devotion, they asked: 

->**"Where has your beloved gone, O fairest among women?"**<-

Her intention was that even though she is in a bitter exile—
->**"Where has your beloved turned, that we may seek him with you?"**<-
—distanced, downtrodden, and tossed about in displacement, she has nevertheless not forgotten the covenant of her God. All the goodness and kindness she received from Him are engraved upon her heart. They say: "Where has your beloved gone, O fairest among women?" meaning, it is fitting that such a generation should wonder at His love, since you received so much good from Him in ancient times and witnessed ancient miracles. You were worthy of being called "the fairest among women," meaning the most choice of nations, because even now, while receiving evil and troubles, your grace compels you to remember your Beloved's goodness. You do not kick against Him—God forbid—turning your back or straying from the yoke of His Torah and His commandments. Therefore, they asked, "Where has your beloved gone?" which is explained as a language of wonder. It is as if to say: God forbid that your Beloved should depart from you entirely! "Where has your beloved gone?"—since you are the "fairest among women," as stated, surely this is only a "hiding of the face" <term>Hester Panim: a theological state where God’s presence is concealed during times of suffering</term> for a moment, for in the end, He will return and show Himself to you. Therefore they asked, "Where has your beloved turned, that we may seek him with you?" This "seeking him with you" may mean: we will seek Him and pray for His favor, that He may return to be with you and in your company and splendor, and no longer hide His face from you. Perhaps the "Daughters of Jerusalem" refers to the **angels**, and this matter would then be understood more simply.

She answered with her fine discernment that this is indeed the case, for He has not departed entirely:

->**"My beloved has gone down to his garden, to the beds of spices,"**<-

And He has not distanced himself permanently, for even now:
->**"To browse in the gardens and to gather lilies."**<-

He delights in the assemblies of Israel and takes pleasure in the fruit of their deeds and their good actions in the "garden." He strolls between the rows and partitions of the **Righteous** <term>Tzadikim: individuals who live in total accordance with Divine will</term> and the **Pious**. This is the meaning of "My beloved has gone down to his garden, to the beds of spices"—these are the partitions of the righteous who have raised the fragrance of the spices of their Torah and their deeds in this world for Him to delight in. This matter exists in both the Higher Garden <note>The celestial Paradise</note> and the Lower Garden <note>The earthly realm of spiritual practice</note>; this is the secret of "browsing in the gardens." The primary focus of this delight in its proper time is the righteous alone, for even from there, He delights in the study of Torah that the righteous engage in within this world, and in the fragrance of their good deeds—particularly those who study Torah at night, from midnight onwards. This is as stated in the **Book of Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note> on the verse: "You who dwell in the gardens, companions listen for your voice" <note>Song of Songs 8:13</note>. This is the secret of "gathering lilies"—her intention is that even now, while in exile, God desires and delights in the righteous and their actions.

<summary>This page interprets the dialogue between the "Daughters of Jerusalem" and the "Bride," explaining that God's absence during exile is only a "hiding of the face" and that He remains present in the study halls and the deeds of the righteous.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs, Chapter 6, specifically addressing the Divine praise of the Assembly of Israel despite the hardships of exile. It transitions from the nations' questioning to God’s response of enduring love.</meta>

### Song of Songs

...to Jerusalem regarding the public sacrifices, as it is written: "Like the holy flock, like the flock of Jerusalem during her festivals" <note>Ezekiel 36:38. The author links the "flock" in the Song of Songs to the abundance of sacrificial animals in Jerusalem</note>. 

Furthermore, the Sages said in the Midrash regarding the verse "You are beautiful, my darling, as Tirzah" <note>Song of Songs 6:4</note>: you are as "pleasing as desire" <note>The Hebrew word *Tirzah* is homiletically linked to *Ratzon*, meaning "favor" or "desire"</note>; when you desire something, you do not need to learn from anyone else. This means, according to his path, that even though the **Assembly of Israel** <term>Knesset Yisrael: the spiritual personification of the Jewish people</term> was in exile and distanced from her home, everything still depends on her. Her own actions will bring her close—swiftly and quickly—and she will return to her former state in an essential "clinging" <term>Dveikut: a state of intense spiritual attachment</term> with her God. 

At first glance, her actions and the awakening of her repentance might not seem as pure or fitting in exile as they were in the Holy Land. For this reason, He compared her to Jerusalem—meaning: "Your actions are close and similar to the deeds done in Jerusalem," even if they are not perfectly identical in a visionary sense. This is why the verse says "**as** Jerusalem," using the "as" of comparison. Whatever the case may have been—that her actions and repentance might not be fully worthy due to the limitations of her location—they will still be "pleasing" before the Lord until they improve to their former state. Then, their return will be cast upon the nations, just like the days they went out from the land of Egypt and were "bannered hosts" in the wilderness. For the nations heard and trembled; "pain and quaking took hold of them" <note>Exodus 15:14-15</note>. This is the meaning of "Awesome as the bannered hosts."

Regarding what our Sages of blessed memory interpreted about the city of **Tirzah** <note>A capital of the Northern Kingdom of Israel</note>—not according to its literal sense, but as is fitting—they noted that while Tirzah was a royal city for the kings of Israel, they also sinned there and did "evil in the eyes of the Lord." It did not have a "moment of fitness" after becoming a royal city because idolatry always passed through it. Therefore, even though it was a large, beautiful, and important city, it would not have been appropriate to praise the Assembly of Israel by comparing her to it if only bad deeds were found there. Especially during all the days it was a royal city, they "shuddered and trembled," for their blood was lacking... <note>The text here is somewhat fragmented, suggesting a comparison between the historical reality of the city and its poetic use in the verse</note>.

> **"Turn your eyes away from me, for they have overcome me."**
> **"Your hair is like a flock of goats streaming down from Mount Gilead."**
> **"Your teeth are like a flock of ewes that have come up from the washing; all of them bear twins, and not one among them is bereaved."**
> **"Like a slice of pomegranate is your temple behind your veil."**

<column-break/>

Behold, the praise of the eyes, the teeth, and the temple has already been explained above in the section: **"Behold you are beautiful, my darling, etc."** <note>Referring to Song of Songs 4:1-3</note>. That was the praise with which the Holy One, Blessed be He, praised the Assembly of Israel *before* the exile, while they were still dwelling on their land, the Holy Land. He considered their circumstances more significant then. 

However, during the time of the exile, He already included all of them in what He said: "You are beautiful, my darling, etc.," which is a general praise for all the members of the body. He specified these particular features because when the members [of the nation] are in the Diaspora, they are more "screened" <note>i.e., hidden or protected from spiritual influence</note> and closer to seeking refuge. When they were in exile amidst the ruins of the nations and assimilated among them, they were inclined to learn from the peoples of the land. 

This leads to "tilting the scales" toward repentance for the **sin of the eyes**, for it is easy to "set one's eyes" on the daughters of the land when they are mixed together. The elders must learn from them... <unclear>distinction</unclear>... regarding immorality and the "brokers of transgression" <term>Sarsorei de'averah: a rabbinic term for the eyes and heart, which act as agents that lead a person to sin</term>, which are the eyes, as it is written: "And do not scout after your hearts and after your eyes" <note>Numbers 15:39</note>. 

We have seen that they already stumbled during the first exile—the Babylonian exile—and abandoned their souls like [wild] creatures. This was the reason that only a small portion of Israel returned, and they did not begin with a "high hand" <note>i.e., with open defiance or triumph</note> or with miracles and wonders as they did during the Exodus from Egypt. This is as "The Investigator" <note>likely referring to a philosophical commentator like Isaac Arama or a similar medieval thinker</note> wrote in the first chapter.

<summary>This page provides a homiletic commentary on Song of Songs 6:4-7, explaining that God praises the Assembly of Israel even in exile. The author distinguishes between the praises given while in the Holy Land and those given in the Diaspora, emphasizing the dangers of assimilation and the "sin of the eyes."</summary>

<keywords>Song of Songs, Jerusalem, Tirzah, Assembly of Israel, Exile, Repentance, Midrash, Exodus from Egypt, Purity, Assimilation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs 6:4. The previous section discussed the transition of the Assembly of Israel from the depths of exile back into the favor of the Divine. This page focuses on the specific praises God gives to the soul (or the nation) even while she remains in a state of spiritual "thorns" or hardship.</meta>

# Commentary

Among the horses, he is a shepherd of horses, even though the thorns surround them and no one else is able to shepherd horses like him. This means the Shepherd of horses—unlike those of Charos <note>Original: "חרוס" — likely a reference to a specific geographical location or a symbolic name for a place of hardship</note>, whose waters are white <note>Original: "מימיו לבן" — This may refer to the clarity or lack of turbidity in certain waters as a metaphor for divine judgment</note>. Thus, the Holy One, Blessed be He, leads His world measure for measure. As it is written: "If your sins be as scarlet, they shall be white as snow" <note>Isaiah 1:18</note>. And when it says "even though the thorns surround them," etc., it appears that this refers to the **Levush** <note>Original: "הלבש"ו" — Likely referring to the 'Levush HaOra,' a commentary by Rabbi Mordecai Yaffe</note>, as he explains shortly hereafter. Regarding "by his strength," the two explanations are close to the two paths—one path being shorter—and it is easy to understand.

Behold, Solomon said through the Holy Spirit that in this verse, the Divine Speech is:
->**"You are beautiful, my darling, as Tirzah, lovely"**<-
->**"as Jerusalem, awesome as the bannered hosts."**<-

After it was mentioned previously that the **Assembly of Israel** <term>Knesset Yisrael: the mystical personification of the Jewish people</term> was in exile under the dominion of the **husk** <term>Kelipah: literally 'shell'; in Kabbalah, this refers to the forces of impurity that conceal the Divine light</term>, and despite her suffering, she remained joyful, her "taste" <note>meaning her character and essence</note> stayed within her. Her spirit did not lead her to exchange the religion of the Living God, even though she is "sick with our love." God returned to speak to her heart to comfort her and inform her that she is not excluded from His mercy. Even though she was distanced from the Holy Sanctuary, she is nonetheless beloved by Him, and He desires her actions and service. For she was more upright than all the "walls" <note>a metaphor for other nations or spiritual barriers</note> and their defenses. Even after she was destroyed by guilt and sin, leading to a "hiding of the Divine Face," she still rises in the levels of lack and goodness above all other nations in a most significant way. There is always a "clinging" <term>Dveikut: spiritual attachment to God</term> and we were never entirely separated. 

Therefore, He began to praise and laud her, saying, "**You are beautiful, my darling, as Tirzah.**" This means: even now, during the time you are in exile and in the land of the nations, you are beautiful like the city of **Tirzah** <note>Original: "כתרצה" — interpreted here as being related to the word 'Ratzon' (desire/favor)</note> through the observance of the Negative Commandments <note>prohibitions</note>, just as you are like **Jerusalem** through the observance of the Positive Commandments. You are "**awesome as the bannered hosts**" through the study of Torah; for anyone who studies the Torah, the awe of his vitality is cast upon all creatures. As it is written: "And all the peoples of the earth shall see that the name of the Lord is called upon you, and they shall be afraid of you" <note>Deuteronomy 28:10</note>. Our Sages of blessed memory said the Torah is entirely composed of the names of the Holy One, Blessed be He.

**Or it may mean:** You are beautiful, my darling, in matters between man and his fellow, just as you are like Jerusalem in matters between man and the Place <term>HaMakom: a Rabbinic name for God, emphasizing His omnipresence</term>. You are "**awesome as the bannered hosts**" regarding the decrees, enactments, and vows of the Scribes <note>Rabbinic laws</note> which they decreed upon the people out of awe and fear, lest they break through the essential bodies of the Torah's commandments—this is the concept of "making a safeguard for My safeguard." 

Furthermore: "You are beautiful, my darling, as Tirzah" in how a person conducts themselves; "as Jerusalem" in how one conducts their household; and "awesome as the bannered hosts" in the conduct of the state, where the imposing of awe for the sake of Heaven is more relevant.

Additionally: "You are beautiful, my darling, as Tirzah" in thought; "as Jerusalem" in speech; and "awesome as the bannered hosts" in action. 

And in the **Great Midrash** <term>Midrash Rabbah: an ancient collection of homiletic interpretations of the Bible</term>: "You are beautiful, my darling, as Tirzah"—these are the sacrifices, for the Scripture says "it shall be accepted [**v’ratzah**] for him to make atonement for him" <note>Leviticus 1:4</note>. "As Jerusalem"—these are the holy things in Jerusalem... This is a great praise for the Assembly of Israel: that even during the time of exile, when there are no sacrifices and no Temple service, she is as beautiful as the time when they were offering sacrifices. This is either because of the prayers, which were established to correspond to the sacrifices—the daily offerings in their order and the additional offerings according to their laws—or because of the study of the laws of the sacrifices. As it is written: "This is the law of the burnt offering..." <note>Leviticus 6:2</note>. And how much more so according to the Midrash of our Sages: "This is the law for the burnt offering and the grain offering..."—that anyone who occupies themselves with the Torah is considered by Scripture as if they had offered all the sacrifices. 

He divides the sacrifices into two parts, saying "as Tirzah" and "as Jerusalem" to correspond to the two types of sacrifices: individual sacrifices and public sacrifices. He says "**as Tirzah**" regarding the individual sacrifices, as it is written: "And it shall be accepted [**v’ratzah**] for him to make atonement for him," which speaks of individual sacrifices. And He says "**as Jerusalem**"...

<summary>This page provides a multi-layered commentary on Song of Songs 6:4, interpreting the beauty of the Assembly of Israel through the lenses of commandments, Torah study, social conduct, and the sacrificial system. It emphasizes that even in exile, prayer and study maintain the Jewish people's spiritual standing and "beauty" before God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs, specifically exploring the interpretations of the "sixty queens and eighty concubines." It links the purity of lineage and the dedication to Torah study as the reasons why the Divine Presence remains with the Assembly of Israel even during the hardships of exile.</meta>

### Song of Songs
<page-num>44</page-num>

...recognizing their fathers and their mothers, and so on. Even Israel is thus: "**and they declared their pedigrees after their families, by the house of their fathers**" <note>Numbers 1:18</note>. It is therefore clear that there is no greater deficiency or degradation than being devoid of the knowledge of one's fathers or mothers, or both. As has been said, this indicates a corrupt source and a root that is divided and filthy. How then could the **Shekhinah** <term>Shekhinah: the Divine Presence, often viewed as the feminine aspect of God</term> dwell upon them? For it is already written: "**to be a God unto thee, and to thy seed after thee**" <note>Genesis 17:7</note>, as our Sages of blessed memory have expounded. 

Also, the words of Rabbi Hiyya—which are placed in the Midrash before the words of Rabbi Levi—regarding the absence of a written script or a language, or both, align with our approach. Examine them, and it appears that an error fell into the books, and we should read "sixty" in place of "forty," as in the words of Rabbi Levi. It is easy to understand the truth, though it is slightly difficult according to the Midrash, for it would have been appropriate to write: "Sixty are the kingdoms, and eighty are the concubines," meaning the **Erev Rav** <term>Erev Rav: the 'Mixed Multitude' who left Egypt with the Israelites; often used to refer to internal spiritual corruption</term>, and eleven from one part with the other part. 

When we reflect on this matter, there arises the difficulty of the two ranks—the Kingdom over the Concubines—in terms of the recognition of fathers or mothers, and also in terms of language or script. For these alone would not suffice to improve them enough to call these "Queens" and those "Concubines," unless there are nations that are significant in their own right, in their structure, quality, and virtue, more so than those others, even while they too are similar and distinct from all other nations. These are divided for the Queen according to their importance, and they are Queens, Concubines, and Maidens. Each group among them follows the written number: sixty, eighty, and without number. From now on, it is possible that ten from the second group are similar to the first group in their knowledge of script or language, or in the recognition of fathers or mothers. Therefore, Rabbi Hiyya joined and included them, as did Rabbi Levi, within the first group according to the tradition they held on the matter, and the calculation of "the foundations of the generation" was hinted at within its total. The essence of the matter is that he was precise regarding importance, and the masters of the Midrash were precise regarding knowledge or recognition, and all is truth.

Furthermore, our Sages of blessed memory expounded in the **Midrash Rabbati** <note>Original: "מדרש רבתי" — Likely referring to Midrash Rabbah on Song of Songs</note>: "**Sixty are the queens**"—these are the sixty tractates of laws <note>The sixty tractates of the Mishnah</note>; "**and eighty are the concubines**"—these are the eighty sections in **Torat Kohanim** <term>Torat Kohanim: 'The Instruction of the Priests,' another name for the book of Leviticus or the Sifra</term>; "**and maidens without number**"—there is no end to the **Toseftas** <term>Tosefta: 'Additions'; a collection of Jewish oral laws from the same period as the Mishnah</term>, etc. 

They further expounded: "**Sixty are the queens**"—these are the sixty groups of righteous people who sit in the Garden under the **Tree of Life** and occupy themselves with the Torah, etc.; "**and eighty are the concubines**"—these are the eighty intermediate groups who sit and occupy themselves with the Torah outside of them, under the Tree of Life; "**and maidens without number**"—there is no end to the students, etc. 

Behold, the words of these two Midrashim also align well with our path. For the Holy One, Blessed be He, praised the **Assembly of Israel** during the time she was in these exiles; even while she was in the land of her enemies, she was beautiful and faithful in His eyes, whether because of the Torah and all its parts which she busies herself with, or because of the righteous and holy ones who are in the land. For through their merits and the goodness of their hearts, their connection and their levels—according to their importance, purity, and righteousness—their merit is always before His eyes to continue His providence and His cleaving with Israel. He will not turn to the wicked and the spreaders of lies among them who caused confusion. Their survival and their standing are clear matters.

And behold, in truth I have seen that **Rashi** <note>Rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki (1040–1105), the preeminent biblical commentator</note> sought a proper path according to the literal meaning, intending to be precise with the numbers sixty and eighty and the "without number" of the maidens. So too the **Translator** <note>Referring to the Aramaic Targum</note> found it so according to his path regarding the war of the Greeks with the **House of the Hasmoneans** <note>The Maccabean revolt</note>. Seek it out from their books and take it, and it will be pleasant to you. The grammarian will find that according to their paths, the purchase of our path follows correctly and...
