<meta>This page continues the analysis of the virtues of the Assembly of Israel as described in the Song of Songs. While the previous page discussed the importance of genealogical purity and the "Mixed Multitude," this page moves into a specific commentary on the verses describing the bride’s features (eyes, hair, teeth, temples) as allegories for the leaders and spiritual states of Israel during the Babylonian exile and beyond.</meta>

# Commentary

Be careful; therefore, it says: **"Turn away your eyes from before me"** <note>Song of Songs 6:5</note>, meaning that within your collective whole, your eyes are beautiful. They are praised for their caution in maintaining themselves against the hints of transgression, and their grace is spread like the eyes of one who seeks the dreams of Hillel <note>original: "סחור חלומות הלל", likely a reference to a specific pious figure or a local tradition of dream interpretation</note>. Nevertheless, "turn them away from being before Me" and before My service, for grace requires great caution to attract those who are entirely refined, clean, and pure. This is because **"they have emboldened me"** <note>original: הרהיבוני (harhivuni)</note>, which is to say, they have weakened my strength. This follows the path of the explanation of **Ibn Ezra** <term>Ibn Ezra: Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a major Spanish Jewish scholar and biblical commentator</term> of blessed memory. Adjacent to this is: **"your hair is like a flock of goats,"** etc. According to the **Midrash Rabbah** <term>Midrash Rabbah: A collection of ancient homiletical interpretations of the Torah and Megillot</term>, we explained there: just as this goat is lowly, so is Israel lowly among the nations. The Sages of blessed memory interpreted this regarding the nations, and according to our way, it precisely fits the Babylonian exile, for there too was an intermingling with the daughters of the nations, which was like a donkey <note>A metaphor for base or animalistic behavior</note>, and it was proper in the eyes of the Creator, as has been explained. 

Furthermore, **"your teeth are like a flock of ewes,"** etc., means that even there they were close in the exile to being flawed and darkened. This is because their intermingling with the aforementioned nations caused them to eat and drink together with a lack of milk <note>A metaphor for spiritual nourishment or purity</note>, and through intermarriage, they would corrupt their form and the beauty of the **Shekhinah** <term>Shekhinah: The Divine Presence</term>. Nevertheless, "your teeth are like a flock of ewes," according to the way we explained above. For this reason, it also said **"the temple [of your head]"** <note>Song of Songs 6:7</note> to indicate the absence of "brazen-facedness" like a donkey, which is also easily acquired in exile among the aforementioned nations and the **am ha’aretz** <term>am ha’aretz: literally 'people of the land,' here referring to those unlearned in Torah or lacking religious refinement</term>. He said "like a flock" because of the shame and humility upon their faces, as it is known that they naturally possess a strength of character.

According to the words of the **Targum** <term>Targum: The ancient Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Bible</term> of blessed memory, who translated the "eyes" as referring to the Rabbis and teachers of Law, the "teeth" as the Priests and Levites, and the "temple" as the King—it even says that after the exile, the Holy One, blessed be He, crowned her with three crowns: the Crown of Torah, the Crown of Priesthood, and the Crown of Kingship. Regarding the **Crown of Torah**, it says that even if there is no Torah like the Torah of the Land of Israel (because it is more holy and pure than that which is outside the Land), nevertheless, "turn away your eyes," etc., meaning: set a seal around Me and before Me, as the Targum translated. This is because "they have emboldened Me" and made Me King in the exile, and their words are pleasant to Me. Also, "your hair," which represents the collective of those who study Torah, is "like a flock of goats," etc. Regarding the **Crown of Priesthood** and Levites, specifically those who eat the **Terumah** <term>Terumah: the 'heave offering' given to priests</term> and tithes, it says: "your teeth," etc., to indicate that the crown of their holiness stands in its place even in the time of exile. They can benefit from the heave offerings and tithes in this time when they prepare their bodies for purity and holiness, for they merit from the "Table of the Most High," and their food is an atonement from God. And when it is completed with the letter *He* <note>A grammatical/mystical note on the Hebrew spelling</note>, it is already by force of law, even if in this time it is a Rabbinic decree. Regarding the **Crown of Kingship**, it says: **"like a slice of pomegranate,"** etc. This refers to the **Exilarchs** <term>Exilarchs: 'Resh Galuta', the political leaders of the Jewish community in Babylon</term> and the Princes; they are the leaders and providers who stand in every generation after the exile.

**THERE ARE SIXTY QUEENS AND EIGHTY**
**CONCUBINES, AND MAIDENS WITHOUT NUMBER.**

<column-break/>

He concluded with the praise of the Assembly of Israel, which is significant in its own right, its recognition, and its birth-status, despite being in exile. Even though her dwelling is far from her home among the nations, it is impossible to sever her clinging to Him. For what are the other nations? Even the most significant of them are not worthy to stand in her place or to take her position in the essential clinging to the Holy One, blessed be He. For the text says, **"There are sixty queens,"** etc. This is understood through the way of the Midrash Rabbah of blessed memory: Rabbi Levi said, sixty and eighty make one hundred and forty. Seventy of them recognize their fathers but do not recognize their mothers, and seventy of them recognize their mothers but do not recognize their fathers. And **"maidens without number"** refers to those who recognize neither...

It also introduces a Midrashic calculation regarding the 140 nations and the unique genealogical clarity of the Jewish people.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs Chapter 6. While the previous page focused on the physical metaphors of the bride (hair, teeth, etc.) representing the people of Israel in the Babylonian exile, this page transitions to the internal unity of the Torah and the dawning of the final Redemption.</meta>

# Song of Songs 45

In the Midrash <note>original: "במדרש"</note>, "His lips are lilies" <note>Song of Songs 5:13</note> refers to the Master of Secrets, who is like a garden bed. According to the interpretation of the paths regarding the merit of the righteous and the merit of the Torah, it is possible to explain this verse as we did previously: continuing to praise the Assembly of Israel <term>Assembly of Israel: Knesset Yisrael, the personification of the Jewish people in relationship with God</term> both in its essence and its specific components and principles—such as compassion, mercy, and voluntary kindness—and to continue in the way of the Midrash as well.

For regarding the Sages <note>original: "על הכוף וכחם", likely a reference to the Sages who "bow" in humility before the law</note> of blessed memory, it may be that they differ with one another, yet they all interpret from one logic, from one law <note>original: "מטעם א' מהלכה א'"</note>, and from one tradition. This is a great praise for the Torah: that it is entirely a single unity. Even the Sages and the righteous who occupy themselves with it all arrive at the same place; "both these and those are the words of the Living God." This is especially true when they reach the "heat" of the matter <note>original: "לחמתו של דברים", referring to intense intellectual debate</note>, for they all seek from a single logic without any conflict at all.

Behold the expansion of this idea: observe that in the Midrashim, they precisely analyze the verse that says, **"My dove, my perfect one, is unique; she is the only one of her mother"** <note>Song of Songs 6:9</note>. Correspondingly, they said: "from one logic, from one law, from one tradition." This refers to how the Torah is interpreted through the power of the biblical verses, primarily through the **Thirteen Attributes** <term>Thirteen Attributes: The hermeneutical rules of Rabbi Ishmael used to interpret the Torah</term> of Rabbi Ishmael. One aspect is the "weighty logic" <note>original: "חומרה", likely referring to *Kal VaChomer* or a fortiori reasoning</note> from a single reason, which is then interpreted as a "Law given to Moses at Sinai" <term>Halakha LeMoshe MiSinai: Laws considered to have been spoken to Moses but not written in the Pentateuch</term>. It is further interpreted through the tradition <note>original: "גמרא", here meaning the received oral tradition</note> and through human understanding. This is the general rule mentioned in the Midrash.

If the "weighty logic" of a single reason differs from that which is written explicitly <note>original: "בקרתי בקרתי לבד"</note>, it is because this third rule includes all thirteen attributes. This is not a distant interpretation. One should examine the "heat" and the "birth" <note>referring to the verse's mention of "the unique one of her mother"</note> in the final two rules through which the law is born; even if it is not explicit in the Torah, these rules are as foundational as the first rule which is written explicitly.

Regarding the Torah, it is said that everyone occupies themselves with it according to their own value and the level of their perception. Thus they shall praise and glorify the Torah according to the first Midrash, and a person reaches the level of the second Midrash because there they will see and behold the depths of the Torah and the many truths of its history—much more than they perceived in their own time. Therefore, they will praise the "weighty" and "limitless" nature of the Torah.

According to the final path, which is the way of **R. Saadia Gaon** <term>R. Saadia Gaon: 882–942 CE, a pioneering figure in medieval Jewish philosophy and linguistics</term> and the **Targum** <term>Targum: Ancient Aramaic translation of the Bible</term> of blessed memory, this speaks of the praise of the Assembly of Israel, which was unique in its importance and integrity above all others, without any comparison at all. This uniqueness is both from the perspective of contemplation and study of the Torah—this is **"My dove, my perfect one, is unique"**—and from the perspective of action. Where do the verses say, "Turn from evil and do good"? This is **"she is the unique one of her mother,"** referring to the performance of deeds; **"she is pure to her who bore her,"** referring to those who are essential for the purity of the soul, its merit, its memory, and its cleansing. It is possible to categorize these into three praises: thought, speech, and action. Alternatively, they refer to the conduct of the individual, the conduct of the household, and the conduct of the state. For all these parts are perfect within the Assembly of Israel and became so famous among them that "the daughters saw her," etc.

->**"Who is she that looks forth like the dawn, fair as the moon,**<-
->**clear as the sun, awesome**<-
->**as the bannered hosts?"**<-
<note>Song of Songs 6:10</note>

Until now, the text spoke of the time when the Assembly of Israel was in exile, yet remained chosen by God. Now, the speaker moves through the Holy Spirit to speak of the time when the Redemption shall be awakened and the congregation shall be gathered in the morning. It says: **"Who is she that looks forth like the dawn,"** etc. The intention follows the Sages of blessed memory in the Midrash: Rabbi Hiyya and Rabbi Shimon ben Halafta were walking in the Arbel Valley at daybreak and saw the "Doe of the Dawn" <term>Doe of the Dawn: *Ayelet HaShachar*, a poetic term for the first rays of light before sunrise</term> as its light broke through. Rabbi Hiyya the Great said to Rabbi ben Halafta: "So shall be the redemption of Israel—flickering and then arriving" <note>meaning it starts slowly and increases in intensity</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual unity of the Torah despite the differing opinions of Sages and interprets the bride's uniqueness in Song of Songs 6:9-10 as a metaphor for Israel's transition from exile to the dawning of the final Redemption.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Assembly of Israel, Thirteen Attributes of Hermeneutics, Halakha LeMoshe MiSinai, Doe of the Dawn, Redemption, Midrash, Rabbi Saadia Gaon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs Chapter 6, specifically focusing on verse 9. While the previous page established the unity of the Torah and the Sages, this page delves into the lineage of the Patriarchs—Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob—as the foundation for the "Assembly of Israel."</meta>

# Commentary

For all the descendants of Noah, as well as all the descendants of Abraham and their ilk, of blessed memory, and all the chieftains of the kingdom of Ishmael—even though they trace their lineage back to Abraham and Isaac—how much more so the rest of the nations of the world! What is their portion and what is their lot compared to the Assembly of Israel <term>Assembly of Israel: Knesset Yisrael, the personification of the Jewish people in their relationship with the Divine</term>? For the Blessed One mentions them in the upcoming verse as having a strength that surpasses the others. He does not exempt them from judgment, but He severs their attachment to Him, treating them like a beast of burden <note>original: "כחמור", literally "like a donkey," implying a lack of spiritual soul-connection</note>.

<columns>2</columns>
According to the first path, regarding the strength of the Assembly of Israel from the perspective of its root and its birth, the following Midrashic interpretation of our Sages, of blessed memory, clarifies the matter well. In the *Midrash Rabbati* <note>original: "מדרש רבתי"</note>, they taught: **"Unique"** <note>original: "אחת", literally "One"</note>—this refers to Abraham, as it is said, "Abraham was but one" <note>Ezekiel 33:24</note>. **"She is the only one of her mother"**—this refers to Isaac, who was the only child of his mother <note>Sarah</note>. **"She is pure to her who bore her"**—this refers to our father
<column-break/>
**"My dove, my perfect one, is unique; she is the only one of her mother; she is pure to her who bore her. The daughters saw her and acclaimed her; the queens and concubines praised her."** <note>Song of Songs 6:9</note>

Jacob, who was clearly pure to the one who bore him, for he was a completely righteous man. **"The daughters saw her and acclaimed her"**—these are the Tribes <note>the twelve sons of Jacob</note>, as it is said, "And the report was heard in Pharaoh's house..." <note>Genesis 45:16</note>. **"The queens and concubines praised her"**—this refers to Joseph, as it is said, "And Pharaoh said... Can we find such a one as this?" <note>Genesis 41:38</note>.
<columns>1</columns>

Behold, it is clear that they interpreted this verse as referring to the status of the Assembly of Israel and its essence. They are powerful and strong "as a molten mirror" <note>Job 37:18</note> in holiness and purity. Without a doubt, the Assembly is worthy of being special and set apart as a holy nation to the Lord our God, without any interruption at all. "Many waters" <note>Song of Songs 8:7</note> from the murky rivers and streams of the gathered masses and the nations cannot extinguish the flame of fire—the fire of God—from being our God. And the "threefold cord" <note>Ecclesiastes 4:12</note> of essential holiness and purity shall never be severed.

One who examines this more closely will see clearly how the scripture hints at and demonstrates how Abraham was the first to begin the repair <term>repair: tikkun, the process of fixing the spiritual flaws of the world</term> and the refining of the "refined truth," to purify and remove the dross from the silver. Even though he was "but one," Abraham clung to the unity of the Blessed Name to begin fixing what was corrupted by the first Adam, who was a single man in the world. 

And behold, with Isaac, the refining of the impurity began further, specifically because of Ishmael <note>who was excluded from the covenant</note>. After Isaac, the "Primordial Knowledge" <note>original: "ידע קדום", a Kabbalistic reference to Divine foreknowledge</note> knew that the refining was not yet complete, for the "furnace" of God had not yet finished its work. One was outside of it and another was outside of it <note>referring to Ishmael and Esau</note>, but Jacob was "the unique one of his mother." 

Thus, it was through the power of Jacob that the refining was finally completed, for all his children were the "firstborn of God." As it is written, "and two nations shall be separated from your bowels" <note>Genesis 25:23</note>—the refuse was separated from the food. Therefore, afterwards, "his bed was perfect" <note>term: "Matah Shlemah," a Rabbinic idiom meaning all of Jacob's children remained righteous and within the covenant</note>, for there was no mixture of "thorns" within him at all. This is the meaning of **"She is pure to her who bore her"**—meaning he was clear and clarified to his mother. This is why "Rebecca loved Jacob" <note>Genesis 25:28</note>. This subject is a long and deep inquiry, but for our purposes here, what we have written will suffice.

We return to **"My dove, my perfect one"**—this is also a praise of the Assembly of Israel in two aspects: the aspect of the body and the aspect of the soul. For the bodies of the members of the Assembly of Israel—and it goes without saying their souls—are more precious and of greater value than anything else. They are suspended from and held by the strength of holiness and purity, which is the imprint of all that exists, as explained previously regarding the verse "His palate is sweetness" <note>Song of Songs 5:16</note>; look there. 

Therefore, it says here that because of her being "my dove, my perfect one"—which are hints at two aspects of her own essence—she also possesses the praise of her "roots" and "essentials" as mentioned above. The end of the verse says, **"The daughters saw her..."** to say that the praise of this "stature" <note>original: "קומה", often referring to the spiritual structure of the soul</note> has already been recognized by the mouths of the other nations. Even wicked kings have answered "Amen" against their will, as explained in the Midrash...

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of Song of Songs, transitioning from the foundational merits of the Patriarchs to the refinement of the Assembly of Israel through suffering. It focuses on verse 6:11, "I went down to the garden of nuts," using Midrashic comparisons to explain how trials lead to spiritual growth and eventual redemption.</meta>

# Song of Songs

...and you shall be burned, they will become heirs. This is as our Sages, of blessed memory, taught in *Midrash Rabbah*, Chapter 7, and these are their words: "Why are Israel compared to an olive? Just as this olive, while it is still on its tree, is beaten, and after it is beaten it is brought up to the vat and ground, etc., and when they press them in the mill and afterwards grind them, and then tie them with ropes and bring stones, and only then do they give their oil—so too with Israel: the nations come and beat them from place to place, and strike them, and bind them in collars, and surround them in arenas <note>original: "טרטינוין" (tartinon), likely referring to Roman theaters or circuses where Jews were persecuted, or generally "arenas of trial"</note>, and only then do they perform repentance, and the Holy One, Blessed be He, answers them," and so on, as mentioned in *Bar Lev* and *Melchon* <note>likely titles of specific commentaries or sections</note>.

This is the imagery of the nut. Even though it does not contain all the harsh details found in the imagery of the olive, it nonetheless contains the imagery of blows and suffering. For the fruit of the nut is not gathered except by beating—it must be beaten to harvest its fruit. Afterwards, it is also impossible to eat the fruit except by a blow and the breaking of the shell. This aptly justifies the matter of troubles and suffering, as stated, and concludes with the cause for redemption.

Therefore, the Holy One, Blessed be He, said: **"I went down to the garden of nuts."** The matter is that these verses were spoken through the Holy Spirit regarding the theme of redemption. Just as the previous verse was explained as the awakening and beginning of redemption, it now states the reason and cause for bringing it to its ultimate completion. This is because He "went down" to oversee the matter of the suffering of the Assembly of Israel and their troubles. However, the seal <note>meaning the final deciding factor</note> is that they have already been pressed like a "garden of nuts" due to the multitude of troubles, decrees, wanderings, and sufferings that have passed over them. Therefore, "I went down" to look over them after they have already reached this state—to see if there are other assisting factors among them to bring the redemption to its completion.

This is the secret of the phrase: **"To see the shoots of the valley..."** The intention is that, besides the general hardship of the nation—where everyone suffered under the yoke of exile and tribulations—He also wanted to look and see the individual parts of the nation to see if assistance for redemption could be found in them. This is the secret of "the shoots of the valley," meaning those unique individuals who are planted by the "streams of water" <note>Psalm 1:3</note> of the Torah and the commandments.

Since these individuals are divided into two parts:
1. There are **Masters of Torah** whose Torah study rises far above their good deeds, even though they are meticulous in the commandments; however, their primary "good name" comes from their wisdom, as they are famous as legal authorities and are distinguished in wisdom.
2. There are **Masters of Deeds** and commandments who are not distinguished in wisdom and Torah, even though their desire is for the Torah of the Lord and they meditate upon it constantly or in fixed times as their schedule allows.

Regarding these two groups, the verse specifies and says: **"To see if the vine blossomed"**—this refers to the group of Torah Masters; **"If the pomegranates budded"**—this refers to the group of Masters of Deeds.

It is possible that His saying "to see these" carries the meaning of one who doubts a matter—"to see" if an awakening for this will be found or not. But according to the meaning I have written, it means "to look and oversee." The intention is that after all these things and troubles have passed over the Assembly of Israel in the end of days, and after the awakening of redemption—at which time the parts of the nation will undoubtedly be stirred to grasp the Torah and the commandments—each according to their level, the Holy One, Blessed be He, will oversee them to assist them in reaching their goal and to complete and fulfill what they began to do. For in the matter of "one who comes to be purified," they assist him <note>a famous Talmudic principle: *ha-ba l'taher mesay'in oto*</note>.

Regarding the mentioned matter, "The Lord went..." etc., this is "to see the shoots of the valley." And it repeats and says that the matter of this oversight will occur after He sees the awakening and beginning within the parts of the nation. This is the secret of "to see if the vine blossomed, if the pomegranates budded," which is the completion of the blossoming and budding. And in *Midrash Rabbati*, our Sages, of blessed memory, taught: "To the garden of..."

<meta>This page continues the allegorical discussion of Song of Songs 6:11, moving from the theme of suffering (symbolized by the nut) to the process of redemption. It connects the previous page’s mention of "the garden of nuts" to the gradual nature of the future salvation, drawing heavily on the Zohar and Midrashic traditions to explain why light appears in stages rather than all at once.</meta>

# Commentary

For kindness is in His name, the Lord <note>The Hebrew name of God, the Tetragrammaton, is traditionally associated with the attribute of Mercy/Kindness</note>. His light initially shone bit by bit <note>original: "קימעה קימעה" (kim'a kim'a)</note>, and afterward it grew stronger and built up, and then it bore fruit and multiplied, and thereafter it continued to spread. So it was in those days, as in the verse: **"And Mordecai sat at the King’s gate"** <note>Esther 2:21; the Sages see Mordecai’s position as the beginning of a gradual redemption</note>, etc. Regarding the miracle, our Sages of blessed memory interpreted it as a matter of the Redemption. From the interruption of what we lacked in the days of the Hasmoneans <note>The Maccabees</note>, we are to learn how to interpret the matter of the future Redemption, which shall happen speedily in our days with the help of Heaven. For it will occur in this manner: the light of redemption will return to us slowly, level by level, and not all at once.

This is the meaning of the verse: **"Who is she that looks forth like the dawn?"** <note>Song of Songs 6:10</note>. Our Sages of blessed memory said that the Holy One, Blessed be He, will say concerning the Assembly of Israel <note>original: "כנסת ישראל" (Knesset Yisrael), the personification of the Jewish people</note> at the time the Redemption is awakened: "Who is this that looks forth like the dawn?" The **dawn** is a small light that truly grows stronger. One should not say that the matter ends there, for afterward she will be **"fair as the moon,"** which is an additional amount of light. And afterward, she will be **"clear as the sun,"** which is complete and absolute light. And finally, she will be **"awesome as the bannered hosts,"** meaning that her dread will be cast upon the nations of the world, etc.

This is also interpreted in the *Book of Zohar*, in the portion of *Terumah*: "Like the dawn—so it is in the eyes of one who looks for the light; and afterward, like the moon... and afterward, like the sun," etc. This is a profound matter, and we do not need to delve into its depths here; nevertheless, we have learned from them the explanation of the stated verse.

After writing this, I found proof in the *Zohar*, portion of *Vayishlach*: "**'Who is she that looks forth'**—these are the times when the Holy One, Blessed be He, raises them up and brings them out of exile. Then He will open for them an opening of light that is narrow, thin, and small. Afterward, He opens another opening that is larger than the first, until the Holy One, Blessed be He, opens for them the high gates, open to the four winds of the world. And so it is with everything the Holy One, Blessed be He, does for Israel and for the righteous among them—it happens bit by bit, and not all at once... until the light of Israel, which was small, grows strong, and the Holy One, Blessed be He, says to them: 'Rise, shine, for your light has come.' And what is the reason for this? Following the verse **'Who is she that looks forth like the dawn'**—which is the appearance of the darkness of the morning, a thin light—then **'fair as the moon'** because the moon’s light is more beautiful than the dawn; and then **'clear as the sun'** because its light is powerful and shines more than the moon; and finally **'awesome as the bannered hosts'**—powerful in light as is fitting." I saw this and rejoiced, for it follows our exact path regarding the future Redemption, with the help of Heaven.

It is possible that the hint in **"looks forth like the dawn, fair as the moon, clear as the sun"** refers to the quality of the future light, which will be perfect in its ultimate perfection through all the levels of excellence. Regarding that which does not contain the divisions of goodness within it, it first says **"like the dawn,"** because just as the dawn does not remain in one state but continues to grow, so will be the light of the Redemption.

<margin>
Behold, it is a known truth
from the words of our Sages
that the future Redemption,
speedily in our days,
will be drawn from the power
of the suffering and troubles
which the Assembly of Israel
shall pass through.
</margin>

And to show that even after it becomes great, it will not be harmful like the light of the sun despite its strength, etc., it therefore says **"fair as the moon,"** for the moon is beautiful, established, and not harmful. But because the light of the moon is not so clear, it says **"clear as the sun,"** etc. In this way, that light will contain the clarity of the sun’s light and the beauty and stability of the moon’s light, and it will be ever-increasing like the light of the dawn. Thus, all parts of goodness will be within it. This has already been interpreted in *Midrash Rabbati* in a manner similar to this; look into it.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Song of Songs 6:11-12, expanding on the previous page's theme of gradual redemption. # Song of Songs

The explanation of all the goodness of the Blessed One and His gift to the one who understands it is as was explained above according to the plain meaning. 

Behold, how much we should focus and be precise regarding the Midrash of our Sages, of blessed memory, in *Deuteronomy Rabbah*, section *Va’etchanan*, where these two verses were explained correctly. It states there: Rabbi Elazar said, "When Israel left Egypt, they were redeemed only through these five things: through **distress**, through **repentance**, through the **merit of the forefathers**, through **mercy**, and through **the appointed end** <note>original: "הקץ" (Ha-Ketz) - the pre-determined time for salvation</note>, etc." He derives all of them there from the scriptures. And it says there further: "Even in the time to come, they will be redeemed only through these five things: through **distress**, as it is written, 'In your distress, when they find you'; behold, through distress. 'And you shall return to the Lord your God'; behold, through repentance. 'For the Lord your God is a merciful God'; behold, through mercy. 'Save us, O Lord our God, and gather us and rescue us from the nations'; behold, through the appointed end," thus far his words. 

And after these matters—they being the truth and the right—let us say that here, as it speaks of the matter of the future Redemption and the arrival of its time, it spoke of the general matters from within which the Redemption shall emerge. Regarding what it said about "through distress," it is written here: **"I went down to the garden of nuts,"** and as we explained it above, this refers to the sufferings and the tribulations <note>The nut is a common metaphor for the Jewish people: a hard, protective shell (suffering/exile) surrounding a precious interior</note>. 

Regarding what it said about "through repentance" as an obligation, it says here: **"To see the fruits of the valley"** <note>The Hebrew "Invei Ha-Nachal" literally refers to the greenery of a stream or wadi</note>, where the intention is regarding his entry to purify himself in the waters of the ritual bath <term>Mikvah|A pool of natural water used for ritual purification</term> of the "purity of repentance." For it is known that there is no purity except through water; therefore, to attribute the purity of repentance [to the text], it said here "the valley." And in this, it called out in its physical substance, for the purity of repentance was found among the captive strongmen of the nation. This is what it means by: **"To see if the vine had blossomed,"** etc., in the way that was explained above. 

And regarding what it said about "through mercy," it is written here: **"I did not know, my soul set me..."** meaning to say: I did not know, as the <term>Assembly of Israel|Knesset Yisrael, the personified spiritual community of the Jewish people</term>, a repentance sufficient to be redeemed, but rather it was according to My will that I set out to redeem them from My side and from the side of the mercy which is in Me and according to My measure to act. 

Furthermore, from the side of the "merit of the forefathers," it says: **"The chariots of my noble people"** <note>Song of Songs 6:12</note>. This follows the path explained above in the commentary on "the chariots of my noble people," which refers to the Patriarchs <note>Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</note> who became <term>Chariots|Merkavah, a mystical concept where the righteous serve as vehicles for the Divine Presence in the world</term> for the Divine Presence <note>original: "שכינה" (Shekhinah)</note>. This is not just any explanation, but refers to the Patriarchs themselves, as our Sages of blessed memory said: "The Patriarchs are themselves the Chariot." And it mentioned the explanation of the "father" of their will, who is Abraham, as our Sages of blessed memory said on the verse **"O noble’s daughter"** <note>Song of Songs 7:2</note>, that the intention is Abraham <note>Abraham is called "Nadiv" (Noble) because he was the first to recognize God of his own free will</note>. 

From now on, this verse is also the words of the Holy One, Blessed be He, just as the depth of the one preceding it was the words of the Assembly of Israel. Four of the five things mentioned in the Midrash of our Sages were explained in them. And it did not lack the fifth here, which is "through the appointed end," for when the four are joined, they require the fifth, for if they merit, "I will hasten it" <note>A reference to Isaiah 60:22: "In its time, I will hasten it." The Sages explain that if Israel is worthy, redemption is hastened; if not, it comes at the fixed "end" (Ketz)</note> in the future redemption, speedily in our days, Amen. 

And what it mentioned with the hundred was to say that the fifth must be joined to all four, and no more are needed. According to the definition of repentance, it is closer to say that here, at the time of the <unclear>end</unclear>, as was explained above regarding the verse **"Who is she that looks forth like the dawn,"** etc., the Redemption has already begun to stand. That is to say, the time of the "end" has come, and especially afterward in the scriptures of the tidings, which are matters close to the Redemption, as has been clearly explained, and this is certain.

# 28
# Commentary

"Although I went down to this world to look at the **fresh growth of the valley** <note>original: "באבי הנחל" (be-avi ha-nachal) — referring to Song of Songs 6:11</note>—these are the people of Israel; to see the **blooming of the vine**—these are the synagogues and study halls; and if the **pomegranates have budded**—these are the school children who sit and engage in the Torah, sitting in rows like the seeds of pomegranates," so far the quote. And behold, it is true that the interpretation of the word "toward" <note>original: "אל" (El)</note> at the end of the verse comes correctly according to our path and is easy to understand. Furthermore, our Sages of blessed memory brought in the Midrash regarding the phrase **"I went down to the nut garden"**: why is this specific language used? Just as a nut has a shell that protects it, there are those among the nations who are intermediate, and those in the gardens who are contentious; so too among Israel, there are those who engage in Torah or charity as finely as the words from their mouths, and there are those whose finest flour <note>a metaphor for their best efforts or wealth</note> is demanded and they give, and some whose finest is demanded but they do not give, etc. Behold, this deep explanation relates to the matter of **charity** <note>term: "צדקה" (Tzedakah) — often translated as charity, but meaning 'righteousness' or 'social justice' in a religious context</note>, and this also aligns with the path we have chosen. For in the merit of charity, Israel will be redeemed, as it is written: "Zion shall be redeemed with justice, and those who return to her with charity" <note>Isaiah 1:27</note>. Regarding this, it says after the awakening of the Redemption: "I went down to the nut garden," meaning that the merit of charity is what stood for them to be redeemed. And even if there is a likeness of smallness or sin in them, He will not look at it, for they are nullified by the majority of the virtuous, and the Scripture chose this path according to the ways mentioned above. This is the understanding from the Midrashim on this verse:

->**"Before I was aware, my soul set me among the chariots of"**<-
->**"my noble people."**<-
<note>Song of Songs 6:12</note>

These are the words of the **Assembly of Israel** <note>term: "כנסת ישראל" (Knesset Yisrael) — the mystical personification of the Jewish people</note>, and we also leave it as it is written, following the footsteps of our Sages of blessed memory. It means that the Assembly of Israel will say at that time that she does not know or recognize within herself that she is worthy of being redeemed on her own account, for she was not purified as is proper from the filth of her ways and deeds. Therefore, she concludes by saying that this redemption is nothing other than the compassion of the Lord upon her, stemming from the Soul—a free, strong, high, and exalted kindness from a holy place. This is what enabled her to rise and return to her original strength and prophetic stature, and to the parts of her essence, in such a way that every individual becomes a **chariot** <note>term: "מרכבה" (Merkavah) — a 'throne' or 'vehicle' for the Divine Presence (Shekhinah)</note> for the Divine Presence, each according to their value and importance. This is the meaning of "I did not know"—that I had no personal merit to be redeemed, but that which has been prepared for me is "set me as the chariots of my people." The word "my people" <note>original: "עמי" (Ammi)</note> is related to the word "noble" <note>original: "נדיב" (Nadiv)</note>, referring to the kindness of the Blessed One, meaning the "Noble One of the world." The verse should be read as "My people [from] the Noble One." And it is stated in the Midrash of our Sages: "Before I was aware, my soul set me"—the verse speaks of the Assembly of Israel. The Assembly of Israel says to the nations of the world: "Rejoice not against me, O my enemy; though I have fallen, I shall arise; though I sit in darkness, the Lord shall be a light unto me" <note>Micah 7:8</note>. She applied to herself the phrase "Before I was aware, my soul set me," thus far the quote. Behold, the verse is explained as the Assembly of Israel speaking about the future Redemption, and the intention is that she wonders at herself: how, while being in the depths of the darkness of exile where it was not known or imagined that she would be redeemed, did light and the salvation of the Lord come to her in the blink of an eye? They said "my soul set me" to refer to the flow of great kindness, as if it were written: "I did not know *how* my soul set me," etc. Alternatively, "my soul" refers to the Holy One, Blessed be He, as stated in the **Book of Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note> on the verse "With my soul have I desired Thee in the night" <note>Isaiah 26:9</note>. And at the end of the Midrash, they said "my noble people"—my people was walking like the word of the Life of the Worlds. Thus, the explanation of the verse is: "I did not know how my soul [God] set me as chariots," or "my soul," which is His blessed kindness according to these interpretations, "set me as chariots," meaning chariots for the Divine Presence as mentioned above. Or, it could mean being a rider upon the high places of the nations to pursue them, and this is only through the kindness of the Master of the world, for even if a decree of destruction was made against me, the Holy One, Blessed be He, removed it from me. "My people" refers to His kindness, the Life of the Worlds. And the main distinction of the interpretation...

It uses the metaphor of the "nut" and the "chariot" (Merkavah) to explain how the people become a vessel for the Divine Presence.</summary>

# 48
# Song of Songs

A great merit was spoken of with wisdom and purpose; it too is necessary to be a force for the household <note>meaning the people of Israel</note> to return, to adorn, and to refine them, helping to awaken the redemption. This is what was taught in the Midrash Rabbati: "A wall from yourself, peace between Me and My world." For, God forbid, had they not accepted My Torah, I would have returned the world to void and chaos <note>original: "תהו ובהו" (tohu va-vohu) — referring to the primordial state of the world in Genesis 1:2</note>, etc. This comes to teach about a specific kind of strength. And the explanation of the verse until this point says: and in this way, the intention was great for us and in my grace and my actions. And afterward it says, **"What will you see in the Shulammite?"** <note>original: "מה תחזו בשולמית" (mah techazu ba-Shulamit)</note>. He returned to speak with His heavenly entourage <note>term: "פמליא שלו" (pamalia shelo) — the divine court or assembly of angels</note>: "Have I not included you with Me?" And He said of her merit, so you shall know, **"What will you see in the Shulammite? Like the dance of the two camps,"** referring to the latter paths mentioned above.

**How beautiful are your steps in sandals, O noble daughter!** ->Behold, how good and how pleasant are these words!<-
**The curves of your thighs are like ornaments, the work of...** ->Rashi, of blessed memory, in his commentary, noted their praise.<-
**...an artisan’s hands:** ->The Holy One, Blessed be He, praises Israel, and they listen.<-

From the top down, they begin from his head of **fine gold** and descend and examine until his **legs are pillars of marble** <note>referring to the description of the beloved in Song of Songs 5:11-15</note>. This is because they come to describe their softness, to bring down His Divine Presence <note>term: "שכינה" (Shekhinah) — the indwelling presence of God in the physical world</note> from the upper realms to the lower ones. And he merits this praise from the bottom up: **"How beautiful are your steps,"** referring to the feet, and he continues praising upward until **"Your head is upon you like Carmel"** <note>Song of Songs 7:6</note>, up to the place of the daughter for the union.

However, one might wonder about this, for we have seen other praises from above to below where He praises her. If he says this, it is to improve the previous praise which does not exit and descend from the "mercies of the womb" <note>likely referring to divine compassion</note>. Therefore, he did not speak in this order because it is from above. Yet it still stands in the acclamation that descends from the womb to the witnesses. It is possible to say, with some distance, that since it does not include all members of the body down to the feet, he did not speak to measure this strength. 

But the closer explanation, according to our path, will be more precise: since this acclamation occurs after the Redemption, and its cause is to bring the Assembly of Israel <note>term: "כנסת ישראל" (Knesset Yisrael) — the personification of the Jewish people</note> close and to unite her to Him—after she had been in a state of "hiding of the face" <note>original: "הסתר פנים" (hester panim) — a theological concept where God's presence is not felt during times of suffering</note> and distant for a long time—and as he said, "the good ones of the Shulammite," and as was chosen. Therefore, the acclamation was entirely from the bottom up, following the intention written by Rashi, of blessed memory, and the matters are clear.

Behold, He began by saying: **"How beautiful... in sandals."** This means that at the time when the feet are adorned to enter their inheritance, they are quick to run toward what is desired. This is in contrast to your previous delay and laziness in performing a commandment <note>term: "מצוה" (mitzvah)</note>. As the teacher said: "Be light as an eagle and run like a deer" <note>referring to Ethics of the Fathers (Pirkei Avot) 5:20</note>, etc. Then, after the Redemption in the days of the Messiah, it will not be so. For the material of the human body will be refined and pure, adorned for the service of the Name <note>God</note>. As the scripture says: **"And I will remove the heart of stone..."** <note>Ezekiel 36:26</note>. In this way, the power of the Evil Inclination will not be in its strength and might as it is now, before the coming of our Messiah. Therefore, even the lower limbs, which are more material, coarse, and thick, will then be in good repair, so that they will not be flawed in divine service at all. This is why the Holy One, Blessed be He, praised her and said: **"How beautiful are your steps"** and your feet **"in sandals."** 

He mentioned "sandals" to hint at the density of their physical matter, which is greater than the other limbs of the body, for the sandal is a hint to the material world. This is as the commentators said on the verse: "Remove your sandals from your feet" <note>Exodus 3:5</note>, etc. He stated the reason for her steps being in sandals, yet nonetheless "very beautiful," to say that there is no flaw in them at all. And He said **"noble daughter"** <note>original: "בת נדיב" (bat nadiv)</note> to indicate that then she is worthy to be called the daughter of the "Noble One of the World" <note>God</note> because of her complete devotion and attachment to Him. Afterward, He ascended to praise the part of the **thighs**, which are higher limbs, and said that they possess perfect beauty and purpose...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically analyzing the spiritual significance of the beauty and return of the "Shulammite" (the Assembly of Israel) and her relationship with the Divine and the angelic hosts.</meta>

## Commentary

To the Assembly of Israel: **"Return, return, O Shulammite,"** etc. After Solomon wrote in his holy words regarding the inheritance, "Return, return to your former state and your original grace." Because this "return" involves many matters: his desire to return to the Land of Israel—which is the Ingathering of the Exiles—and from there to return to being cleaved to the Blessed Name through the building of the Holy Temple and the indwelling of the *Shekhinah* <note>term: "Shekhinah" — the Divine Presence or the feminine aspect of God dwelling within the world</note> within it. Therefore, he doubled the words and said, **"Return, return, O Shulammite,"** and then repeated, **"Return, return, that we may look upon you."**

The intention is this: even the angels, upon seeing the words of the Blessed Name, show that they desire the inheritance of Israel and their goodness, their perfection, and their Holy Temple. Even if it follows from this that the *Shekhinah* will be among the lower beings <note>meaning humans on Earth</note> and not among the upper beings <note>meaning the angels in Heaven</note>, they nonetheless say, "Return, return." For although they might lose out in one respect, they gain from another. And that gain is **"that we may look upon you,"** for through Israel they will merit to gaze upon the renewal of God's light and the great abundance that flows into the world of angels through the "unifications" <note>term: "Yichudim" — Kabbalistic meditations or deeds intended to harmonize different aspects of the Divine</note> that Israel performs for God, as well as the fulfillment of the Torah, the commandments, and the service of the Holy Temple. Regarding the question "Why do you desire the *Shekhinah* among the lower beings?"—we shall address that.

And this is what is meant by **"that we may look upon you"**—specifically *in* you, *because* of you, and *through* you. The Holy One, Blessed be He, said to the angels: "What will you see in the Shulammite?" And they replied and said: **"Like the dance of the two camps"** <note>original: "כמחולת המחנים" (ke-macholat ha-machanayim)</note>. This means: just as we were happy and delighted with Jacob when he came from the house of Laban. As our Sages of blessed memory said—Rabbi Berekhiah in the name of Rabbi Levi—that sixty thousand <note>The Hebrew text says "shishim ribo," which actually means 600,000, the traditional number of the Israelites in the desert</note> angels were dancing and leaping before our father Jacob when he left the house of Laban. They were saying "camp" and "twenty myriads," etc. And "Jacob was left" <note>A reference to Genesis 32:24</note> when he arrived... a camp of angels, this makes sixty thousand, and they called the name of that place "Mahanaim" (Two Camps). 

There is no doubt, Heaven forbid, that this was not in vain, but for their benefit, since they attain great abundance through the renewal caused by him. This is what they were destined to answer regarding the standing of the sixty thousand who were destined to come from him and receive the Torah and the commandments—sixty against sixty. Heaven forbid, the double of sixty which is 120 myriads, and they were gazing and dancing before him. Therefore, since they know that it will be so for them through the inheritance of Israel—and even more so—they said, "Return, return," etc. And this is what is meant by "Like the dance of the two camps," according to the words of the Sages.

And it is possible to interpret **"Return, return, that we may look upon you"** more simply. The intention is to say that the angels said to the Assembly of Israel: "Return, return to your land and your Temple." And they did not only dance then, but also in the blessed future, which involves a great level and an exceeding increase until the ultimate end. The Holy One, Blessed be He, said: "What will you see in the Shulammite?" and they answer: "Like the dance of the two camps." This means: just like the dance of the camps that was made for Jacob when he came from the house of Laban, so shall we see in the Shulammite in the future. 

This is what our Sages of blessed memory said in the Midrash: "In the future, the Holy One, Blessed be He, will lead a dance for the righteous in the time to come." For it is written, "Mark well her ramparts" <note>Psalm 48:14; the Hebrew word for 'rampart' (chel) is homonymic with the word for 'dance' (chol)</note>, and they will point to Him with a finger, saying: "For this is God, our God forever and ever" <note>Psalm 48:15</note>, etc. And through the wisdom of God, they will attain a level of attachment and perception greater than this.

It is also found in the *Midrash Rabbati*: Rabbi Samuel bar Hanina bar Isaac said in the name of Rabbi Hanina: Four times "Return" is written here (**Return, return... return, return**), corresponding to the four kingdoms <note>The four historical empires that exiled Israel: Babylon, Persia, Greece, and Rome</note> that ruled over Israel. And the nations will say to them: "Come under our hand in peace, and we will grant you peace," etc. Here they explained all the repetitions of the word "Return." 

According to the plain meaning, all these are the words of God. According to their way of interpreting it, God said "Return" four times for four reasons for the return and the inheritance: Repentance, Mercy, the Merit of the Ancestors, and their portion in the future. They attain this from two sides: the first two reasons belong to Israel themselves, and the last two come from the outside. 

And He called her "The Shulammite" <note>related to the word 'Shalom' (Peace) or 'Sheleimut' (Perfection)</note> to hint at the matter of:
->merit<-

<summary>This page provides a homiletic commentary on Song of Songs 7:1, explaining the repeated call for Israel to "return" as a reference to both physical and spiritual restoration. It highlights the role of angels who joyfully anticipate Israel's redemption because the people's service on Earth generates spiritual abundance for the heavenly realms.</summary>

# Song of Songs

A great merit is found in the "wall" <note>The "wall" is often a metaphor in Midrash for the Torah or the steadfast faith of the Jewish people</note>, and its "branches" are a miracle; it joins to become a "wing" for many of the aforementioned commandments, to recognize, awaken, and clarify them, and to be of benefit in awakening the redemption. This is as stated in the seventh chapter of *Midrash Rabbah* <note>A classic collection of ancient homiletic interpretations of the Bible</note> regarding the verse: "I am a wall—from now on, let there be peace between me and my world." For God had said that if they did not accept the Torah, He would return the world to "chaos and void" <note>original: "tohu va-vohu"</note>, and so on. Then the verse is explained until it says:

"And we shall rest in You, and be desired," for the intention was that they would merit my investigations and my actions. Afterward, it says: "What will you see in the Shulammite?" Had He returned to speak with His "treasured people" <note>term: "Am Segulah" — a common biblical term for the people of Israel</note>, for I have included you with Me and said, "And we shall rest in You, you shall find pasture." "What will you see in the Shulammite? Like the dance of two camps," following the paths taught previously.

### How beautiful are your steps in sandals, O prince's daughter!
### The curves of your thighs are like jewels, the work
### of a master's hands.

->Behold, how good and how pleasant are<-
->the words of our Sages of blessed memory in their explanation of the praise<-
->of the Holy One, Blessed be He. Israel praises Him<-
->from the top down; they begin with "His head is like the finest<-
->gold" and they descend until "His legs are like pillars of marble."<-

This is because they seek the merit of bringing down His *Shekhinah* <note>term: "Shekhinah" — the Divine Presence dwelling in the physical world</note> from the upper realms to the lower realms. But His praise for Israel is arranged from the bottom up: "**How beautiful are your steps**"—these are the feet—and it continues going until "**your head upon you is like Carmel,**" until it reaches the praise of her jewelry, etc. However, one must look closely at this, for we have seen other praises from above where He praises her from the top down. If one tries to resolve the previous praise by saying it does not descend from the parts of the head, and therefore did not bother to explain this because it is "above," it stands in the first praise which descends from the head to the extremities. One might say, albeit distantly, that since it does not include all the members of the body down to the feet, it did not bother to explain this intention.

But the closer explanation, which aligns with our approach, is that since this praise occurs after the Redemption, its purpose is to draw the Assembly of Israel near and pull her toward Him after she had been in a state of "hiding of the face" <note>term: "Hester Panim" — a theological concept where God's presence is not felt, often during times of suffering</note> and distant for a long time. As it was said, "Return, return, O Shulammite," as has been explained. Therefore, the praise here was from the bottom up, according to the intention written by our Sages, and the matters are clear.

Behold, He began by saying, "**How beautiful... in sandals,**" with the knowledge that the feet are crowned when they are quick to run to perform a "desire" <note>a good deed or God's will</note>, or conversely, from the perspective of their lingering and being lazy in going toward a commandment <note>term: "Mitzvah" — a religious duty or good deed</note>, as the Tanna <note>a sage from the era of the Mishnah</note> said: "Be light as an eagle and run like a deer," etc. However, after the Redemption in the days of the Messiah, it will not be so; for the matter of the human body will be refined and pure, crowned with the service of the Name, as the verse says: "And I will remove the heart of stone," etc. In such a way, the power of the "inclination" <note>term: "Yetzer" — the human drive or impulse, often the "evil inclination"</note> will be completely decided in its strength and might as it is now before the coming of our Messiah. Therefore, even the lower limbs, which are more "material," coarse, and thick, will then be in a state of physical and intellectual repair, such that they will not be flawed by transgression at all.

This is why the Holy One, Blessed be He, praised her and said, "**How beautiful are your steps**" and your feet "**in sandals.**" The mention of "sandals" hints at the coarseness of their material nature, more so than the other limbs of the body, for the shoe is a hint to materiality, as the commentators said on the verse "Take your sandals off your feet," etc. <note>referring to Moses at the burning bush</note>. And He gave the reason for her steps being in sandals, yet they are very beautiful, meaning there is no flaw in them at all. And He said, "**O prince's daughter**" to indicate that she is then worthy to be called the daughter of the "Noble of the World" <note>referring to God or Abraham</note> because of her righteousness and her cleaving to Him. Afterward, He ascended to praise the thighs, which are higher limbs, saying that they possess perfect beauty and purpose.

The text emphasizes that in the Messianic age, even the most "coarse" or physical parts of the human experience (symbolized by sandals and feet) will be refined and holy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically focusing on the verse "Return, return, O Shulammite." It explores the dialogue between God, the angels, and the Assembly of Israel regarding the future redemption and the restoration of the Divine Presence.</meta>

## Commentary

To the Assembly of Israel <note>term: "Knesset Yisrael" — the mystical personification of the Jewish people</note>: "Return, return, O Shulammite" <note>original: "shuvie shuvie ha-Shulamit"</note>. All this follows the words of Solomon regarding the matters that necessitate the redemption: return, return to your former state and your initial vitality. Because this promise contains many things that seem distant—the goodness of the Land and the praise that comes from the gathering of the exiles to be attached to the Name [of God] through the building of the Holy Temple and the dwelling of the Divine Presence <note>term: "Shekhinah" — the localized, feminine aspect of God's presence on earth</note> within it—therefore he doubled the words and said, "Return, return, O Shulammite," and repeated it again, saying, "Return, return, that we may gaze upon you." The intention is that even the angels <note>original: "malachim"</note>, who are under His authority, repeat the words of God, showing that they too desire the redemption of Israel and their well-being for the sake of their perfection and their Temple.

Furthermore, it follows from this that the Divine Presence will be among those below [on earth] and not just those above [in heaven]. Nevertheless, they too shall say "Return, return," for they are desiring supporters who clarify the strength and gain from the opening. They say, "That we may gaze upon you," because through Israel they will merit to see and gaze upon the renewal of the sweetness of the Lord and the great light that flows into the world of angels through the unification <note>term: "Yichud" — a Kabbalistic act of unifying divine attributes through prayer or mitzvot</note> that unifies God, and the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments <note>original: "mitzvot"</note>, and the service of the Holy Temple. This is like what the Sages said: "The Divine Presence among the lower beings is a 'higher need'" <note>original: "tzorech gavoah" — suggesting that God's presence on earth fulfills a divine purpose, not just a human one</note>. This is the meaning of "that we may gaze upon you"—meaning *in* you, because of your intelligence and because of you.

Then the Holy One, Blessed be He, said to the angels: "What will you see in the Shulammite?" And they answered and said: "As the dance of the two camps" <note>original: "ke-macholat ha-machanayim"</note>. That is to say: just as we were happy and delighted with Jacob when he left the house of Laban. As our Sages of blessed memory taught in the Midrash in the name of Rabbi Levi: sixty myriads <note>note: "sixty myriads" equals 600,000, the traditional number of adult males in the Exodus</note> of angels were dancing and leaping before our father Jacob when he left the house of Laban, and behind him was a camp of twenty myriads. This is what is written: "And Jacob said when he saw them, 'This is God's camp'"—this refers to sixty myriads—"and he called the name of that place Mahanaim" <note>note: "Mahanaim" means "two camps" or "double camp" in Hebrew</note>, which equals 120 [myriads]. Without a doubt, this was only for their own benefit, for they were attaining a great flow of renewed light because of him. For they were destined to attain through the 600,000 [souls] destined to emerge from him and receive the Torah and the commandments—sixty corresponding to sixty. Thus, the heavens were doubled by them to 120 myriads. Therefore, they were dancing and leaping before him. Thus, because they know that it will be the same for them during the redemption of Israel, and even more so, they say, "Return, return," etc. And this is the secret of "as the dance of the two camps" according to their words, of blessed memory.

And it is possible to explain "Return, return, that we may gaze upon you" more simply: the intention is that the angels said to the Assembly of Israel, "Return, return to your land and to your Temple." Not only did they dance then, but in the future, we shall see a vision of great and exceeding loftiness without end. And the Holy One, Blessed be He, says, "What will you see in the Shulammite?" and they answer, "As the dance of the two camps." This means: just as the dance of the camps was performed for Jacob when he left the house of Laban, so shall we see for the Shulammite in the future. This is what our Sages of blessed memory said in the Midrash: "In the future, the Holy One, Blessed be He, will lead a dance for the righteous." It is written that He will invite them to the dance and point to Him with a finger, saying: "For this is God, our God, forever and ever" <note>Psalm 48:15</note>. And through this, they will reach a level of attachment and attainment greater than this.

And it is brought in the *Midrash Rabbati*: Rabbi Samuel bar Hiyya bar Yudan said in the name of Rabbi Hanina: "Return, return" is written here four times, corresponding to the four kingdoms <note>note: Traditionally Babylon, Persia, Greece, and Rome/Edom, representing the major exiles of Jewish history</note> that rule over Israel; yet Israel enters under their power in peace and exits in peace. Behold, they have explained all the repetitions of the word "Return." And according to the plain meaning <note>term: "Peshat" — the literal or direct level of biblical interpretation</note>, these are all the words of God. And according to their way, all four mentions of "Return" refer to the four corners of the earth, the gathering and the redemption, everything mentioned above—their repentance, mercy, the merit of the ancestors, and their portion... because they are of two types: the first two [mentions of "Return"] pertain to Israel themselves, and the last two come from the outside. And the name "Shulammite" hints at the matter of...

The repetition of the word "return" is linked to both the four historical exiles and a future "dance of the righteous" led by God.</summary>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on the Song of Songs. Having previously discussed the "Shulammite" and the collective identity of Israel, the author now moves into a limb-by-limb anatomical allegory, common in medieval and mystical Jewish exegesis, to describe the spiritual virtues and modest behaviors of the Jewish people.</meta>

# Song of Songs

Regarding the verse "You shall eat no fat or blood" <note>original: "kol chelev ve-kol dam lo tokhelu" — Leviticus 3:17</note>, this refers to the choice to distance oneself from forbidden foods. For us, it is proper and correct to add even more, as in the practice of "fencing" <note>term: "seyag" — a rabbinic "fence" around the law to prevent accidental transgression</note> oneself within what is otherwise permitted. Concerning this, it is said that one who is "enticed by dangers" <note>referring to the risks of over-indulgence</note> should be cautious. From this, there is great praise for the purity and merit of the companions, and for the vessels of eating and drinking being established in a proper way. It also mentions "You shall kindle no fire" <note>Exodus 35:3</note> in them at all.

### Your two breasts are like two fawns, twins of a gazelle:

<margin>
Behold, it was explained... "Your two breasts," etc. It was further explained earlier in the "First Praise" regarding the hands that influence and pour out charity <note>term: "tzedakah" — righteous giving</note> constantly.
</margin>
And he spoke in the likeness of breasts that constantly pour out milk to nourish. The matter is clear, as it will be correctly explained: look closely and you will find above where he said "like two fawns," referring to the lightness and speed in performing charity and kindness, where both hands are equal—the left hand acting like the right. He said "twins of a gazelle" <note>original: "te’omei tzeviyah"</note> regarding their speed in performing the commandments <note>term: "mitzvot" — religious deeds or obligations</note> that are carried out through physical action. <margin>It is also possible to interpret this regarding actual physical modesty and the role of women within the Assembly of Israel <note>term: "Knesset Yisrael" — the personification of the Jewish people</note>.</margin> The intention is that even though breasts are part of the beauty and praise of a woman, the people of Israel did not use them for evil purposes or for pride and self-display to incite the "Evil Inclination" <note>term: "Yetzer Ha-Ra" — the innate human impulse toward sin or selfishness</note> in the hearts of the young men of Israel or to cause them to stumble. These limbs remained without blemish, even though they are "dangerous" <note>referring to their potential for sexual attraction</note> and close to corruption. He compared them to fawns and twins of a gazelle because it is said that when a gazelle sleeps, it keeps one eye open and one eye closed. So too is the metaphor here regarding the daughters of Israel: they are modest and pure. It is as if they have one eye open to what concerns their husbands during their time together, and one eye closed to all others. This is a beautiful metaphor that holds true for the subject.

### Your neck is like an ivory tower; your eyes are pools in Heshbon by the gate of Bath-rabbim; your nose is like the tower of Lebanon looking toward Damascus:

<margin>
This too will be explained according to the method used in the "First Praise" regarding the distancing of ugliness, which depends on the neck, for the neck is the beauty of the whole body. As the Sages <note>original: "Zal" — of blessed memory</note> said: "What constitutes the primary beauty of a person? An upright stature." And here it says "Your neck is like a tower..."
</margin>
...of ivory <note>original: "shen"</note> to indicate purity, merit, and "whiteness" <note>an allegory for spiritual cleanliness</note>, as the Assembly of Israel is then in holiness and purity. Throughout all this time, despite the purity of the Assembly of Israel above all other nations, there is still the potential for the dominion of the Evil Inclination, and the "heart of stone" still exists. Therefore, he previously said "like the Tower of David," but here he says "like a tower of ivory"—which is white and bright—to indicate the merit and purity of this union, which is more refined than it was previously. He then follows with the eyes, which are the first things hinted at regarding the laws of the body, for they are the "brokers of sin" <note>original: "sarsorei d'aveira" — a classic Rabbinic concept that the eyes see and the heart desires, leading to sin</note>. But in this state, the eyes shall be like "pools in Heshbon" <note>original: "breichot b'cheshbon"</note> that draw many waters; so too shall the eyes draw holiness and purity in abundance.

<sig>13 I B 5</sig>

<meta>catchword: על (Upon)</meta>

<summary>This page provides an allegorical commentary on the Song of Songs, interpreting the beloved's breasts as symbols of charity and the modest behavior of Jewish women, and the neck as a symbol of spiritual purity. The author emphasizes the importance of "fencing" the law and maintaining physical and spiritual "whiteness" to resist the Evil Inclination.</summary>

Having discussed the feet and shoes on the previous page, the author now interprets the "thighs," "navel," and "belly" as symbols of the craftsmanship of God, the covenant of circumcision, and the disciplined consumption of physical and spiritual nourishment.</meta>

**Page 25**
->**Commentary**<-

...like ornaments, some of which are still [refined], and then, besides being sweet, they are the most important of the crafts—beautiful and perfect in their workmanship, as if by the hands of a master. Furthermore, our Rabbis of blessed memory <note>original: "Zal" — abbreviation for Zichronam Livracha</note> explained this as a reference to a "link" <note>term: "huliya" — a link in a chain or a segment of a necklace</note> of pearls. And one who is amazed by this says: "the work of a master's hands" refers to the work of the hands of the Master of the Universe, the Holy One, Blessed be He. As the final form of the verse explains, the matter of "ornaments" <note>original: "hali'im" — Song of Songs 7:2</note> does not refer to jewelry made of gold, but rather to that which is the handiwork of the Holy One, who is the Craftsman of the world. This is something made of pearls, more precious than all else. This refers to the "Sanctification of the Name" based on the demonstration of the merit and clarity of the "thighs"—all are cleansed from every sin, except for that which is between the thighs, which is the "Sign of the Holy Covenant" <note>term: "Berit Kodesh" — circumcision</note>. This indicates that through this alone is the Community of Israel revived and protected from promiscuity. They also hope to sanctify themselves even in what is permitted to them, so that the union of life with one's wife is done in holiness and purity, observing the appointed times for fertility in their proper season, according to the custom of the abstemious and the holy. 

In the Great Midrash <note>term: "Midrash Rabbah" — a collection of ancient homiletic interpretations of the Bible</note>, Rabbi Jonathan translated "The curves of your thighs" <note>Song of Songs 7:2</note> as: all the wheat and delicacies <note>original: "hittin u-perchikim"</note> with which Israel is sustained and delighted in this world, they merit because of the "danger" <note>referring to the self-control required regarding the sexual organs</note> between the thighs. From these words, we have learned the explanation of the verse according to our way, as has been clarified.

Behold, these "companions" are the friends 

## Your navel is a moon-shaped bowl, let it not lack
and the vessels that surround the food

## the mixed wine; your belly is a heap of wheat hedged
and the drink, for within them is their place to rest

## with lilies.

for the nourishment of the entire body. It is known that nothing damages or gladdens a person in this world and the World to Come like heavy and sweet foods and drinks. As we mentioned above regarding the verse "Your teeth are like a flock of ewes," etc., regarding the matter of drinking, he said: "Your navel is a moon-shaped bowl." Furthermore, Rashi <note>the preeminent medieval French commentator Rabbi Shlomo Yitzchaki</note> of blessed memory explained in his commentary: "Your navel is like a bowl of 'included' waters," etc., to show that it does not drip or lack, like silver wine or the like. Rather, the "sweetening" that enters it is like the "mixture" <note>term: "mezeg" — the dilution of strong wine with water to make it palatable and temperate</note> in which there is no deficiency at all. 

Not only this, but even the sweetening and the crowning decoration belong to those who lack; for within us are enclosed all the statutes of the Torah and its fruits, such as the matter of the "Heave-Offering" <note>term: "Terumah" — a portion of the harvest given to the Priests</note> and the "Tithe" <note>term: "Ma'aser"</note> and similar things, based on the "purity of the holy," which is the likeness of the "included waters." Also, to indicate the absence of excess and overabundance, he said, "let it not lack the mixed wine," to show that drinking should be according to a balanced mixture. This also hints at the distancing of folly, for the drinking of wine should be diluted and not "raw" <note>undiluted/straight</note>. The explanation of "let it not lack the mixture" is as they researched in the Midrash: its mixture shall not be lacking... "The Lord is my shepherd, I shall not want" <note>Psalm 23:1</note>. The intention is that there should be no lack in drinking of the "will of the mixture" <note>the proper ratio</note>. This represents the world in its highest material quality, for without order, all these are lacking. He promises that it is clean from every flaw and corruption; rather, it is refined, pure, and bright like the "radiance of the firmament." 

Regarding the portion of food, which is on the level of purity, permission, and holiness, he said: "Your belly is a heap of wheat," to indicate the gathering of food within it. It is of the finest kind, such as wheat, and it is in the necessary and essential amount, just as wheat is essential to the world and the mainstay of sustenance, as is known. Being under the protection of the laws of the Torah and the Sages, he said: "hedged with lilies." 

Furthermore, the Sages of blessed memory said in the Great Midrash: regarding one to whom they pointed out a <unclear>stack</unclear> of wheat and told him it was [ritually forbidden], he immediately withdrew his hand and did not taste it. What caused him not to taste? Did a snake bite him? Did a scorpion sting him? No—rather, he draws near and tastes the laws and reasons of the words of the Torah, which are <unclear>stubborn/firm</unclear> as a hedge...

It emphasizes moderation in physical pleasure (wine and food) and the "fence" of the Torah that prevents transgression.</summary>
