This page moves upward to the head, focusing on the intellect, thoughts, and the historical triumph of monotheism.</meta>

**Page 3**

->**Song of Songs**<-

...since He whitens their sins <note>original: "melabin avonoteihem" — a reference to Isaiah 1:18, where red sins become white as snow</note> and so on, all hearts rejoice in Him. The interpretation is: even if you have strayed like a crown, you should relate yourself to the likeness of the attribute of shame and humility. Behold, in this way, the face and the forehead will be pure and clean, and every blemish encountered in them will be washed away, leaving no defect or pulse of sin at all.

# Your head upon you is like Carmel, and the hair
# of your head is like purple; a king is held captive
# in the tresses. 
<note>Song of Songs 7:6</note>

Come and see <note>original: "Ta Chazi" — a common Aramaic opening in mystical texts</note>: the praise here is for the purity of the head and what is within it—the secret of the brain. For there lies the dwelling place of the intellect and the thoughts. It is the primary and essential member of the body, which can be very dangerous if it turns to think evil thoughts or wicked contemplations, whether regarding a positive commandment or, even more so, a negative commandment. This is especially true if it becomes wallowed in the mire of base opinions and evil beliefs regarding the fundamentals of the Torah, its secrets, and its cornerstones, which touch upon His divinity, may He be blessed, and His holy Torah. Upon these things, the absolute destruction of the living soul depends. 

It is even more critical if the ears are joined to the collective of the head, for they are the companions and the vessels of the sense of hearing, upon which the inner self of a person and the purity of the soul depend. As the scripture says: "Hear, and your soul shall live" <note>Isaiah 55:3</note>. And as our Sages of blessed memory said in explaining the verse: "Behold, the head..." For without doubt, from this perspective as well, the head is beholden to the King of the Universe. Any blemish, damage, or corruption in it corrupts all the links and members of the body along with it.

Therefore, the text here praises the Community of Israel in the time of the Days of the Messiah. For then, the head in its entirety will be established in the service of God, may He be blessed—pure and clean from every blemish. The impurity of evil thoughts and beliefs shall not pass through it. On the contrary, it will be pure and clear with true beliefs and holy thoughts in the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments. For then "the earth shall be full of the knowledge of the Lord" <note>Isaiah 11:9</note>. 

Furthermore, as a man opens his ears from hearing of blood <note>referring to violence or gossip</note>, his ear will be like a hopper <note>term: "hapharkas" — a funnel-like vessel used in the Temple, here meaning the ear is wide open to receive holiness</note> for the Torah and the Testimony. This is the meaning of **Your head upon you is like Carmel**. Our Rabbis, of blessed memory, interpreted this in the *Midrash Rabbah* as a reference to Elijah the Prophet ascending Mount Carmel. The intention is that just as his ascent to Mount Carmel crushed and conquered the belief in the Ba'als <note>term: "Ba'alim" — local Canaanite deities whose worship Elijah challenged</note>, which was the "father of the fathers of impurity" <note>term: "avi avot hatumah" — the highest level of ritual impurity, here applied to false beliefs</note> and evil faiths, and he proved and compelled with a decisive sign and miracle the acceptance of His divinity, may He be blessed, before the eyes of all Israel until they all woke up as one, crying: "The Lord is God!" <note>1 Kings 18:39</note>. He repeated the words for the sake of strengthening the acceptance of His divinity and His Torah. 

So too is **your head upon you**. The text is precise in using the word **upon you** <note>original: "alayich"</note> to say that from the head, a distance from defiling thoughts shall spread over the rest of your body, bringing instead the closeness and fulfillment of pure and true thoughts. From it also come the vessels of hearing, used for good and not for evil. It is not that the hollow space of the head is blemished or corrupted; on the contrary, from it is drawn the purity and clarity of the body and its members. 

Furthermore, the text praises by saying: **and the hair of your head is like purple**. This indicates that even the hair—which is something superfluous and not an essential limb of the body—is also without any blemish at all. This is not only regarding the prohibition of "rounding the corners of the head" <note>a biblical prohibition against certain types of haircuts</note>, but also regarding how a person might groom themselves. This relates to the matter said of Joseph, who used to curl his hair <note>a Midrashic tradition about Joseph's youthful vanity</note>, and the matter of that young man who became a Nazirite <note>term: "Nazir" — one who took a vow of holiness, including not cutting their hair</note> in order to shave his head for the sake of Heaven. This is as Simeon the Righteous <note>note: a famous High Priest from the Second Temple era</note> said, that he only ate the guilt-offering of a Nazirite like that young man, who sought to shave his hair because he wanted to subdue his evil inclination. This is according to our explanation above. Thus, the matter is clear: the praise is balanced and consistent. **And the hair of your head is like purple** means it is clean, clear, and pure like purple wool <note>term: "argaman" — a precious royal purple/red dye used in the Tabernacle</note>. See further what is said in the Midrash regarding Daniel...

<meta>The previous page concluded with the symbolism of the head as the seat of the intellect and the brain, and touched upon the sense of hearing. This page continues the upward progression of the "limbs" of the Community of Israel as described in the Song of Songs, focusing on the eyes and the nose/face as representations of moral calculation and the tempering of anger.</meta>

**Commentary**

Upon the **"gate of the daughter of many"** <note>original: "bat rabbim" — Song of Songs 7:5</note>, it is as if they are at the gate of the body to warn them by distancing the stumbling blocks of sins and by drawing near to the fulfillment of the performance of the commandments. And if we interpret the "daughter of many" as the soul that is a partner to the body—in which many companions are included—it will also align with our path. 

"Your eyes are pools in Heshbon" <note>Song of Songs 7:5</note>. Regarding the eyes, there is a much greater praise in the language than there is in the praise of the tongue. For "your eyes are doves" <note>Song of Songs 4:1</note> indicates a multiplication of Torah, just as it is here, for it is written "pools in Heshbon" <note>Heshbon means 'calculation' or 'account'</note>. According to their number, the eyes are very far from corruption and defect, and the guidance there is in the measure of purity. They earn merit and grant merit to all the members of the body, as our Sages of blessed memory said: "There are 248 limbs in a man, and they all follow the eyes." 

Afterward, he praises her further and says: **"Your nose is like the tower of Lebanon,"** etc. Remi <note>Likely Rabbi Meir ibn Arama, a 15th-century commentator</note> of blessed memory wrote in his commentary: "Perhaps it can be interpreted as the literal nose, but only in terms of the plain meaning and not as a metaphor; for what praise is there in a nose that is large and upright like a tower? Rather, perhaps it refers to the face." This is balanced, for perhaps it is a singular term, and it does not say "your nostrils" <note>plural</note> above the forehead; and this speaks of the primary recognition of the face.

And the Ibn Ezra <note>Rabbi Abraham ibn Ezra, the 12th-century scholar</note> of blessed memory wrote: "Your nose is like the tower of Lebanon"—that tower was built without any crookedness at all. Thus, he interpreted it according to its plain meaning as a mention of praise. That which was hinted at according to the Sages of blessed memory, he sought from the harmony of the tower of Lebanon as a sign of balance without any crookedness at all.

And according to our way of praising the members, whose purity and merit can shield a person even before their face, according to the interpretation of Ibn Ezra, this is a simple preciousness. He spoke of the purity of the "nose" and the "nostril" <note>original: "af" and "hotem"</note> as being distanced from anger and wrath <note>In Hebrew, "Af" means both 'nose' and 'anger'</note> because of criminal sin. As our Sages of blessed memory said: "Anyone who is angry is as if he worships idols." Not only does he cause damage in this by tainting the member that is a partner to him, but he also brings damage and defect to all members of the body, as is known. 

Therefore, he praised and said, "like the tower of Lebanon." This is from the words of the Sages in the *Midrash Rabbati* <note>The Great Midrash on Song of Songs</note>: "But the 'whites' <note>lebanot</note> are open..." This is beautiful because, unlike the forehead, no wrath or fury comes out of this companion, only silence. One who is "small of nose" <note>meaning short-tempered</note> and hard of anger is difficult to appease, but here it is "like the tower of Lebanon looking toward Damascus"—for all hearts rejoice in him.

And it is possible to say, "looking toward Damascus," to say that if wrath and anger are attributed to the nose/nostril of the Assembly of Israel, it should only be against the wicked and the walls of this world, such as Damascus. This is because in this matter, it would be a "great blow," as it is written: "The mercies of the wicked are cruel" <note>Proverbs 12:10</note>. Then it "looks toward Damascus"—meaning, in the likeness of the tower of Lebanon which looks toward Damascus and is far from it, so too your "nose" <note>wrath</note> in its fury and anger looks from afar toward the face of the walls of this world and the wicked. 

Furthermore, they said in the Midrash: "Lebanon" is as it says, "This good mountain and the Lebanon" <note>Deuteronomy 3:25</note>. Rabbi Meir says it is called Lebanon because it "whitens" <note>malbin</note> the sins of Israel. According to these words, the verse and the intention are explained: "Your nose"—that is, the anger and wrath that exists in your nostrils—is only for the service of Heaven and to cast fear upon the wicked so that they may improve their way, separate from forbidden things, and achieve repentance. Like the "tower of Lebanon" which whitens the sins of Israel, this comparison is undoubtedly relevant to the purity and merit of the member of the nose.

And by way of the explanation of Remi, who interpreted it as the essence of the face, which is the forehead, it is correctly explained as distancing the "brazenness of the forehead" <note>chutzpah/arrogance</note> and the "hardness of the forehead" mentioned by the disciplined and the empty ones. He mentions before you the attribute of shame and humility, which is the foundation for all the distinguished attributes and the fulfillment of the service of the Creator. And as it will come: "a face towards the back," and this is "Your nose is like the tower of Lebanon," etc., according to the two Midrashim I wrote above.

<summary>This page interprets the "eyes" and "nose" from Song of Songs 7:5, viewing the eyes as tools for moral calculation and the nose as a symbol of controlled anger, which should only be directed at wickedness to inspire repentance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the anatomical and allegorical progression of the "limbs" of the Community of Israel, moving from the eyes and nose discussed on the previous page to the crown of the head and the hair. It explores how the intellect and even the most superficial aspects of a person must be dedicated to purity and correct belief.</meta>

...as mentioned above, which "whitens their sins," etc., so that all hearts rejoice in Him. For since they are associated with the attribute of kindness, they relate to the likeness of the attributes of modesty and humility. Behold, in this way, the face and its covering will be pure and clean from any trace of blemish, and from the presence of any defect or weakness whatsoever.

<margin>7:6</margin> **"Your head upon you is like Carmel, and the hair** <note>The Hebrew word "dalat" refers to the thinness or flowing nature of hair.</note> — Here he praises the purity of the head. **of your head is like purple; a king is held captive in the tresses."** That which is within it is commendable, for the head is the dwelling place of the intellect and the thoughts. It is the chief of the limbs and its principles are uniquely situated. If one turns to think evil thoughts or wicked impulses—whether regarding positive commandments or especially regarding negative "thou shalt not" commandments—[it is a great defect]. This is even more so if one errs and sins regarding inferior opinions and bad beliefs concerning the fundamental principles of the Torah and its foundations, which touch upon His Divinity, may He be blessed, and His holy Torah. Upon these beliefs, the destruction of the soul depends when it departs to the next world. And how much more so if there join to the general "head" the ears (which are limbs, etc.), where the sense of hearing is located, upon which the perfection of man and the purity of the soul depend. As the scripture says: "Hear, and your soul shall live" <note>Isaiah 55:3</note>. This is also as our Sages of blessed memory explained the verse "Behold, here I am," <note>original: "Hineni"</note> for without a doubt, the head is obligated to the King of the Universe to be clear and clean, lest the person corrupt all the other limbs of the body along with it.

Therefore, he praises here the Assembly of Israel in the time of the Days of the Messiah. For then, the head will be properly established in its entirety in the service of God, may He be blessed—pure and clean from every blemish. The impurity of bad thoughts and false beliefs shall not pass through it. Rather, its path will be pure, encompassed by true beliefs and holy thoughts in the fulfillment of the Torah and its commandments. For then "the earth shall be full of knowledge to know the Lord," etc. <note>Isaiah 11:9</note>. Like a person who "stops his ear from hearing of bloodshed" <note>Isaiah 33:15</note>, his ear will be like a hopper <note>original: "apharkas" — a funnel-shaped vessel</note> for the Torah and the testimony.

Thus, "Your head upon you is like Carmel." Our Sages expounded in the Midrash regarding the prophet Elijah, who ascended Mount Carmel. The meaning is that just as Elijah at Mount Carmel shattered and uprooted the belief in Baal—which was the "father of the fathers" of impurity for all false and evil beliefs—and he proved and decided with a definitive sign and wonder the acceptance of His Divinity, may He be blessed, before the eyes of all Israel, until they all called out: "The Lord, He is God" <note>1 Kings 18:39</note>. By repeating those words, they strengthened their acceptance of His Divinity and His Torah. So too, "your head is upon you." The emphasis on the word **upon you** <note>original: "alecha"</note> suggests that from the head, there shall spread over your entire body the distancing of corrupting thoughts and the closeness of pure and true thoughts. This also includes the sense of hearing, using the organ of hearing for good and not for evil. In this way, the head is not blemished or corrupted, but on the contrary, from it is drawn upon you the crown of purity and the merit of the body and its limbs.

Furthermore, he praises her by saying: "and the hair of your head is like purple." This indicates that even the hair—which appears to be a superficial thing and not one of the essential limbs of the body—is also without any blemish at all. It is kept from the violation of "rounding the corners of the head" <note>original: "hakafat pe'at harosh" — the prohibition in Leviticus 19:27</note>. Nor is it used for vanity, as was said regarding the biblical Joseph, who would curl his hair, or the youth who became a Nazirite <note>A person who takes a vow of holiness, including not cutting their hair</note> in order to shave it for the sake of Heaven. As Simeon the Just said, he shaved his hair because he sought to remove his vanity from the world. As we explained above regarding the first fulfillment, it is clear that while the hair is a place prone to corruption, nevertheless the verse praises it: "the hair of your head is like purple." This means it is clean, clear, and pure like the finest purple dye. As they said in the Midrash regarding Daniel, where it is written...

It emphasizes that in the messianic age, thoughts and beliefs will be as clear as Elijah's victory at Mount Carmel, and even superficial traits like hair will be dedicated to God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically focusing on chapter 7, verse 5. It transitions from the description of the "head" and "hair" discussed on the previous page to the "eyes" and the "nose," interpreting these anatomical features as symbols for spiritual discernment, the control of anger, and the pursuit of humility.</meta>

### Commentary

Regarding **"the gate of Bath-rabbim"** <note>original: "בת רבים" (*Bath-rabbim*). Literally "Daughter of Multitudes," referring to a gate in the biblical city of Heshbon mentioned in Song of Songs 7:5.</note>, behold, the beauty of the body is called "Bath-rabbim" because it contains many limbs. And the **eyes**, in their strength, are at the gate of the body to return the limbs to God by removing the stumbling blocks of sins and by bringing near the fulfillment of the performance of the commandments. Specifically, "Bath-rabbim" refers to the soul that spreads throughout the body, which includes many members. This can also be interpreted according to our method.

From now on, there is a greater praise here than in the praise where it says, "Your eyes are doves," etc. There, the praise refers to the fear of God and the abundance of Torah. Here, however, it is written that there are "pools" <note>A reference to the first part of Song of Songs 7:5: "Your eyes are like the pools in Heshbon."</note> in a numeric sense, as the eyes are completely filled, far from imbalance or defect. Their chambers are on the path of purity and merit; they achieve merit for themselves and bring merit to all the members of the body. As our Sages of blessed memory said: **"There are 248 limbs in a person, and they all follow the eyes."** <note>In Jewish tradition, the body is said to have 248 limbs, corresponding to the number of positive commandments in the Torah. The eyes are seen as the leaders of the body's actions.</note>

Afterward, he praises further and says: **"Your nose is like the tower of Lebanon,"** etc. The Remez <note>Rabbi Moses Alsheich (1508–1593), a prominent 16th-century biblical commentator.</note>, of blessed memory, wrote in his commentary: "How can he interpret this as referring to the nose? For in terms of literal meaning or allegory, what praise is there in a nose being large and upright like a tower?" He suggests it refers to the appearance of the face, meaning uniqueness and strength. Regarding the word **"your nose"** <note>original: "אפך" (*apekh*). In Hebrew, this word can mean both "your nose" and "your anger."</note>, he says it refers to the forehead, which is the primary site for recognizing a face.

And the R'abe <note>Rabbi Abraham ibn Ezra (1089–1167), a foundational Spanish Jewish scholar and grammarian.</note>, of blessed memory, wrote: **"Your nose is like the tower of Lebanon"**—this means the tower was plastered smoothly without any roughness at all. He explains the meaning literally, following Rabbi Melitus. The Remez suggests that the comparison to the tower of Lebanon is about evenness without any roughness.

According to our method of praising the limbs for their purity and merit so that they may protect their surroundings, Ibn Ezra's interpretation can be explained more simply. It refers to the purity of the nose and nostrils as being far from **anger** and wrath, which is a specific sin. As our Sages of blessed memory said: **"Anyone who becomes angry is as if he worships idols."** Not only is there a defect in that specific limb, but damage and defect are drawn to all the members of the body, as is known. 

Therefore, he praised it by saying, **"like the tower of Lebanon."** In the *Midrash Rabbah*, Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai says: "Everything is in a 'house' that is open..." <note>A complex midrashic reference comparing the nose and the breath to a gate that should remain open for peace rather than closed in anger.</note> No anger or wrath should emerge from it, only joy and gladness. This is due to "shortness of nose" <note>A Hebrew idiom for patience; the opposite of "long-nosed" or "slow to anger."</note>; being hard to anger and easy to appease, like the tower of Lebanon that **"looks toward Damascus,"** which all hearts rejoice in.

It is possible to say that **"looking toward Damascus"** means that the piety of the Community of Israel regarding the nose is that anger and wrath should only be directed against the wicked and the nations of the world, such as Damascus and its ilk. For in this matter, it is a great commandment, as it is written: "The mercies of the wicked are cruel." <note>Proverbs 12:10</note> This is the secret of **"looking toward Damascus."** Just as the tower looks toward Damascus, which is far from it, so too his nose, in its bitterness and anger, looks from afar toward the nations of the world and the wicked.

Furthermore, they said in the Midrash regarding **"like the tower of Lebanon"**: this refers to "the good mountain and the Lebanon." <note>Deuteronomy 3:25</note> Rabbi Tavyomi says: "It is called Lebanon because it whitens the sins of Israel." <note>original: "מלבין" (*malbin*), meaning "to whiten." The word *Levanon* (Lebanon) shares the same Hebrew root as *lavan* (white).</note> Based on these words, the verse and its intent are explained: "Your nose"—meaning the anger within your nose—is used only for the service of Heaven and to cast fear upon the wicked, so that they may improve their ways, turn from their sins, and perform **repentance** <term>Teshuvah: the Jewish process of return and repentance</term>, like the tower of Lebanon which whitens the sins of Israel. This comparison stands without doubt regarding the purity and merit of the organ of the nose.

According to the explanation of the Remez, which refers to the main part of the face, the forehead, it is correctly explained as the distancing of "brazenness of forehead" and stubbornness. This is the trait of the upright and the righteous, who are modest, and the attribute of shame and **humility** is visible on their faces. Humility is the gateway to all important traits and the fulfillment of the service of the Creator. As it is said: "One who is bashful is destined for Paradise." This is the secret of **"Your nose is like the tower of Lebanon,"** etc., according to the two Midrashic interpretations I wrote above.

<summary>This page provides a spiritual and anatomical commentary on Song of Songs 7:5, interpreting the "eyes" as leaders of the body's limbs and the "nose" as a symbol for controlled anger and humility.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Bath-rabbim, eyes, nose, anger, Lebanon, Midrash, Rabbi Moses Alsheich, Ibn Ezra, humility, repentance</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded with a detailed anatomical praise of the beloved's features. This page shifts toward a more holistic and spiritual interpretation of that beauty, moving from the individual parts to the "delightful" whole of the community of Israel.</meta>

נא
Song of Songs
Catchword: In holiness

## How beautiful and how pleasant you are, O love,
## with delights.

<gloss>After the praise of all [the limbs]</gloss> Behold, after he has praised all her limbs and members, now, because light and purity have spread through all the members of the body by means of the commandment performed within the Company of God <term>Chever Hashem: A reference to the righteous community or a holy assembly</term>. And even if the "Company of God" is the helper and the primary actor, being the first to receive light, holiness, and purity, nevertheless everyone sits together and receives illumination because of their connection, shared responsibility, and mutual sanctification. Regarding the praise and the physical matter of the body's totality: "How beautiful" refers to the individual parts, while "how pleasant" refers to a matter more subtle, spiritual, and precious than mere beauty. As it says, "And let the beauty <note>original: *no'am*, often translated as pleasantness, grace, or divine favor</note> of the Lord our God be upon us" (Psalm 90:17), which refers to the collective. For innovation is added upon innovation, and subtlety upon subtlety, until freedom is perceived in every single limb with a special luster; for here there is an additional quality and a communal light that reflects back. There is no "love with delights" greater than this: when the love of the Assembly of Israel <term>Knesset Yisrael: The personification of the Jewish people in mystical thought</term> clings to the Holy One, Blessed be He, and He distinguishes Himself with her through the enjoyment of delights. For He shall not choose to hide His face, but rather gazes to perceive all the intentions and the ephemeral things she wishes to attain, as there is no separating screen at all caused by the physical matter of the body. Furthermore, the Holy One, Blessed be He, strengthens them so that the Assembly of Israel is worthy of this level—the boundary of ultimate cleaving <term>deveikut: mystical attachment or union with the Divine</term> to God. This is unlike the passion of [worldly] lovers who never truly achieve the ultimate end of their desire; their hearts delight only in a hope that is drawn out forever and ever.

## And it is possible to interpret "How beautiful" in the eyes of the body, for they are all for the purpose of the intention of serving Heaven;
and "how pleasant" in the eyes of the soul at rest, for there is a "love with delights" here—the flame of the soul within the delights of the body. In our way, it is also possible to interpret "How beautiful" as the beauty in this fleeting world, and "how pleasant" as the Days of the Messiah <term>Yemot HaMashiach</term>. Thus, we also interpret: "How beautiful" in this world, and "how pleasant" in the Days of the Messiah. The intention is to give thanks for the additional level and loftiness in this praise over the first praise; for the first attributes it to beauty, while this attributes it to pleasantness, which is a term for something more subtle and spiritual. As we have explained, the time of the Messiah is mentioned... and even the kindness of the World to Come <term>Olam HaBa</term>... it is more subtle than the individual aspects in this world, for it will be the World of Resurrection after the Days of the Messiah. According to what we have understood from the Book of Zohar <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note> regarding the interpretation of the verse "And let the pleasantness..." (Psalm 90:17), "pleasantness" refers to something very refined, subtle, and exalted. And we have found and seen this in the words of our holy Rabbis and their secrets. Furthermore, according to the interpretation of this specific stature of the elite and the righteous of the Assembly of Israel and the groups of its pious ones, it is a matter of following in their footsteps, may their memory be a blessing. And as has also been explained in the first praise, to know and make known how many good levels this "stature" has over the first with greater excellence. And as explained in the text, it began to praise: "How beautiful are your steps in sandals" (Song of Songs 7:2). The intention here concerns the general mass of the common people <note>original: *chomer*, referring to the 'matter' or the bulk of the nation</note>, and it likens them to "banners" or "steps"; for just as the feet are the lowest members of all the parts of the body, so too are these members of the nation who are the least refined in their Jewish physicality and are more immersed in their material nature than the other groups, as has been explained. Despite all this, they are worthy of praise and glory, for "all Israel has a portion in the World to Come," and from their holy source, they shall receive a portion.

<summary>This page provides a mystical commentary on Song of Songs 7:7, contrasting physical beauty with spiritual "pleasantness" (*no'am*) and explaining how the entire community of Israel, including its lowliest members, achieves divine union.</summary>

It transitions from the general beauty of the community to the specific spiritual purity required of its leaders and members, drawing a parallel between the "Company of Friends" and the biblical Daniel’s refusal of the King of Babylon's delicacies. It explores why certain physical attributes are praised or omitted in this specific "Measurement of the Stature" (Shi'ur Qomah).</meta>

## Commentary

**As it is written** concerning him: "And they clothed our teacher," <note>original: *ve-hilbishu le-rabbenu*</note> and may the height of his crown of understanding and greatness be raised—our purity. And we have examined how, although he was great in status and authority, he used it for nothing other than the service of Heaven. His wondrous asceticism at the beginning of his path—in the matter where he did not wish to sustain himself with the king’s delicacies <note>original: *patbag ha-melekh*, referring to Daniel 1:8</note> and the wine of his drinking—is well-known and famous. Thus, he is a complete and essential likeness to the purity of the Measurement <note>original: *si'ur*, likely a variant of *shi'ur* from the mystical 'Shi'ur Qomah' or the 'Measurement of the Stature'</note> and its clarity. This is far greater than the first praise, of which it was said: "Your hair is like a flock of goats" etc. In this way, the King—the King of the World—is bound and tied in the thick ropes of love for the disciples, the "Friends of the Cave" <term>Chavrei Me'ara: A reference to the circle of Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai, the traditional authors of the Zohar, who hid in a cave</term> of the Assembly of Israel. Due to the great refinement and purity of anyone who clings and is bound to the matters of the "Friends of the Body," God desires to dwell and rest upon them. For they are all beloved, all clear, all holy, destined for the purity of the holiness of the Torah and the commandments, as it is said, until they "shine like the brightness of the firmament" (Daniel 12:3).

And the explanation of "firmaments," according to our way, is the "Battlements" <term>Talpiot: A poetic name for the Temple, interpreted as a place toward which all mouths (piyot) turn (tel) in prayer</term> which we explained, may their memory be a blessing. And even if we explain it as a term of abundance, it would be in the language of the Targum <note>Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Bible</note>, just as Rashi also explained in the commentary on <unclear>shabi</unclear> <unclear>chachai</unclear> <unclear>lerodan</unclear> well. The intention is that the Holy One, Blessed be He, is bound and tied to the flow of the ministry found among the Friends, and the abundance of its spiritual influence which He poured into them—like the flow of water in the troughs—into the world of the Assembly and their holiness. This continued until they were transformed into spiritual and soulful beings, despite their small material nature, such that they possess an affinity for the indwelling of the Divine Presence <term>Shekhinah: The feminine, indwelling aspect of God</term> and the Holy One, Blessed be He, upon them.

<unclear>And</unclear> it is worthy <unclear>for</unclear> the heart and mind to learn: How does this "Measurement of the Stature" differ from the language we find here, which lacks the praise of the lips, the instruments of speech, and the <unclear>miracle</unclear> of the teeth? Previously, in the first praise, it mentioned and said: "Your lips are like a thread of scarlet," etc., and "Your teeth are like a flock of ewes," etc., yet not one of them is mentioned here. One should not wonder at the absence of the praise of the teeth, for it can be said that since the purpose of the praise is the absence of the blemish resulting from food (as mentioned) and the removal of impurity—this was already stated in the verse: "Your navel is a rounded bowl," etc., as has been explained. Therefore, there was no need to mention the praise, for from the praise of the belly, the teeth are understood. As for the "binding on the lips" and the "breath of the <unclear>distah</unclear>," it is possible to say they are included in the "murmur," as in "May your murmur be upon you," etc. It would be included at the top of the lips, like the cantors, as we wrote above.

Even if we intended to mention the praise of the eyes and the nose specifically, there is no difficulty from this at all. For the instruments of speech are more related to the spirit, as the brain in the head is the dwelling of the intellect, and it is known that speech is the messenger of the intellect. If so, the instruments of the intellect and the instruments of speech are paired; they both come in one coupling, and they relate to the term "murmur" <note>original: *rachash*</note> with an essential uniqueness, as stated. It is likely that the praise of the instruments of speech comes later in the verse: "And your palate like the best wine," etc. (Song of Songs 7:10). Due to the great hiddenness and secrecy of Torah study and the intention of the praise and prayer for which the instruments of speech serve <unclear>1</unclear>, they are all within a "closed palate"—lacking, hidden, and more concealed than the lips. And even if it is somewhat difficult why they were not mentioned in order, it can be said that since the change of the instruments of speech in this "Measurement of the Stature" is for praise, a certain emptiness, refinement, and purity occurs there more than in the rest of the Friends. This is either from the side of the world of the Torah—where there will then be added that which is impossible to attain in this time—or from the side of the praise and prayer which will be said with them in the ultimate knowledge of the hidden intention in the completion of all the firmaments. For then, everyone from the small to the great will know the depth of all things in their clarity. Therefore, he left this praise for the end, to show that it rises above them all, as we will explain with the help of Heaven in its place. It is also stated and uncertain regarding the category of the instruments of speech in an internal and hidden "Friend," as was said.

It highlights the "instruments of speech" (lips and palate) as the ultimate messengers of the intellect and the hidden depth of Torah study.</summary>

# Song of Songs

Springs of holiness, which are secrets within secrets. As our Sages of blessed memory <note>original: *Chazal*</note> said, these are the words of Torah which are numerous in their connections. And the Sages emphasized that this refers to the "Words of the Scribes" <note>the rabbinic traditions and enactments</note>. It is written with a missing letter to indicate a "Buyer" <note>original: *Koneh*</note>, for most of them are safeguards and fences <term>Siyagim: protective rabbinic decrees meant to prevent the accidental breaking of Biblical laws</term>, as is known. 

Regarding the phrase "Your two breasts" <note>original: *shnei shadayecha*, though transcribed here with variations</note>, according to this path, it presses the words of the Translator <note>referring to the Aramaic Targum</note> of blessed memory, who translated them as your two redeemers. The intention is to praise the Captivity of the Messiah <note>original: *Shvi ha-Mashiach*</note> to dwell therein forever as a statute for Israel. For both of them are good, upright, and righteous before the Lord. 

The Sages taught that the **Messiah son of David** will be greater than the **Messiah son of Ephraim** <term>Mashiach ben Yosef/Ephraim: a figure in Jewish eschatology who precedes the final Messiah and often dies in battle</term>, for to him belongs the right of greatness, excellence, and sovereignty. Nevertheless, both are good, upright, righteous, and significant in the eyes of the Blessed One. They are also balanced in likeness, resembling the Sages of Sevorin and Suran <note>ancient Babylonian centers of Jewish learning</note>. This is the praise of Israel. 

Just as our Sages of blessed memory spoke in the likeness of the Sanctuary, so it is said regarding the "two fawns" <note>another reference to the "breasts" metaphor in Song of Songs 7:4</note> who are far-reaching. It is possible that the doubling refers to their knowledge and their acquisition of deeds, acting as "Buyers" in the matter of the Redemption—ministers and messiahs sent to inherit Israel. They shall not turn their hearts away in the face of the dread of the war of the kingdoms and their depths, as is known. 

And it says: **"Your neck is like a tower of ivory"** <note>Song of Songs 7:5</note>—this refers to the **High Priest** who shall exist in those days. **"Your eyes are like pools in Heshbon"** refers to the **Prophets and Seers**, who shall multiply at that time, as it is written: "I will pour out my spirit upon all flesh" <note>Joel 3:1</note>. Thus, the Redemption will grow through names and wisdom; this is what is meant by "pools in Heshbon by the gate of Bath-rabbim." 

**"Your nose is like the tower of Lebanon"** refers to the collective of the **Sages**. It is written of them: "They shall put incense before your nose" <note>Deuteronomy 33:10, a play on the word for 'nose' and 'anger'</note>. In this manner, their service will bring benefit close to Israel. This is why it says "like the tower of Lebanon," for it shall block and humble the "seal" <note>the forces of evil</note>. He said these are from the side of the "Husk" and impurity <term>Kelipah: 'husks' or 'shells,' the kabbalistic term for the forces of evil or impurity that conceal the divine light</term>, which is why it says, "looking toward Damascus." 

And the verse **"Your head upon you is like Carmel"**—the explanation of this verse is found in the *Midrash Rabbah*: "The purple of your head," etc. And "the hair of your head" refers to the secrets and the princes of the Messiah, etc. According to our way, this refers to two groups within the "Walls of Israel." Though one level is higher than the other, in their plain meaning, they are revealed as a level above the first group mentioned earlier in the verse "How beautiful are your steps." For there, it spoke of the general masses, but these two groups are distinguished by their deeds and their fear of sin. This is their likeness and their "hair" <note>a play on the word *daltat*, meaning hair or hanging locks</note>, for they have attained the wisdom of the Torah and its internal, hidden knowledge. 

This is the plain meaning of "Your head shall be chosen upon you" in the final way. Specifically, "your two breasts" refers to the Prophets and Sages among them, even though they are part of the Sanhedrin; they are singled out because of their excellence and specific importance. This verse "Your head" refers to the **King Messiah**, and "the hair of your head" refers to the two groups [of followers]. Our humility shall be toward his masters and his servants who are close to him, and the "speech of the city" shall be as I wrote above.

->This your stature is like a palm tree, and your breasts<-
->to clusters :<-

Behold, according to our way, I have explained well:
They said "This your stature" to show
that the measure of this stature rises very high.
</insert>

With an advantage of importance and excellence over the first, for the perfection of the **Assembly of Israel** <term>Knesset Yisrael: the spiritual personification of the Jewish people</term> in all its parts—both in all parts of the body according to the interpretations—is unto the ultimate perfection. This occurs in the days of the King Messiah, as is known and as I wrote above regarding the matter of praise and glorification. Therefore, it said: "This your stature is like a palm tree." 

Perfection and the likeness of the **palm tree** <note>Hebrew: *Tamar*</note> are chosen because it is a tall and great tree, exceeding almost all other trees in height. Its fruit is sweeter than honey, and it is also customary to take its branches as a **Lulav** <term>Lulav: the palm frond used during the festival of Sukkot</term> for the commandment. So it is with the general "Measurement of the Stature" of the Assembly of Israel.

<meta>This page continues the mystical and allegorical commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically interpreting the physical descriptions of the "Beloved" as representing different classes of spiritual leadership and the refinement of the soul in the Messianic age.</meta>

# Commentary

The Torah given at the revelation at Mount Sinai <note>original: *Ma'amad Har Sinai*</note> was like a royal garment of crimson. This is even more true regarding the inheritance in the time of the Messiah, when physical matter <term>Chomer: the material substance of the world or the human body</term> will be much more refined than in the days of this world. Then, the "good portion" will be clearly recognized. 

The verse says, "How beautiful are your steps in sandals, O daughter of a noble" <note>Song of Songs 7:2</note>. The mystery of "in sandals" refers to the refinement of materiality. This means that although this group is covered by its physical nature—just as feet are covered by sandals—even so, "how beautiful" they are. This is because the physical matter itself will be thin and pure like a donkey <note>original: *chamor*, a play on words with *chomer* (matter). In Jewish mysticism, the "donkey" often represents the physical body that a holy person "rides" or masters</note>, and its nature will not be harmful. On the contrary, it will be helpful. 

This follows the teaching of our Sages <note>original: *Chazal*</note>: "His sandal is in his shoes" <note>a Talmudic idiom for something being ready or well-fitted</note>. According to our approach, this is perfectly aimed at showing that the people of this group lock and bolt the door against their evil inclination and their physical nature. Their "materiality" <note>*chomro*</note> is like the "daughter of a noble" <note>*bat nadiv*</note>, referring to their association with Abraham, who was called a "noble" <note>original: *nadiv*</note>. This suggests that the people of this group are worthy of being attributed to the name of the children of Abraham, for they are like a family, looking toward the "rock from which they were hewn" in their holiness and purity. Even if "daughter of a noble" refers to the Holy One, Blessed be He—who is the Noble of the world—it is explained according to the aforementioned intention.

The phrase "The curves of your thighs" refers to another group: those who establish the Torah and support it. As it is written: "those who support her are happy" <note>Proverbs 3:18</note>. They are like the thighs that support the body and upon which everything stands. This is why it says "the curves of your thighs are like ornaments" <note>Song of Songs 7:2</note>. Following what was explained previously based on the words of our Sages, they are like the "links of a pearl" <note>original: *chulyot shel margalit*</note>. This means that even if they only left behind silver or gold <note>referring to financial support for Torah study</note>, they will rise to a higher level of excellence, and it will be considered as if they themselves studied the Torah. Therefore, they will rise in their purity like a pearl, whose curve is clear and bright, and whose price is greater than gold. This is similar to the verse: "In all wisdom, in every decree..."

The text says: "Your navel is a rounded bowl" <note>Song of Songs 7:3</note>. This refers to another, more distinguished group: those who pursue the secret of the life of the **Assembly of Israel** <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*, a mystical term for the collective soul of the Jewish people</note> and its spirituality. Therefore, it uses the metaphor of a singer/poet. In this regard, our Sages said: "Just as a fetus in its mother's womb is alive because it is nourished from its navel..." It says "a rounded bowl" <note>original: *agan ha-sahar*</note> because they are refined like a "half-circle of a threshing floor" <note>referring to the semi-circular seating of the Sanhedrin, the high court</note>. Thus, it compares them to the circle of the moon <note>original: *sahar*</note> to show that from them, light comes to the world through the secret of the Torah. From them, the truth will be proven to all who see it through their teachings and judgments. Through this, there is no doubt that their "innards" are a source of life for the entire world. 

This is the meaning of "it never lacks blended wine" <note>Song of Songs 7:3</note>. Our Sages also said regarding the "lack of blended wine" in the world: Rabbi Huna said, "The Lord is my shepherd, I shall not want" <note>Psalm 23:1</note>. I, the Lord, will not let the "blended wine" be lacking, for there will be no lack of the "heat" of the Torah's blend in abundance and in many ways. All the laws of the Torah will be clear to them without any doubt regarding their source. In that time, there will certainly be no doubt, whereas sometimes in this world, something might be kept in doubt, as our Sages said: "Righteous is the judgment..."

Furthermore, it says: "Your belly is a heap of wheat" <note>Song of Songs 7:3</note>. This refers to the collective of Torah learners and the masters of both internal and external wisdoms. It compares them to a "belly" because the belly is the vessel that includes all the organs of food and nourishment, and they are the "internal friends." So too, the Sages who grasp the Torah are the interiority of the entire nation; from them comes the abundance and nourishment to exist in the world. As our Sages said: "It is impossible for the world to exist without wheat." Therefore, it says "a heap of wheat." 

It is possible that it says "a heap of wheat" to indicate the piety and the goodness of the nourishment that will then be poured upon them, pure and clean, to complete the members of the body and bring them closer to a spirituality similar to the **Manna** <term>Manna: the miraculous "bread from heaven" that sustained the Israelites in the desert</term> which descended for Israel in the wilderness. Therefore, it compares it to a "heap of wheat," which is the most significant of all foods. All of this is in their merit and in the merit of their Torah—the Written Torah and the Oral Torah—for "the words of the beloved are more precious than wine."

<summary>This page provides a mystical interpretation of the Beloved's physical features in the Song of Songs, linking the "sandals," "thighs," "navel," and "belly" to different spiritual ranks, including Torah supporters, the Sanhedrin, and scholars whose wisdom sustains the world's spiritual existence.</summary>

This page moves upward through the metaphors of the body to discuss spiritual nourishment and the "palate," representing the internal source of speech and the revelation of mystical secrets.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
### Song of Songs

Great benefit accrues to the many parts of the Assembly of Israel <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*</note> by My dwelling being among them. For through this, a spirit of holiness and purity will surely spread among them all. Those among them who have been awakened and have begun to attain spiritual perfection <note>original: *ha-shlemut ha-nafshi*</note>, but have not yet fully grasped it because they require assistance, will now attain it completely. Those who were already perfect will attain an even higher level of perfection and a greater spiritual rank. Regarding the first group, who are like "walls" in My dwelling, all those "branches of the wall" that require support and strengthening will be established and assisted to reach perfection. Their vitality, which is at the level of "the face"—having reached a state of perfection to the point where they have the power to pour out abundance <term>Shefa: the divine flow of energy or blessing</term> and nourishment to the entire wall—will now, through this lack being filled among them, become like the "insights of the vine," which are more significant than the "insights of the date palms." Your breasts <note>metaphor for leaders who nourish the generation</note> shall also be like clusters of the vine. Regarding those who have the "scent and moisture of taste" in them—meaning those who perform good deeds but are not yet distinguished in the knowledge of the Torah—they will attain both "scent and taste," for they will be filled with the knowledge of the Lord. Then, **the scent of your nose will be like apples**, for they will possess both scent and taste.

---

# And your palate is like the best wine, going down for my beloved
# smoothly, making the lips of sleepers speak.

---

In the explanation of the commentators, "your palate" <note>original: *chikech*</note> is like "your speech." It has already been explained previously that here the text praises and lauds the Assembly of Israel regarding the "vessels of speech" <note>the throat, palate, tongue, teeth, and lips</note>. It is not merely that there is no aspect of a "barrier of blemish" <note>a spiritual obstruction</note> in them at all; on the contrary, they are pure, refined, and clear to the ultimate degree. It was also explained why all the vessels of speech were characterized by the "palate"—because it is hidden and concealed. We also explained why this praise was delayed until now (look back at the end of the verse "Your head upon you..." and you will find it). "Palate" refers to the speech of the Torah and the speech of prayer. Regarding the Torah, it intends to show that this palate, in the days of the Messiah <note>original: *Yimei ha-Mashiach*</note>—after the Lord has established His name in Israel and the secrets of the Torah <note>original: *sitrei Torah*</note> are revealed in Israel—will be such that the Torah will almost seem to spread directly from the heart, without any separating screen, in the attainment of its mysteries and its inner essence. The study of Torah will follow the "hidden path" in addition to the "plain meaning" <term>Peshat: the literal or surface level of scripture</term>. The literal meaning will remain in its place, but it will no longer be a burden or a hindrance to attaining the Torah's secrets and mysteries like physical matter. This is why it says, **and your palate is like the best wine**. For "wine" indicates revelation and the likeness of a voice, as stated in the Book of the Zohar <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note>: "The secrets of the Torah are in the palate," as it is written, *Honey and milk are under your tongue*. That refers to this current time, which is a preparation within the "wall." But the "breasts of the wall" in the days of the Messiah will be pure and clean; even in the body, and certainly in the soul, the secrets of the Torah will be revealed and <unclear>ו</unclear> <unclear>ו</unclear> will be manifest to the eyes of all living things. Therefore, **and your palate is like the best wine**, which causes no harm at all. And it says, **going down for my beloved smoothly** <note>original: *le-meisharim*, literally "to uprightness"</note>, meaning there is no fear that they might err in them, or reverse their order, or come to damage and distort the order of the "flow of lights" and the "upper worlds" <note>kabbalistic concepts of divine emanation</note> where divisions might be touched. For everyone will know how to serve the Lord with order and uprightness; therefore, it "goes down for my beloved smoothly." It says that not only do they not cause harm, but they also bring benefit to all the worlds and renew a great abundance through the study of their Torah. Those things that were previously prevented from bringing abundance to that which is below them, or receiving from that which is above them—and were like "sleepers"—now, because of them, they shall "awake and be aroused" to receive.

<sig>i 4</sig>
<meta>catchwords: [He shall wake]</meta>

It contrasts the current hidden nature of divine wisdom with a future state where even simple practitioners will gain profound understanding without error or distortion.</summary>

# Commentary

<page-num>25</page-num>

<columns>2</columns>

Israel's grace after the Redemption will be of even greater value, high standing, and distinction in the eyes of God and man—grace in the eyes of the body and grace in the eyes of the soul. In the *Zohar* <note>The primary text of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah).</note> at the end of the portion of *Mishpatim*, it is written: 
"Rabbi Yehuda opened: 'This your stature is like a palm tree...' (Song of Songs 7:8). How beloved is the Assembly of Israel <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*</note> before the Holy One, Blessed be He, for she never separates from Him. Just as with this palm tree, no part of its frond ever separates from it, and no wind rises without another spirit <note>referring to the internal unity and spiritual balance of the tree</note>, so too the Assembly of Israel does not separate from the Holy One, Blessed be He." 
By way of our interpretation, this relates to the "spirits," for this comparison is very accurate. At that time, through the preparation of this "stature," grace will be of greater value. The connection of the Assembly of Israel will be an essential union without any separation or interruption at all.

And it says **and its branches** <note>original: *sansinav*</note>, etc., meaning the clusters of the palm tree. The intention here could be explained as referring to the **two Messiahs** <term>Messiah son of David and Messiah son of Ephraim: In Jewish tradition, two redeemers who work together to bring the final peace.</term>, as explained in the commentary on page 120. Since they are the beauty, praise, splendor, and glory of Israel, he compares and likens them to "clusters," which are the fruit of the palm. This indicates that from their choice, all parts of the nation will be influenced—each according to their individual preparation—with a spirit of knowledge and fear of the Lord. 

And although the primary source for all of this is the Messiah son of David, nonetheless, the Messiah son of Ephraim follows him, and his vitality comes through him. The Messiah son of David will also be a great support for all the matters that will be drawn forth due to the greatness of the Messiah son of David over Israel.

However, what seems more likely to me is based on what is known elsewhere: the "two powers" are not the focus here. I previously explained "your breasts" <note>original: *shadayikh*</note>, and therefore the interpretation that these powers are a nickname for all the heads of the Assembly of Israel—the pious ones, the "pillars of the world," and the high sages. They serve as a "Chariot" <note>A Kabbalistic term for a physical vehicle for divine presence.</note> for the system that receives the law of all the <term>Shefa: divine flow of blessing and energy</term> poured into the lower world. They are the ones who pour out the "good milk"—the abundant flow—to all the lower beings. 

This is why it says **and its branches like clusters**, to indicate the wisdom of the "books of its life," as completed in its secret and its understanding in great quantity as well. Therefore it says "like clusters," and this explanation is more correct and focused on the verse that follows this one, as will be explained in the book.

According to a more distant path—the way of physical beauty in the organs of the body—the "breasts" are placed first as a reference to the hands. <unclear>Because</unclear> he said it is possible to interpret them as "seals," being the primary "ministers" and tools of action. This teaches that in those days, the primary use of the hands will be for wisdom, for there is grace in the matter of the service of Heaven, which is the purpose of their creation. This is like the clusters of the palm, which are the primary reason for planting the palm. 

Furthermore, "grace" is chosen over "water" <note>likely a metaphor for physical desire</note>, a great thing. For the "destruction" of desire will be cut off and severed, because a man will have a strengthening of the will. He will not be drawn after the dross of blemish, such as the <note>original: *kersum*</note> of the face or of promiscuity, Heaven forbid. Instead, there will be a purely spiritual increase in strength for the sake of the commandment of procreation. This is what is meant by **like clusters**, which are the fruit of the palm as stated. And the arm will also be like the palm tree. As our Sages of blessed memory said: "Just as the palm tree has <gloss>only</gloss> one heart..." <note>A midrashic teaching that the palm tree grows straight up toward heaven, symbolizing a heart focused solely on God.</note>

After he explained the praise and acclaim of the Assembly of Israel in its entirety—this whole "stature" being likened to a palm tree—God says that because He desires her, she is worthy and prepared for His Divine Presence <note>original: *Shekhinah*</note> to dwell within her. All her parts are attached to Him as if they were His own essence. This great attachment is desired before Him, may He be blessed. Thus He said: "I have decreed to say that I shall attach Myself to this palm tree and dwell upon it." This is the meaning of **I will climb the palm tree**, etc. Wisdom...

-> **OF ITS BRANCHES. LET YOUR BREASTS BE LIKE CLUSTERS** <-
-> **OF THE VINE, AND THE SCENT OF YOUR BREATH LIKE APPLES.** <-

It emphasizes the total unity between God and Israel in the Messianic era, where even physical desires are transformed into spiritual service.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, Chapter 7. The previous page discussed the spiritual unification of the Assembly of Israel with the Divine in the Messianic era, using the metaphor of a palm tree. This page focuses on the expansion of holiness from the centers of worship out into the peripheral areas and villages.</meta>

# Song of Songs

It is stated in the Great Midrash <note>original: *Midrash Rabbati*; referring to Midrash Rabbah, a collection of ancient rabbinic homilies on the Bible</note> on the Book of Ruth that there are three places where—aside from the uncertainty regarding the Days of the Messiah <term>Messianic Era: A future time of universal peace and spiritual enlightenment in Jewish tradition.</term>—the Canaanites still hold possession. These correspond to the verse "Come, my beloved, let us go out into the field," and so on. Despite my efforts to locate this in another Midrash where the version is not quite the same, who is to say they are not simply differing traditions? Regardless, I have already returned from a perspective that was "near yet far" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning the author has reconsidered a previous thought or reached a new understanding after much deliberation.</note>.

<margin>Behold, since the Assembly of Israel <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*</note> desires this time in the highest state and at the most fitting moment during the Days of the Messiah, as has been explained, she expresses her longing to the Holy One, Blessed be He.</margin>

## COME, MY BELOVED, LET US GO OUT INTO THE FIELD; LET US LODGE
## IN THE VILLAGES.

The remnants of the various groups that make up the "Wall" <note>referring to the protective spiritual structure of the community</note>, whether in study or in action—of which this level represents the ultimate refinement—stood outside. They were outside even of Jerusalem, which lacked the Temple and the dwelling place of the Divine Presence <term>Shekhinah: The localized, indwelling presence of God in the world.</term>. They were not only in the large cities where many of the people of Israel reside, including numerous and distinguished sages and scribes, but even in the "field" and the "villages"—the open towns where there is a large population, though scattered "here a little, there a little." Because of this distance, knowledge and the fear of God can easily diminish in those places. Therefore, she says: "Come, let us go and search there, and we shall find that all of them, from the smallest to the greatest, know the Lord." They are all whole and perfect, such that the perfection of the Wall is inclusive of everyone. There, too, the "Groom" <note>God</note> will find for Himself righteous people, sages, and those who grasp the Torah, just as in the large cities. This is the meaning of **Come, my beloved, let us go out into the field**, which is not a settled area, and **let us lodge in the villages**. This means that even though there is no great population there, nevertheless, it will be good in our eyes to lodge there in the villages, for the place will be a place of meditation <note>original: *higayon*</note>, worthy of staying in. We shall delight in them and their company, for we shall find satisfaction in them and their deeds. This is why it says **let us lodge in the villages**—meaning we desire it, and it will be pleasing to us to stay there. We do not go as if we are forced or merely passing through the way all day long, but we specifically desire to lodge there at night, because we see that the place is prepared.

<margin>The intention is that even there, in the villages, is found the grace of all parts of the perfection of the "Wall." For among them are those who excel in Torah, wisdom, and knowledge; and among them are the young schoolchildren <note>original: *tinokot shel beit raban*</note>, whose breath sustains the world.</margin>

## LET US GET UP EARLY TO THE VINEYARDS; LET US SEE IF
## THE VINE HAS BUDDED, IF THE GRAPE BLOSSOMS HAVE OPENED,
## IF THE POMEGRANATES ARE IN BLOOM; THERE I WILL GIVE YOU MY LOVE.

And among them are those who excel in the fear of God, in piety, and in the exertion of good deeds. Thus, the "Groom" <note>God</note> has there sages and righteous people just as in the large cities. This is the meaning of **Let us get up early to the vineyards**, referring to the synagogues and study halls. **Let us see if the vine has budded** refers to those who excel in knowledge, wisdom, and the depth of the Torah. **If the grape blossoms have opened** refers to the young schoolchildren who rouse themselves toward God with complete grace and pleasant voices. **If the pomegranates are in bloom** refers to those who excel in piety, the fear of God, and the exertion of good deeds. **There I will give you my love**, for even there "loves" <note>original: *dodim*; referring to expressions of affection/devotion</note> will be found from the righteous, the pious, and the wise.

And in the Great Midrash: **Let us get up early to the vineyards**—these are the people of Israel, as it is said, "For the vineyard of the Lord of Hosts is the house of Israel." **Let us see if the vine has budded**—this refers to the Recitation of the Shema <term>Shema: The central declaration of Jewish faith ("Hear, O Israel...").</term>. **If the grape blossoms have opened**—these are the synagogues and study halls. **If the pomegranates are in bloom**—these are the children who sit and occupy themselves with the Torah. **There I will give you my love**—there I provide the righteous men and women, and the prophets and prophetesses, who have arisen from me.

<meta>The catchword "Your eyes" at the bottom indicates the next page begins with a discussion of the "eyes," continuing the anatomical/metaphorical progression of the Song of Songs.</meta>

<summary>This page interprets the Song of Songs as a prophecy that in the Messianic era, spiritual excellence and the knowledge of God will spread beyond Jerusalem and major cities into small villages and rural fields. The author uses botanical metaphors (vines, blossoms, pomegranates) to represent different groups like scholars, schoolchildren, and the pious.</summary>

# Commentary

<meta>catchword: from her</meta>

Also to bestow influence, and this is the spiritual secret of "He who rides upon the clouds" <note>original: *rokhev*; a reference to the Divine as a rider directing the "chariot" of the world</note>. Its physical substance "flows for my beloved" <note>referring to Song of Songs 7:10</note>, which is like "milk and wine" <note>original: *v'chalvama hamar*; comparing the flow of spiritual influence to rich, pure liquids</note>. Regarding prayer, it will also be clarified that it must be pure and clear, without any blemish, dross, or any mixture of "loathsome thoughts" <note>original: *pigulei hamachashavot*; referring to distracting or impure thoughts that disqualify a prayer, similar to how "pigul" refers to a disqualified sacrificial offering</note>. When the intent is good, pure, clear, and clean without any harmful mixture at all, it is beautiful in its appearance and good in its taste. So shall the prayer of Israel be: like milk, beautiful in its appearance—representing the clarity of language and pleasantness of the words—and very good in its taste, representing the ultimate perfection of the worshippers' intention. It shall be without any blemish or defect, being clean from the mixture of divisive thoughts which create a "fissure" in the speech. This is the meaning of "it flows for my beloved," and "He who rides," etc. I have written more on this elsewhere regarding the "steps," and it is easy to understand.

The Assembly of Israel <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*; the personification of the Jewish people in relation to God</note> says: Wait now—

-> **I am my beloved’s, and his desire is toward me.** <-

This is specifically intended for the Days of the Messiah.

I draw near and cling to my beloved with an essential "cleaving" <term>Devekut: A state of deep spiritual attachment or "clinging" to the Divine.</term>. The reason for this merit is that in this future time, the world will have already been scoured clean, and the "filth of the serpent" <term>Filth of the Serpent: In Kabbalistic tradition, the spiritual impurity or "stain" brought into the world by the primordial serpent in Eden.</term> will have ceased. The "flaw"—the corruption of the world caused by the sin of the first man, Adam—will be repaired. The world will return to its original strength and state, for the substance of the human body will be refined and pure, without any murkiness, dirt, or filth at all. One's entire aim will be toward Divine matters and the service of the Creator, as it was in the beginning before man sinned. Consequently, all the worlds, upper and lower, will be established upon their proper foundations, with the appropriate choice and connection. That is to say, they will rest as a throne and a sign; His will and desire, may He be blessed, will be fulfilled. What is the purpose of man on the earth? It is that they should be worthy to unify and link the worlds together, maintaining them in their proper repair and order. For this they were created, and this was the ultimate intent of their creation. When He, blessed be He, endeavored to make man, he was initially pure, but "they sought out many schemes" <note>quoting Ecclesiastes 7:29</note> and corrupted the worlds and the holy honors—that is, the channels through which divine flow descends and returns. Since then, He, blessed be He, has always loved and longed for the time when Israel would complete and prepare themselves to "restore the crown to its former glory" <note>A traditional idiom for returning something to its original, perfect state.</note> and improve the world to its former status. This vision will be completed in the Days of the Messiah, as is known. And this is the secret of "and His desire is toward me"—as if to say, upon seeing me in this perfected state, His desire, may He be blessed, returns to the world. I also know that the plain meaning of the verse is: I wait for my beloved with complete devotion, and my beloved also desires to cling to me because He desires me and finds me worthy. The pulse of the verse suggests that this is not a small thing, for it follows from the beginning of the verse, and the whole verse is established upon this structure.

One can further explain: "I wait for my beloved" means I have already achieved the state of waiting for and clinging to Him, yet "His desire is still upon me." This means that His desire and my longing for Him still continue, for my mind is not yet "cooled" or satisfied by the level of clinging I have already achieved. I desire to achieve even more than this. May this longing be for the "Great Assembly"—namely, the Resurrection of the Dead—which is the second stage of clinging. The smaller states of being are like nothingness to Him, for they continue without end, involving both body and soul, like burning coals. Or, this desire might refer to another "Assembly," which is for all the vitality of the Assembly of Israel to become a "Chariot" <term>Chariot: Or *Merkavah*; a spiritual vehicle through which the Divine Presence is revealed in the physical world.</term> for the Divine Presence <note>original: *Shekhinah*</note>. Then the Divine Presence will rest upon them, just as the Patriarchs themselves were the Chariot. This is the secret of "and His desire is toward me"—meaning His Presence shall dwell upon me, for that is the ultimate desire. We also have a slightly more distant interpretation: that "His desire" refers to the desire of Israel for the Holy One, Blessed be He, although the plain meaning remains that it refers to the desire of the Holy One for Israel.

<summary>This page provides a mystical commentary on Song of Songs 7:11, explaining how the Messianic era will bring a total refinement of the human body and the removal of the spiritual "filth" from Adam's sin. It describes the ultimate goal of creation as the human ability to unify the upper and lower worlds, making the people of Israel a "Chariot" for the Divine Presence.</summary>

<header>Song of Songs</header>

<meta>catchword: who draws</meta>

Holy wonders [show] that even among young men and women, they are found to be at rest from the [agitation of physical] humors <note>original: *lachot*; referring to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval medicine believed dictated temperament and health</note> and possess few [bad traits], being good in their youth and in their old age. All this is in purity and cleanliness from the filth of evil deeds, being both God-fearing and [spiritually] awake. 

It says, "My beloved, let us go out into the field" <note>Song of Songs 7:12</note> to give thanks for the hope [found] below, as was explained above. It is possible to explain these verses in another way, close to the aforementioned intent: since those who dwell in villages and unwalled towns are not as complete in Torah, in the fear of God, and in the fulfillment of the commandments as those who dwell in the great cities of scholars and scribes—where everything is [ordered] because of the many leaders, rebukers, and teachers of knowledge, as well as the abundance of synagogues and study halls—and we see this with our own senses in these days and at this time. Therefore, the Assembly of Israel <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*; the personification of the Jewish people</note> said to her Beloved: "Come, my beloved, let us go out into the field," etc. 

This means: those who dwell in Jerusalem and in places of large settlement are already complete and whole; they hardly need assistance to flourish and perfect themselves, for they have attained perfection on their own. However, so that perfection may be inclusive of the entire nation, [she says] "let us go out into the field and lodge in the villages," so that we may supervise them with a spirit of greater purity and holiness, providing them with more support to reach the goal of ultimate perfection. 

This is what is written: "Let us go early to the vineyards; let us see if the vine has budded," etc. <note>Song of Songs 7:13</note>. That is, we shall oversee them after they have begun the awakening to attain perfection, to give them strength until the end. This is "if the vine has budded." And it says "the vine has budded, the grape blossoms have opened, the pomegranates are in flower" to include all the different groups of the nation, each according to its preparation, as we explained above. 

She says that after this preparation and support, they will specifically reach the goal of perfection in haste, for "there I will give you my love," as has been explained. Afterward, it says "The mandrakes give a fragrance" <note>Song of Songs 7:14</note> to clarify what we said above: that the hidden potential of this assistance is for the people of the villages and unwalled towns. For the large cities do not need it as much, as they have already reached the category of perfection with only a little help. This is "the mandrakes give a fragrance, and at our doors are all manner of precious fruits," etc., according to the paths we explained above. And behold, it is clear that in a physical sense, "at our doors" applies to this path, and there is no need to elaborate further as the matters are clear.

---

# O that you were like a brother to me, who nursed at my mother’s breasts! If I found you outside, I would kiss you, and no one would despise me. <note>Song of Songs 8:1</note>

Behold, it is correct that anyone who attains perfection finds that this quality is superior for them; they will be pleased and their mind will be cooled by what they have achieved. Yet, as much as they achieve, they will also come to recognize and know their own smallness compared to that which is higher than what they have attained. They will take hold and strengthen themselves to reach those higher levels and to live them out, never resting from striving with all their might to attain as far as their hand can reach. This matter is clear, and further discussion of it is redundant for you.

And behold, after these things, she [the Assembly of Israel] cries out, saying that even though the Assembly of Israel, after the Redemption in the days of the Messiah, will be in a state of physical and spiritual height and excellence, attached to her God with an essential cleaving—knowing the depths of the Torah and its secrets as has been explained—her mind is not satisfied with this alone. Rather, she longs, and her soul even faints, to attain a greatness even higher than that, which is the matter of the World to Come <note>original: *Olam HaBa*; the final state of spiritual reward</note> that follows the Resurrection. For without doubt, that is a great perfection without end, even while being in body and soul, a state far above the days of the Messiah. For in the days...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically exploring the metaphors for the spiritual achievements of the Jewish people.
