# Commentary
### ג ג
<meta>catchword: Kofyah</meta>

And your eyes see things that are close to our words, and here is one [explanation] following our way, and we shall speak further on this verse later on, with the help of Heaven:
Since it was said above, "let us see if the vine has flowered," etc., and each one is dependent and in doubt, he therefore returned and said that all of them were [once] in a state of uncertainty and freshness. And so, "The mandrakes give a fragrance"—this refers to the righteous and the wise 

> **The mandrakes give a fragrance, and at our gates are all precious fruits, both new and old, which I have hidden for you, my beloved.**

of action and the masters of the commandments whose scent of merit and righteousness has already ascended like the scent of spices. Therefore, he compared them to "mandrakes," which are fragrant and aromatic herbs, as Rashi <note>Rabbi Shlomo Yitzhaki (1040–1105), the preeminent medieval commentator on the Bible and Talmud.</note> of blessed memory wrote in the portion of *Vayetze*, that it is an herb called in the foreign tongue <term>Albaruz</term> <note>original: *Albaruz'*; likely a variant of the Arabic *al-yabruh*, referring to the Mandrake (Mandragora), known in folklore for its fragrance and medicinal or mystical properties.</note>. From them, they make crowns and secrets. 

Regarding the knowledge of Torah and wisdom, it says: "And at our gates are all precious fruits, new and old," etc. This means to say that one [interpretation] promises at the gates of synagogues and study halls <note>original: *Batei Kneset* and *Batei Midrash*</note> that there are many <term>innovations</term> <note>original: *Chiddushim*; original insights or legal interpretations derived through study.</note> in the Torah. Another [interpretation] is that at every single gate of the collective of Israel, there are indeed many innovations in the knowledge of the Torah—in all kinds of Torah knowledge: in the literal <note>original: *Peshat*</note>, the revealed <note>original: *Nigleh*</note>, and the hidden <note>original: *Nistar*; referring to mystical or Kabbalistic teachings</note>. These come either from what was known and grasped by Israel before the time of the Messiah, or from what will be said to them in the understanding of the Torah in his time, for "the earth shall be full of the knowledge of the Lord" <note>Isaiah 11:9</note>. This is what is meant by "at our gates are all precious fruits, new and old." 

And it says, "I have hidden for you, my beloved," to say that all of these were intended and gathered for the sake of His blessed Name. The action and the study were for His sake, and not for any other intent or ulterior motive, or in a way that everything should be characterized by an essential and absolute attachment [to God]. 

Close to these words, the Translator <note>referring to the *Targum*, the ancient Aramaic translation and paraphrase of the Bible.</note> of blessed memory translated, and his intention follows our aforementioned way: 
<margin>Targum</margin> 
The understanding of the righteous is revealed before me like the scent of spices, and the sages of the generation are established at the gates of the study hall, occupied with the words of the scribes and the words of the Torah. Therefore, I have prepared this for the masters of the commandments and the masters of Torah, as we have explained. 

One might say that according to our method, it will be even more precise when we said, "And at our gates," etc. It is also clear that the Translator differs in the explanation of "new and old," applying "new" to the words of the Scribes <note>Rabbinic enactments and interpretations.</note> and "old" to the words of the [Written] Torah. This too aligns perfectly with our path; look closely and you will find it. 

Behold, we have seen in the words of Rashi of blessed memory, in his explanation of the Scroll [of Song of Songs], one explanation: "The vine has flowered" refers to the masters of Scripture; "the pomegranate has budded" refers to the masters of Mishnah <note>The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions.</note>, who are close to providing benefit through the holy fragrance mentioned; "the vineyards" refer to the masters of Gemara <note>The part of the Talmud that contains rabbinical commentaries and analysis of the Mishnah.</note>, who possess complete wisdom but are [sometimes] far from rendering a [practical] ruling. 

And your eyes see that this path aligns perfectly with our own method, and this path is pleasant and desirable. For until now, he spoke in praise of analysis and study, and now in this verse, he speaks in praise of action. He says, "The mandrakes give a fragrance," meaning he promises generally that the sages who were leaders will see that their good deeds flourish far and wide because of their purity and cleanliness. There is no "evil eye" <note>original: *bar ayin*; here likely referring to a flaw or negative influence.</note> to darken, soil, or make their deeds stink so as to prevent their good scent. Rather, even among the youths and the tender-faced, they too are in this category. This is "The mandrakes give a fragrance," following what the Sages said in the *Midrash Rabbah*: "These are the young men of Israel who have not tasted the taste of sin." 

And "at our gates" they shall merit all precious things. Or, it is possible to explain this according to the way of the Sages when they said: "These are the daughters of Israel who cleave to their husbands and do not know another man," etc.

<summary>The author interprets Song of Songs 7:14, explaining that "mandrakes" symbolize the righteous and "gates" symbolize centers of Torah study. He explores how both traditional teachings (old) and new insights (new) are preserved for God's sake, referencing Rashi, the Targum, and the Midrash.</summary>

<keywords>Song of Songs, Mandrakes, Rashi, Targum, Midrash Rabbah, Torah study, Scribes, New and Old, Righteousness, Innovation</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Song of Songs, moving from the description of the "mandrakes" (the righteous) to the spiritual state of humanity following the Resurrection of the Dead and the nature of Torah study in the World to Come.</meta>

After the Resurrection, human beings will be elevated in the study of the Torah according to its true standard. This is as it says in the holy *Zohar* <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism, traditionally attributed to Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai.</note> on the portion of *Vayeshev*, of blessed memory: "What is 'dark'? It is because in the future, when those who sleep in the dust arise, the Torah will not be hidden from them. For then they will know all that Torah which they left behind when they departed from this world. For the Torah is like their own skin from that time, and it shall enter their innards as it did originally, and it shall speak in your mouths. All matters will be more properly established than they were at first. For all those things they were worthy to cling to as is fitting, and they desired to cling to them but did not [in their first life], all of them will enter their innards properly. The Torah itself will speak through them, as it is written: 'When you wake, she shall talk with you' (Proverbs 6:22)." This ends his quote. 

Behold, all of this praise begins immediately at the time of the Resurrection, and how much more so afterward during the primary life of the World to Come <note>original: *Olam HaBa*; the eternal spiritual realm.</note>. For there, the primary occupation will be the study of the Torah, and we shall all be "taught by the Lord" (Isaiah 54:13) in all the depths of the Torah and its inner secrets, which was impossible to achieve in this world. 

Regarding what was said, "to drink of spiced wine..." (Song of Songs 8:2), it is because there, the flavor will be refined and sweetened by the "Daughter of the Voice" <note>original: *Bat Kol*; a divine echo or heavenly inspiration.</note> through the vision and the deeds of the righteous who labored and acquired [wisdom] in this world and in the days of the Messiah. For then, they will return to the Presence of the Blessed One without any separating veil, in perfection, with the limbs of the body as refined as the limbs of the soul. Also, the essence of what they receive from the Blessed God is to place the essence of their intellect—both in body and soul—at rest, following His ways and according to His deeds in the "arrangements" and attainments which "no eye has seen" (Isaiah 64:3). 

This is the meaning of "of spiced wine," referring to study that is deep and hinted at through metaphors. It is also possible to interpret "of spiced wine" to mean that my occupation will be with the inner dimensions of the Torah and its secrets. The measure of the "tasting" that will reach you through the occupation with the Torah at that time will be very great, comparable to drinking spiced wine, which is a very distinguished wine. It is also compared to "the juice of my pomegranates," which has no mixture of bitterness, and heaven forbid that drinking much of it should cause any harm at all.

# His left hand is under my head, and his right hand embraces me.

In a literal sense, this means that these matters cause a state of clinging <note>original: *deveikut*; spiritual attachment or union with the Divine.</note> and a complete, perfect union. He said "under my head" and it is not written "for my head" as it was written above, to indicate a total union. Regarding what will be his in the World to Come and the attainment of the Divine arrangements—to gaze upon the sweetness of the Lord and to see the bright light of the King's face—he said, "His left hand is under my head." And regarding the attainment of the inner dimensions of the Torah, he said, "and his right hand embraces me." For the "right" indicates the Torah, as it is written: "from His right hand went a fiery law for them" (Deuteronomy 33:2). He says "and his right hand embraces me" to indicate that the mysteries and secrets of the Torah will be revealed to them; the Torah will move of its own accord to cling to the Community of Israel <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*; the spiritual collective of the Jewish people.</note> due to its great purity, clarity, holiness, and the merit of its parts. And he said:

# I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, why should you stir up or awake love until it pleases?

"I adjure you," etc. Here too, it is explained as was written above: that the Community of Israel adjured the ministering angels who are appointed over the affairs of her business and her existence with her Beloved regarding the totality of the World to Come—that they should not stir or be a cause for removing the love of her Beloved from her, as a matter of special merit.

<summary>This page explores the spiritual elevation of humanity after the Resurrection, focusing on the Zohar's promise that the Torah will become an internal, natural part of the righteous. It interprets the metaphors of spiced wine and pomegranate juice as the deep, painless attainment of divine secrets in the World to Come.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection, World to Come, Zohar, Spiced Wine, Torah Study, Divine Union, Community of Israel, Right Hand of God, Inner Secrets</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spiritual transformation that follows the Resurrection. The previous page concluded by noting that in the World to Come, the Torah will be "written on the hearts" of people, and the divine voice will be clearly heard.</meta>

->Commentary<-

The **Messiah** <note>original: *Mashiach*</note>, even though he will achieve a great perfection in the fear of the Lord, in the Torah, and in the purity and refinement of his physical matter and body—reaching an essential cleaving to the Divine God—nonetheless, the world will still follow its natural course <note>original: *olam keminhago noheg*</note> regarding eating, drinking, procreation, and other bodily events. This is especially true according to the opinion that states there is no difference between this world and the Messianic days except for our liberation from the subjugation of foreign kingdoms. This is not the case, however, in the **World to Come** <note>original: *Olam HaBa*</note>, for life there involves neither eating nor drinking, and so on. As **Nachmanides** <note>Rabbi Moses ben Nachman (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar</note>, of blessed memory, explained at length in *The Gate of Reward* <note>original: *Sha'ar HaGemul*</note>, his words are correct. What is required according to our tradition, and without doubt, is that there will be an essential **cleaving** <term>devekut: a state of deep spiritual attachment or "clinging" to God</term> and a complete, clear perception without any hindrance or separation. For physical matter will be so refined that it transforms into **form** <note>In medieval philosophy, "form" refers to the spiritual essence or soul, while "matter" refers to the physical body.</note>, and it will be entirely life; there will be no matter there at all. They will be worthy of being instructed in wisdom and saying, "Behold, this is our God." This is as our Sages of blessed memory said in the Midrash: "In the future, the Holy One, blessed be He, will lead a dance for the righteous in the time to come... and they will point to Him with a finger, as it is said: 'For this is God, our God forever and ever'" (Psalm 48:15). Regarding this matter, the **Assembly of Israel** <note>original: *Knesset Yisrael*; the personification of the Jewish people</note> said: "O that you were like a brother to me, who nursed at my mother’s breasts!" (Song of Songs 8:1). This means a brother from both father and mother, representing an essential cleaving without any pulling away, disconnection, or separation. This is a metaphor for the essential cleaving we mentioned regarding the World of the Resurrection. Concerning the intensity of that cleaving and perception in that time, she says: "If I found you outside, I would kiss you." This means that you would always be prepared to achieve divine perceptions without any interruption at all, without the need for seclusion <note>original: *hitbodedut*</note> or making special preparations, similar to the way one perceives a visual sight. Our power would be like all the prophets in this matter, as is known. Also, "I found you outside" means even in a public place, outside of our secret places and [places of] seclusion—even there "I would kiss you." This means you would be prepared for an essential cleaving, using the metaphor of a **kiss**, which is the "cleaving of spirit to spirit" <note>original: *itdabkut rucha be-rucha*</note>, as it says in the **Song of Songs** <note>original: *Shir HaShirim*</note>, a perfect cleaving with the lips. She also says: "Nor would they despise me." This means that besides being prepared for that perfect cleaving mentioned, the ministering angels or those who see my cleaving with You will not despise me. For such a perfect cleaving will be visible to all, and they will see that I am in a state worthy of this, despite being composed of body and soul.

# I would lead you, I would bring you to my mother's house;
# You would teach me. I would give you spiced wine to drink,
# From the juice of my pomegranates. (Song of Songs 8:2)

Behold, in the previous verse, the text spoke of the time after the physical Resurrection, which is near and immediate. Now, in this verse, it concludes with praise for the time of the World to Come, for which the soul longs. She says that after the King leads her through the time after the Resurrection, arriving at the perfect state that will be chosen later—which is the second time, the life of the World to Come where there is no eating or drinking but the "righteous sit [with their crowns on their heads]"—then I will have the readiness to lead You entirely away from the upper beings and to bring You to "my mother's house." This refers to the **Garden of Eden** <note>original: *Gan Eden*</note>, which was originally the "mother's house," for Adam and Eve were there before the sin. However, because of the sin, they were driven out from there, and neither they nor their descendants were worthy of it because of the nature of the body and soul. Afterward, it was set aside to be a dwelling place for souls alone. But in the time of the World to Come, they will return to their original beloved state. They will be worthy, in both body and soul, to dwell there, for the purity, merit, and thinness of the body will allow it to dwell in that place, even though it is very subtle and spiritual. Then "I will lead you to my mother's house," which is the Garden of Eden, in a physical sense. It is attributed to the "mother" more than the "father" because the "house" is typically associated with the mother. Furthermore, she was the primary cause of the expulsion, so this hints at the cause of the ruin now becoming the place of **rectification** <note>original: *tikkun*</note>. And it says, "You would teach me," for my words are well-known...

<summary>This page discusses the transition from the Messianic Era to the World to Come, emphasizing the transformation of the physical body into a refined spiritual form capable of permanent union with God. It interprets the Song of Songs as a metaphor for this "essential cleaving" and the return of humanity to the Garden of Eden.</summary>

### Song of Songs
#### Signature: 86 H I 15
#### Page: 57
<meta>catchword: gathered</meta>

...[their] union shall be permanent and eternal, without ever separating. Therefore, when the soul remembers the past—when their union did not turn out well and a separation occurred, with the soul departing from its rank <note>the soul leaving the body at death</note>—it fears that such a disaster might strike them again in the future after the Resurrection. It therefore says to the body: **"O that you were like a brother to me"** <note>original: *Mi yitnecha k’achi*, Song of Songs 8:1</note>, meaning that our union and closeness should be like that of a brother, a bond that is never severed. This will occur through the "nursing" of the lights of the *Shekhinah* <term>Shekhinah: the feminine, indwelling presence of God</term> which will rest upon them constantly at the time of the Resurrection to bring them to life. This is the meaning of **"nursing at my mother’s breasts"** <note>Song of Songs 8:1</note>, referring to the *Shekhinah*. 

Regarding the phrase **"If I found you outside, I would kiss you,"** <note>Song of Songs 8:1</note> it calls "outside" the place where the body receives the soul at the very end of its stay in its place of burial. As it is written in the *Zohar* in many places, the soul only enters the body in the Land of Israel, for the bodies will arrive there through "tunnels in the earth" <note>original: *mechilot*; a traditional belief that bodies buried outside Israel will roll through subterranean tunnels to reach the Holy Land for Resurrection</note> and there they will receive their souls. In the *Midrash HaNe'elam* <note>a mystical section of the Zohar</note>, they explained clearly that at that hour, the body stands in the Land of Israel and there its soul enters it. Rabbi Yochanan asked: "Who leads the body to the Land of Israel?" Rabbi Zera replied: "The Holy One, Blessed be He, makes tunnels under the earth and they walk to the Land of Israel." This is what is meant by "the land of the shades you will bring down," etc.

Thus, **"If I found you outside, I would kiss you"** mentions "kissing" alongside "bringing in" to indicate an essential cleaving. For a "kiss" represents the "cleaving of spirit to spirit," <note>original: *devekuta de-rucha be-rucha*</note> as is known. The soul then says: **"And no one would despise me,"** <note>Song of Songs 8:1</note> for in this state, there will be no disgrace or shame for the soul, even though it descends from its high, holy place to enter the bodies of men. This is because the body will now be prepared and worthy for its union with the soul to be permanent and eternal forever—unlike in the past, when separation occurred due to the lack of refinement in the physical matter of the body.

The soul further says: **"I would lead you and bring you into my mother’s house, who would instruct me..."** <note>Song of Songs 8:2</note> This is explained in the same way as before, except that "my mother" refers to the *Shekhinah*, and "instruct me" <note>original: *telamdeni*</note> refers to "the living," <note>original: *le-chai*</note> which is *Binah* <term>Binah: 'Understanding,' one of the higher divine emanations or Sefirot</term>. The intention is that the soul will receive the flow of the Torah in its depth and inwardness from the *Shekhinah* and will then channel it into the body connected to it. This is the meaning of **"I would give you spiced wine to drink, the juice of my pomegranates,"** <note>Song of Songs 8:2</note> following the path explained above. 

Regarding their essential cleaving and union that will exist between the soul and body after their "marriage" and bonding together, she says: **"His left hand is under my head"** <note>Song of Songs 8:3</note>. This is like one who clings to her beloved in an essential union. **"And his right hand embraces me."** It is possible that "left" and "right" indicate the causes of this permanent union: namely, the two acquisitions and virtues the body gained through its connection to her. Regarding the performance of the commandments <note>original: *mitzvot*</note>, she says **"his left hand,"** and regarding the contemplation of the Torah, she says **"right,"** as it is written: "From His right hand went a fiery law for them."

**"I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem..."** <note>Song of Songs 8:4</note> It says there in the *Midrash HaNe'elam* that for forty years before the Resurrection of the Dead, the body waits to be brought into the Land of Israel, and in the fortieth year, the bodies arise from their dust. Truly, there is no doubt that the souls, while waiting for the bodies to be transformed, seek to hasten the "end" <note>original: *ketz*; the appointed time of redemption</note> of the Resurrection so they can reconnect with the bodies and quickly merit the taste of the life of the World to Come. Therefore, the servant Metatron <note>a high-ranking archangel often described as the heavenly scribe or guide</note>, who is appointed over this matter, appeases them and speaks to their hearts, telling them not to hurry to bring the "end" until the time designated for it arrives. This is the meaning of **"I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem,"** for the souls are called "daughters." As it says there: What is the significance of this name at that time? Because that is their place while waiting for the bodies—as will be explained later—they are destined to come by way of the Mount of Olives and be brought by the aforementioned servant. **"Why should you stir up or awaken love until it pleases?"** <note>Song of Songs 8:4</note> Such is the nature of the union of the soul and the body.

<summary>This page provides a mystical commentary on Song of Songs, interpreting the biblical "kiss" and "embrace" as the permanent, spiritual union of the soul and the refined body after the Resurrection. It describes the soul's journey back to the body in the Land of Israel and the angel Metatron's role in moderating the souls' longing for the afterlife.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically focusing on the transition from the Messianic era to the Resurrection of the Dead. The previous page established the soul’s desire for a permanent, unbreakable bond with the body, likened to the bond between siblings.</meta>

# Commentary

Regarding the phrase "until love pleases" <note>original: *ad shetechpaz*; Song of Songs 8:4</note>, this is interpreted by our Sages (may their memory be a blessing) as a subtle hint. If you examine it closely, you will find it. For us, the meaning is closer to what was written regarding the heart: what the soul seeks here by saying **"Why should you stir up..."** <note>Song of Songs 8:4</note> rather than "If you stir up..." This is because the oath here was intended for the deceased <note>literally "the dead," referring to those waiting for resurrection</note>, as explained previously. The intent was to bring about the actual benefit and to serve as a help and salvation through the Name (Blessed be He), until He desires to lead the prayer and grant her request.

This is the secret of the verse **"Why should you stir up or awaken love"** <note>Song of Songs 8:4</note>. It is as if to say: I have adjured you concerning the great abundance and intensity of the awakening of love between me and my Beloved, until she <note>the soul</note> desires to wait for the fulfillment of this, my passion. In this context, the word "Why" <note>Hebrew: *mah*</note> functions as it does in the verse "How [mah] I love Your Torah!" <note>Psalm 119:97</note>. This is easy to understand.

After I wrote regarding these verses, I saw in the *Midrash HaNe'elam* <term>Midrash HaNe'elam: The "Hidden Midrash," a mystical, early layer of the Zohar</term> for the Torah portion *Toldot*, where Isaac begins his discourse on the verse **"The mandrakes give off a fragrance"** <note>Song of Songs 7:14</note> in the context of the Resurrection of the Dead. It is taught: 

"Rabbi Isaac began: 'The mandrakes give off a fragrance.' Our Rabbis taught: In the future to come, the Holy One, Blessed be He, will revive the dead and shake them from their dust. They will be a 'building of dust' just as they were when they were first created from actual dust. Thus, the words of our brothers are fulfilled, as it is written: 'And the Lord God formed man of the dust of the ground' <note>Genesis 2:7</note>. At 그 hour, they will shake themselves from the dust of their sicknesses <note>referring to the decay of the grave</note> and will stand as a completed 'building,' established to have permanent existence. This is the meaning of the verse: 'Shake yourself from the dust; arise, and sit down, O Jerusalem' <note>Isaiah 52:2</note>. They will be firmly established, rise from beneath the earth, and receive their souls in the Land of Israel."

"At that hour, the Holy One, Blessed be He, will cause all kinds of fragrances from the Garden of Eden to overflow. This is the meaning of **'The mandrakes give off a fragrance.'** Rabbi Isaac said: These 'mandrakes' <term>Dudaim: Hebrew for mandrakes; the word shares a root with 'Dod' (beloved/friend)</term> refer to the body and the soul, which are 'beloveds' and 'friends' to one another."

"Rab Nachman said: They are literal mandrakes. Just as mandrakes give birth to love in the world and bring life, they 'give off a fragrance' so that a few may know and recognize their generation. And **'at our doors are all manner of precious fruits'** <note>Song of Songs 7:14</note>—these 'doors' are the openings of the bodies which are opened to receive the souls. **'All manner of precious fruits'** refers to the souls. **'Both new and old'** <note>Song of Songs 7:14</note>: 'Old' refers to those whose souls departed many years ago, and 'new' refers to those whose souls departed only a few days ago. All those who were found worthy through their deeds to enter the World to Come are destined to descend all at once to enter the bodies prepared for them."

"Rabbi Acha bar Jacob said: A Heavenly Voice <note>original: *Bat Kol*</note> goes forth and says: **'New and old, my beloved, I have laid up for you.'** I have hidden them in those higher worlds for you, for your sake. At that moment, the body is pure. Rabbi Chiya said: 'The mandrakes give off a fragrance' refers to the Angels of Peace. 'They give off a fragrance' refers to the souls, which are the fragrance of the world. They give their justification, as it is said: 'But Sihon did not give [allow] Israel' <note>Numbers 21:23; a linguistic play on 'giving'</note>. As it was taught: Rabbi Judah said: Three groups of ministering angels go every New Moon and every Sabbath to escort the soul to its place of stature, and they are pure. **'And at our doors are all manner of precious fruits'**—Rabbi Judah said: These are the bodies that stand at the 'doors' of the graves to receive their souls. The verse hints that they are 'openings' that are found, and the soul cries out and says: 'Master of the Universe, **new and old**—those buried for many days and those buried recently—I have hidden them all for You, to bring them forth in the final account.'"

Thus far are the words of the Midrash. As your eyes can see, the conclusion of these "pleasant words" aligns perfectly with the literal meaning of the scripture regarding the Resurrection of the Dead. Since this is the case, I say it is possible to continue interpreting the verses according to our path. Until this point, we have dealt with the Messianic Era as explained; from here on, we shall move to speak through His Holy Spirit regarding the Resurrection of the Dead. Concerning the first verse, there is no need to add to their words. 

From them, we shall learn to extend the rest of the verses in this manner. In the following section, it is written: **"O that you were like a brother to me"** <note>Song of Songs 8:1</note>. The intention is that it is well known that the closeness of brotherhood is an essential kinship and a [permanent] attachment...

<summary>This page uses the Zohar's Midrash HaNe'elam to interpret the Song of Songs as a prophecy of the Resurrection. It identifies the "mandrakes" and "precious fruits" as the souls—both those long dead and recently deceased—reuniting with their physical bodies in the Land of Israel.</summary>

נח <note>Page 58</note>
->### Song of Songs<-

His way of explaining the phrase **"There your mother travailed with you"** <note>original: *shama chiblatka imeka*; Song of Songs 8:5</note> is to hint at the receiving of the Torah. They received it amidst fear and danger, as we mentioned previously—that their souls departed <note>A midrashic tradition suggests the Israelites died from the intensity of the Divine voice at Sinai and were resurrected</note> and it was the Torah itself that stood by them during the giving of the Law to revive them. This merit and the sight of that divine strength at that time, and even this "birth," are all for the sake of the Resurrection of the Dead. It stems from the merit of the Torah they received. It is as if that "birth" took place there as well. This explains the double phrasing: "There your mother travailed with you, there she was in travail who bore you" <note>Song of Songs 8:5</note>. These words of ours, which we have interpreted according to the words of the Holy One, Blessed be He, to the Congregation of Israel, are refined in the eyes of the spirit. And if the language used is masculine, it follows the language of the Midrash we wrote above; it is not surprising, for all of the Congregation of Israel is called by the name "Israel" <note>The Hebrew grammar shifts between masculine and feminine when addressing the collective nation</note>.

It is also possible to specify the phrase **"Under the apple tree..."** <note>Song of Songs 8:5</note> based on the introduction of the Translator <note>likely referring to the Aramaic Targum</note>, who says that at the time of the Resurrection, all the dead will rise and sprout from beneath the Mount of Olives. Even the righteous who died in the exile outside the Land of Israel are destined to come there through underground tunnels <term>tunnels: *mekhilot*, legendary passages through which the bodies of the righteous roll to reach the Holy Land for resurrection</term> and will rise from beneath the aforementioned mountain. Based on these words, I say that he called that mountain an "apple tree." This is the meaning of **"Under the apple tree I awakened you"**—to say that for their benefit, the Resurrection will occur by way of that mountain, so they will be close to Jerusalem and the Holy Temple to complete their purification and perfection. And he said **"there your mother travailed with you"** regarding the dead of the Land of Israel, for it is appropriate to say "your mother" concerning those who died there. This is a precise and correct interpretation. According to the second path, the matter continues with the subject of the Resurrection of the Dead at the time of the Resurrection itself, as will be explained, and it is easy to understand.

<margin>The verses "Set me as a seal," etc., concern the nature of life in the World to Come, which follows the Resurrection. They deal with the specific distinctions between life in this world and life in the World of Souls, to know the true essence of these matters. Our Sages, called "the words of a holy mouth," speak on this matter, and from them we clarify the explanation of the verse.</margin>

## **Set me as a seal upon your heart, as a seal**
## **upon your arm; for love is strong as death,**
## **jealousy is cruel as the grave; its flashes are**
## **flashes of fire, a most vehement flame.**

This is explained by what is written further on according to the commentary of the Rif <note>This may refer to a specific mystical commentary or a thematic connection to Rabbi Isaac Alfasi, though the content is highly kabbalistic</note> regarding the **Blessing of the Moon** <term>Blessing of the Moon: *Birkat HaLevanah*, a prayer sanctifying the new moon, often associated with the future renewal of Israel</term>. He writes that they are destined to be renewed like the moon after the time of the Resurrection of the Dead. The Holy One, Blessed be He, will prepare for each and every one of them a body of completely refined matter. Each one will have a dwelling within it; they will stand, eat, drink, and give birth. Every part and limb of the body will perform its proper function and receive what is appropriate for it, doing what it ought to do without any hindrance or obstacle, whether from within or without. So too will be all the wise men of that time. Therefore, every body among them will be in the ultimate state of health possible to achieve according to its temperament, and in the ultimate perfection possible for its nature, each according to his rank. 

And after many days, once they have achieved the ultimate perfection possible for them, it is His desire and will that they shall ascend to a state of transformation. This means that their bodies, which are made of the matter of the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>, are destined to change and take on another form. The matter of their bodies will become like a simple, enduring secret—swift in movement, clear in appearance, "like the work of a paved work of sapphire stone, and like the very heaven for clearness" <note>Exodus 24:10</note>. This is because they will be prepared to receive the radiance of God and the light of the face of the King of Life, just as the moon is a body that receives its light, and so on. This ends his words.

# Commentary

->With a taste of wisdom and easy to understand<-

<margin>According to the first method, and it appears that after something was missing, Solomon concluded...</margin>
<margin>According to the first method</margin>

**Who is this coming up from the wilderness, leaning upon her beloved? Under the apple tree I awakened you; there your mother was in travail with you; there she who bore you was in travail.** <note>Song of Songs 8:5</note>

This precedes the discussion of the Days of the Messiah <term>Yemot HaMashiach; the era of ultimate peace and redemption</term> and the longing and yearning of the Congregation of Israel <term>Knesset Yisrael; the collective soul of the Jewish people</term> for the life of the World to Come <term>Olam Ha-Ba; the spiritual afterlife or the perfected world</term>, which is a world that is entirely good, a world that is eternal. It speaks of the life of that world and the greatness of its enduring nature.

He began by saying, in the name of the Blessed Name: "Who is this coming up from the wilderness..." etc. Before explaining the verse, let us first present the words of the Sage <note>original: "the MaChaRaN"M," an abbreviation for a specific commentator, possibly a reference to a local or specific rabbinic authority</note> regarding this verse, from which we learned to follow this path in these verses. He said: Solomon said, as it were, that when the dead are seen, the Mount of Olives <note>original Aramaic: *Tur Mishcha*</note> is destined to be split open, and all the dead of Israel are destined to emerge from beneath it. Even the righteous who died in the exile are destined to come through the paths of the tunnels <note>original Aramaic: *orchach kocheya*; referring to the tradition of *gilgul mechilot*, where bodies roll through underground passages to reach the Land of Israel for resurrection</note> beneath the earth, and they will emerge from beneath the Mount of Olives. But the wicked who died... etc. Then all the inhabitants of the earth will say: "What was the merit of this nation that ascends from the earth, tens of thousands upon tens of thousands, like a dove that ascends from the wilderness to the Land of Israel, and they delight in the mercies of their Master, just as they delighted beneath Mount Sinai to receive the Torah?" etc.

The essence of his commentary is that the scripture chose the Resurrection of the Dead as its subject. The reference to the "Wilderness" is used as a metaphor, as he said: "Like a dove that ascends from the wilderness..." and the phrase **"Under the apple tree I awakened you"** means he describes the opening of the "apple" <note>likely a metaphor for the womb or the opening of the grave</note>. This follows the Midrashic interpretation <term>Midrash; ancient rabbinic homiletic teachings</term> that **"Under the apple tree I awakened you"** refers to Sinai.

Therefore, the flow of the verse and its hidden pearls concern the World to Come after the Resurrection, according to the plain meaning of the scriptures. He spoke in the name of the Holy One, Blessed be He, regarding the Congregation of Israel: "Who is this" who has merited to "ascend from the wilderness"? This is a metaphor for the place where bodies are buried, which is like a desolate wilderness. Regarding the trust and the ascent from there, it says: "coming up from the wilderness." The meaning of "leaning" <note>original: *mitrapekhet*</note> is that she ascends from the wilderness and is worthy to cling to and be united with her Beloved, for the filth of the physical body has already been removed in the dust, and everything is found to be pure and clean silver.

And he said: **"Under the apple tree I awakened you"** regarding the matter of the Resurrection that occurred for Israel during the standing at Mount Sinai. As our Sages of blessed memory said regarding the verse "My soul departed when he spoke" <note>Song of Songs 5:6</note>—search for its place in the palm <note>an idiomatic or mystical reference to God's "holding" the souls</note>—for there He awakened and aroused Israel like a woman in labor <note>playing on the word *shovavah*</note> regarding the future when He will revive the dead. Furthermore, "Under the apple tree I awakened you" means you tasted the taste of the Resurrection of the Dead and merited the Supreme Taste that awakens one to this. That resurrection, as well as the one that will be in the future, were in the merit of the Torah you received there.

Also, "the opening of your wisdom was cut," and this will be understood with what our Sages of blessed memory said: The Holy One, Blessed be He, opened for them [the choice], saying, "If you accept My Torah, it is well; but if not, I will overturn this mountain upon you like a cask <note>original: *kigigit*</note> and kill you." And that "cradle of the bush" <note>referring to Sinai/the burning bush</note> was "where your mother travailed with you" <note>original: *chiblatka*</note>... The end of "travail" <note>original: *chevlah*</note> occurs with a weakening, meaning that one walked in a place of danger and fell, or a companion met him. The letter *Chet* is emphasized for "strength." Similarly, you passed through many dangers, and now "she who bore you" has "brought you forth." Just as you passed through the fire, you were then created anew in a new embrace <note>referring to a spiritual rebirth</note>. These are his words, and the words of the Midrash are clear, and from them we learn...

<summary>This page interprets Song of Songs 8:5 as an allegory for the Resurrection of the Dead, connecting the "wilderness" to the grave and the "apple tree" to the experience of receiving the Torah at Mount Sinai.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection of the Dead, Mount Sinai, Mount of Olives, World to Come, Days of the Messiah, Midrash, Aramaic Targum, Song of Songs commentary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Song of Songs, transitioning from the mechanics of the Resurrection of the Dead to the transformation of the physical body into a spiritual state. It addresses the nature of the "World to Come" and the specific meaning of the request to be "set as a seal."</meta>

# Song of Songs

Moreover, for one who examines these matters closely, many issues will be settled according to the plain meaning <note>Hebrew: *peshat*</note>, such as the matter of the **Feast of the Leviathan** <note>A traditional metaphor for the reward of the righteous in the afterlife</note> and its announcements. To conclude the first matter: it will extend from the time of the Resurrection until the end of the sixth millennium. The second period is in the seventh part—namely, a "Sabbath to the Lord"—and that is indeed the **World to Come** <term>Olam Ha-Ba; the era of ultimate spiritual perfection</term>, a world that is entirely good, a world that is eternally long. We shall not expand further on this here; it is enough for us what I have written in hint. 

Behold, this is a great insight for understanding the sayings of **our Sages of blessed memory** <term>Raza"l; the collective term for the rabbinic authorities of the Talmudic era</term> and the verses of the Prophets written regarding the Days of the Messiah, the Resurrection of the Dead, and the World to Come.

It is proper to know what the **Ch"i** <term>likely Rabbi Hayyim Vital (1542–1620), the primary student of the Arizal</term> **of blessed memory** wrote in the second part <insert>**of blessed memory**</insert>: 

> "And the matter is that in the future, their body—which is the material of the four elements—is destined to change and receive a different quality. It will become a refined material, like a simple, eternal element, etc." 

This is not to say that it will change in its actual existence or its form, or that it will lack any part of the body's members or its elements, **Heaven forbid** <note>Hebrew: *Chas V'Shalom*</note>. Rather, this is the essence of the intent: as time passes, as they continue to achieve great and hidden spiritual attainments little by little until they are attached to **the Blessed One** with an essential cleaving <term>Dveikut Atzmit; a state of total spiritual union with the Divine</term>. For according to this value, the bodies will continue to be refined, level after level, until the earthliness of the body's material is removed and nullified. It will change to become refined, thin, spiritual, glowing, and shining like the **radiance of the firmament** <note>A reference to Daniel 12:3</note>, while maintaining all the body's parts and elements. They will simply turn into spiritual, refined, and eternal entities like the soul, like a "simple, eternal element," etc. 

This is easy to understand. However, what he [the *Ch"i*] wrote regarding the matter of **Enoch** <note>The biblical figure who "walked with God" and was taken by Him without dying normally</note> is not agreed upon by all of us. For the calculation of the words in the **Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note> proves there that when he departed from his body, he left it in a cave, remaining there like a golem <note>An unformed physical mass</note> while he wore another spiritual garment. When he descends to this world, he returns and clothes himself in his first body which is in the cave, and uses it in the world. 

The conclusion of these matters is that the **World of Souls** is after death, where there are only souls alone. But in the time after the Resurrection, they will merit it in both body and soul. Through it, they will be elevated until they achieve the status mentioned: to be like the soul—refined, clear, spiritual, and glowing like the radiance of the firmament. They will rise above the level of the angels, until the angels will need to ask the righteous: "What has God wrought?" as **our Sages of blessed memory** said regarding the verse, "At this time it shall be said to Jacob and to Israel..." <note>Numbers 23:23</note>. Furthermore, they will merit what **our Sages of blessed memory** <term>Chaza"l</term> said: 

> "In the future, **the Holy One, Blessed be He** <term>Ha-Kadosh Barukh Hu</term>, is destined to be a dance-circle for the righteous, and they will point with a finger at the face of the King." 

And "they shall behold the likeness of the Lord," so to speak. We all admit to these things, as it is written: "For this is God, our God..." <note>Psalm 48:15</note>. 

Regarding these great attainments, it is possible to explain the verse and arrive at the intent of our specific verse, which says: **"Set me as a seal upon your heart, as a seal upon your arm..."** <note>Song of Songs 8:6</note>. The intent is that after the Congregation of Israel has begun to enter the general merit of physical refinement and thinness, and is now being lifted level after level until she reaches the ultimate thinness and spirituality, she then achieves—as soul to soul—an "essential cleaving" and great, hidden attainments. 

Immediately upon reaching this level of knowledge, she prayed to **the Name, Blessed be He** <term>Shit'; an abbreviation for Ha-Shem Yitbarakh</term>, that He might assist and help her to merit an easy and swift union, so that she might achieve and reach that "essential cleaving" from which these aforementioned attainments flow—and more than them, which "no eye has seen," etc. 

He compared this essential cleaving to a **seal**. A seal is lacking [in its own material] as it is engraved and marked into the place where it is pressed, and the rest of its existence is marked and engraved within it. Therefore, she said: **"Set me as a seal upon your heart."** And because the heart is a hidden and concealed organ, where the matter of the engraving and marking of the seal is not recognized or famous, he then repeated: **"as a seal upon your arm,"** which is in a revealed and open place, so that every onlooker may recognize it.

It interprets the "seal upon the heart" as a prayer for "Essential Cleaving" (Dveikut), a state of union with God that surpasses even the angels.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the transition between the physical world and the spiritual future. It builds on the previous page's quote regarding the refinement of the human body into "simple, eternal element" by reconciling different rabbinic traditions about the timeline of the Resurrection and the Messianic Era.</meta>

# Commentary

<meta>catchword: and what</meta>

The **Great Rabbi** <term>Ha-Rav Ha-Gaon; a title for a preeminent Torah scholar</term>, may his memory be for a blessing in the world to come, guided us regarding our subject. The understanding of his words is that after the **Resurrection** <term>Tchiyat Ha-Metim; the belief that the dead will be brought back to life in the future</term>, life will be divided into two parts. For a certain period of time, even though human bodies will be in a state of ultimate health and perfection, the "way of the world" will continue its course. People will eat, drink, and procreate, and everything found in the world will continue its function. 

However, after many days have passed, the body will be refined and take on a different character. It will acquire a "refined matter"—swift in movement, radiant, and clear—which exists forever. It will live a life that does not require eating or drinking, nor will it be subject to any of the physical accidents or ailments of the body, even though they still possess a physical form. 

Now, one who examines these words carefully will see that the Great Rabbi apparently holds the opinion of the **Translator** <note>original: "ha-Metargem"; referring to the Targum, the ancient Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Bible</note>, who translates the time of the Resurrection of the Dead as occurring at the moment of the Messiah's arrival and the time of the **Redemption** <term>Geulah</term>. He translates the previous verse as follows: 

> "Then all the inhabitants of the earth shall say: 'What is the merit of this people who come up from the earth?' For on the day they come up from the desert to the Land of Israel, they shall delight in the mercies of their Master, just as they did on the day the Torah was given at Mount Sinai. In that time, Zion shall see Israel and complete the building of its children, and Jerusalem shall receive the children of the exile." <note>original: "בכן יימרון כל דיירי ארעא מה הוה זכותא דעמא הדין..." quoting the Aramaic Targum on Song of Songs</note> 

These words indicate that the **Ingathering of the Exiles** and the Resurrection of the Dead both occur at the same time. However, the Rabbi further explains that for a duration after the Messiah’s arrival, the world will follow its natural course regarding all bodily matters, such as eating and drinking. During this time, Israel will be at the height of the world in status and authority, enjoying all physical goodness to the ultimate degree, as well as reaching a very high level of spiritual perfection. The earth will be filled with the knowledge of the Lord, until they grasp the Torah, its secrets, and its inner depths beyond measure, and they will be cleaving to the Lord their God with an essential attachment. 

This period, which will last for some time, is called the **Days of the Messiah**. Afterward, the souls will be purified until they are refined into spiritual beings. They will cease to experience bodily needs and will instead feast upon the "radiance of the Divine Presence" <term>Ziv ha-Shekhinah</term>, attaining infinite, hidden perceptions. In this state of existence, they will endure forever. This second part is called the **World to Come** <term>Olam Ha-Ba</term>. 

Truly, one who studies the Midrashim and the sayings of **our Sages of blessed memory** <term>Raza"l</term> will find many statements that seem to contradict one another. By dividing the time into these two parts as mentioned, one finds a proper resolution to them. 

However, according to what is verified in some Midrashim and clarified in the **Zohar** <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah)</note> (in the portions of *Shemot* and *Vayera*), their words do not necessarily seek to harmonize these two worlds together. Rather, both sources clearly indicate that the time of the Resurrection is not exactly at the Messiah's arrival, but some time afterward—unlike the premise we understood according to the Great Rabbi. 

Nevertheless, I see room to interpret his words: perhaps the two parts of time he mentioned both occur *after* the Resurrection, separate from the period of the Messiah's initial arrival. There is support for his view that for some time immediately following the Resurrection, the natural functions and habits of the human body will not cease. Even if this is not explicitly stated in the Sages' words, it is a clear tradition <note>original: "Kabbalah"</note> and a sound opinion. For it is difficult to say that immediately after the Resurrection, bodily functions would be instantly abolished. Indeed, the Sages were careful to say that the dead will rise in the same garments they wore before death so that people would not say they are "new creatures." This opens the door to the idea that physical nature will not be abolished from them immediately...

<summary>This page discusses the transition from the Messianic Era to the World to Come, explaining how the resurrected body eventually transforms from a physical state to a refined, spiritual one. It attempts to reconcile conflicting rabbinic and mystical traditions regarding the timing of the Resurrection and the Ingathering of the Exiles.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection of the Dead, Days of the Messiah, World to Come, Olam Ha-Ba, Zohar, Targum, spiritual refinement, Ingathering of the Exiles, Shekhinah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the physical body during the Resurrection. It moves from the general timeline of the Messianic era into a specific Kabbalistic explanation of how physical matter transforms into spiritual light, reconciling the views of the Remaz (Rabbi Moshe Zacuto) with the Zohar's account of Elijah the Prophet.</meta>

# Song of Songs

Behold, even for one who examines these matters closely, many things will be settled for him according to the plain meaning <note>original: "peshat"; the literal or surface level of scripture</note>, such as the matter of the **Feast of the Leviathan** <term>Seudat Leviathan; a legendary banquet promised to the righteous in the afterlife</term> and similar topics. It is possible that this first period will extend from the time of the **Resurrection** until the end of the **Sixth Millennium** <note>In Jewish chronology, the world as we know it lasts 6,000 years, followed by a cosmic Sabbath</note>. The second period occurs in the seventh part, namely, the **Sabbath to the Lord**; this is the **World to Come** <term>Olam Ha-Ba</term>, a world that is entirely good, a world that is eternally long, unlike the fleeting nature of our world here. Let what I have written on these matters suffice, as it provides a great awakening and power for understanding the words of our Sages and the verses of the Prophets written concerning the Days of the Messiah, the Resurrection of the Dead, and the World to Come.

It is proper to know what the **Remaz** <term>Rabbi Moshe Zacuto (1625–1697), a prominent Kabbalist</term>, may his memory be for a blessing, wrote in the second part: "The matter is that in the future, the body—which is composed of the matter of the four elements—will be refined and take on a different form. It will become a refined and thin substance, like a simple, eternal element..." 

This is not to say that their existence or form will be fundamentally altered, or that a part of the body's elements will be removed or destroyed, Heaven forbid. Rather, the core intention is that over the course of time, through spiritual refinements, the coarse perceptions are gradually removed until they cleave to the Divine with an "essential attachment." According to this progression, the bodies will go on refining themselves, level after level, until the coarseness of the body’s matter is clarified and nullified. The desire is to become refined, thin, and spiritual, shining and radiant like the **Radiance of the Firmament** <note>Reference to Daniel 12:3</note>, while maintaining all parts of the body and its elements. They simply turn into strong and enduring spiritual entities, as if they were a "simple, eternal element."

This is easily understood from what was written regarding the matter of **Elijah** <term>the Prophet</term>, which is agreed upon by all of us. This consensus is supported by the **Book of the Zohar** <term>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</term>, where it is proven that he stripped off his physical body and left it in a whirlwind, and there he clothed himself in another, spiritual garment. When he descends to this world, he returns and clothes himself in his first body, which remains in a cave. 

The ultimate point of these things is this: the "World of Souls" exists after death, where only the souls reside. However, in the **World to Come**, which follows the Resurrection, they merit existence in both body and soul. They will ascend until they achieve a state where both body and soul are refined, clear, and spiritual. They will be like the "Radiance of the Firmament" and will rise higher than the level of the angels. Indeed, the angels will ask those who are so closely attached to the Divine: "What has God wrought?"—as our Sages said regarding the verse: *“Now it shall be said of Jacob and Israel, [what has God wrought]”* <note>Numbers 23:23</note>. 

Furthermore, they will merit that the Holy One, Blessed be He, will make His spirit like a dance for the righteous. They will perceive the light of the King's face and "behold the image of the Lord" <note>Numbers 12:8</note>, as it were, pointing with a finger as it is written: *"For this is God, our God"* <note>Psalm 48:15</note>.

Regarding these great perceptions, it is possible to explain the verse and arrive at the intention of our specific verse, which says: **"Set me as a seal upon your heart, as a seal upon your arm"** <note>Song of Songs 8:6</note>. The intention is that after the **Congregation of Israel** <term>Knesset Yisrael; the personified spiritual community of Israel</term> enters into the general merit of refined matter and its thinness, the soul knows that tomorrow it will ascend level after level until it reaches the ultimate refinement and spirituality. Then, both body and soul will achieve an essential attachment and great, hidden perceptions. 

Since the "vessel" <note>the physical body</note> is currently too narrow in its power to contain this knowledge, she [the soul] acts with wisdom toward the Holy One, Blessed be He, so that He might assist her and help her merit this life quickly. Secondly, she seeks to prevent any delay in this essential attachment, from which these perceptions flow—perceptions even greater than those of which it is said: *"No eye has seen [what God has prepared]"* <note>Isaiah 64:3</note>. 

He compares this essential attachment to a **seal**, which is engraved and imprinted upon its designated place, leaving its existence marked and carved within it. Therefore, it says: "Set me as a seal upon your heart." And because the heart is like a hidden world, and the engraving and marking of the seal is not recognized or publicized there, he repeats and says: "As a seal upon your arm," which is a visible and recognized place, so that everyone who sees it will recognize it.

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual refinement of the physical body following the Resurrection, explaining how matter becomes "radiant like the firmament." It uses the example of Elijah the Prophet's dual nature and the biblical metaphor of the "seal" to describe the soul's ultimate attachment to God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical transformation of the body following the Resurrection. The previous page introduced the view of the Remaz (Rabbi Moshe Zacuto) regarding the refinement of material elements into a spiritual substance. This page expands on that by citing the Ra'ah (Rabbi Aaron ha-Levi) and reconciling various rabbinic sources concerning the two-stage transition from the Messianic Age to the World to Come.</meta>

# Commentary
### [Page 8, Side 2]

The **Ra’ah** <term>Rabbi Aaron ha-Levi of Barcelona, a 13th-century scholar</term>, may his memory be for a blessing in the World to Come, has guided us regarding our current subject. From his words, it is understood that after the **Resurrection of the Dead**, life will be divided into two distinct periods.

For immediately [after the Resurrection] and for a certain period of time, the bodies of men will be in a state of ultimate health and perfection. Nevertheless, the natural order of the world will continue as usual; they will eat, drink, and bear children, and all their actions will be carried out in the usual manner. However, after many days have passed, the body will change and take on a different nature. It will gain a substance that is swift of movement, radiant, and clear—one that exists eternally. They will live a life [without] <note>The context suggests the negation of physical needs in this higher state</note> eating or drinking, nor will they experience any of the physical "accidents" <term>In medieval philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential properties like hunger, sickness, or fatigue</term> of the body, even though they remain physical beings.

Now, one who examines these words carefully will see that it appears the Ra’ah’s opinion follows that of the **Targum** <term>The ancient Aramaic translation/paraphrase of the Bible</term>, of blessed memory. This implies that the entire time of the Resurrection of the Dead occurs at the coming of the **Messiah** and the time of the Redemption, and that no one will be resurrected at any other time. For thus it is translated in the verse mentioned above: "So that all the inhabitants of the earth shall say: 'What is the merit of this people that comes up from the land of Cush? Myriads of people on the day they go up from the desert to the Land of Israel, pampered by the mercies of the Lord of Spices, like the day they stood beneath Mount Sinai to receive the Freedom [the Torah].' And at that future time, when He sees the children of Israel, He will return His children to Jerusalem to receive the children of the exile." <note>original Aramaic: "בגין וימרון כל דיירי ארעא... לבני גלותא"</note>.

These words indicate that the **Ingathering of the Exiles** and the **Resurrection of the Dead** will occur at the same time. However, it is further explained that after the coming of the Messiah and for a certain period, the world will follow its usual course regarding all physical matters—eating, drinking, and all bodily functions. Israel will be at the pinnacle of the world in terms of status and authority, enjoying all physical goodness to the ultimate degree. Furthermore, the soul will be filled with intellectual light, and "the earth shall be full of the knowledge of the Lord," until they achieve comprehension of the **Torah**, its secrets, and its inner dimensions. They will live a precious life, cleaving to the Lord their God with an essential attachment.

This first period will be called the **Days of the Messiah**. Afterward, the bodies will be so refined that they transform into spiritual beings; all physical "accidents" will cease, and they will instead be nourished by the **Radiance of the Divine Presence** <term>Ziv ha-Shekhinah; the spiritual light that sustains the souls of the righteous</term>, achieving hidden perceptions without end. In this state, they will exist forever. This second stage is called the **World to Come** <term>Olam Ha-Ba</term>.

Truly, one who delves into the **Midrashim** <term>Ancient rabbinic homilies and legal interpretations</term> and the sayings of our Sages will find many things that seem contradictory. However, with this introduction—that time is divided into two parts—a proper reconciliation can be found. This is particularly clarified in several Midrashim and specifically in the **Zohar** <term>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</term> in the portions of *Shemot* (Exodus) and *Vayera* (Genesis). These sources speak with one voice to clearly state that the time of the Resurrection of the Dead is at the coming of the Messiah, but the transformation occurs some time later—not exactly according to the premise we established based on the Ra’ah’s opinion.

Indeed, there is room to detail the two parts of time that the book mentions after the Resurrection, separate from the period of the Days of the Messiah. It is implied by the Sages that immediately after the Resurrection, the natural characteristics and habits of the body will not cease until some time has passed. Even if this is not explicitly written in the Sages' words in a clear book, it was likely their opinion. For it is a difficult thing to suggest that immediately after the Resurrection, the physical functions of the body would be nullified. Perhaps this is why our Sages were careful to say that the dead will return to life wearing the same "garments" <note>Their physical bodies and clothes from their previous life</note> they had before their death—so that people would not say they are "new creatures." This mistake might have occurred if the physical characteristics and functions of the body were to vanish immediately.

<summary>This page explains a two-stage process following the Resurrection: first, a period of physical perfection where the natural order remains, and second, a transformation into spiritual beings nourished by Divine Radiance. The author reconciles the views of the Ra'ah, the Targum, and the Zohar to clarify the timeline of the Messianic Age and the World to Come.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion on the physical nature of the resurrected body to a mystical commentary on the Song of Songs (Shir HaShirim). It specifically focuses on the famous verses in Chapter 8 regarding the unquenchable nature of love, interpreting "love" as the intense, insatiable bond between the soul (or the People of Israel) and God, which even the highest angels cannot surpass.</meta>

# Song of Songs

...my essence, which was mentioned by the title "love," as it says, "**for love is strong as death**" [Song of Songs 8:6]. It does not say [this] unless the Name <note>A common substitute for the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God</note> grants her its special virtue in great abundance and overflow, comparable to **many waters**. Nevertheless, one will not reach a state of "drowning"—that is, being inclined to be fully satisfied by its powers or to quench the thirst of the thought. On the contrary, when one reaches a level of great attainment, they will not reach a final conclusion; rather, they have achieved only one in a thousand thousand millions, like a drop in the ocean. They will be like the eye of a needle <note>A metaphor for a tiny opening or a point of focus</note> burning to attain more. For as much as attainment increases, so too will desire and longing increase.

And this is what is meant by: "**Many waters cannot quench love, nor can rivers wash it away**" [Song of Songs 8:7]. "**If a man should give all the wealth of his house**"—meaning all that exists in the preparation of his body and soul to attain through the practice of the commandments, good deeds, and the study of the Torah—it would not lack for even a hair's breadth of power to actually attain all that is within the potential of his soul and body in perfection, in love for Him forever, until the ultimate end of love and attachment <term>Deveikut: a mystical state of "cleaving" or spiritual union with the Divine</term> to the Blessed One. In this, they would utterly scorn him <note>Original: "boz yavuza lo"</note>, because it is impossible to reach its ultimate end, as stated.

And in the manner explained previously regarding the fear of the jealousy of kings and their accusations, it is possible to specify that the Blessed Name replied that you should not fear nor be dismayed by all the kings and their jealousy. For even if a multitude of kings gathered like the multitude of **"many waters"** to accuse her <note>The community of Israel</note> and to hinder their love and the attainment of her attachment with her God—they are nothing at all. And even if they were the high celestial kings, since they are the classes of the angels of the Chariot <term>Merkabah: the "Chariot" of Ezekiel's vision, representing the divine hierarchy</term> known to be of a high level—even they could not wash her away or hinder her. And this is what is meant by: "**nor can rivers wash it away.**"

And do not only be assured that they will not hinder [you], but even if they arouse their words to join with you and to attain the level of attachment to the Blessed One like you, so that your prayer is completed—their words will not be accepted. And this is what is meant by: "**If a man should give all the wealth of his house,**" etc. It is known that the angels are called "men" <term>Ishim: literally "men," this is the name of one of the ten ranks of angels</term>. This is to say that if they were to give and mention before the Blessed Name all types of praise, song, holiness, and the fulfillment of His Blessed word—which they have possessed from their creation until this day—all of this would be considered "the wealth of their house." And if they gave it all into the flood of the attainment of love, they would utterly scorn him.

The verse uses the term "man" in the singular to say that even the most unique among them, the "Living Creature" <term>Hayyot: a high order of angels from Ezekiel’s vision</term> of their creatures who are of the highest level—they would utterly scorn him. And in all of this, we prepare to crown **Gabriel**, who is called "man," as it is said: "**And the man Gabriel...**" [Daniel 9:21]. And the Living Creature <note>Original: "Hayyah"</note> which pours out like wine of life hints at the **Prince of the Presence** <term>Sar HaPanim: a high angelic title, often associated with Metatron</term> who was previously a man—for this is **Enoch**, who is **Metatron**. It is appropriate to say of him that "he gave all the wealth of his house," for before he was taken up, he possessed commandments, good deeds, and Torah study. Nevertheless, the strength of the Righteous, who are at the level of "Sons," shall rise to a higher level.

And it is possible that the Blessed Name replied that "**many waters**" refers to the internal attainment, a burning flame called "water." Even if many attainments are reached, like many waters, they "**cannot quench love,**" for they do not descend into the dwelling of those who attain, as mentioned above. And "**rivers**" refers to the Providence in attachment to the Blessed One; they "**shall not wash it away,**" as explained above. And even in what is possible to attain, the body and soul will not be equal, for certainly the soul will attain more according to its preparation. And this is what is said: "If a man should give water"—referring to the matter of the physical body—into the flood of the attainment of essential love like the soul, they would utterly scorn him.

->Sister<-

<image-desc>Triangular purple library stamp at the bottom of the page from the University of Haifa Library (המכון האוניברסיטאי של חיפה הספרייה).</image-desc>

<summary>This page provides a mystical commentary on Song of Songs 8:7, arguing that the love between God and the "Sons" (Israel/the Righteous) is so profound that no amount of spiritual attainment can exhaust it, and even the highest angels like Gabriel and Metatron cannot equal it.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Shir HaShirim, Many Waters, Love, Deveikut, Gabriel, Metatron, Enoch, Angels, Ishim, Merkabah</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the mystical analysis of Song of Songs 8:6–7. The previous page established that the intense love and "cleaving" (Deveikut) between the human soul and the Divine is an infinite process that cannot be fully satisfied. This page explores why this relationship causes jealousy among the angels and how it manifests differently for scholars of Torah versus those who focus on practical good deeds.</meta>

# Commentary

Because they—the seed of those blessed by the Lord—distance themselves [from physicality], their cleaving, in its burning intensity, becomes an essential union almost without distance or interruption. Because of this, [the angels] will not be jealous of them; therefore, she <note>The "she" refers to the Congregation of Israel or the human soul, the female protagonist of the Song of Songs</note> spoke of there being accusers against them out of jealousy over the degree of their superiority over them. For **love is as strong as death**—meaning the love they desired to give in order to achieve the aforementioned union. And **jealousy is as cruel as the grave**—meaning the jealousy of the angels who will envy them for this, because their own intent and desire is also to achieve such love. And it appears [the soul says], "my accusers and enemies will eventually tear me apart with the breath of their mouths." This is the meaning of: **its flashes are flashes of fire, a very flame of the Lord.** Therefore, **set me as a seal upon your heart, [as a seal upon your arm]** as was said.

And it is possible that she said two things—a "seal upon the heart" and a "seal upon the arm"—corresponding to two types of living beings: the Masters of Torah <note>Scholars who dedicate themselves to the intellectual and spiritual study of Divine law</note> and the Masters of Commandments <note>Those whose primary spiritual path is through practical action and good deeds</note>. For each group will achieve according to its preparation in this world—"this day to do them" [Deuteronomy 7:11], which refers to this world—and the nature of receiving their reward, which is in the World to Come in tranquility. 

There is no doubt that the reception of reward will be according to the value of the deed. As it is written in the *Midrash HaNe'elam* <note>A mystical commentary forming part of the Zohar</note> on the portion of *Toldot* regarding Isaac, of blessed memory: Rabbi Eleazar said that in one place we learn "in these" and in another place we learn "when they merit." For if the colleagues, the righteous ones, had not merited so much in these visions they saw, they would not have attained so much. For all the righteous saw, for we see, until he achieves a complete understanding—this is the end of his words. 

It is also clear that the reward of the "Masters of Deeds" will not be like the reward of the "Masters of Torah." Therefore, the attainment and union of the latter will be of a higher degree than the "good" Masters of Commandments. Regarding the group of the Masters of Torah, she said: **set me as a seal upon your heart** [the seat of intellect/soul], and regarding the group of the Masters of Deeds, she said: **as a seal upon your arm** [the instrument of action]. Regarding the preciousness of placing this seal, she said: **for love is as strong as death**, and regarding the difference in attainments for each group according to its value, she said: **jealousy is as cruel as the grave, etc.** This is to say that the [accusing] group can achieve much through the power of jealousy, wanting to attain what is proper for it without concession. Therefore, the placing of the seal is divided into two, one for each group according to its value.

Furthermore, it is possible that she said two things corresponding to the two parts of man: body and soul. For it is known that there will be no refinement or clarity of the Indwelling Presence of God in the body unless the soul rises in its levels of refinement and purity, just as the soul rises according to its preparation after its spiritual source. Therefore, she said **upon your heart** for the soul, and **upon your arm** for the body, and the scripture will be clarified as above.

Furthermore, it is possible that she said two things corresponding to two general and great attainments that will exist at that time: the attainment of "cleaving" to Him, may He be blessed, to live in the pleasantness of the Lord and to behold the light of the King’s countenance, as mentioned; and the attainment of the Torah in its inner depths until its finality. Regarding the first attainment, which is more hidden, she said: **a seal upon your heart**, and regarding the second, she said: **upon your arm**, for the Torah was given from the "right arm." And even though in another place "arm" refers to the left, here it is forced to be interpreted as the right, especially in a place where "right" is not explicitly written, as it is written elsewhere, "In His right hand" and "with His mighty arm." 

And she said **for love is as strong as death** regarding the first attainment, and **jealousy is as cruel as the grave** regarding the second—as in the matter of "the jealousy of scribes increases wisdom" <note>A Talmudic proverb (Baba Batra 21a) suggesting that healthy competition among scholars leads to greater knowledge</note>. **Its flashes are flashes of fire** refers to the attainment of the Torah, as it is written: <unclear>Is not My word like fire?</unclear> [Jeremiah 23:29] regarding the attainment of union. This is a precise grammatical point.

### Many waters, etc.
### These are the responses of the Blessed One to the claim of:
### **Many waters cannot quench**
### the Congregation of Israel in her desire to attain the union of
### **love, nor can rivers wash it away.**
### **If a man should give all the wealth of his house for love, he would be utterly scorned.**

<summary>This page provides a mystical interpretation of the "seals" in Song of Songs 8:6, distinguishing between the spiritual rewards of Torah scholars and those who perform practical good deeds. It explains that the intense bond between humans and God provokes the jealousy of angels and that spiritual growth is driven by a "holy jealousy" that increases wisdom.</summary>
