<meta>This page continues the mystical commentary on the soul's ascent. The previous page discussed the intense "love as strong as death" and the jealousy of angels toward humans who achieve divine union. This page moves to Song of Songs 8:9, exploring the two states of the soul—the "wall" and the "door"—and how each state determines the soul's ultimate refinement and reward in the afterlife.</meta>

# Song of Songs

And a soul that has been worthy to attain these great and hidden perceptions <note>perceptions: spiritual insights or mystical grasp of the Divine</note> will no longer be hindered by the materiality of the body. In this way, it is possible to interpret the status of the children of the righteous and of the Congregation of Israel <note>Knesset Yisrael: a mystical term for the collective soul of the Jewish people and the feminine aspect of the Divine</note> as a king over the matters of the body. The "clay vessel" <note>original: "guf ve-gaviyat ha-cheres," referring to the physical body as a fragile earthen pot</note> which was once beneath one's feet is elevated, for the Holy One, blessed be He, for her benefit, shared in the forty-nine walls <note>Refers to the 49 levels of understanding or purity</note> according to the path I wrote above; this is not far-fetched.

**"If she is a wall..."** etc. The angels perceived with good taste and knowledge of the soul, even if she had not yet been perfected as much as was necessary—meaning through the Torah and the fulfillment of the <term>248 positive commandments</term> <note>original: "Ramach mitzvot aseh." In Jewish tradition, there are 248 active requirements, traditionally corresponding to the parts of the human body</note> in their essential power, their details,

<column-break/>

->**If she is a wall, we will build upon her a silver battlement; and if she is a door, we will enclose her with a cedar board.**<-

---

and their components. Nevertheless, this [effort] is doubled: to be heavy <note>meaning: carries great weight or merit</note> on the great Day of Judgment, and also "light" enough to reach the ultimate goal of refinement and spirituality. But the main thing is whether she has been perfected in "turning away from evil" <note>Refers to the ethical imperative to avoid sin</note> and whether no breach was made in her "fence" by becoming soiled with iniquities and sins. Therefore they said: **If she is a wall**—meaning she is fenced in by the fence of a high wall so that the "Breaker" <note>original: "Ha-Poretz," a reference to the Accuser or Satan who seeks to breach the soul's defenses</note> did not ascend against her. It is he [the Accuser] who comes to break in and enter her midst with great bundles of sin and guilt—the "guilt of sacrilege." [If she is a wall], **we will build upon her a silver battlement**—meaning we will recognize her in the assembly and build her up in the world, as the Sages explained in the <term>Midrash</term> <note>Ancient Rabbinic homiletic interpretations of the Bible</note>: she will merit eternal life because the physical matter will be transformed into clarity and brightness like silver. This is the "silver battlement."

**And if she is a door**—meaning a breach was made in her and in her fence, like a door that allows the Breaker to pass through her with the filth of sins and transgressions. This is a very difficult matter. Yet, despite all this, **we will enclose her with a cedar board**—meaning like one who tries to block the door with a cedar plank. The intention behind mentioning all the harsh and evil decrees, the sufferings, and the exiles that have passed over the Congregation of Israel is that they should specifically [use] this strength to keep [the law]. This is like the verse "I will call you to judgment, and I will not utterly clear you," as they explained in the <term>Zohar</term> <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah)</note>. This is the meaning of "enclosing her with a cedar board"—which refers to the sealing of sins so they are no longer seen or remembered, similar to the sealing of a door. However, they are not as if they never existed, unlike the "wall" which was never breached at all. 

He further hinted with the term "cedar board" to say that when they are at this level, it is difficult to reach the rank of the "silver battlement"—which meant existing for eternal life and attaining great perceptions in the Torah, the commandments, and the divine flashes—for there would be no hindrance or dividing screen from the side of physicality. That is, [the wall-soul] reaches the ultimate purity and whiteness as I wrote. Rather, [the door-soul] comes to the rank of "cedar board." Since the cedar is mighty, strong, and enduring, so too will the matter of the body be; it will acquire strength and endurance to exist for eternity and will rise to a high level to attain immense perceptions, but not at the [highest] spiritual level. For the clarity, thinness, and spirituality of the body's matter will not be like the other level I described.

I know that the reader will find my explanation of **door** difficult, as it does not say "opening" <note>Hebrew: "petach"</note> but "door" <note>Hebrew: "delet"</note>. A "door" refers to the panels that seal the opening. In truth, there is no difficulty here: even if the opening has a door, it is still a breach compared to a solid wall. It is also possible to interpret "If" <note>Hebrew: "Im"</note> here not as a doubt (i.e., either this or that), but as "whether... or..." meaning if she has fixed her character like a wall...

<summary>This page interprets Song of Songs 8:9 as a metaphor for the soul's spiritual integrity. A "wall" represents a soul that resisted sin, earning a "silver" (refined/transparent) reward, while a "door" represents a soul that sinned but repented, earning a "cedar" (strong but opaque) protection.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Kabbalah, Zohar, Midrash, silver battlement, cedar board, 248 commandments, refinement, repentance, Knesset Yisrael, Day of Judgment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mystical commentary on Song of Songs, specifically moving from the general nature of the soul's love to the specific dialogue between God and the angels regarding the status of the "Congregation of Israel" following the resurrection and preceding the final judgment.</meta>

# Commentary

"We have a little sister," etc. Behold, according to our approach, the plain meaning of these words is that they are the words of the Holy One, blessed be He, spoken with the hosts of the heavenly angels concerning the Congregation of Israel. He linked these matters with

<column-break/>

->**We have a little sister, and she has no breasts. What shall we do for our sister on the day she is spoken for?**<-

---

the previous subject, which concerned Moses and the giving [of the Torah]. This takes place after the Resurrection <note>original: "techiyah," referring to the resurrection of the dead</note> but before the Great Day of Judgment, as investigated and chosen in the *Book of Grace* <note>original: "Sefer Chen," a title for several mystical or philosophical works, likely referring here to a specific Kabbalistic commentary</note> to show the good intentions of the angels toward Israel—to bring them benefit and to offer a defense <term>sanegorya</term> <note>original: "sanegorya," a legal term borrowed from Greek meaning advocacy or defense in a heavenly court</note> for them. They must say, "Forgive them," rather than prosecuting them or questioning their status and attainments. As it was researched, they thought regarding the children of Israel that they were worthy of being chosen for the upper realms in some ways. The intent of these words is that the Holy One, blessed be He, speaks with the angels after the Resurrection to bring ease and reveal the grace of His love for Israel before the eyes of all.

He says, "We have a little sister"—"little" refers to a smallness of value due to a lack of merit and the fulfillment of the Torah and its commandments in the past, while they were in this world and during the times of the exiles. "And she has no breasts"—meaning, she lacks the signs of spiritual maturity and proof of the perfection of their souls in His image. Furthermore, it refers to the merit and the refinement of the material of their bodies to support life, so that they might be worthy of the Great Day of Judgment that follows the Resurrection, and thus merit eternal life. In this state, the materiality of the world would not be able to harm them through the accusations <note>original: "kitrunam," referring to the spiritual prosecution or "Satanic" accusation in the heavenly court</note> of the "Princes of the Nations," as our Sages of blessed memory said regarding the "eternal walls" <note>The "Princes" or guardian angels of the nations are often depicted as walls or barriers</note> who are destined to prosecute and say: "Israel received the Torah, but where did they fulfill it?" and so on.

This is found in other places as well, specifically in the *Great Midrash* on this verse. They said: In the future, the ministers of the world’s nations will come and prosecute Israel before the Holy One, blessed be He, saying: "Master of the Universe! These [nations] worshipped idols, and these [Israel] worshipped idols; these committed sexual immorality, and these committed sexual immorality; these shed blood, and these shed blood. Why do these [the nations] descend to Gehenna <note>Gehenna: a place of spiritual purification or punishment in the afterlife</note> while these [Israel] do not?" This is the meaning of "What shall we do for our sister?"—how to protect her and turn the judgment in her favor on "the day she is spoken for," which is the Great Day of Judgment when the court is opened and the decree is sealed.

It is also possible to explain that since they will be alive and enduring forever in body and soul after the Resurrection, the material of the body will become refined. Its nature will transform into a subtle substance, a constitution fit for this purpose, allowing them to rise from level to level until they merit many perceptions and delights, to "behold the pleasantness of the Lord" and to see "by the light of the King's face" in an essential cleaving <note>original: "deveikut," the mystical state of attachment or union with the Divine</note>, as was chosen above. This requires great assistance. For even though the material of the body was refined in the dust and was renewed like "the dew of lights" at the time of Resurrection, it still requires great help. At the beginning, the body seems distant from this perfection; therefore, the Holy One, blessed be He, speaks with the angels about this matter to assure them and show them that they are worthy of this, and eventually they will reach the ultimate perfection.

Thus, "We have a little sister" refers to her status and value from the perspective of the material body, and "she has no breasts" refers to the lack of evidence of spirituality and the refined preparation of the feminine aspect in the material of the body, which is its brilliance, splendor, and beauty—likened to breasts which are the beauty of a woman's body. Or the intention of "breasts" may be the ability to produce wisdom and good deeds, and as the Midrash explains, it says she was not properly perfected through commandments and Torah to transform her physical matter into light and spirit. Everything goes to one place: "What shall we do for our sister?"—meaning, what assistance and help shall we provide for our sister on the day she is spoken for, to grant her eternal life in both body and soul?

<header>Song of Songs</header>

And a soul that comes to achieve these great and hidden attainments will not be hindered by the physical matter of the body. According to this path, it is possible to explain that in our gatherings of the righteous and the Congregation of Israel <note>original: "Knesset Yisrael," a mystical personification of the Jewish people</note> before the King, regarding the matter of the human body and corpse—which served as a sign for the soul—the Holy One, blessed be He, obscures its intention even according to the sign and the vanity <note>original: "hevel," meaning breath or futility</note> in the manner I wrote above; and this is not far-fetched.

->**"If she is a wall..." etc. The explanation of the Enlightener:**<-

<margin>With good discernment and knowledge of the soul, even if it has not been perfected as fully as required—meaning, through the Torah and the fulfillment of the 248 positive commandments <note>original: "ramach mitzvot aseh"</note> with the essential intent and subtle nuances.</margin>
**If she is a wall, we will build upon her**
**a battlement of silver; and if she is a door, we will enclose**
**her with a panel of cedar.**

And their parts; in any case, this is how one might easily be saved on the Great Day of Judgment and also easily reach the goal of purification and spirituality. But the main thing is whether the soul was perfected by "turning away from evil" <note>original: "sur me-ra," a reference to Psalm 34:14</note> and whether no breach was made in its fence by becoming soiled with iniquities and sins. Therefore, they said, **"If she is a wall,"** meaning she is fenced in like a high wall, such that the "Breaker"—who is Satan, the Evil Inclination <term>yetzer hara</term> <note>the internal impulse toward selfishness or sin</note>—has not climbed up to breach it and enter within her with bundles of great sins and trespasses. In such a case, **"we will build upon her a battlement of silver,"** meaning we will sustain her in the assembly and among the children of her life in the world. As the Sages of blessed memory explained in the Midrash, this refers to meriting eternal life, for the physical matter will be transformed into a lucidity and brightness like silver; this is the secret of the "battlement of silver." 

**"And if she is a door,"** meaning a breach was made in her and in her fence—a "door" through which the Breaker passed to soil her with iniquities and sins—the matter stands differently. But even so, **"we will enclose her with a panel of cedar,"** meaning as if one were to block the door with a cedar plank. The intention refers to all the harsh and evil decrees, the sufferings, and the exiles <note>original: "galuyot"</note> that passed over the Congregation of Israel. These forces acted upon them, and they emerged cleansed. This is similar to the verse, "I will call you to judgment," yet He says, "I will not uproot," as detailed in the *Book of Zohar* <note>The foundational work of Jewish mysticism</note>. This is the meaning of **"we will enclose her with a panel of cedar,"** which represents the sealing of iniquities so they are neither seen nor remembered anymore, like the sealing of a door. However, they are not quite as if they never existed at all, which is the comparison to a "wall" that was never breached. 

The text further compares this by saying **"a panel of cedar"** to suggest that when they are at this level, the matter stands for them to reach the level of a "battlement of silver," which is to endure for eternal life and to achieve great attainments in the Torah, the commandments, and the laws. For there, there will be no hinderance or dividing screen caused by physical matter—meaning one reaches the ultimate purity and lucidity, as I have written. Alternatively, one reaches the level of a "panel of cedar," for the cedar is mighty and strong and endures. So too shall the physical matter of the body be, for it will receive strength and permanence to endure for eternity, rising to a high level to achieve immense attainments. Yet, it will not be at the first level, for the brightness, refinement, and spirituality of the body's matter will not be equal to the second level <note>the level of the 'wall'</note>, as I have written. 

In my opinion, the reader may find my words difficult regarding the word **"door"** <note>original: "delet"</note>, as it does not say "entrance" <note>original: "petach"</note> but "door." A "door" refers to the actual panels that close the entrance. In truth, there is no difficulty here; even if the entrance is closed with a door, a breach has nonetheless been made there, and it is not the same as a wall. It is possible to explain that the expression "If [she is...]" regarding the visitation of judgment is not meant as a doubt—meaning either this or that—but rather a distinction between states. The intention is that if, from the perspective of our gatherings, we are like a wall...

<summary>This page provides a mystical interpretation of Song of Songs 8:9, comparing the spiritual integrity of the soul and the Congregation of Israel to either a "wall" or a "door." A "wall" represents a soul untainted by sin, earning a "battlement of silver" (spiritual lucidity), while a "door" represents a soul that has sinned but is repaired through suffering and exile, symbolized by "cedar."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mystical commentary on the Song of Songs, specifically focusing on the dialogue between God and the angels regarding the spiritual status of Israel (or the soul) during the era of the Resurrection and the final Judgment. It builds upon the previous discussion of how the physical body is refined into a spiritual vessel.</meta>

<header>Commentary</header>

<columns>2</columns>

**"We have a little sister, and she has no breasts;**
**what shall we do for our sister on the day**
->**when she shall be spoken for?"**<-

<column-break/>

"We have a little sister, etc." Behold, according to our path <note>original: "al pi darkenu," referring to the author's specific method of mystical interpretation</note>, the plain meaning of these matters is that these are the words of the Holy One, blessed be He <note>original: "HaKadosh Baruch Hu"</note>, speaking with the heavenly hosts <note>original: "hamonei ma'alah," the angelic camps</note> regarding the Congregation of Israel <note>original: "Knesset Yisrael," a mystical personification of the Jewish people</note>. He connected these words with

<column-break/>

the previous subject, as the arrival of the Messiah and this gift of resurrection occur after the Resurrection but before the Great Day of Judgment, as we shall explain in this book. This is to demonstrate the angels' good intentions toward Israel—to bring them benefit and to act as their advocates <note>original: "limed sanigorya," a legal term for defense in the divine court</note>. Heaven forbid one should say they would prosecute them <note>original: "yekatregu," to act as spiritual accusers</note>, or challenge their status and their spiritual attainments, as was their desire regarding the Children of Israel during the time they were chosen on high in some of these ways.

The intent of these words is that when the Holy One, blessed be He, consults with the angels after the Resurrection to revive and reveal His love for Israel before the eyes of all, He says: "We have a little sister"—meaning one small in value due to a lack of merit and the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments <note>original: "mitzvot"</note> in the past, while they were in this world and during the times of exile. "And she has no breasts," meaning there are no signs of maturity or a disposition toward the perfection of the powers of their souls; moreover, [this refers to] the merit and refinement of their physical matter <note>original: "homer gufam"</note> to assist their spirit in being saved from the Great Day of Judgment, which follows the Resurrection. This judgment is for the purification of those who merit eternal life, so that the materiality of the world will not be able to harm them through its spiritual accusations <note>original: "kitronam"</note>, as they suffered from most of the world's physical defects.

For in the future, the accusers are destined to prosecute and say: "Israel, who received the Torah—did they fulfill it?" and so on. Similarly, in other places, and specifically in the *Midrash Rabbah* <note>an ancient collection of homiletic biblical interpretations</note> on this verse, it is said: "In the time to come, the guardian princes of the nations of the world are destined to come and prosecute Israel before the Holy One, blessed be He. They will say: 'Master of the Universe! These [the nations] practiced idolatry, and these [Israel] practiced idolatry; these committed sexual immorality, and these committed sexual immorality; these shed blood, and these shed blood. Why do these go down to Gehenna <note>original: "Gehinnom," a place of spiritual cleansing</note>, while these do not go down?'" etc. And if so, what shall we do for our sister to protect her and argue for her merit on the day she is spoken for? This refers to the Great Day of Judgment, after the opening and closing of the legal proceedings <note>original: "petch u-khisgita b'dina," an Aramaic legal phrase referring to the court process</note>.

And it is possible to explain that since it is true that after the Resurrection they will live and endure forever in body and soul—because the physical matter of the body will be refined and become a subtle spiritual substance, possessing a disposition suitable for this, to ascend from level to level until one merits many attainments and worlds, to gaze upon the pleasantness of the Lord, and to return to the light of the King's face in essential union <note>original: "dvekut atzmi," the highest state of mystical attachment to God</note>, as was explained above—this requires great assistance. For even though the physical matter of the body was gathered in the dust and was restored by the "dew of lights" <note>original: "tal orot," a mystical reference to the dew of resurrection mentioned in Isaiah 26:19</note> at the time of the Resurrection, it still needs great assistance. Initially, it would seemingly be distant from perfection, as it is prone to being hindered from reaching this spiritual height. 

Therefore, the Holy One, blessed be He, speaks with the angels about this matter to bring them to demonstrate for themselves that Israel is worthy of this, and here they will reach the goal of this perfection. This is the meaning of "We have a little sister": small in her preparation and value because of her materiality. "And she has no breasts": the intention refers to the spirituality and refinement in the physical body, which is its splendor, light, glory, and beauty—likened to breasts, which are the beauty of a woman's body. Or the intention of "breasts" may be the capacity to produce spiritual influence and good deeds, which she lacks, as explained in the Midrash. This suggests she was not properly perfected through the commandments and the Torah to transform her physical matter into light and spirituality. Everything leads to one conclusion: "What shall we do for our sister?"—meaning, what assistance and help shall we provide for our sister on the day she is spoken for, to grant her eternal life in body...

<header>Song of Songs</header>
<sig>MS 16 2</sig>

### **Solomon had a vineyard at Baal-hamon; he gave the vineyard to the keepers; everyone for its fruit would bring a thousand pieces of silver.**

<margin>
"Solomon had a vineyard, etc." We seek to deepen our understanding of this path through the words of our Sages of blessed memory <note>original: "Zal"</note>, as they explained in the Midrash Rabbah: "This vineyard is Israel," as it is said, "For the vineyard of the Lord of Hosts is the House of Israel" (Isaiah 5:7). "To Solomon"—this refers to the King to whom peace <note>Hebrew: "Shalom," the root of Solomon/Shelomo</note> belongs, etc.
</margin>

Rabbi Saadia Gaon <note>original: "RSG," a preeminent 10th-century scholar and philosopher</note>, of blessed memory, wrote: "At Baal-hamon" refers to Jerusalem, for she is "great among the nations" and possesses a "great multitude" <note>Hebrew: "hamon," meaning a crowd or roar of people</note>. "He gave the vineyard to the keepers"—referring to the Diadochi <note>The Greek successors of Alexander the Great who ruled the regions surrounding Israel</note> and governors in every province and region. "Everyone for its fruit would bring"—this means all that they were able to collect from them in poll taxes <note>original: "gulgaliot," a tax paid per head</note>, land taxes <note>original: "arnoniot," an agricultural tax</note>, and other levies; they collected everything to bring into their own treasuries. This ends his quote. 

Behold, the intent here is to say that the people of Israel are worthy of reaching the ultimate state of refinement and purity <note>original: "dakut ve-zikukh," a process of spiritual distillation</note>. They were meant to improve their spiritual preparation in both body and soul, as stated above, for from the beginning they were very precious in the eyes of the Blessed One. They are His portion and His inheritance, for they are holy and pure in their root and essence. Furthermore, they were raised in a place of holiness and purity: Jerusalem, the Holy City. 

Even though they corrupted their way before the Blessed One, He did not utterly abandon them when their ways and deeds turned for the worse. Instead, they were sent into exile under harsh and evil decrees, suffering in both body and wealth. In this way, they "received from the hand of the Lord double for all their sins" (Isaiah 40:2). Through this suffering, they were refined from all the dross of iniquity and the rust of sin, returning to their original state and the root of their holiness and purity. 

This is especially true after the Resurrection <note>original: "Techiyah"</note>. Beyond the general suffering of the exiles, the body of each individual is purified while hidden in the dust, as was explained previously regarding the "Dew of Lights" of the Resurrection <note>original: "Tal Orot," a mystical dew that Jewish tradition teaches will be used to revive the dead</note>. Afterward, they are worthy of every holy thing, for they are renewed in holiness and purity, prepared to ascend to higher levels without end.

It is also possible to say that these words are intended to show that it is just to execute judgment upon those who deny God—those who enslaved Israel with all the might and strength of these difficult and evil days. They claim to have rightfully enslaved the holy children, the children of the Living God, His inherited portion. These nations took them in ancient times to enslave them in body and wealth during harsh and powerful eras. However, even though it was by His permission—for the Blessed One "gave the vineyard to the keepers" due to the people's evil deeds—the nations were too strong and exceeded the proper measure. This is like the verse: "I was but a little angry, and they helped for evil" (Zechariah 1:15). Consequently, their punishment will be greater than their gain, and they will be bound in chains on the Great Day of Judgment. 

In this way, after the previous verses spoke of the promise of the benefit destined for Israel in the life of the World to Come—showing there is no argument to withhold it from them—the text now speaks of the promise of the damage that should be done to those who enslaved them. This is to take vengeance on their enemies and to show that they are deserving of harsh and strong judgments, based on honest and clear arguments before the eyes of all the nations.

One may also interpret "Solomon had a vineyard, etc." as referring to the Torah, which is the essential and primary cause for the Resurrection and the World to Come. As our Sages said at the end of Tractate Ketubot: Rabbi Eleazar said, "The unlearned <note>original: "amrei aratzot," those who do not study Torah</note> will not live [at the Resurrection]... but I derive it from another verse: 'For Your dew is the dew of lights' (Isaiah 26:19). Anyone who uses the 'Light of Torah,' the Light of Torah will revive him; but for anyone who does not use the Light of Torah, the Light of Torah will not revive him." The Talmud concludes there that those who support Torah scholars with their wealth will also merit the Resurrection of the Dead as a reward for sustaining the Torah. In this way, if there is no Torah, there is no Resurrection. This also applies to the afterlife, as it is written in the Zohar (Section Vayeshev): at the time when the service of God is completed, that is the time they are destined to rise and be revived...

<summary>This page interprets the "vineyard" of Song of Songs 8:11 as the people of Israel and the Torah. It explains that Israel’s suffering in exile serves as a spiritual refinement, preparing them for the Resurrection, while also justifying the eventual divine punishment of the nations who overstepped their role as "keepers" of the vineyard.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the Song of Songs, moving from the description of the soul's "silver" and "cedar" qualities (verse 8:9) to the triumphant declaration of spiritual maturity and reconciliation in verse 8:10. It focuses on the transformative power of repentance and the Day of Atonement.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>Commentary</header>

...their being spiritual and inclusive, a part of the Divine from above <note>original: "Helek Eloha Mima'al"</note>, and having regard for the hidden aspects within the imagination. The "door" mentioned previously allows for the Holy One, blessed be He, to be doubled <note>i.e., to manifest His presence more intensely</note> with you. Within them exists the thickness and coarseness of material dust, whether in large or small measure, as our Sages of blessed memory <note>original: "Zal"</note> intended when they spoke of being "impoverished of commandments." It was already explained above that it would be a loss if their levels of perception were in perfect equality, like a physical leveling. Therefore, regarding the variation of spiritual virtues, it said "a door of silver" and "a board of cedar."

---

<margin>
"I am a wall, etc." The Congregation of Israel <note>original: "Knesset Yisrael," the personification of the Jewish people</note> returns in her spirit, knowing the "give and take" of the Holy One, blessed be He, with the Heavenly Host <note>original: "Pamalia shel Ma'alah"</note> regarding the change in levels. All this is the meaning of "I am a wall."
</margin>

# I am a wall, and my breasts like towers; then was I in his eyes as one that found peace.

---

That is to say: even though I have fallen into the deep mire of iniquity and the filth of sins, and I was not refined in the furnace of suffering in this world, behold, the entire past is as if it never existed at all. It is as if I never sinned, for there is a power to build through the great abundance of suffering and harsh decrees. Furthermore, there is the matter of Repentance <note>original: "Teshuvah"</note>, atonement, and the purity of the Day of Atonement <note>original: "Yom HaKippurim"</note every single year. This is a great thing: to cleanse and purify Israel and return them to their original tension <note>meaning their original state of spiritual readiness</note> as if they never sinned.

As we find in the Midrash on the verse "Stay, let us go" <note>original: "Layu[n] lekhu nitekhah"</note>—meaning the Holy One, blessed be He, responds favorably against the accusations of the seventy Princes <note>the guardian angels</note> of the world's nations. As we explained above: "What does this 'little one' lack?"—referring to the small child for whom one does not wait. So it is with Israel: though they become soiled throughout the year, the Day of Atonement comes and atones for them, as it is written: "For on this day He shall atone for you." This comparison to a "little one" is very difficult <note>to reconcile</note>, for they have no strength at all, but this is merely a metaphor. Just as the filth of a debt of sin does not take hold in a small child and leaves no mark, so does the atonement of the Day of Atonement purify them so that it is as if they did not sin and no mark remains at all—as if there were no iniquity or sin here from the beginning. 

This is the meaning of "I am a wall" regarding the matter of "turning from evil" and "doing good." And "my breasts like towers" refers to the attainment of spiritual levels and perfection <note>original: "Shlemut"</note>. The intention is that since there is no sin here to act as a barrier, there is no place for the residue of the body's material coarseness. There is nothing to prevent the material of the body from being ready to shine and radiate like the "brightness of the firmament" <note>original: "Zohar HaRakia"</note> and to rise from level to level until it becomes entirely spiritual through the fulfillment of the Torah and the commandments <note>original: "Mitzvot"</note>. When the sin that previously darkened them is removed, they are further purified. 

Even if one lacks the perfection of levels attained through study and the seclusion <note>original: "Prishut," ascetic withdrawal</note> through which one gazes at the Torah and cleaves to the Divine—for by attaining spirituality, perfection is drawn into the soul—this perfection draws out even more hidden perceptions. This continues forever until one reaches the edge of perfection and perception. This is the meaning of "my breasts like towers," referring to the signs of greatness which are the markers of maturity and perfection. 

Therefore, she says: "then was I in his eyes as one that found peace," because the Holy One, blessed be He, agreed with her words with the consent of the entire Heavenly Host. For she is certainly worthy of every Divine and lofty thing. It is as if her strength was already seen, and she attained a cleaving <note>original: "Dveikut"</note> to the Holy One, blessed be He, who is called "Peace" <note>original: "Shalom"</note>, as is known. For this reason, there is no accuser or adversary, and no accusation at all. This is "then was I in his eyes as one that found peace."

<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of the Song of Songs, moving from the soul's declaration of its own spiritual maturity (the "wall" and "towers") to the distribution of rewards for those who guard the "vineyard" of the Torah. It transitions into the final verses of the book, focusing on the intimate relationship between God, Israel, and the angels in the World to Come.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Song of Songs</header>

On your part, it is established for them in their hand. For when we come to the accounting of what is fitting for them, we find that the "thousand is for you, O Solomon" <note>original: "Ha-eleph lecha Shlomo"</note>—referring to the King to whom peace <note>original: "Shalom," a play on the name Solomon</note> belongs—who sustains Israel, those who "guard its fruit" <note>original: "notrim et piryo"</note> in hand. For by strict law, they might not be worthy to receive the reward prepared for them. The words of our Sages of blessed memory <note>original: "Chazal"</note> are also correct in this regard. This follows the second explanation of the first verse, which discussed the necessity of caution regarding the "walls" <note>protective boundaries of religious law</note> in this world; look closely and you will find this to be true.

According to the second path we explained, where the "vineyard" refers to the Torah, it says that the words of this verse are the words of Israel speaking to the nations before the Blessed One: "My vineyard, which is mine, is before me." My Torah, which belongs to the world, was mine even in the exiles and through hardships, when we were tossed about like the poor and the stranger. Now, it is before me, clear and explained. All has chosen the good, attaining perfection "in hand" <note>original: "be-chofin," meaning tangibly or fully possessed</note>. For now I have come to a full understanding, seeing that the little I had before in my grasp was minimal compared to what I have now received from it. I have already said that it is beyond human capacity to grasp this in this world "in hand." For my palate says: "The thousand is for you, Solomon"—meaning the "Aleph" <note>the first letter, or "Chief"</note> of God <note>original: "Helek Eloha"</note> which was yours in the world that was crowned <note>the world of the divine</note>. What they grasped of it in this world is but a small portion, like "two hundred" alone. This is the meaning of "He who sustains those who guard its fruit."

You can see that in this interpretation as well, all the words of the verse are the words of another <note>Israel speaking to God/Nations</note>, and it is not a sharp break in the verse as was publicized in the second explanation of the previous path. Also here, it is possible to interpret this regarding the matter of Divine help and assistance, to show that Israel holds the Giving of the Torah in high regard because of the recompense and reward given to them. This reward is an act of pure grace, for they are only worthy of it in a small measure "in exchange" <note>original: "be-chelef"</note>, as has been explained. In this way, the verse calls out to the children of Israel, and it is easy to understand.

# Thou that dwellest in the gardens, the companions hearken <margin>“Thou that dwellest, etc.” Behold, some of these words were spoken by Solomon...</margin>
# to thy voice: cause me to hear it. <margin>...through the Holy Spirit, regarding the conclusion and the aftermath...</margin>

...of the Great Day of Judgment, when the children of Israel will be finished <note>purified</note> and will merit the life of the World to Come <note>original: "Olam Ha-Ba"</note>—a life eternal and enduring. In the future, the Holy One, blessed be He, will delight with the righteous and with the study of their Torah, and He will be strengthened by their voice, until He says: "Thou that dwellest in the gardens"—referring to the eighteen congregations <note>original: "chai kinasiyot"</note>—the one who dwells in the Upper Garden and the Lower Garden <note>the levels of Paradise</note>. They are the "companions" <note>original: "chaverim"</note> and His heavenly retinue <note>original: "pamalia dileyh"</note> who listen to your voice. Not only this, but they long and yearn to hear our righteousness. For the "Companion" <note>God</note> is the one who delights in your voice even more than they do, with greater excellence. Therefore, He says, "Cause me to hear it," using the singular form.

The reason why He calls the ministering angels "companions" was explained in the *Midrash Rabbati* <note>The Great Midrash</note> of blessed memory. Bar Kappara said: Why does He call the ministering angels companions? Because among them there is no enmity, jealousy, hatred, or rivalry, etc. They further said there in the Midrash: When Israel enters the synagogues and recites the *Shema* <note>the central declaration of Jewish faith</note> with focused intent, with one voice, one mind, and one melody, the Holy One, blessed be He, says to them: "Thou that dwellest in the gardens, when you recite, the companions—My beloved ones and My heavenly retinue—listen to your voice; cause Me to hear it."

Behold, they have explained that the "companions" refers back to "the one who dwells in the gardens." How good and how pleasant this fits the sequence according to our path, which I wrote regarding the time of the life of the World to Come. For then, all will be in a state of affection, all will be "companions"; there will be no Satan and no Evil Inclination <note>original: "Yetzer Hara"</note> to cast jealousy, [lack of] affection, or rivalry among them. Rather, all will be pure, clear, and shining like the brilliance of the firmament, turning toward perfection to behold the pleasantness of the Lord, clinging together in His service with love, fear, and clear purity. And it is possible to interpret "gardens" as the levels of the Lower Garden <note>Eden</note>, for many...

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of the Song of Songs, specifically focusing on the rewards for those who study Torah and remain faithful during the exile. It explores the transition from the physical "vineyard" to the spiritual "Palace of Life" and the resurrection of the dead.</meta>

### Commentary

In the beginning, before all other people of the world, those who rise from the "right hand" of the Torah <note>A reference to the verse "From His right hand went a fiery law for them" (Deuteronomy 33:2).</note> and cherish it—because they occupied themselves with the "waters" of the Torah and strengthened themselves in the **Palace of Life**—they shall be the first to go forth. The power of life will cause them to rise first. As has been stated regarding every doubt that flees from what is distant: it is clear that this is an essential principle attached to the matter of the **Resurrection of the Dead**. Therefore, while speaking of the resurrection, the text speaks in a balanced script regarding the Torah and in praise of the <term>residue</term> <gloss>that they fulfilled</gloss>—they fulfilled it and occupied themselves with it until they merited the result brought about by it, as mentioned. This is the secret of the verse: *“Solomon had a vineyard.”* He labeled the Torah with the name "vineyard," for just as a vineyard exists for enjoyment, with leaves and wine that "gladdens God and men," so too does the Torah have for its builders <note>Meaning the scholars/students.</note> layers within its "garments"—the revealed, the <insert>more</insert> revealed, and the inner secrets. Behold, at first, the Torah was among the celestial beings; this is the secret of the phrase *“Solomon had a vineyard,”* to say that the King to whom peace <note>original: "Shalom," a pun on Solomon's name</note> belongs—the Multitude of Intentionality over the broad expanse of the "waters" <note>The spiritual realms</note> located in the angelic forces and the heavenly hosts—He gave the vineyard (which is the Torah) to the **keepers**. There, they are called keepers because they guard it. And behold, every man who merits his nation shall "bring for its fruit a thousand pieces of silver." Not literally for its fruit, but for his labor in the Torah. "A thousand pieces of silver" is a metaphor for the greatness of the reward and the abundance of delights fitting for those who occupy themselves with the Torah and fulfill its statutes. Also, "silver" <note>original: "kesef"</note> is a metaphor for the merit of whiteness, purity, and clarity. It is also possible to say that "a thousand pieces of silver" refers to the <term>thousand worlds of longing</term> <note>original Aramaic: "aleph almin de-khisuffin," a Kabbalistic concept of spiritual reward/desire</note> prepared and arranged for the righteous who see the Torah and for those who support it. This is not meant to be an exact number, but rather indicates the abundance and greatness as mentioned, according to the seventh path.

-> My vineyard, which is mine, is before me: the thousand is for you, O Solomon, <-
-> and two hundred for those who guard its fruit. <-

This is to say that the people of Israel, even though they have now been exiled and tossed about in a bitter and difficult wandering—to the point that they were struck for their sins twofold—nevertheless, their character has not changed among the nations. Not only did their "taste" <note>Their spiritual essence</note> remain within them and their "scent" did not change to cast off the yoke of the Covenant from upon them, but they remained attached to the Torah and the Divine Service to which they were accustomed; they were there to sanctify the Heavenly Name. In this way, they are worthy to stand before Him, living and enduring for all days. This is the secret of "My vineyard, which is mine, is before me"—all my vineyard is named for the beginning, the delight, and the ancient sweetness mentioned above. "Before me" refers to the time of their being in exile, for they were always suffering the yoke of His Kingdom and His presence as chosen, in a way that they are worthy to stand before Him all their days and receive their full reward. Specifically, in the distribution of reward, each and every one has a role. For the Teacher (represented by Solomon, who was the teacher of all Israel, as it is written: "And Solomon was wiser than all men," and it is written of him that he "probed and sought out and taught the people knowledge") shall take a thousand, and the student takes two hundred. This is the secret of "two hundred for those who guard its fruit." From here we learn that there are levels in the entering of reward, and each one will take according to his value and level—such as those who learn through **suffering** who take two hundred, and those who learn without suffering who also take two hundred <note>The author likely means there are specific ratios for different types of effort</note>. These things, in the plain meaning of the verse, follow the path of the Midrash of our Rabbis; look into it. And it is possible to explain that the plain meaning of the verse refers to the "walls of the world" <note>The nations/protectors of civilization</note>, as **Rashi** (of blessed memory) explained in his commentary. The intention is that it is the way of the nations to say to the Holy One, Blessed be He: "Do not be so glorified and praised through Israel, for all of this was not from their own knowledge or great strength; rather, it was because of Your giving them the Torah that they already cast off the yoke..."

<summary>This page provides a homiletic commentary on Song of Songs 8:11-12, interpreting the "vineyard" as the Torah and discussing the rewards awaiting the Jewish people for their faithfulness during exile. It establishes a hierarchy of spiritual reward, comparing the teacher's merit to the student's, and linking these rewards to the future resurrection of the dead.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Solomon, Torah, Vineyard, Reward, Resurrection, Exile, Silver, Rashi, Suffering, Kabbalah</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the commentary on the Song of Songs. The previous page served as an index for various mystical and rabbinic interpretations found earlier in the manuscript. This final section provides a homiletic conclusion to the biblical book, focusing on the intimate relationship between God and the people of Israel compared to the angelic hosts.</meta>

# Commentary

For there, the place is more firmly established to sustain both body and soul than in the Upper Garden <note>The "Upper Garden" refers to the celestial Garden of Eden/Paradise.</note>. Furthermore, in every place where they stand, it shall be referred to by the name "Garden," in the sense that they take pleasure and delight there with every kind of delight and supreme spiritual attainment—much like a person who takes pleasure and delights within a physical garden among its plantings and trees, enjoying their shade and their fruit. This matter is clear.

->**“Hurry away, my beloved, and be like a gazelle or a young stag upon the mountains of spices.” Be strong!**<- <note>This is the final verse of the Song of Songs (8:14), often recited as a prayer for redemption.</note>

"Hurry away, my beloved," etc. The sages said in the Midrash <note>Rabbinic homiletic literature</note>... following our own path: the Assembly of Israel <term>Knesset Yisrael; the personified spiritual community of Israel</term> awakened the passion of the Holy One, Blessed be He, toward her and His desire for her voice and the voice of her engagement with the Torah. Even though He is with His heavenly entourage and His companionship is with the upper beings, and the heavenly multitude is highly esteemed by Him, may He be blessed, she says: "Hurry away, my beloved." This means: I know that the angels and the celestial beings also desire and long for Your company and Your dwelling among them, and they seek to be close to You. However, leave the dwelling of Your presence among the angels and be with us, so that we may be more closely attached to You than they are. "Hurry away, my beloved" from the ministering angels and cling to us. This should happen with the swiftness of a "gazelle," which is the fastest of animals. This is why it says, "and be like a gazelle or a young stag." 

And if the general Assembly of Israel does not possess enough merit for this, then do it for the sake of the distinguished righteous ones <term>Tzaddikim</term> among us, and for the sake of the Patriarchs of the world <term>Avot HaOlam; Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</term> who are the high mountains in Torah and commandments, and whose fragrance wafts like spices even to distant places. This is the meaning of "upon the mountains of spices." The Midrash similarly explained in its own way: "upon the mountains of spices"—there was a pleasant scent for You through the merit of our ancestors, whose scent rises before You like spices.

It is also possible to interpret "be like a gazelle" according to the saying of our sages: "Just as a gazelle, when it sleeps, keeps one eye open and one eye closed," etc. The intention is that the Assembly of Israel said: "Even though I say 'Hurry away, my beloved' from the ministering angels, let this 'fleeing' not be a total removal of Your providence and Your constant flow of goodness from them. Rather, be with them like the image of a single open eye, whereas toward us, You shall look with both eyes," representing a deeper, more essential, and far greater attachment to us than to them. 

This is what is meant by the Midrash at the end of the verse, which are the words of the Holy One, Blessed be He. Rabbi Simon said: "The Holy One, Blessed be He, said: Wait for Me until I return in judgment upon their princes," etc. Regarding this, the text suggests that the Holy One, Blessed be He, responded as if agreeing and consenting to the request of the Assembly of Israel, saying: "I shall do so; I will make My dwelling with you, and you shall be attached to Me with an essential bond because of the 'mountains of spices.'" As explained, these are the righteous, the pious, and the blameless individuals who exist in every single generation, and the Patriarchs of the world, whose scent of Torah and good deeds wafts like spices.

->With the Help of God, May He be Blessed<-
->With Good Fortune<-
->So may it be His Will<-
->Rest and Peace<-

The commentary on the Song of Songs is finished and completed; praise and glory to the Creator of all things.

Blessed is the Merciful One who helped us from the beginning until now. So may the Name <note>Hashem/God</note> grant us merit to finish what we have started, and to produce other books, and to meditate upon the Torah of the Lord—I, my offspring, and my offspring’s offspring forever. Amen.

<summary>This page concludes the commentary on the Song of Songs, interpreting the final verse as a plea from Israel to God to prioritize His relationship with humanity over the angels. The author concludes with a traditional scribe's prayer for the merit to continue writing and studying Torah.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Knesset Yisrael, God and Israel, angels vs humans, Patriarchs, righteous ones, mountains of spices, gazelle metaphor, colophon, scribal prayer</keywords>

The page ends with a traditional Hebrew closing formula and a formal printing license from Venice, typical of 16th-18th century Jewish book production.</meta>

# Index

Interpretation of the statement: Rabbi Abba bar Kahana said, etc. "What is the nature of a stylus <note>Hebrew: *churta*, a writing or engraving tool</note> for erasing a tower," etc.: Folio 32b

Interpretation of the words of our Sages <note>Hebrew acronym: *Chazal*, referring to the ancient Rabbis of the Talmudic era</note> on the verse: "How is your beloved better than another?": Folio 36

Interpretation of the statement: "They said to her: To me, in breeches <note>Hebrew: *michnasim*</note>," etc.: Folio 35a

Interpretation of the statement: "Establishing this world, which is destined for a hundred days," etc.: Folio 39a

Interpretation of the statement: "You are beautiful, my love, as Tirzah; like a table, God desires <note>A wordplay on the city name *Tirzah* and the Hebrew root *ratzah*, meaning 'to desire'</note> you in the city," etc.: Folio 43a

Interpretation of the statement: Rabbi Levi said, "Horses and medicines, behold, he erased and forty," etc.: Folio 66b

And the interpretation of the statement: "Horses are the kingdom; they trembled <note>Hebrew: *chalu*</note>, horses, images," etc. And the interpretation: "Horses are the kingdom; they trembled, horses, companies of righteous men": Folio 44a

Interpretation of the statement: Rabbi Samuel bar Chiyya bar Yudan in the name of Rabbi Hanina: "Four times it is written here *Sokhi, Sokhi* <note>Likely referring to 'booths' or 'lookouts'</note> corresponding to the four kingdoms," etc. <note>The 'Four Kingdoms' is a common Jewish historical-mystical concept referring to the four empires that exiled the Jewish people: Babylon, Persia, Greece, and Rome.</note>: Folio 47b

Interpretation of the statement: Rabbi Tavyomi says, "They make the sins of Israel kings <note>Or 'they cover/counsel,' a homily on the transformation of sins</note>," etc.: Folio 49b

Interpretation of the statement: "Just as with this creature, while it sleeps, one eye remains open," etc. <note>A metaphor often used to describe God's providence over Israel.</note>: Folio 63b
